## Liber I

{{1}} «Thou whom they proclaim to sing in bridal chambers, born of a Camena mother, sacred bond of the gods, who, binding discordant seeds in arcane chains and cherishing dissonant unions in a hallowed embrace, dost link the elements by turns, wed the universe, and join the breath of mind to bodies by an approved covenant beneath which Nature is yoked, reconciling the sexes and fostering fidelity under love; O comely Hymenaeus, chiefest care of Cypris—for from thee gleaming Cupid flashes with ardent countenance—whether it hath pleased thee to lead the choral dance because Bacchus is thy sire, or thou holdest it from thy mother to sing beside the nuptial couch, or the threefold Grace hath granted to her kinsman to wreathe flowering thresholds with vernal garlands: Calliopea, composing the marriage of the gods, attests that thou dost nod assent at the auspice of her song».

{{2}} While I was frequently chanting these little verses to Hymenaeus, laboring over something unlooked-for and hitherto untouched, Martianus—his crown sprinkled with whitening hairs and enrolled in the decuries by lustral increments of years—unable to endure my babbling of idle trifles, broke in, saying: «What is this, my father, that thou makest haste to sing a theme not yet divulged, and after the manner of a winking hierophant utterest hymns (*ὑμνολογίζεις*) before unbarring the doors and threshold? Nay rather, declare what thou bringest, and reveal to what end thy prelude hath sounded». «Truly», said I, «art thou wholly bereft of sense, and, recognizing the waking-song (*ἐγέρσιμον*) of a manifestly lucid work, dost thou yet savor of twilight obscurity? Is it not plain that when Hymenaeus tastes the first offering, the arranged nuptials leap forth? Yet if, as a hasty inquisitor, thou seekest from the vein of what wellspring these conceptions have flowed forth, I shall unfold to thee—unless its prolixity dismay thee—the fable which Satire, inventing during a winter’s vigil, taught to the fading lamps and to me».

{{3}} When among the gods sacred marriages were being celebrated, yielding abundant offspring on every side, and illustrious children along with an ethereal multitude of sweet grandchildren possessed one another in a certain heavenly embrace and covenant—and most of all, when the nuptial tie blessed the mightiest deities, and loquacious humanity, devoted to the world, leaped forth at every crossroads to proclaim it, while the poets, chiefly following the Oeagrian citharist and the sweet-voiced old age of the blind Maeonian, harmonized everywhere upon epic and lyric page, declaring that nothing among ethereal delights was sweeter to Jove than his single consort; and to these reports there acceded a readier faith, which at the prompting of haruspicy summons aged pontiffs to bear witness that whenever Jove, while mortals’ vows hung suspended in the anxious maze of cares, inexorably denied much despite their sacrificial victims, his matron, once entreated, would come forth and, soothing him by the embraces and behest of a persuasive spouse, set aside whatever he had dictated by pronounced decree under the keeping of the Fates’ clenched hand;

{{4}} and report bore witness not only that the King of the Celestials was devoted to his wife, but that this same purpose held for Dis, and for Portunus, and that it was certain Gradivus burned with love for his spouse Nerio Neria (nor was Aesculapius’s affection unlike to this), and that the gloomiest elder of the gods was won over by a like persuasion through his consort Ops and a placated Cybele (while Janus marvels at Angerona in either of his aspects); for the Memphitic queen so hung upon her husband that, beset with perpetual mourning, she was never content merely to have found him.

{{5}} Moved and stirred, therefore, by this rumor and by these reciprocal loves of the gods, the Cyllenian—and at the same time because, while obeying the many, he beheld the affections and bridal chambers of all—resolved to take a wife. His anxious mother had urged him toward this resolve when, in his annual circuit through the zodiac, he greeted her among the number of the Pleiades, especially since his body, exercised in the palaestra and by frequent errands, gleamed with a certain manly amplitude in brawny muscles wrought to the excellence of youthful vigor, and his now downy cheeks no longer suffered him to go half-naked, clad only in a scant chlamys that left the rest uncovered and veiled but the tip of his shoulders, without provoking the loud laughter of Cypris. By a reasoned purpose, therefore, he determined to banish celibacy.

{{6}} Accordingly, as befitted the dignity of his diligence, he weighed all candidates in the balance of a protracted deliberation, considering whom it were meet to take. For Sophia herself he desired with a wondrous ardor, because she is prudent and holy, more inviolate and fairer than all maidens; yet because she seemed the foster-sister of his own sister and her friend by an indivisible bond, and thus to have passed over likewise into the company of the unwed, it did not please him to match himself with her to the injury of Pallas. No lesser brilliance of desirable beauty and grace had inflamed him toward Mantice also; for both nobility of lineage (since she is the elder of Pronoea’s daughters) and a foresighted genius of keen prudence commended her. Yet in those very days, by chance, through the impatience of an immense love, she had pursued the youth of her own accord and been joined to Apollo.

{{7}} He wished at least to sue for the daughter of Endelechia and the Sun, because she was comely in the highest degree and had been reared with great care by the gods; for to Psyche (*Ψυχή*) herself, on her birthday, the deities invited to the banquet had bestowed many gifts. Jupiter, indeed, set upon her head a diadem which he had taken from his more honored daughter, Eternity. Juno too had added to her locks a nuptial fillet wrought from a gleaming vein of purer gold. Tritonia also, having unfastened her under-tunic, covered the maiden—herself a maiden—with a veil and a flame-hued breast-band of scarlet, and with the very mantle of her sacred and prudent breast. The Delian likewise, as he habitually bears a laurel branch, showed her with that same divining and conjectural wand the flight of birds, the hurling of thunderbolts, and the very courses of the heaven and the stars. Urania, moreover, with clement benignity bestowed a resplendent mirror which Sophia had dedicated among the votive offerings in her shrine, wherein recognizing herself again she might even seek out her own origin. The Lemnian artificer too kindled for her little fires of sleepless perpetuity, lest she be overwhelmed by murky shadows and blind night. Aphrodite, in turn, applied every allurement around all her senses; for she taught her, anointed with unguents and wreathed with flowers, to feed upon and be cherished by fragrant breath, soothed her with honey, and persuaded her to gape after gold and necklaces and to bind her limbs in the affectation of lofty honor. Then, as she rested, she applied rattles and tinklings wherewith to lull an infant to sleep. Furthermore, lest any moment pass without allurement and delight, she placed beneath the itch of desire a titillating pleasure about the lowest parts of the body. Yet the Cyllenian himself had given her a chariot and winged wheels wherewith she might course at wondrous speed, although Memory weighed her down, bound fast with fetters of gold. Thus the Arcadian, bereft of the former maidens, longed to take in marriage Psyche (*Ψυχή*), enriched with supernal gifts and adorned by the manifold contributions of the celestials. But Virtue, who happened to be clinging close to the Cyllenian, announced almost in tears that she had been snatched away from her companionship into the power of a quiver-bearing, winged god, and was held captive by Cupid in adamantine bonds.

{{8}} While the allurements of a deliberated choice thus frustrated him concerning the bridal chambers of these maidens, and no other readily occurred to his mind who might be chosen with fitting parity as daughter-in-law to the Thunderer, Virtue suggested that he deliberate further, and that he ought neither to decide anything without Apollo’s counsel nor was it lawful to stray from his company, since as he measured out the zodiacal inns in advance, Apollo never suffered him to be absent by a monthly precursor’s course. It was resolved, therefore, that in whatsoever place his brother might be, they should seek him out.

{{9}} And thereupon, according to custom, he entrusted his winged wand to Virtue, so that she might traverse the innermost recesses of the universe and burst into the ethereal retreats at equal speed with himself. He bound the golden winged sandals to his own feet, and now in shrines where either oracles were poured forth in oblique riddles or, after the announced slaughter of cattle and the slicing of sacrificial entrails, the vitals spoke, and where lots were wont to issue forth or responses to resound, they sought him with keen inquiry.

{{10}} Yet in these pinnacles and caverns of sanctuaries—widowed of their god save for a very few leaves of withered laurel and half-torn fillets which, in the Cumaean grotto after the Sibyl’s time, the gnawing of moths and decay were consuming—nothing of him could be found. Through the tracts of air as well, wherein he was wont to shape the varied courses of birds, the tongues of prophetic fowl, and the omens of swift-winged flight, he was sought in vain and to no purpose. For long since, offended by the contamination of those requiring admonition, the Pythian had disdained to be styled “augur.” Likewise they tracked him to Helicon, Delos, and Lycia; but in one place they found laurels in first bloom alongside parched ivy, in another a rotting tripod, slippers moldy with neglect, and the obliterated memory of prophecies.

{{11}} At length, at the report of Fame, they learned that the Parnassian crag was rejoicing in Phoebus. Although men declared that thence too he later migrated to a secret retreat of an Indian mountain and a cliff overshadowed by perpetual cloud, nevertheless at that time they came together at the Cirrhaean recesses and the loquacious caverns of the hallowed grotto. There stood round about in order whatsoever awaits the ages to come: the Fortunes of cities and of nations, of all kings and of every people. Some were seen fleeing after measuring out a completed course; others stood still before the gaze, and very many were arriving, while for not a few their severed span vanished afar so swiftly that it seemed like the incredible breath of smoky mist. Amid these wondrous spectacles and the courses and motions of the Fortunes, a melodious modulation of the groves also rustled with a certain musical percussion beneath the whispering breezes. For the loftier crowns of the tall trees, stretched taut as it were, rebounded with a shrill pitch; whereas whatever lay near and close to the earth with drooping branches was shaken by a hoarse depth of tone. Meanwhile, the middle portions, linked by fixed proportions in consonant harmonies of the octave (*duplis*), the fifth (*sesqualteris*), the fourth (*sesquitertiis*), and even the whole tone (*sesquioctavis*) without discrepancy at their junctures—even though quarter-tone diesis intervals (*limmata*) intervened—sang together in concord. Thus it came to pass that that grove resounded with the entire harmony and song of the celestials through the congruence of its modulations.

{{12}} As the Cyllenian expounded this, Virtue learned that in heaven too the spheres by a like ratio either utter symphonies or accord in accompanying harmonies. Nor was it any wonder that Apollo’s wood harmonized by so fixed a proportion, since the same Delian also modulates the spheres of heaven in the Sun—whence it is that there he is called Phoebus, and here Auricomus, the Golden-Haired;

{{13}} (for the august head of the Sun, suffused and encircled with flaming rays, imitates as it were the gilded locks of a ruddy crown), hence too Sagittarius, the Archer, and hence Vulnificus, the Wound-Dealer, because he is able to pierce what is struck by the javelins of his rays.

{{14}} Furthermore, the Cyllenian pointed out to Virtue certain rivers flowing down from heaven, which he declared must be crossed in order that they might reach the god himself whom it was their care to find. Yet a wave of discordant currents of diverse hues swept those same rivers along; for the first whirlpool, of wider and more sprawling compass, lingered with a misty volume of livid water and exceedingly chill and sluggish courses. Further inward, another river, gentle in every part and tranquil in motion like milk and white light, rolled forth silvery waves. A third, however, glowing crimson with excessive and ruddy fire, twisted its headlong, crashing courses in hurried rapidity, sulphurous with panting speed. The one that followed this was gilded and gleaming, glowing red with flashing flames; yet through the diversity of the streams, being joined on either side by certain intermingled rivulets, it tempered itself insofar as moderation held the balance. Further inward still, a river resplendent more purely than electrum shone forth, which that stationary throng of Fortunes desired above all others: some had been allured by its fragrance and exhalation, others soothed by the melodious modulations of its gentle wave, while very many thirsted for a taste and draught from that same sweetest flood. Nor were there wanting those who longed to be cherished and laved in that water and to cast themselves into it. Besides these, two narrower streams of small bend and compass swept along further inward, each of which, colored according to the proximity and border of the others, drew a scant draught of its own savor through much mutable admixture. For the one was borne along hasty with excessive speed, yet for the most part halting and gliding backward; while the other, bearing a kind of wellspring of waves and wandering in winding bends, foamed with all the seeds of flowing waters.

{{15}} These rivers of diverse-colored courses, therefore, first encompassed the aforesaid Fortunes of states and nations with immense bends. Then the diverse and violent rapidity of the waves, pervading each one singly, dragged them by unforeseen force in swift eddies down the headlong descents of a sloping channel, in such wise that one stream frequently poured those same Fortunes over into another river, and whichever Fortune the one had harassed and worn down by prolonged collision, the other either restored to the bank or submerged in its flood. Yet those sanguine or dark-blue whirlpools did not sweep away all the Fortunes enwrapped within them. For the most part, the resplendent wave of that milky stream bore them aloft, suddenly snatched up upon the crest of a towering expanse; sometimes too, after they had been raised on high and held suspended by a loftier billow, it cast them back into that surge of bloody semblance, or spat them out into the livid torrent to be devoured by its pitch-black chasm. By an alternating commixture of the rivers, then, that populace of Fortunes was driven to and fro; nor indeed was there any at all that remained immune from every onset or kept holiday from every flood.

{{16}} At last Virtue, following the Cyllenian, while she alone crossed all the streams undaunted, though they dashed against her with a mighty roar, yet could not overwhelm her. Finally, beyond the rivers, which were borne toward a certain spectacle of Phoebus, Mercury halted together with Virtue. And then they beheld the son of Leto seated upon a lofty and steep dais, uncovering in turn and by alternating inspections four small covered urns before him, which were fashioned of diverse appearance and metals. For one seemed to be of harder iron, so far as could be conjectured; another of the brighter substance of silver; the third of the molten strength of livid lead; while the one nearer to the god gleamed with the sheen of translucent glass. Each one, moreover, bore certain seeds and elements of things.

{{17}} For a fiercer flame, drawn from the very fuel of Cecaumene, panted forth from the aforesaid urn of iron, which nevertheless was called the Crown of Mulcifer. Another also, which was of the substance of silver, displayed a bright serenity and gleamed with the mildness of a vernal sky; this they called the Laughter of Jove. That one, however, of heavier metal, full of billowy winter and chill frost and hoarfrost as well, was named the Bane of Saturn. Whereas the urn of resplendent glass, situated at the god’s very right hand, was brimming with the seeds of the entire atmosphere; this they recorded as the Breasts of Juno.

{{18}} From these urns, therefore, the god drew in turn as much as sufficed for his ordinances. For as often as he administered wholesome breezes of vital breath to a favored world, he tempered them by mingling seeds of drawn air from that clemency of silver; when, however, he threatened dire pestilence to deserving mortals, he mingled in like manner panting fires or the venoms of numbing cold with the air and compelled them to pass into the world that was to be afflicted.

{{19}} Put in mind by such a temperament of the god, and the more so when she beheld him mingling salutary breezes, Virtue—with Mercury’s approval—recalled the Greek verse of the blind poet: *Phoebus of the unshorn locks wards off the cloud of pestilence* (*Φοῖβος ἀκερσεκόμης λοιμοῦ νεφέλην ἀπερύκει*), whereby Mercury reminded her that plague could be put to flight if the first words were joined to his footsteps; yet they must be subordinated to the Clarian sounding upon his lyre and wreathed amid laurel garlands with the smooth, intertwined wool of sacred fillets.

{{20}} As the Pythian beheld them approaching from afar in such discourse and recognized at first glance the cause of their coming, rising from the throne whereon he sat, he bade the Muses go to meet them; who, although they seemed to hasten to perform an office for Maia’s son, yet moved with measured paces. And then, having received his brother into a shared undertaking and session, Phoebus first began:

{{21}} «When anxious judgment wavers amid trembling affairs, or an unknown lot fluctuates with uncertain futures, let the mortal race take counsel—whom care, destitute of truth, renders doubtful, or uncertain hope wearies; but for us foreknowledge is needless, and there is no hesitation: what the celestials have willed is lawful; from a fixed breast preference is absent, if that which pleases is also necessary. Yet because an abiding will hath not yet come to thee, thou wishest to take my counsel: thus ever from every will thou takest an ally, and things added make up the mind.

{{22}} There is, then, a most learned maiden of ancient lineage, privy to whom the stars shine in the Parrhasian host, from whom neither bars can conceal the recesses of Tartarus nor the arbitrament of Jove his gleaming thunderbolts; beholding things beneath the flood like wave-born Nereus, one who knows thy recurring paths through the realms of thy brothers, ever-watchful, penetrating secrets with unmeasured toil, who can anticipate by learned care all that is granted to the celestials to foreknow. Nay more, full oft she holds sway over us, urging the gods to commands they are compelled to obey; and what no power of the celestials can essay, she knows she can achieve even against the will of Jove. Let lofty things stand with the great; each enhances the other when one hath deserved a consort equal to oneself».

{{23}} Rejoicing at these words of Apollo, because she saw that a marriage with so surpassing a maiden was urged that she deemed nothing lost of the dignity of those named before, Virtue nonetheless inquired her name. And when she learned that it was Philology concerning whom the bond was pressed, she was stirred with such congratulation and alacrity that, descending somewhat from her native austerity, she even moved her body in delight. For she recalled that she was a kinswoman and the patroness of that much-praised Mantice, and toward Sophia herself most bounteous in the bestowal of abundant treasure. For she asserted that Psyche (*Ψυχή*), uncultivated and living after the manner of wild beasts, had been polished in her company, so much so that whatever beauty and adornment she wore, she had appropriated to herself from the refinements of Philology, who had lavished so much affection upon her that she ever labored to render her immortal. There must be no delay, therefore, since she knew the Cyllenian to be tireless in action. And having received the oracles of Apollo, the son of Maia himself replied:

{{24}} «It is certain, O Lord of the Laurel and glory of the gods, that our hearts spring from kindred sources, and that whatsoever I, joined to thee, shall find an allied deity to summon, I approve in deed. Yet never do we diverge in will, and it becomes our pleasure to abide by commands, more than when we are admonished by that Delian utterance, care, and arbitrament to go forth: for to doubt this oracle we deem impious, and whatsoever will existed before perishes. Wherefore, in more seemly duty, the Arcadian willingly obeys illustrious prophecies when bidden to enter the bridal chamber. Yet do thou, O Delian, press forward so that a like purpose and willing nod of the Thunderer may stand forth; for, accustomed as thou art to stir his breast and vigilantly to forewarn his anticipated thoughts, incline him to favor the commands, and upon our undertaking a sacred will shall shine».

{{25}} As Mercury spoke thus, «Why rather», said Virtue, «should not both of you win over Jove with your voice? For both he is privy to his counsels and thou art the secret confidant of his precepts; he knows his mind, thou composest his words. To Phoebus, when he presses, Jove is wont to yield; to thee he is accustomed to open his breast. I add that it is never fitting for you to be separated; and although this runner is for the most part outstripped by the swiftness of Apollo’s chariot and, halting in delayed station, strives to forestall the hurried course of the Delian, yet while he follows, so poised does he outstrip him that, often running backward in retreat, he rejoices to be overtaken. Come together, therefore, in unison before Jove, ye dutiful offspring. For it is certain that both, yielding to the splendor of Phoebus, he gives way, and, advancing with Stilbon, he delights in the bonds of marriage».

{{26}} At these words, Virtue, stirred by the duty of going before and by the breath of Mercury’s wand, was borne aloft to ascend into heaven. Meanwhile, augural birds stood before the chariot for the Delian, so that he might mount and be borne up by whichever he wished; for, shaping things to come, he had been wont for the most part to prophesy by these. Mercury, having roused his petasus and winged sandals, began to go ahead. But as Phoebus ascended, the attendant and clinging retinue of the Muses was borne upon a gleaming, melodious swan.

{{27}} Then indeed might one behold the joys of the entire universe converging. For both Earth, illumined with flowers, had beheld Mercury, the god of spring, flying upward, and at the sight of Apollo the airy mildness gleamed with cloudless tracts. Meanwhile, the supernal globes and sevenfold spheres sang together with harmonic chimes of a certain sweet melody and with a sound sweeter than their wont, inasmuch as they had sensed beforehand that the Muses were approaching; who indeed, each having measured out her respective circle, halted where they recognized the pulse of their own modulation. For Urania occupied the outermost sphere of the starry universe, which was swept along with a shrill, sonorous chime;

{{28}} Polymnia held the Saturnian circle, Euterpe the Jovial, Erato entered and modulated the Martial, Melpomene the middle sphere, where the Sun adorns the universe with flaming light, Terpsichore was joined to the golden orb of Venus, Calliope embraced the Cyllenian sphere, and Clio established her lodging in the innermost circle, that is, in the Moon, which indeed resounded with deeper beats in hoarser modes. Alone, because her bearer, the swan, impatient of its burden and of flying upward too, had sought its native pools, Thalia was left behind and lingered in the very richness of the flowering plain.

{{29}} Meanwhile, Phoebus had already passed beyond the tracts of air, when suddenly the fillet on his hair was transformed into rays, the laurel which he held in his right hand was kindled into a torch of mundane splendor, and the birds that bore up the Delian chariot became panting wing-footed steeds of flaming light. And he himself, with ruddy mantle and the threshold of the starry pole unbarred, shone forth suddenly as the bright Sun. The Cyllenian too was converted into a quivering star and constellation.

{{30}} And thus, fairer by celestial metamorphosis, borne through Gemini by a certain propriety of their kindred sign, they shone resplendent in the august heaven, and presently sought the palace of the Thunderer. When they “had entered within and leave was granted to speak before him,” as the Clarian saw the Father clinging to the companionship of Juno—whom he knew to lend the greatest suffrage and favor to marriages—rejoicing at the first omen and winning over her in whose arbitrament he knew her husband’s will to lie, he addressed her gently thus:

{{31}} «With lesser confidence might I, hesitant, approach the Thunderer alone on behalf of a pledge of our blood, scarce yet trembling at a father’s precepts, did not the yoked consortships of the celestials prepare a prosperous omen, and did not the languishing bond of the gods now admonish us. Which of the gods would be unwilling to sue for a bridal chamber with Juno as confidante, since she, as future Pronuba, will assuredly lend her suffrage to every tie? Prepare beforehand, therefore, the soothing nods of wedlock, so that Persuasion (*Suada*) may delight in our endeavors.

{{32}} Thee now, parent and greatest prince and our fate (for the chorus of the Fates weighs human affairs, and thou the lot of the celestials; thy will precedes foreknowledge, and in thy mind thou bearest whatsoever shall befall the gods; by thy nod necessity is begotten, thy decree binds things to come, and whatsoever thou canst will, even late, is straightway at hand)—thee, thee therefore I beseech by that divinity wherein thou art benign, O gentle tempering of heaven and pious summit, who art by right the father of the gods: grant to thy offspring whereby he may advance the number of thy grandchildren, whom the stars quivering in the supernal poles celebrate. Maia’s sacred pledge and thine entreats that he be joined in the bridal chamber of a most learned maiden.

{{33}} Yet if a parent’s pious care constrains thee, it is meet that thou, mighty one, summon the assembly of the gods, thyself sanctioning the marriage with thy consort, whereby the nuptials of thy son may stand by supernal law and an everlasting bond may seal the glory of the celestials».

{{34}} Here, after the Delian fell silent, Jupiter, turning to his spouse, inquired what her will held. And she, soothed by many a reason—first, because Phoebus, who was wont to bring placidity, was entreating her, and he had also caused his own learned daughters to fly up into the sight of their parents; next, because Juno was not accustomed to oppose nuptials; moreover, she loved the Cyllenian, because, nourished at her breasts, he had drained the cup of immortality, and thus she bestowed upon him a mother’s favor as well; and it added weight to his prayers that the Clarian had bound her to himself by much conciliation—persuaded him that it ought indeed to be done and hastened, lest in like manner the Cyllenian, allured by the blandishments of Cypris, should burn with desire to beget a brother to Hermaphroditus.

{{35}} A slight misgiving pricked Jove, lest the Cyllenian, grown sluggish by the caresses of a wife, should suddenly grow torpid with drowsy languor and, taking holiday as it were on a nuptial furlough, refuse to run his errands beneath Jove’s commands.

{{36}} «For I perceive», said he, «that he hath long since been consumed with love for Philology, and through devotion to her hath acquired very many Disciplines in his service, and hath himself attained an exceeding grace of speech through the ornaments of a distinguished tongue in order to please the maiden, and further resounds upon the barbitos, the gilded lyre, and learned strings. I add that he practices a wondrous illusion in the elegance of painting, when as an animating sculptor he breathes living countenances even into bronze or marble; assuredly everything hath found favor with him that adorns the charm of youthful graces». He said, therefore, that he had slightly deferred their union, bound together though they had long been in mutual love, lest, hastening into the bridal chamber with youthful passion when he ought to be running his courses, he should feign an irregular orbit by lingering somewhat through whole nights.

{{37}} Then Juno spoke: «Nay rather», said she, «it is fitting that he submit to the bond of that very maiden who will not suffer him to close his eyes even when he longs to rest. Or is there indeed anyone who would declare himself ignorant of Philology’s laborious vigils and the pallor of her perennial studies by lamplight? Who else, moreover, throughout entire nights, hath been accustomed with slender, intent application to examine heaven, the seas, and Tartarus, and to traverse the abodes of all the gods in curious investigation; who else to reckon the fabric of the universe and the revolutions of the spheres—whether circular, parallel, or oblique—the intersecting circles, the poles, the zones, and the whirlings of the axes, together with the multitude of the stars themselves, save this Philology? How often have I heard the gods complain of her compulsion and insistence, when, as they rested in the silence of early slumber or the dead of night, she forced them by an unheard-of adjuration to come to her!

{{38}} So far is it from true that the Cyllenian could grow sluggish and entangled beneath her sway, that, his wings stirred and roused by that same maiden, he will be urged to seek latitudes beyond the universe. Why then, O best of kings, are they delayed, when in place of the sole diligence of Atlas’s grandson I promise thee two watchful guardians?».

{{39}} While Juno, leaning close as she clung to the far taller Jove, murmured these words into his inclined ears, gleaming Pallas, gliding down gradually from a certain region of purer and more vibrant light, descended, and flying above so as to seem to cling to the crown of Jove’s head, halted at last leaning upon a somewhat loftier dais. Looking up at her from close at hand as he stood beside his spouse, Jupiter began thus: «O maiden, better part of ourself, opportunely mingled in the prayers of Maia’s son—whether thou descendest soothed by Delian voices, or without thee Jove could not form a counsel, or thou hastenest lest our consensus seem maimed—know nevertheless that the Cyllenian sues for nuptials with Philology. Not yet is my judgment ready; I await what thou advisest. For I know how acceptable to thee that same maiden’s unceasing toil hath ever been, and that she is numbered among thy attendants; it is meet, therefore, that thou thyself above all determine whatsoever thou dispensest in foresight concerning her marriage».

{{40}} Then Pallas, somewhat more demure and suffused with the blush of maidenly modesty, veiling her eyes with the crimson peplos she wore about her head, disapproved somewhat that a virgin should be consulted upon nuptials, and especially those of one whom, on account of the duties of their companionship, she desired to remain forever untouched. She disdained, moreover, to lend assent of this kind, since she is deemed so devoid of all union that, as Arithmetic bears witness, she is shown neither to have been begotten of any commingling nor herself to engender anything. And thereupon solitary virginity uncovered her crown of seven rays, lest she be present at causes of offspring and unions. Because, however, Jupiter had desired and exacted her counsel, she advised that the married gods and the aged goddesses be brought together to decide these matters; for it was fitting for the Cyllenian that, as a reward for his services, the favor of the mightiest celestials should sanction his bonds. Jove’s decree would also become more august when issued with the assembly of the gods bearing witness, and the bride herself could not be matched with a god unless, by a decree of the supernal senate, she ceased to be mortal. As Tritonia urged many considerations of this kind, both of the royal spouses consented.

{{41}} And presently the scribe of Jove was commanded to summon the celestials according to their rank and fixed orders, and especially the senators of the gods, who were styled the Penates of the Thunderer himself, whose names, since celestial secrecy did not suffer them to be divulged, were given a collective title by agreement because they promise all things equally.

{{42}} Jupiter himself, however, summoned Jovial Vulcan, although he never descended from his gleaming seat. Then also he bade that among others the mightiest colleagues of Jove himself be invited, who are numbered twice six together with the Thunderer, and whom the Ennian distich embraces:

Juno, Vesta, Minerva, Ceres, Diana, Venus, Mars, Mercury, Jove, Neptune, Vulcan, Apollo.
{{43}} Likewise from the seven, those remaining who are not called among the twelve. After these, very many lofty celestials according to their ranks and the whole populace of the gods, save those not concerned, were to be convoked.

{{44}} Without delay the soldiers of Jove hastened through the diverse regions of heaven. For each of the gods dwelt in widely separated places, and although several had titled single or paired houses with living creatures throughout the zodiacal tract, yet they abode in other habitations.

{{45}} For the entire heaven is said to be divided into sixteen regions, in the first of which, after Jupiter himself, are recorded as having their seats the Dii Consentes, the Penates, Salus and the Lares, Janus, the hidden Favores, and Nocturnus.

{{46}} In the second likewise dwelt, besides the house of Jove—which there too is lofty, as he holds estates in all—Quirinus Mars and the military Lar; Juno also possessed a domicile there, as well as Fons, the Lymphae, and the Dii Novensiles.

{{47}} From the third region, however, it was decided to invite but one. For the houses of Secondary Jove, of Opulent Jove, and of Minerva are established there; yet all of these were already present about Jupiter himself. Who, indeed, would invite Discord and Sedition to sacred nuptials, especially since they had ever been hostile to Philology herself? From that same region, therefore, Pluto alone was summoned, because he is the bridegroom’s paternal uncle.

{{48}} Then sylvan Lynsa, Mulciber, the celestial and also the military Lar, and Favor came from the fourth region.

{{49}} From the next, passing by the houses of the royal spouses, were invited Ceres, Tellurus, and Earth’s father Vulcan, and Genius.

{{50}} Ye too, children of Jove, Pales and Favor, together with Celerity, daughter of the Sun, were summoned from the sixth; for Mars Quirinus and Genius had been called above.

{{51}} So also Liber and Secondary Pales were called from the seventh. Fraud, indeed, from that same region, after long deliberation, it was decided to admit, because she had frequently been compliant to the Cyllenian himself.

{{52}} The eighth, however, was passed over, since all from it had been summoned above, and from it alone the Fruit of Spring was admitted.

{{53}} From the ninth was summoned the Genius of Juno Hospita.

{{54}} Neptune, moreover, the universal Lar of all, and Neverita, and thou, Consus, assembled from the tenth.

{{55}} From the next came Fortune and Valitudo and Favor the shepherd, the Manes being refused admittance, inasmuch as they could not come into the presence of Jove.

{{56}} From the twelfth Sancus alone was called down.

{{57}} The Fates, indeed, were summoned from the next; for the remaining deities there tarried among the Manes.

{{58}} From the twice-seventh Saturn and his celestial Juno were summoned in order.

{{59}} Vejovis and the public gods were convoked from the thrice-fifth boundary.

{{60}} From the last region Nocturnus and the terrestrial Janitors were likewise called.

{{61}} When the gods had thus been summoned from all the regions of heaven, the rest, whom they call the Azoni, were convoked at the reminder of the Cyllenian himself.

{{62}} Then the governors of the elements, the guardians of public utility and of minds, and the entire populace of powers (whom the abundant succession of Numa points out), all straightway convoked by the command of Jove, came into the celestial hall amid quivering constellations.

{{63}} Then Janus took his stand upon the threshold and the soldiers of Jove before the royal doors; Rumor also, acting as herald, called by name all who were about to enter.

{{64}} Meanwhile, within the king’s consistory, a certain woman who was called Adrastia was whirling the celestial urn and the supernal lot with the swiftness of an irrevocable sweep, while Heimarmene caught in the fold of her curved breast the spheres falling from the revolving wheel.

{{65}} Clotho, Lachesis, and Atropos, moreover—since with the orthographic zeal of truth they take down the sentences of Jove, being the clerks of the celestials and custodians of the archive—when they beheld the senate and curia assembling and the Thunderer himself publicly arraying himself in his vesture, sharpened their styluses and arranged their wax tablets to record the magistracies in the acts and the decree of the celestials.

{{66}} Then Jupiter, taking up the public vestments which he assumes when about to convene the senate, first placed upon his crown the flaming wreath of a royal diadem, and covered his head from behind with a certain crimson veil which Pallas herself, patroness of the craft, had woven for him. Next, over a tunic of dazzling white he drew hyaline mantles, which, studded with starry eyes, frequent flashes of fire illumined. Then with his outstretched right hand he grasped two spherical globes, one of which shone with gold and the other with electrum, while with his left, as though leaning upon it, he pressed down a nine-stringed lyre. Earth, moreover, bound to his feet grassy sandals of emerald greenness. He sat upon a mantle interwoven with peacocks’ feathers and covered with eyes, from which a painting variegated with many-colored markings bloomed like spring; beneath his sandals he pressed down a trident.

{{67}} Placed below his dais was the throne of Juno, adorned in no unseemly wise. She herself shone resplendent with her head veiled by a milky wimple, upon which was set a diadem inlaid with precious gems; for neither the green brilliance of the Scythian emerald nor the quivering, flashing light of thunder-stones nor the wave-colored depth of the jacinth was believed to be absent, but Thaumantias was said to have presented that entire wreath of her flashing head to the Queen of the Celestials. The countenance of the goddess herself, translucent with constant grace, was wholly like her brother’s, save that he shone with immutable gladness, whereas she was more frequently troubled by the cloud of continual changes; for her robe was hyaline, yet her peplos was misty, which nevertheless, if touched by the impact of any light, shone forth amid overshadowing clouds with the grace of cloudless transparency. She held a thunderbolt in her right hand, and in her left a timbrel resounding with terrifying booms, beneath which, sweating profusely, she seemed to inundate the regions beneath her with dewy streams. Her sandals were exceedingly dark, whose soles in particular were colored with the blackness of pitch-black night. Moreover, a many-colored girdle encircled her knees, which now gleamed in a dazzling orb, and now the attenuated variety of its vanishing grace banished so utterly that it seemed as though it had previously held nothing of diverse hues.

{{68}} Thus fittingly arrayed in these vestments, both of the sovereigns beheld, set before their session upon a starry dais, a certain sphere engraved with manifold variety, which had been so compacted of all the elements that nothing was lacking of whatsoever is believed to be contained by all Nature. There were the entire heaven, the air, the seas, the diversity of the earth, and the bars of Tartarus; cities too, crossroads, and the species of all living creatures, numbered both in species and in genus. This sphere indeed seemed a kind of image and Idea of the universe. In it shone forth, as in the transforming mirror of Pytheus, what all and what individual peoples of all nations were enacting in their daily motions. There Jupiter formed with his own hand whom he wished to be exalted, whom cast down, whom born, and whom slain; what portion of the lands he desired to lay waste, what to bless, what to make desolate and what populous, the arbitrary artificer varied at will.

{{69}} Beholding and ordering this public destiny, therefore, he commanded the senate of the gods to be admitted. Although there entered within those whom sacred power attests to be unnameable, nevertheless both sovereigns rose even for the princes of the gods, and especially for their parents.

{{70}} Now their sire advanced with slow and lingering steps, his head covered with a grey-blue mantle. In his right hand he held forth a fire-breathing dragon devouring the tip of its own tail, which men believed taught the number of the year by its name. His hoary hair gleamed white with frosty snows, although it was believed he could also become a boy.

{{71}} His aged and corpulent spouse, a mother though fertile and surrounded by her offspring, had nevertheless covered her flowery, many-colored robe with a grassy mantle, wherein the entire census of gems and metals and the yield and fruits of all sowings were borne in exceeding abundance.

{{72}} To her clung Vesta, who was also of equal age with her, and because she was reported to have been the nurse of Jove himself and to have sustained him in her lap, she dared to kiss the king’s head.

{{73}} After these, gilded Sol was sought with his radiant sister; and as soon as he began to draw near the entrance, a certain crimson effulgence of glowing purple went before him, and the grace of his rosy splendor illumined the entire curia of the hall itself while all other ornaments stood dazzled. And when, about to enter, he sent forth the first rays of his honored head, even Jupiter himself stepped back a little and grew dim beneath the divinity of that boundless brilliance, while the spheres and globes which he held in his right hand shone back as though from a mirror of kindred light:

{{74}} Juno, however, illustrious in ornaments of diverse hues and resplendent with the varied light of gems, shone forth with dazzling serenities.

{{75}} Upon his brow was a gleaming crown drawn into a circle, which flashed with twelve flames of fiery stones. For in front were three gems—lychnis, astrites, and ceraunos—which concealed his venerable visage from the recognition of beholders by the quivering flashes of their rays; one of these was said to have been taken from the brain of Cancer, the second from the eyes of Leo, and the third from the forehead of Gemini. Six others glowed ruddy on either side, of which one was called smaragdos, the second scythis, and the third iaspis; amid their greenness (lights engendered by the sea), a certain inward sweetness of coruscation shone resplendent upon his brow. On either side were also set hyacinthos, dendrites, and heliotropios, stones which by their colors clad the earth in grass at the fixed turns of the seasons, and which Spring and Autumn, returning to pay homage to his divinity, were said to have bestowed upon him as a gift. The hinder part of the crown was bound with the stones hydatis, adamas, and crystallus; for billowy Winter had begotten these. The hair and locks of the god himself one would have believed dyed with gold and overlaid with gold leaf.

{{76}} Moreover, at the moment he entered, he appeared with the face of a beaming boy, midway in his advance with that of a panting youth, and at the end with that of a setting old man, although by some he was believed to assume twelve forms. His entire body was fiery, his footsteps winged, his mantle scarlet, yet gleaming ruddy with abundant gold.

{{77}} In his left hand he bore forth a flashing shield, in his right a blazing torch, and his sandals were likewise of bronze-gold pyropus. Beside him, Luna, with a smooth and tender countenance, gathered back her brightness from her brother’s lamp.

{{78}} After these were admitted the brothers of Jove, one of whom was ever greener from the inundation of the sea, while the other grew pale from light-shunning shadow.

{{79}} Upon his head each wore a diadem of sovereignty according to the condition of his realm;

{{80}} for the one wore a wreath white as bleached salt and of one hue with the hoariness of sea-foam, the other one of ebony, darkening with the gloom of Tartarean night; and he indeed was far wealthier than his brother, ever opulent through the gathering of all things that are begotten, whereas the other, by reason of the bulk and loftiness of his body, was stripped bare and spurned riches acquired by oppression.

{{81}} Each, however, had a different spouse; for the naked god led with him the nurse of all things and hostess of the gods, while the other led a maiden rejoicing in approaches, who for the most part so bestows harvest upon those who ask that the vows of a great deity pay back to her a hundredfold.

{{82}} Next were admitted the sons of the Thunderer himself, among whom the first walked as a ruddy youth, devourer of all things and thirsting even for blood; the second, sweet and affable, bearing a pruning-hook in his right hand and a sleep-inducing mixing bowl in his left, was carried along prone to wantonness. His steps were unsteady and entangled in the moisture of strong-scented wine.

{{83}} After these came two of a single, twin countenance, yet the one shone with the star of light, the other with that of night.

{{84}} Next came one of unheard-of strength, ever prepared to extirpate adversities; yet Juno looked askance at his wondrous muscles and his Cleonaean lion’s jaws.

{{85}} Moreover, among them entered deciding goddesses, one of whom was reputed a virgin, the other the mother of all generations; the former bore a bow with a quiver, the latter a garlanded interweaving of roses bound crosswise. How one would long to behold her shining, to hear her speaking sweet allurements, to inhale her redolent with the exhalations of most fragrant breath, to cover her with kisses, to touch her body, and to sigh with desire for her! Yet although she was believed the mother of all loves and pleasures, to her nevertheless they awarded the primacy of chastity.

{{86}} With these was seen gracious Ceres, an exceedingly grave woman, foster-child of the lands and nurse of mortals.

{{87}} There came also a certain lame artificer, who, although he was believed to be Juno’s son, was called by Heraclitus the delayer of the entire universe.

{{88}} Then too, most talkative of all maidens and ever rushing to and fro in contrary excess, leaped forward a nymph swift with vaulting levity; whom some assert to be Sors, several Nemesis, and very many Tyche or Nortia. Because she carried in her ample lap the ornaments of the entire world and, apportioning them to others, bestowed them with sudden motions—snatching the hair of some in girlish sport, shattering the heads of others with a rod, and wounding with frequent blows and knuckles bent into joints the very crowns of those whom she had coaxed with caresses—

{{89}} as soon as she beheld the Fates noting down all that was being enacted in Jove’s consistory, she ran to their books and writing tablets and, with a somewhat lawless boldness, seized upon what she had seen in an unexpected record, so that certain events bursting forth suddenly disturbed as it were the sequence of things, while others, which the foreseen chain of causes had already made public, since she could not render them unforeseen, she nevertheless arrogated to her own works.

{{90}} After her the rest arrived in a throng. Jupiter then sat back upon his throne and commanded all to be seated according to the order of their merit. And thereupon the flaming benches received the starry assembly; meanwhile, a certain garlanded boy, pressing his forefinger to his lips, enjoined silence. “All fell silent and held their countenances intent.”

{{91}} Then Jupiter began:

«Were not our known benignity, ye star-bearers, compelling us to bring our innermost arbitrament into council, and had it been our pleasure to keep within our own breast whatsoever it sufficed for a silent will to decree, I could bring forth certainties by my own guidance, nor would anyone striving with unlawful efforts desire to annul the commands of the Father of the gods. Yet a grim judgment is better shut away, and silence presses down unspeakable thoughts, lest being divulged they stir hearts with grief. But when it befits a father to bring forth joyful gladness and openly to unite pledges by a sure covenant with perpetual nods, it is vain to refuse to speak seemly thoughts. With you, therefore, O gods, a grateful kinship, it is my zeal to confer upon the vows of my offspring. For I deem it just that in the assembly of the celestials those undertakings be approved which ought to be pursued.

{{92}} Ye know that Maia’s son, an illustrious pledge, deservedly abides in our affections. Nor is this love implanted in me in vain, as is wont to bind a father’s breast. For he is our fidelity, speech, benignity, and true genius, our faithful courier and the interpreter of my mind, the sacred *nous* (*ὁ νοῦς sacer*); he alone can set forth the number of the celestials, he can know the quivering stars, what measure belongs to the poles, how great is the depth, what number belongs to the draughts of the sea and how many hinges it sweeps along its margin, and what bond links the dissonant elements; and through him I myself, the Father, sanction covenants. Yet perchance affection alone might recount these things, which a parent’s partiality weighs as gifts: he who often flies before the steeds of Phoebus, doth he not sink back into the lot of a servant? He also serves his paternal uncles’ honors in such wise that it is doubtful which may claim him more as his own. Ages of well-earned toils urge that he be duly yoked in marriage, and his manly vigor demands a bridal chamber added thereto.

{{93}} Now the maiden hath found favor—learned indeed beyond measure and matched in zeal, yet one whose birth is earthly, though her purpose strains toward the stars; full oft she even flies ahead of the swift spheres and, passing through, frequently transcends the globe of the universe. Let her humble cradle, therefore, be no hindrance in anything, ye celestials—ye especially who remember that your own emblems are kept upon earth, sacred things which temples conceal in hidden sanctuaries. Let them be joined under equal auspices, for it is fitting, and let them crown the stars with our grandchildren!».

{{94}} As soon as Jupiter made an end of speaking, the entire senate of the gods was stirred to vote, and all acclaimed that it ought to be done forthwith; and they added to the Jovial decree that henceforth mortals whom a distinguished elevation of life and the highest summit of mighty merits should raise to a celestial aspiration and a purpose of starry desire should be co-opted into the number of the gods,

{{95}} and straightway among others whom either the Nile gave forth or Thebes—Aeneas, Romulus, and others whom the renown of learning afterward enrolled among the stars—were named as celestials-designate, so that after their bodily limbs they might become senators of the gods.

{{96}} At Jove’s nod of assent to these decrees as well, a certain grave and distinguished woman who was called Philosophy was commanded to publish this decree of the supernal senate, engraved upon bronze tablets, throughout the spheres and crossroads.

{{97}} Then Juno appointed, on account of the bridal chamber of the aforesaid youths and the nuptial rites to be celebrated, that on the morrow that entire senate of the gods should assemble at dawn in the palaces which, in the Galaxy, form the chiefest habitation of Jove the Arbiter. When these things were done, the King himself rose from his throne, and that entire company of the gods sought again their own seats and courses.

## Liber II

{{98}} Meanwhile Night was ascending the pure sky with starry reins, recalling the gleaming constellations now that Phoebus had sunk beneath the waves. Then aged Bootes kindled his lofty Wain, and the stars shone forth along the coils of the grim Dragon; he too, blazing in gilded armor, gleamed bright who draws forth resplendent Sirius at his summer rising; and that garland likewise, which burns scattered with Nysaean flowers, was wreathed about its circuit with manifold light. Meanwhile Rumor glided through the trembling ears of the maiden, even while she filled the halls of Jove with her mighty clamor.

{{99}} At length Philology herself, having learned of the decrees of the celestials and now keeping vigil far into the night, anxiously revolved many thoughts within herself with immense care: first, that she must enter the senate of the gods and endure the gaze of Jove in unpremeditated encounter, and leap upward into the supernal lot of the celestials.

{{100}} Next, that she was to be united to the Cyllenian himself, whom, although she had ever desired with wondrous ardor, she had nonetheless scarce beheld save once as he ran back after anointing himself in the palaestra, while she herself was gathering flowers amid certain chosen herbs. Moreover, she anxiously doubted whether this nuptial grandeur would prove advantageous to her; for assuredly, not by an idle surmise, she feared that once she had ascended to the supernal realms she would utterly lose her beloved myths, the delights of poetic variety, and Milesian tales and histories of mortals.

{{101}} Accordingly, she first inquired by nuptial congruence of number whether the marriage would profit her, and whether the winged swiftness of the ethereal summit could be joined to her by a fitting covenant.

{{102}} And straightway she distributed upon her fingers and into calculation both her own name and the appellation of the Cyllenian—not indeed that which the dissonant discrepancy of nations or the diverse rites of peoples have fashioned according to local causes and cults, but that name which fallacious mortal curiosity asserts was compounded for him at his birth by Jove himself in starry naming and divulged solely through the commentaries of the Egyptians. From this she took the final letter on either side, which closes the first and perfect limit of number; next that letter by which the lord is adored in all shrines under cubic solidity. The letter also which the prudent Samian judged to signify the forked path of mortality she took into the next place, and thus one thousand two hundred and eighteen numbers shone forth.

{{103}} Reducing these by the rule of nine and subtracting by units substituted for tens, she skillfully compressed them into the third number.

{{104}} Her own appellation likewise, unfolded into seven hundred and twenty-four numbers, she reduced to the quaternary; and each of these numbers is marked by a ratio congruent to both.

{{105}} For the former, because its ratio dispenses beginning, middle, and end, is assuredly perfect; since it alone makes a line and unhesitatingly completes the faces of solids (for they are reckoned by length, breadth, and depth); and further because the triplication of this number first engenders a cube from odd numbers. Who, moreover, is ignorant of the three symphonies in music? And the odd number is attributed to males. All time likewise varies by three turns, and the same number is the seed-bed of perfect numbers—namely the sixth and the ninth—by alternating diversity of juncture. Rightly, therefore, is the number of ratio attributed to the god.

{{106}} Philology, however, because she too is most learned, although reckoned by feminine numbers, is nevertheless perfected by absolute reason: for the quaternary by its parts completes the potency of the decad itself, and is therefore perfect and deemed square, like the Cyllenian himself, to whom the seasons of the year, the climes of heaven, and the elements of the universe correspond.

{{107}} Or doth that oath of the aged philosopher, who kept not silent when he swore by the tetrad (μὰ τὴν τετράδα), confess aught else than the number of perfect reason? Since within itself it holds the monad, the dyad, the triad, and the twice-two itself, by whose ratios the symphonies are wrought. For three to four is styled epitritos in arithmetical ratio and is termed the diatessaron (fourth) in music. Likewise within it lie three to two, which is the hemiolios form and yields the second symphony, called the diapente (fifth). The third symphony is termed the diapason (octave) in harmonics and is fashioned by the double ratio (diplasios), that is, one compared to two. Therefore the quaternary number, perfect in its parts, completes all symphonies and searches out all melodies by the distribution of harmonics. Examining this congruence of number, therefore, the skilled maiden rejoiced.

{{108}} Next she joined the two together, and the triad united with the quaternary made the heptad, which number is the perfection of supernal reason, even as that fullness of the hebdomads (ἑβδομάδων) teaches. Or do the course of fatal temperament, the circles and motions of the stars, and mortality perfected in the seventh month within the hidden recesses of the womb bear witness to aught else? Furthermore, because the triad tripled makes nine numbers, whereas the quaternary doubled by the diplasios ratio yields eight, nine to eight effects the juncture of the epogdoos number [which weighs as much in numbers as the symphony of the diapason in harmonics], which makes the tone, that is, the continuous modulation of consonant unity. Hence there is nothing that disagrees or rebounds in the midst, and it accords in harmonious continuity.

{{109}} Thus the number of the aforesaid names sang in concord: accordingly, a fixed association between them bound the nuptial union by true ratio. Rejoicing from this that the marriage was most advantageous to her, the maiden of manifold insight stirred up another wave within her mind. For, admitting no delay in spirit, she began to fear for the comeliness of her form and bodily substance; since, trembling not without cause, she dreaded the globes of celestial flames and the fires of blazing stars that must be endured by limbs still mortal and parched with slender leanness.

{{110}} Against these perils, however, she prepared a certain unguent of the aged Abderite, wherein she had compounded many herbs and simples mingled with pebbles and twigs. A Colchian charm also, drawn out into a hundred incantations, was sealed by the impression of an adamantine point, which, prepared against supernal fires and the confines of the gods, banished even by its radiance any neglect of beauty; finally, with a shimmering gleam, she anointed her body—set before tables—with an ointment of dewy liquor.

{{111}} Yet while the maiden was compounding such remedies, her handmaiden Periergia, anxious with trepidation—whether sent by the maiden’s mother or acting of her own accord as her foster-sister—kept watch over what she was doing.

{{112}} And when, spying through a chink from the doorway, she perceived her arranging the aforesaid things, she fell to upbraiding another of her handmaids, whose name was Agrypnia and who kept watch within the bedchamber, because she had not suffered the maiden to close her eyes even a little for the sake of preserving her beauty, although she herself was well able to fulfill all these tasks if Philology should enjoin them.

{{113}} For she asserted that she had already gone round to many places and had learned what skill, what adornment, and finally what vestments the dotal handmaids were assuming; nor was it unknown to her what the bridegroom himself was enacting, what was being done in the palace of Jove, whether Leucothea was kindling her torch and crimson light, and whether the winged steeds of the Sun were awake and the courser of Phosphorus was being decked: countless matters of this sort, which she had observed by curious investigations, she added thereto.

{{114}} Suddenly, however, her mother Phronesis burst into the privacy of the bedchamber. When the maiden beheld her, running to her and kissing her venerable breast, she made her privy to the remedies she had prepared. Yet her mother had brought vestments and ornaments for her daughter, clad in which she need not fear to be joined to the assemblies of the gods. Accordingly, she gave her a robe and peplos gleaming like milk, which seemed wrought either from that wool of auspicious herbs wherein the seers of Indian wisdom and the dwellers upon the shadowed mountain are said to be arrayed, or, so far as the custom of that land affords, from threads of dazzling byssus. Next she set upon her crown a maidenly diadem, which shone most brightly with the light of its central gem, out of which there gleamed forth a helmeted virgin with veiled countenance, deeply engraved after the likeness of the Trojan palladium.

{{115}} Moreover, the prudent mother unfastened from herself the girdle wherewith she bound her breast and, lest Philology should lack the ornaments of Phronesis herself, placed it upon her breast that she might be more truly adorned; furthermore, she bound beneath her feet sandals woven of papyrus, lest anything of dead skin pollute her limbs; and the maiden’s hands were laden with a censer heavy with abundant incense, itself also gleaming white.

{{116}} And already Dawn, bringing forth ambrosial modesty, had begun to weave beneath the stars with her rosy peplos, when kindly light glimmers in the twilight, when she shines jeweled with beauty, and when Phosphorus becomes a golden star. Then gleaming hoarfrost is congealed with tender dew, and at morning the flocks shake open their folds into the pastures, when biting cares beat upon languid breasts, and sleep flees banished to the Lethean shores.

{{117}} Lo! before the doors a certain sweet sound was stirred with manifold delights, which the choir of assembling Muses, attending upon the nuptial sacraments, sang together with the chimes of learned modulation: for neither the melodies of flutes nor the sound from stringed instruments nor the harmonic fullness of water-organs was wanting, yet gathered into a soothing song and brought to a measured close, they made a fixed silence in the interval for the maidens’ voice to complete it. And then that entire choir with melodious voices and sweet modulation surpassed all instrumental sweetnesses, and with the measures of a sacred lay these words were poured forth:

«Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{118}} Then Urania, while the rest fell silent for a little while, began:

«The starry assemblies and sacred summits of the poles behold now, conjecturing naught, trusting in thy godhead: once inquiring what whirls the linked spheres, now as president thou thyself shalt give causes to their swift courses. What texture binds the circles, what fastening encloses them, and how many globes the curved orbit encompasses; what drives or retards the starry courses; what ray kindles or diminishes the Moon; what fuel stars the heaven and how vast an orb it revolves; what care or measure belongs to the gods—all this shalt thou behold». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{119}} Then Calliope:

«Ever a friend to the well-pleased Muses, to whom the Permessian streams and the fountain of the Gorgon’s steed bore a draught, for whom the summit of the Aonides blooms green with garlands while Cirrha prepares violets; thou knowest how to reproduce the melodies of bards with the sweet Camenae and the Pindaric lyre; at thy dictation the strings and sacred plectrum moved to sound the Thracian song. O light of ours, accustom thyself to approve sacred chants and to bless them with harmonic spheres». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{120}} And thus Polymnia:

«At length the fruit of thy toil—the gleaming aether, the abodes of the gods, and companionship with Jove—thou pluckest, advanced by an implanted godhead; thou who wast wont erewhile to yoke bloody rhythms and to scatter confused measures by rule, soon shalt thou approve what the triangle curves and the circle twists with horizontal or yoked line, melody and tones and percussive beats and all the arts, and whatsoever things can prepare the summits of the celestials for a mind driven onward». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{121}} Then Melpomene:

«Wont to bring forth buskined songs upon the stage, to wear the comic sock, and to resound those lays which we bore by thy care with favoring rhythmic melody, now I sing to thee, O maiden, our hope and vindication, the tenor of my song transformed. For it delights us to wreathe the bridal chamber; approve thou the garlands that they please thy rites. Mayest thou ever seem worthy of nuptial Olympus and the comeliest of the celestials». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{122}} And thus Clio:

«Thou who wast wont to resound in rhetorical train and to acquit the accused with impassioned breast, who now binding bristling thoughts in syllogistic links heapest up sorites by additions to the pile—didst thou not bind by grammatical rule what confounds the order of speech through ambiguities, skilled to sport with learned reckonings? Now behold the starry thresholds of the pole and enjoy the sacred radiance of the aether, which it is worth the price to know in true light». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{123}} Presently Erato:

«Chief of the arts, illustrious maiden, to whom the hall of the Thunderer lies open, deservedly is the universe, already discovered by thy reasonings, subjected to thee. Why sacred lightnings glow red, whence the rebounding crash thunders forth, what drives moisture through the open skies when clouds are charged with rain, what recalls the brightest springtide as storm-clouds march in column, and what wheels all things in the circle of the year hastening to close the ages—the hidden secrets of reason we sing, known to thee alone». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{124}} And then Terpsichore:

«I rejoice: by the merits of honor thou beholdest the stars, O maiden! This thy skilled genius and toil have begotten for thee; these things watchful care by learned lamps hath bestowed. For laboring by day and by night over sacred scrolls, whatsoever the Stoics, busied with foreknowledge of the future, grant, thou hast ever borne away with docile tinder. For what the vapor on Sabaean altars utters, what the smoky breeze prepares from incense-burning embers, or what sure omens they bear with augural voices, undaunted and unhesitating thou dost anticipate with fearless prophecies». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{125}} Next Euterpe:

«Maiden skillfully prophetic of destiny, who hast been able to ascend heaven and bear sacred doctrines to the pure, whereby they availed to know themselves, and seeing in clear light to discern the divinities of fate and the countenances of Genii; who gavest to Plato and Pythagoras that stars are minds, and to frail mortals, the cloud removed, to behold the powers of righteous heaven: rightly ascend the senate of the Thunderer, thou whom alone it befits to be joined in Mercury’s covenant». «Ascend the temples of heaven, O maiden worthy of so great a covenant: Jupiter thy father-in-law summons thee to mount the lofty stars».

{{126}} Then Thalia:

«Blessed maiden, who amid so great dances of the stars receivest the nuptial chamber and thus, with the universe favoring, art added as daughter-in-law to the Thunderer. Of what a god hath it befallen thee to be the bride! Of him who, with gliding wing, alone flying past the stars of the universe, hath traversed them watchful amid swift tempests; who, when he swims across the supernal seas, courses back to Tartarus; who alone before the chariot and white-yoked steeds of his lofty sire hath power to ply the mindful wand; who willingly separates the fates of sinking Osiris; whom the Father of the gods knows to be burdened with discovered generative sowings; to whom his stepmother gladly gave her milky breast; before whose flourishing wand dire poison stands stupefied; for whom, as he speaks, every venomous breath makes the world twofold. Learned is he among the gods, yet more learned is the maiden. Now, now are the Arts blessed, whom ye both so consecrate that they grant passage to heaven, unbar the stars to mortals, and waft pious vows up to the lucid aether. Through you the watchful and comely nous fills the depths of the mind; through you an approved tongue bears glories throughout the ages. Do ye consecrate all the Disciplines and us, the Muses».

{{127}} While the Muses thus interspersed these strains, now singing solo and now in unison, and their sweet melodies were redoubled in turn, lo! certain matrons, praiseworthy for sober decorum and not grimacing with contrived affectations about the face, but shining forth with a simple courtesy, assembled in the maiden’s dwelling and bedchamber (one of whom, most wary with intent circumspection and distinguishing all things with watchful discernment, was said to be called Prudence;

{{128}} her sister, assigning to all their own and visiting none with aught he did not deserve, was reported to have received the name of Justice;

{{129}} while the third, to be extolled for her contempt of bribes and her abstinence, had taken her name from the temperance of her manners;

{{130}} she who remained, most valiant and ever unbroken in enduring all adversities, prepared also in bodily strength to undergo toils, possessed the appellation of Fortitude). They threw themselves into her embrace and kiss, and when they beheld her within the bedchamber trembling at everything and torpid with hesitations as though shunning the light, stroking her breast and face, they compelled her to come forth into the sight of all and into public view.

{{131}} After these entered a certain grave woman with flowing locks, exceedingly glorious from the fact that through her Jupiter had granted ascent to all into the supernal realms. When the maiden beheld her, she ran to her with all zeal and affection, inasmuch as with a certain confidence in what she had ascertained she had herself augured that heaven must be ascended by her means, and now she had been sent across by Maia’s son himself to invite her to the nuptials.

{{132}} Furthermore, three maidens, equal in countenance and comeliness and resplendent in beauty, their hands mutually bound with garlands and their brows wreathed with rosebuds, came together to the maiden: one of whom kissed Philology’s brow there where the smooth middle space separates the down of the eyebrows, another touched her mouth, and the third her breast—namely, the first, that she might “breathe glad honors upon her eyes,” the second breathed grace upon her tongue, and the third courtesy upon her spirit. For these were called the Graces, and whatsoever they touched they made lovely: who indeed, after they had filled the maiden with light, mingling with the Muses, performed harmonious gestures and hymeneal dances as well.

{{133}} But lo! with a mighty clash of the timbrel and jingling of castanets all things leaped forth to such a degree that the song of the Muses was rendered somewhat duller by the booming of the timbrel, and with loud resonance there was borne in a litter studded with stars—before which rhythmic clatterings resounded in mystic rite—wherein it had been the custom for wedding goddesses to arrive at the companionship of a celestial bridal chamber.

{{134}} Before this litter a certain woman of more august countenance, resplendent with sacred light and aether, stood pre-eminent in venerable ministry; when all who were present beheld her, they revered her illustrious majesty as guardian of all the gods and of the universe: her name was Athanasia. And «Ho!», said she, «maiden, the Father of the gods hath commanded that thou be borne aloft into the palaces of heaven in this royal litter, which indeed it is lawful for no earth-born mortal to touch—nay, not even for thee, unless thou first drink of our cup».

{{135}} And with the word, she gently stroked with her right hand the beating of her heart and her breast, and gazing upon it distended with some inward fullness and great swelling, «Unless», said she, «thou vomit forth by most forced expulsion and pour abroad these things wherewith thou bearest thy breast full, thou shalt in no wise obtain the seat of immortality». And she, with every effort and mighty force, vomited forth whatsoever she had ever stored within her breast.

{{136}} Then indeed that laborious nausea and vomiting was converted into an abundance of all manner of letters. One might behold what books and how vast volumes, the works of how many tongues, flowed forth from the maiden’s mouth. Some appeared to be of papyrus that had been smeared with cedar-oil; others were books wrapped in linen rolls; many also were upon sheepskins, while a few were inscribed upon the inner bark of the linden.

{{137}} And there were some colored with a sacred blackness, whose letters were believed to be the likenesses of living creatures; perceiving the characters of these books, Athanasia commanded that they be inscribed upon certain towering stones and set up within a cavern throughout the sanctuaries of the Egyptians, and calling those same stones stelae, she bade them contain the genealogies of the gods.

{{138}} Yet while the maiden was vomiting forth such things in floods, very many maidens—of whom some were called the Arts and others the Disciplines—straightway gathered up what the maiden had poured forth from her mouth, each snatching it for her own necessary use and faculty. The Muses themselves also, especially Urania and Calliope, heaped countless volumes into their laps. For in some the pages were marked off for musical pitch and drawn in lines; in others were circles, lines, and hemispheres with triangles and squares and polygonal figures fashioned according to the diversity of theorems or elements; next, painting wove together the limbs of multiform animals into a single figure. There were also books which displayed melodies of sounds, signs of numbers, and certain works on the art of singing.

{{139}} After the maiden, therefore, straining with immense effort, had poured forth that library-like abundance, spent with exhausted pallor she implored the aid of Athanasia, who had been witness to so great a toil. Then she spoke: «That thou mayest ascend heaven more refreshed and uplifted, take this to sip».

{{140}} And thereupon, taking from her mother Apotheosis—who had happened to come with her and had already long been touching with her hand and numbering and consecrating the books that had flowed from Philology’s mouth—a certain spherical and living roundness, she took it and held it out to the maiden to drain. Now the very form of the inner egg, smeared round with saffron, gleamed ruddy without, and next with translucent emptiness and whitish humor, yet appeared more solid in its innermost core. When Philology received this, since after the afflictions of so great a labor and the fever of her mind she thirsted exceedingly, having unsealed the secrets of that roundness, as soon as she discovered it to be a thing most sweet, she drained it wholly without hesitation:

and straightway her limbs were strengthened with new vigor, her slender leanness perished, her earthly force gave way, and an ethereal age came upon her without the laws of death.
{{141}} Yet when the goddess saw that she had drunk dry the cup of immortality, in order that she might instruct her—as though by the riddle of a garland—that she was passing from earth to heaven and had been made immortal, she crowned the maiden with a certain rustic herb whose name is aeizos (ever-living), commanding her to cast out everything which, while still mortal, she had fitted to herself as a safeguard against supernal force; for she reminded her that those things were the smallest trifles of perishable and mortal substance. All of which her mother stripped from her, once she recognized that she had transcended mundane pursuits.

{{142}} Then Philology first made supplication to Athanasia from the prepared incense and her own censer, and paid thanks to her mother with abundant sacrifice, because she had neither beheld Vedius with his wife, as Etruria counseled, nor feared the Eumenides, like Chaldaean prodigies, nor burned with fire, nor laved with water, nor scourged the phantom of her soul after the dogma of a certain Syrian, nor consecrated an immortality wrapped in the hands of Charon under the auspice of death after the rite of the old man Phasis.

{{143}} Meanwhile, being bidden to mount the litter—which, because it stood upon a lofty dais, she deemed exceedingly difficult for herself, not to say impossible—in order to accomplish this task in due course she summoned her foster-child, beloved above all others, leaning upon whom she overcame every difficulty of the supernal ascent; and he, who was called Toil (Labor) by her, not only lifted her onto the summit of the litter, but tirelessly traversed heaven together with his mistress.

{{144}} For having associated with himself a certain beaming boy—who was not the son of voluptuous Venus, and yet was styled Love (Amor) by the wise—they strove to bear up the litter from the front;

{{145}} while at the rear Epimelia (Diligence) and Agrypnia (Wakefulness), handmaids beloved by the maiden, lifted it; for thus Athanasia had commanded, in order that both sexes might be able to ascend heaven with Philology.
