Straightway the procession of the harmonizing Muses and the venerable multitude of the aforesaid companions went before her as she ascended;

{{146}} Periergia, however, accompanied by other handmaids and dotal servants, followed, curiously investigating and questioning all things: and now that retinue was drawing near with the maiden to the summits of the aerial citadel.

{{147}} And lo! suddenly the Pronuba of the gods was announced to be approaching, before whom Concordia, Fides, and Pudicitia ran ahead;

{{148}} for Cupid, the enticer to bodily pleasure, although he ever flies before her, dared not be present at the meeting with Philology.

{{149}} Now when the goddess came into the sight of the bride and the maiden, as was her custom, had made offering with incense, she entreated the goddess in such words as these: «Fair Juno, although the celestial company hath bestowed another name upon thee and we call thee Juno from helping (iuuando), whence we also name Jove—whether it befits to style thee Lucina, because thou grantest light to those being born, and Lucetia (for it is not needful for me to invoke Fluonia, Februalis, and Februa, since, undefiled in sex, I have endured naught of bodily contagion; mortal maidens ought to summon Iterduca and Domiduca, Unxia and Cinctia to their nuptials, that thou mayest protect their journeys, lead them into their wished-for homes, affix a fortunate omen when they anoint the doorposts, and not forsake them as they lay aside their girdle in the bridal chamber; women whom thou hast protected either in the peril of childbirth or in war will pray to thee as Opigena, the commons ought to invoke thee as Poplona, and warriors as Curitis)—here I call upon thee rather as Hera, named from thy realm of the air: grant to thy suppliant to know what living beings this aerial expanse and these fields translucent with colliding atoms bear, or what divinities are said to fly upward here. For I inquire not concerning the lowliness of that air which is traversed by birds, which the summit of Mount Olympus surpasses, and which rises scarce ten stades in height, but I search out the lofty regions. And now I deem it lawful to behold whatsoever I had understood while reading Concerning Daemons (περὶ δαιμόνων)».

{{150}} Here Juno, not refusing the prayers of the ascending maiden, led her with herself into the aerial citadels and thenceforth instructed her in the diversities of many powers. «Those», said she, «whom we look up to as being of fiery and flaming substance, passing down from the very aether and the circuit of the upper sphere as far as the solar circle, are themselves called gods, and elsewhere are styled celestials, and they order the secrets of hidden causes: for they are purer, nor do the vows of mortal cares greatly trouble them, and they are called impassive (ἀπαθεῖς). There it is most certain that Jove reigns.

{{151}} Beneath the course of the Sun, however, as far as the lunar globe, are divinities of secondary blessedness and slightly lesser power, through whom nevertheless oracles, dreams, and prodigies are fashioned: these cleave entrails in haruspicy, transmit certain admonitions and voices, and speak by augural omens; for very often they warn inquirers either by the course of a star or the bolt of lightning or a portentous novelty.

{{152}} Yet since each several one of these serves each single one of the higher gods, by the arbitrament of those and from the retinue of these is assigned both a general president of all and a special Genius for individual mortals, whom men have also called Praestes (Guardian), because he presides over all things to be done: for supplication is made both to the Genius of the people, when a general one is invoked, and each individual pays homage to his own governor; and therefore he is called Genius because, as soon as any human being is begotten, straightway he is joined to him. This protector and most faithful brother guards the spirits and minds of all,

{{153}} and since he announces the secrets of thoughts to the supernal power, he may also be styled an Angel (Angelus).

{{154}} All these the Greeks call daemons (ἀπὸ τοῦ δαιομένου, from their dividing or knowing), while in Latin they have styled them Medioximi (Intermediate Powers). All of whom indeed are proved to be of a less bright and resplendent nature than those celestials, even as thou beholdest, nor yet are they so corporeal as to be caught by the gaze of men.

{{155}} Here therefore dwell the Lares, here after the bond of the limbs abide purer souls, which for the most part, if they be borne upward by the excellence of their merits, leap even across the circle of the sun and the flaming barriers.

{{156}} Next, from the lunar circle all the way to the earth, whatsoever space lies open between is distinguished by a partition of its own, and a middle region is marked off from the lunar orb. Yet the upper portion, as thou beholdest, encloses those whom they call hemitheoi, and whom in Latin it is fitting to record as Semones or Semidei (Demigods). These bear celestial souls and sacred minds, and are begotten under human semblance for the advantage of the entire world:

{{157}} who indeed have for the most part created faith in the celestials by their own miraculous power—as at the birth of Hercules the compliance of a doubled night and the same babe crushing serpents proved the power of godhead; Tages leaped forth from the furrows and straightway showed to the nation ritual and extispicy; Hammon appeared with ram’s horns and displayed wool-bearing raiment and a fountain’s wave to the thirsting.

{{158}} Why should I speak of those who first afforded to mortals the use of things and greater benefits—as Dionysus discovered the vine at Thebes, and Osiris among the Egyptians the draught and use of wine? Isis in Egypt and Triptolemus among the people of Attica taught the cultivation of grain, and that same Isis showed the use and sowing of flax; Italy marks Pilumnus as inventor of the crushing of grain and the grinding of spelt, and Greece ascribes medicine to Asclepius.

{{159}} Other mortals of this kind also were begotten for the practice of divination and foreknowledge—such as Carmentis in Arcadia, commemorated from the song poured forth through her prophecies; the Sibyl, whether the Erythraean and she who is Cumaean, or the Phrygian, whom thou art not ignorant were not ten, as they assert, but two—that is, Herophile the Trojan, daughter of Marmessus, and Symmachia, daughter of Hippotes, who, born at Erythrae, prophesied at Cumae also. From this capacity for divining, Amphiaraus and Mopsus were celebrated.

{{160}} From the middle of the air, however, as far as the confines of mountains and earth, Hemithei and Heroes move—who are styled Heroes from the fact that the ancients called the earth Hera—and there too are the Manes, that is, guardians assigned to the human body, who have flowed forth from the seeds of parents.

{{161}} Finally, this entire diffusion of air from the moon downward stands under the power of Pluto, who is also called Summanus, as it were summus Manium (Supreme of the Manes). Here the Moon, who presides over this air, is recorded as Proserpina.

{{162}} Now those Manes, because they are assigned to bodies at that time when first conception takes place, even after life delight in those same bodies, and abiding with them are called Lemures; who, if they have been aided by the uprightness of their former life, are turned into Lares of homes and cities,

{{163}} but if they are depraved from the body, they are termed Larvae and Maniae. Here therefore are established Manes both good and fierce, whom the Greek distinction records as agathoi and kakoi daimones (ἀγαθούς and κακοὺς δαίμονας).

{{164}} In these regions also dwell the Summanes and their guardians Mana and Mantuona, gods too whom they call aquili (dark), likewise Fura and Furinna, and Mother Mania, the Intemperiae, and other avenging deities (triptes) of the gods.

{{165}} About the very circle of the earth, however, the air, troubled by heat from above and by exhalation and moisture from below, dashing against souls as they depart from their bodies with a kind of flowing surge, does not readily suffer them to fly upward;

{{166}} and hence the ingenuity of poetic shadowing alluded to the tract of Pyriphlegethon, and in it the impiety of souls whom Vedius—that is, Pluto, whom they have also called Dis and Vejovis—hath adjudged is rolled about and dashed together with perpetual roaring.

{{167}} The earth itself also, where it is pathless to men, is thronged by choirs of long-lived beings who inhabit woods, groves, sacred thickets, lakes, springs, and rivers, and are called Pans, Fauns, †Fones, Satyrs, Silvani, Nymphs, Fatui and Fatuae, or Fantuae, or even Fanae—from whom shrines (fana) are named because they are wont to prophesy. All these die after a protracted span of life, like men, yet they possess a most immediate power both of foreknowing, of assailing, and of harming.

{{168}} Among the former Genii, therefore, thy goddess-self, hitherto mortal, shall take her place: for lo! ethereal Juno—or Vesta she is—commands thee as one already immortal and a goddess, saying: “Now take thy seat straightway in the council of Jove,” and at length takes a portion from the maiden’s censer».

{{169}} Then the bearers of the goddess, having taken up the litter, bore her upward with mighty effort; yet after they had ascended one hundred and twenty-six thousand stades, borne aloft by aerial lightness, and had completed the first tone of the celestial pitches, the maiden, having entered the lunar circle and making supplication to the goddess with fitting incense-fumes, beheld close at hand a certain spherical and tender body, compacted out of the lightness of supernal dew, reflecting back like a gleaming mirror the rays of brightness cast upon it.

{{170}} In it were seen the sistra of the Nile, the Eleusinian torch, the bow of Dictynna, and the timbrels of Cybele; a triform and many-colored whirling also glowed red with a certain terrible majesty: which, although it was believed horned and rough, yet opportune for childbirths, displayed upon its countenances both a cat and a hind and twice-two revolutions.

{{171}} Thence, by half the distance she had ascended to the moon, she came to the circle of the Cyllenian; and when this semitone had been traversed, a manifold throng of attendants ran joyfully to meet her as their wedded mistress;

{{172}} among whom a woman of resplendent beauty and bearing, opulent in ornaments of ready abundance, having greeted the maiden, advanced confidently even to kiss her: yet that multitude of followers—which, as a certain Syrian affirms, had been fixed at the number of two thousand—marveled that a woman whom the Etruscans said had herself been wedded to the Cyllenian, pricked by no envy whatsoever, had clasped the maiden in a tight embrace.

{{173}} She, however, was Eloquence (Facundia), for such was her name, who recalled that she had been born and reared in the household of Philology, nor was it unseemly that her foster-mother should be preferred before her, who had ever furnished adornment to herself and nourishment to many Disciplines.

{{174}} There came also a certain comely and most chaste maiden, who was president and guardian of the Cyllenian house, and was called Themis, or Astraea, or Erigone; in her hand she bore ears of grain and a tablet engraved from ebony with emblems such as these:

{{175}} in the center was an Egyptian bird, which is recorded as the ibis by the inhabitants,

{{176}} yet its crown appeared with a petasus and a most fair countenance, which the intertwining of a twin serpent licked; beneath was a certain gleaming wand, whose head stood forth gilded, its middle grey-blue, and its tip pitch-black; beneath the right hand were a tortoise and a threatening scorpion, on the left a she-goat,

{{177}} while it struck a double-crested bird—which is gentler than prophetic fowl—in trial of combat.

{{178}} The ibis itself, moreover, bore inscribed upon it the name of a certain Memphitic month.

{{179}} When the maiden recognized this tablet presented to her, she revered it; although she knew it to be her bridegroom’s emblem, yet she dared not pass by without supplication.

{{180}} Then also the fairer of the daughters of Atlas, exalted by union with Jove and the summit of her offspring, came upon them, who indeed deigned not to appear in the office of mother-in-law without a balanced scale. Revering her, since she well recognized the mother of her betrothed, the maiden began to honor her with two sacrificial victims dedicated together with the god of gain.

{{181}} Hence the ascent was hastened, and across a semitone they flew up even into the circle of Venus; and having entreated Venus herself—who delighted in the nuptials—as much as was fitting, she is reported to have observed this in her: that though exceedingly fair, yet her forelocks were twined about with serpents and plucked with frequent tearing of hair, and she softened with herself a twofold sex joined in union.

{{182}} Presently they strove to toil upward into the solar circle, for the ascent wearied them by a sesquialteral interval, which was reckoned a tone and a half:

{{183}} there she beheld a certain ship governing the courses of all Nature with diverse desire and utterly brimming with a heap of flames, freighted round with blessed merchandise, over which seven sailors, brothers yet like unto one another, presided. On the prow was seen depicted the form of a cat, on the mast that of a lion, and on the stern that of a crocodile.

{{184}} In that same bark, moreover, a certain fountain of ethereal light, flowing with arcane streams, poured itself forth into the lights of the entire universe. Beholding this, Philology, rising up and making supplication with all veneration while slightly closing her eyes, entreated the god in such words as these:

{{185}} «O lofty power or first offspring of the Unknown Father, sense-giving tinder, fountain of mind, origin of light, kingdom of Nature, glory and vindication of the gods, and eye of the universe, effulgence of gleaming Olympus, to whom alone it is lawful to discern the ultramundane Father and behold the Great God, whom the circle of the aether obeys, and who governest the world with immense sweep:
{{186}} for thou runnest the middle path, alone granting friendly temperament to the celestials, driving and restraining the sacred stars of the gods when thou addest law to their courses;
{{187}} hence it is that it is thy right to course in the fourth circle, so that number may be proved to thee by perfect ratio: dost thou not by this principle grant the double tetrachord?
{{188}} Latium calleth thee Sol because thou alone (solus) in honor after the Father art the summit of light, and men declare thy head, consecrated with twice-six rays, to bear golden lights, because thou completest as many months and as many hours.
{{189}} They say thou turnest four wing-footed steeds with reins, because thou alone tamest the four-horse chariot which the elements afford:
{{190}} for banishing darkness thou uncoverest what shines cerulean; hence they call thee Phoebus, revealing the hidden things of the future,
{{191}} or, because thou dissolvest nightly misdeeds, Lyaeus. Thee the Nile worships as Serapis, Memphis reveres as Osiris, dissonant rites hail as Mithras, Dis, and Horus-Typhon;
{{192}} fair Attis likewise, and the kindly boy of the curved plow, Hammon of parched Libya, and Adonis of Byblos: thus the entire world invokes thee by varied name.
{{193}} Hail, true countenance of the gods and paternal visage, whose three letters by the numbers six hundred and eight form the sacred surname and omen of Mind (Mentis). Grant, O Father, to my mind to ascend the ethereal assemblies and to know the starry heaven beneath thy sacred name».
{{194}} Heard in these prayers, she was bidden to pass onward from the abode of the gods: yet, uplifted by a semitone, the circle of Pyrois detained her, wherein was the greatest of the sons of Jove;

{{195}} from which circle the river Pyriphlegethon was seen to flow downward to the realms below.

{{196}} Having crossed this—for it had been no toil to traverse the intervening semitone—they arrived at the splendors of the Jovial star, whose circle resounded with the Phrygian pitch: there was a star of life-giving and wholesome temperament, quivering with effulgence and mild brightness, the nature of whose light, glowing white from warm and moist commixtures, shone ruddy with a certain tranquillity of prosperity. Yet there was only the star of Jove: for he himself, surveying all the limbs of the universe, was said to have gone to the command of the gods and the celestial senate.

{{197}} Having passed beyond this circle also and lifted high by an equal interval, she beheld the most rigid creator of the gods lingering amid chill cold and snowy hoarfrost; yet that same orb which she strove to pass around chimed with a Dorian melody. Upon its president himself appeared now the face of a dragon, now the gaping jaws of a lion, now crests with boar’s tusks, and he raged deadly with utter horror—to whom nevertheless a greater power, proportionate to the vastness of his circle, and pre-eminence over the rest were attributed. At length, terrified by his sickles and boomings, the maiden fled away from so intolerable an encounter.

{{198}} Thence by the greatest efforts they were borne upward through a sesquialteral stretch of the journey: for by a tone and a half one arrives at the globe of the celestial sphere itself and its circuit fretted with stars.

{{199}} And thus, wearied by the ascents of six tones and the spent fatigue of stades, when they perceived that whatsoever distance they had measured out sang together in the symphony of the diapason with the perfection of absolute modulation, refreshed after their greatest toils they rested for a little while.

{{200}} Philology herself, leaping down from her litter, when she beheld the boundless fields of light and the springtide of ethereal tranquillity, and now discerned so many diversities and forms of decans, and then marveled that eighty-four liturgists stood attending in heaven, and saw moreover the gleaming globes of crowded stars and the textures of circles in alternating interlacement, while that very sphere

{{201}} which constrains the outermost circuit was driven onward with wondrous sweeps, and the poles and axis, brandished from the summit of heaven, pierced through the deep earth, and by it the entire mass and fabric of heaven was whirled round—

{{202}} not ignorant that the Father and God of so great a work and so great a reason had withdrawn even from the knowledge of the gods themselves, since she knew that he had transcended extramundane blessednesses, rejoicing in a certain empyrean and intellectual world—beside the very wall of the outermost circuit, resting upon her knees and with the whole edge of her mind concentrated,

{{203}} she prayed long in silence, and after the rite of the ancients calling aloud with the voice of her mind certain names—varied in numbers according to dissonant nations, unknown in sound, breathed forth with yoked and alternating letters—she venerated with words the presiding gods of the intellectual world and their ministers, to be revered by the powers of the sensible sphere,

{{204}} and the universal whole encompassed by the depth of the Infinite Father, and entreated certain three gods and others rayed with the seventh of day and night;

{{205}} she prayed also to a certain Fountain Virgin, and according to the mysteries of Plato, the Once and Twice Beyond (ἅπαξ καὶ δὶς ἐπέκεινα) powers.

{{206}} When she had most earnestly entreated these, the Flower of Fire, and that Truth which exists out of non-existents with her whole breast, then she seemed to behold her apotheosis and to have merited sacred rites,

{{207}} since certain brightnesses of a milky river flowed gently downward with blazing stars:

{{208}} rejoicing therefore and bearing witness to her gratitude, she turned her course into the Galaxy, where she knew the senate of the gods had been assembled by Jove. Now there stood the Jovial mansion, which both possessed a cosmic circuit of wondrous vastness and surpassed the brilliance of the stars in conspicuous beauty and adorned the zodiacal circle with the novelty of its site; moreover, it gleamed with such splendor that one would have believed it fashioned of the substance of silver. There gleaming enclosures and a roof inlaid with snowy borders shone white,

{{209}} where already Jupiter, seated with Juno and all the gods upon a mighty dais and milky benches, awaited the bridal arrivals: who, as soon as he perceived the voices of the Muses and sweet melodies in varied lays at the maiden’s approach, commanded the Cyllenian to come forward in the first place,

{{210}} where Liber and the Delian, faithful and most loving brothers, Hercules too, both of the Castors, and Gradivus, and whatsoever of the gods was begotten of Jove, clung to the service of the Cyllenian;

{{211}} the governors of the elements also, a most fair multitude of angelic hosts, and the souls moreover of the blessed ancients, who had already merited the temples of heaven, followed the steps of Maia’s son.

{{212}} There might one behold Linus, Homer, and the Mantuan bard wreathed and singing; Orpheus and Aristoxenus resounding upon stringed instruments; Plato and Archimedes turning golden spheres;

{{213}} Heraclitus was ablaze, Thales was drenched with moisture, Democritus appeared surrounded by atoms; Samian Pythagoras unfolded certain celestial numbers, Aristotle too was searching most scrupulously throughout the heights of heaven for entelechy, while Epicurus brought roses mingled with violets and all the allurements of pleasures. Zeno was leading a foreseeing woman, Arcesilaus was gazing upon a dove’s neck, and besides these a vast throng of cloaked philosophers clashed in discordant pursuits—all of whom indeed, amid the songs of the harmonizing Muses, though they raised a din, could not be heard for any clamor of pleading.

{{214}} As the Cyllenian entered and advanced, therefore, that entire senate of the gods, revering the head of him who entered, rose up: Jupiter himself placed him in the center near his own throne, with Pallas associated at his right hand.

{{215}} Nor after a long interval was Philology herself also invited, surrounded by the Muses and preceded by her mother. As she entered, and Vesta—nurse of the gods and likewise handmaid—poured forth that fragrant censer of incense, that entire order of celestials, assigning to themselves their fitting portions, rejoiced in the Arabian exhalations.

{{216}} Yet the maiden, modest as she was in all things, although bidden by Jupiter to sit beside his border, wished rather to take her seat there where she had beheld the Muses, apart from the companionship of Pallas.

{{217}} Then, rising up, the maiden’s mother demanded of Jove and all the celestials that in the sight of all whatsoever Maia’s son had prepared under the title of betrothal gifts should be delivered, and that at length the dowry from the maiden should not be wanting, and that then they should permit the marriage tablets and the Lex Papia Poppaea to be read aloud.

{{218}} To whose most just petition the senate of the gods granted that the offerings should be approved in the assembly of the celestials. Hereupon Phoebus rose, not declining a brother’s office, and began to lead forward one by one from the retinue and choice band of the Cyllenian maidens who shone forth as fair to all as they were most richly adorned.

{{219}} We have traversed, O reader, a great part of our fable, which, entangled in such lingering windings, hath compelled the lamp to flicker with slender light. Pressing twilight leans hard upon us, and unless Dawn were purpling the summits with rose-gardens and, rising with her first breath, were adorning and cleaving the windows with light, our yoked page would still be preparing a wider circuit whithersoever led.
{{220}} Now therefore the myth cometh to an end, and the arts begin which the books that follow shall expound: for with true fruit they banish all fiction and shall set forth sober disciplines for the greater part, nor yet shall they forbid playful jest. Thou hast what lies before thee, if the power of the celestials, the Muses, and the Latoian lyre lend their favor.

## Liber III. De arte grammatica

{{221}} Once more the Camena prepares trappings for our little book and wishes first to bring forth fictions clothed in allegory, reminding me that in the chill of bare truth utility can adorn naught, and counting it a fault in a poet to present unmitigated certainties, while she grants playful license to wit and beautifies the page overlaid with abundant color.

{{222}} «Yet», said I, «the very title of the former book admonishes that myths have been banished from our lips, and that the Arts, speaking truths, prepare the precepts of the volumes that follow». But she, with a jesting smile, replied: «Let us utter no falsehood, and yet let the Arts be clothed. Or wilt thou give the band of sisters naked to the bridal pair, and so shall they seek the senate of the Thunderer and the celestials? Or if it please thee to pass over their attire in silence, what order is approved?» «Assuredly», said I, «they shall speak whatsoever must be taught, and their vesture shall follow in bodiless utterance». «That indeed», said she, «is a feigned speech, and thou strayest from thy promise; why then dost thou not confess that without the figure of poetic fiction nothing can be arrayed?» With these words the Camena vanquished me. With swift flights I shall yoke my sport.

{{223}} The son of Leto, therefore, had led forward in the first place one of Mercury’s handmaids, aged indeed in years yet most winning in courtesy, who recounted that she had been born in Memphis while Osiris was still king, and after being long hidden in lurking-places had been discovered and reared by the Cyllenian himself. This woman, although she asserted that in Attica—where she had flourished during the greater part of her life—she went about clad in the Greek pallium, nevertheless, after the rite of Romulus, out of reverence for the Latian godhead, the head of Olus, the Martian race, and the progeny of Venus, entered the senate of the gods wearing the Roman paenula. Moreover, she carried a cylindrical casket of compactly joined pieces, gleaming without with smooth ivory, from which, like a skilled mistress of the healing art, she brought forth the emblems for curing wounds.

{{224}} For from this casket she first displayed a pruning-knife with a flashing point, wherewith she said the defects of infants’ tongues could be circumcised, and then healed again by means of a certain blackish powder, thought to be compounded from soot or cuttlefish ink, applied through reeds. Next she also brought forth a certain exceedingly sharp medicament, which she had concocted from the blossom of the giant fennel and a strip of goatskin, of a deep red color, which she advised should be applied to throats when, afflicted by untaught rusticity, they exhale the foul belchings of a faulty mouth. She also displayed a certain most pleasant draught, labored over late at night with abundant oil, by which, once the harshness of a most unmelodious voice had been driven away, she declared men could even become tuneful.

{{225}} She purged the windpipe and chest as well by the application of a certain salve, wherein were noted wax smeared upon beechwood, a mixture of oak-galls and gum, and glue from the Nilotic shrub. Although the potency of this poultice procured memory and attention, yet by its very nature it also brought on sleeplessness.

{{226}} She produced also a certain file polished with art, which glittered as though divided by diverse joinings into eight gilded parts, with which by a gentle, gradual rubbing she cleansed rough teeth, blemishes of tongues, and the filth which men had contracted in a certain city called Soli.

{{227}} She was believed to know arcane charms and manifold numbers by the work of frequent computation. In fine, as often as she received patients to be cured, it was her custom to treat first of the noun (nomen); nor was she silent regarding cases (casus), how greatly they might either engender faults or be most prudently declined; then the genders of things; she also examined with reason the verbs (verba) of those under her care, lest, as is the custom with the sick, they exchange one word for another. Next she inquired into the moods, tenses, and figures of verbs, and commanded others, in whom a duller fullness and motionless torpor had settled, to run through the degrees and scan as many works as possible, to lean upon prepositions or conjunctions or participles, and to be exercised thoroughly by the whole art.

{{228}} When, therefore, several of the gods judged this woman, skilled in such healing of many ills, to be Medicine (Iatrice), and others Astrology (Genethliace) by reason of her diverse works—and credibility was added to her skill in healing because neither Pallas nor Maia’s son himself denied that she could bring aid to defects of the mouth—it nonetheless seemed inconsistent for a female physician to walk abroad in a paenula. Wherefore her name, her office, and an exposition of her entire art were required of her.

{{229}} Then she, as she had the familiar habit of expounding what was asked and readily teaching what was sought of her, having drawn back her paenula from her right shoulder with modesty and bashfulness, began thus: «I am called Grammatike (Γραμματική) in Greece, because a line is styled gramme (γραμμή) and letters grammata (γράμματα), and to me is assigned the task of tracing the forms of letters with their proper strokes. Hence Romulus ascribed to me the name Litteratura, although in my infancy he wished to call me Litteratio, just as among the Greeks I was originally styled Grammatistike (Γραμματιστική); then too he appointed a master and gathered youthful pupils. Thus he who is now called a man of letters (litteratus), as an upholder of my art, was formerly termed a litterator. This also a certain Catullus, no unpleasing poet, commemorates when he says: munus dat tibi Sylla litterator (“Sulla the schoolmaster gives thee a gift”). Among the Greeks the same man is called grammatodidaskalos (γραμματοδιδάσκαλος).

{{230}} Now my office formerly was to write and read learnedly; at present this too has been added, that it is mine to understand and judge with erudition—two functions which seem to me to be shared even with philosophers and critics. Therefore, of these four, two must be called active and two contemplative: since we expend action when we compose or read anything, whereas we are occupied in contemplation when we understand or judge what has been written, although they are joined together by a certain kinship, as is proved in the other arts. For the actor too first understands what he is able to perform, and the astronomer constructs certain instruments in order that through them he may know what he ought to verify. The geometrician likewise mingles both, since he both constructs and comprehends the figures of theorems with sure reasonings.

{{231}} My parts, moreover, are four: letters (litterae), literature (litteratura), the man of letters (litteratus), and scholarly execution (litterate). Letters are what I teach; literature is the very body of what I teach; the man of letters is he whom I have taught; scholarly execution is what he whom I form handles skillfully. I profess, moreover, to treat of the nature and use of speech (oratio). Nature is the elements out of which speech is formed; use is the name given when we employ that same speech. To these is also added subject matter (materies), so that we may judge concerning what topic we must speak. Speech itself, however, is trained by three degrees: that is, from letters, from syllables, and from words.

{{232}} Now concerning letters, a twofold inquiry arises. For they are either natural or artificially fashioned. At Nature’s prompting their names were brought forth into the substance of speech; whereas artificial formation designed their written lines which we inscribe, to the end that those present might use the one, and those absent the other. And thus from this aspect, wherein they are written, they are called mute (mutae), and from that, wherein they are read aloud, vocal sounds (voces), since the latter can be apprehended by the ears alone, the former by the eyes alone.

{{233}} Letters, therefore, are some of them such as can effect a whole by themselves, and others such as can effect nothing alone. For the vowels—which the Greeks record as seven, Romulus as six, and later usage as five, y being rejected as Greek—can nevertheless in Latium now be lengthened, now shortened, now marked with an acute, now with a grave or even a circumflex accent, now joined together, and now separated without loss of their name. At one time they form syllables by themselves; at another they take on consonants on either side and certain vowels; at one moment they change into one another, and anon they succeed one another with grace. Indeed, a is for the most part transformed into e, for capio makes cepi; now into i, as sacro, insilio; now into o, as plaudo, plostrum; or into u, as arca, arcula. Likewise the letter e is reshaped first into a, as sero, satum; or into i, as moneo, monitus; or into o, as toga from tegendo; or into u, as tugurium from tego. Similarly the vowel i also is converted into a, as siquis, siqua; into e, as fortis, forte; into o, as qui, quo; into u, as ibi, ubi. In no other wise does the letter o pass into a, as creo, creavi; or into e, as tutor, tutela; or into i, as virgo, virginis; or into u, as volo, volui. Likewise u by a similar principle is converted into a, as magnus, magna; into i, as telum, teli; into o, as lepus, leporis; into e, as sidus, sideris. Thus transformed, therefore, they will now be able to be joined on both sides, now on one side, now on neither, now terminating some parts of speech and now none.

{{234}} For a first associates with itself on both sides the letters u and i, for we say both aurum and varus, and both Ianus and Aiax. On one side only it admits e, as in Aeneas, and on neither side o. It terminates feminine nouns, as dea; masculine nouns, as Iugurtha; neuter nouns in the singular number only when Greek, as toreuma, peripetasma, but in the plural when Latin, as monilia; and in verbs the imperative mood, as canta, salta.

{{235}} The vowel e, however, possesses the force of two Greek letters; for when it is shortened it is the Greek ε, as in ab hoc hoste; when it is lengthened it is η, as in ab hac die, and then most especially it holds the circumflex accent. It embraces two vowels, namely i and u, on either side, as in Euro, Veientano, and in Eia, iecore. It terminates neuter nouns, as monile; feminine nouns also, if Greek, as Calliope; pronouns, as ille, iste; also a numeral of all genders, as quinque, although it is indeclinable; likewise verbs of the imperative mood, as sede, curre, and of the infinitive mood, as scribere, scripsisse.

{{236}} The letter i, in turn, is associated on both sides with all vowels; for it embraces a when we say Ianuariae and Aiax; that it holds e the examples shown above have taught; it is joined to the letter o in the name Iovis and Oinone [also oisus is said, for thus the ancients pronounced usum]; and to u, as in iuvando and vita. This vowel sometimes makes a double syllable, and terminates a noun of all genders, as frugi; a numeral likewise of all genders, as viginti; also verbs of the indicative mood, as novi, memini, of the imperative, as sali, veni, and of the infinitive, as iaculari, luctari.

{{237}} With which vowels and on which side the letter o coheres hath been recorded above. It terminates, moreover, masculine nouns, as Cato; feminine nouns, as Iuno; a numeral of all genders, as octo; likewise active verbs, as canto, laudo; in the imperative mood it terminates only one, namely cedo; an adverb, as subito; and a preposition, as pro.

{{238}} The letter u, on the other hand, unites on either side with all vowels except o placed before it. Alone also u does not double a letter of its own kind, but joins it, as in vulgus, Vulcanus. It terminates pronouns, as tu, and neuter nouns, as genu, cornu, yet it closes no verb.

{{239}} Thus far concerning vowels; for I was bidden not to treat merely of vowels, but to set forth all the letters. Now there is no doubt that the remaining letters which follow are consonants, which are likewise divided into semivowels and mutes, whose system I shall pursue.

{{240}} Indeed, the letter f, which is the first of the semivowels, when placed first embraces only two consonants, l and r, as when we say flavus and frugi. Vowels, however, it embraces in such wise that it forbids the vowels i and u to be placed before it, nor does it stand as a final letter closing any word.

{{241}} The letter l, in turn, has a threefold sound. For it renders a thin sound when it is doubled, as in sollers, Sallustius; a middle sound when it terminates nouns, as in sol, sal; likewise it sounds softly when a vowel precedes it, as in lapis, lepus, liber, locus, lucerna; whereas it has a full sound when the letters p, g, c, f are placed before it, as in Plauto, glebis, Claudio, Flavo. Moreover, l is never placed before any semivowel or mute, and it is received in place of d in a preposition when a is placed before it, as in allidit, alligat. It is also preserved when the preposition in is prefixed, as in illepidus, illotus. On either side it embraces vowels, and it terminates several nouns: in the masculine sol, in the feminine Tanaquil, in the neuter when preceded by a, e, i, as bidental, mel, sil. It also closes adverbs, as semel.

{{242}} The letter m, on the other hand, can stand at the beginning of a word as well as in the middle and at the end, as in mores, umbra, triticum. Of consonants it precedes n alone, as in Mnemon, and follows s alone, as in Sminthius; it also changes sometimes into n, as when we say nunquis; and it terminates neuter nouns, as aurum and argentum, likewise indeclinable nouns (monoptota), as nequam, in the masculine gender the singular accusative and the plural genitive, as hominem, hominum, likewise participles and verbs and adverbs, as tractum, legam, cursim, and also numerals of all genders, as novem, decem.

{{243}} The letter n appears fuller at the beginning and end of words, as in Nestor, tibicen, and thinner in the middle, as in mane, damnum. Of consonants it follows the letter m, as in Memnon, while it precedes s, as in fons, mons. It is also converted into m when b, p, m follow it, as when we say imbuit, impulit, imminet. By this same letter are terminated masculine nouns, as tibicen, Greek feminine nouns, as Siren, and neuter nouns, as culmen.

{{244}} R, in turn, embraces vowels on either side. Of consonants it is placed before s alone, as in sors, fors, and sometimes before double x, as in arx. It is converted into l, n, and s, as in niger, nigellus; femur, feminis; gero, gessi. It terminates masculine nouns after all vowels, as par, pater, vir, nitor, fur; feminine nouns after e and o, as mulier, soror; neuter nouns after all vowels except i, as calcar, piper, marmor, sulphur. It terminates verbs and adverbs, as venor, quater.

{{245}} S some have not considered a letter at all; for Messala called it a kind of hissing. Yet it is joined on either side to the letter p, as in spado, psittacus, and embraces c, q, t following it, as in Scaurus, squama, stella. It also makes a transition into several letters: as into l, for we say modus, modulus; into n, sanguis, sanguinis; into r, flos, floris; into d, custos, custodis; into t, nepos, nepotis. It terminates both masculine and feminine nouns after all vowels, and neuter nouns after a, o, u, as vas, os, nemus. To this letter the deified Claudius added p or c on account of the Greek ψ and ξ, as in psalterium, saxa.

{{246}} X, however, no one accounts a single letter, since it is double; for it consists of g and s, as in rex, regis, or of c and s, as in nux, nucis. Sometimes it is transformed into v, as in nix, nivis, and into c, as in pix, picis. In Latin words it is never placed first, though among the Greeks it is so placed, as in Xantho. And in the masculine it is associated with all vowels, as Aiax, frutex, calix, velox, Pollux, and in the feminine, as fax, lex, lodix, celox, lux; it closes no neuter noun.

{{247}} Having dispatched the semivowels, the mutes must be examined, which are called by this name because, unless vowels associated with them come to their aid, they die within the sound of the mouth before they can be uttered.

{{248}} B, the first of them, is connected on both sides with all vowels; it is placed before the consonants l and r, as in blaesus and brevis. It passes into c, as in succurrit; into f, as in sufficit; into m, as in summittit; into p, as in supponit; and into s, as in sustulit.

{{249}} It terminates nothing save the three prepositions ab, ob, sub. C flanks vowels on both sides, and precedes certain consonants—l, t, r, m, n—as in clarus, tectum, crus, Acmon, Cnidus. It terminates demonstratives, which they call pronouns—hic, haec, hoc—and adverbs, as sic, huc, hic. Alone among the mutes it lengthens a preceding vowel and is reckoned as a double consonant, as in hic, hoc.

{{250}} D holds vowels on either side. It is placed before the letter r in Druso. It is never placed before m in Latin words, though sometimes in Greek, as in Dmois; so too before n, as in Ariadne. It passes into c, as in accidit; into g, as in aggerit; into l, as in allegat; into p, as in apponit; into r, as in arripit; into s, as in assidet; into t, as in attinet. It terminates pronouns of the neuter gender, as istud, illud, and the preposition apud.

{{251}} G is placed before all vowels. It follows the letter a, as in aggere, where as often as it is doubled it is joined together. It is placed before the letter r, as in grave; l, as in gladius; n, as in ignis. It is also converted into c, as in rego, rector. It closes nothing.

{{252}} That H is a mark of aspiration is most certain. Sometimes it is added to vowels, as in hospes and heres. It passes into x, as in traho, traxi. This letter the Greeks divided in two; for its right-hand half is the mark of rough breathing, and its left-hand half of the opposite signification.

{{253}} K, in turn, is now deemed a mere mark and now a letter; for there is no doubt that its function is to supply c, save in these words: kapita, kalendae, kalumniae.

{{254}} P, moreover, unites on both sides with vowels; it is placed before the letter r, as in prandere; l, as in placere; t, Ptolomaeus; s, psittacus; n, as in sympnum; yet it terminates nothing.

{{255}} Q certain grammarians did not consider a letter, and they would prevail, were it not expressly apparent in equo and equitatu. It is never placed without two vowels, the first of which will be u and the rest follow thus, as in quartus, questus, Quirites, quotus, equus. It consists, however, of c and u, and therefore is called double and compound, nor is it doubled. It embraces no single consonant, and it terminates nothing.

{{256}} T encloses vowels on both sides, as in conjunctions and prepositions. It precedes l in Tlepolemo, m in Tmolo, n in potnia, and r in Troia. It terminates the neuter nouns caput, sinciput, lact. It terminates demonstratives, as quot, tot; verbs, as legunt; and an adverb and interjection, as ut, attat.

{{257}} Z came from the Greeks, although they themselves also used Greek σ at first; for they used to say Sethum where they now say Zethum. Nevertheless, this is reckoned a double letter even by them, for it is compounded of τ and σ; and that thou mayest note it is double, it can never be doubled. It is placed before the letter m, as in Zmyrna.

{{258}} Out of all these, therefore, eighteen letters fulfill the necessity of all writing; for it pleases me that y be gathered into the number of vowels, nor without it could Hyacinthus or Cyllenius be written. Thus it will come to pass that there are six vowels, six semivowels, and six mutes.

{{259}} For if h be assigned to aspiration, and q and k be superfluous, while x as a double letter is not admitted into the rank of simple elements, and z is excluded by the Latins, eighteen in number, as hath been said, remain.

{{260}} All these, nevertheless—together with those that are superfluous and rejected—being fashioned out of the sound of a single voice, have found diverse causes of natural harmony beneath the consonance of the mouth.

{{261}} For A we utter with breath alone beneath a fitting opening of the mouth; B we pronounce with the lips parted by an impulse of breath; C is pressed out by the molars applied against the edges of the tongue; D is born from the impact of the tongue about the upper teeth; E is made by the breath with the tongue pressed slightly flatter; F by the teeth pressing down the lower lip; G by the breath together with the palate; H the wind breathes out with the throat slightly contracted; I the breath utters with the teeth nearly closed. K is formed by the throat and palate. L is sweetened by the tongue and palate. M is impressed upon the lips. N strikes as the tongue is pressed against the teeth. O is produced by the breath of a rounded mouth. P bursts forth as breath from the lips, Q by the impact of the palate with the mouth drawn tight. R is scraped out while the tongue vibrates the breath. S makes a hissing as the teeth are struck. T is beaten out by the impact of the tongue and the striking of the teeth. V is exhibited with the mouth constricted and the lips protruding. X hisses out whatever C and S have formed. Y proceeds with lips pressed together and a breath. Z, however, Appius Claudius detested for this reason: because, while it is pronounced, it imitates the teeth of a corpse.

{{262}} «Briefly hath been sketched the rule of our first division, which is wont to be stretched across many volumes—wherein is shown what a letter binds at either juncture, where it admits a companion or causes one to lie hidden, which passes into which by usage’s inverted laws and wills a noun to stand transformed, what sounds the mouth or modulation of the tongue engenders, and the lips parted by their own pulse. Now must the syllable be traversed in its compacted forms, how it is raised in pitch and becomes long or short. These two I shall run through; for the letter, going before, hath already taught its junctures under a like principle. This order, therefore, subjoining what remains, undertakes to weave it, if your judgment, O celestials, approves».
{{263}} As Grammar was speaking thus and Jupiter and the Delian were bidding her proceed, here Pallas spoke: «Concerning the juncture of syllables, while Literature here makes haste, she hath passed over the historical part». Terrified at this objection of the maiden goddess, Grammar said: «I know that I must pass over very many things, lest by weaving together minutiae I incur the weariness of supernal blessedness. Hastening along summary paths, therefore, I shall fulfill my purpose, lest overshadowed by thick branches I grow wild as in the density of a thorny thicket.

{{264}} A syllable (syllaba), therefore, is so called because it is formed by joined letters receiving sound simultaneously. Of which, as I have said, there is no doubt that there are three parts: concerning juncture (iunctura), concerning pitch or accent (fastigium), and concerning lengths (longitudines).

{{265}} Of juncture there are four kinds: two natural and two historical. The natural are when it is inquired whether each syllable is joined on one side, on both sides, or on neither, and whether letters which cannot be coupled together of themselves may be associated by the addition of another letter—as m and n, while they do not unite directly, are joined when a vowel intervenes, as in amni and somno; which kinds of junctures may be passed over from the fact that they were recorded under Letters.

{{266}} Historical juncture, on the other hand, is when, out of letters capable of being coupled together, diversity of pronunciation hath associated letters neither identical nor equal in number. For we write the first syllable of Musarum with two letters, the Greeks with three. Lucilius in the dative case joins a and e, saying huic Terentiae, Orbiliae; Licinius joins a and i, as also Lucretius frequently, and our Maro writes aulai, pictai.

{{267}} Likewise there are two junctures when we unite syllables on account of a metrical foot or verse, so that two suddenly merge into one, as in synaloepha, or when letters are elided from a word, as in ecthlipsis.

{{268}} Thus far concerning junctures; now let us consider pitch (fastigium), which topic is called among the Greeks peri prosodion (περὶ προσῳδιῶν). This is divided into three: for every syllable is either grave, acute, or circumflex; and just as no word exists without a vowel, so none exists without an accent. And accent is, as some have thought, the soul of the word and the seed-bed of music, because all modulation is composed of the high pitches and gravity of words, and therefore accentus is so named as if adcantus (song accompanying speech).

{{269}} Every Latin word, therefore, whether simple or compound, has one accent, either acute or circumflex; two acute or circumflex accents it can never have, though it often has grave ones. It has the acute on the first syllable if thou sayest Caelius, on the second in Sallustius, on the third in Curiatius; likewise the circumflex on the first if thou sayest caelum; moreover, the penultimate syllable is never accented with an acute if short by nature itself, but the one preceding it, that is, the third from the end (antepenult), whether it be short or long, as in Cicero, Caelius. The circumflex sound rests solely upon that syllable which precedes the last, and in no other way than when it is itself long by nature and yet the last syllable is short, as in Galenus. But if the last is long, or if the penult is long by position, even though the last be short, an acute accent will nevertheless be produced, as in Galeni, Camille. It is the property of the circumflex that it adheres only to syllables long by nature, whereas the acute is found in both long and short syllables. Every word, moreover, must necessarily have either an acute or a circumflex sound, even if it be monosyllabic; monosyllables, however, lack the grave. Every monosyllabic word, when it signifies something, whether it is short or long by position, will take an acute, as when we say far, ars; if, however, it is long by nature, it will take a circumflex, as lux, mos. Disyllables, on the other hand, take an acute on the first syllable either when both are short, as citus, or when both are long by position, as sollers, or when either one is long by position, as contus, cohors. If, however, the first syllable is long by nature and the following is short, the first will take a circumflex, as luna; if the second is long by position or nature, the first will take an acute, as codex, docte. For no first syllable in a disyllable will be found grave. In trisyllables, if the middle syllable—which we call the penult—be short, it is without doubt pronounced with a grave accent, and straightway its first syllable, that is, the third from the end, will take an acute, as in Catulo; if, however, that same middle syllable is long, it matters in what manner it is long. For if it is long by nature and the last syllable is short, the middle will take a circumflex, as Cethegus, Mancinus; if, however, the middle is long by nature and the final is long, the middle will take an acute, as Catoni, Ciceroni; or if the middle is long by position, it will remain acute whatsoever the final syllable may be, as Catullus, Catullo; Metellus, Metello.

{{270}} Pronouns, however, which are declined in a twofold manner—that is, which either shorten or lengthen the middle syllable in the genitive case—take an acute, as ipsius, illius. If the second syllables of these become short, the first will be acute, as ípsius, íllius. If, however, the middle syllables are long, the first will be grave and the second circumflex, as óccidit sol (“the sun sets”) and occídit hominem (“he slays a man”). Therefore the first syllables are acute when the middle ones are short; when, however, the middle ones [are long by mute and liquid, the first are] acute when the final syllables are long, as tenebrae, latebrae, manipli.

{{271}} These differ from the former words, which are such as Catullus, Sallustius, in this respect: that those have separate consonants, whereas these, although they too have the penult long by position, retain none of the consonants for themselves in the second syllable, but two stand in the third. And hence it came to pass that the middle age pronounced these nouns ténebras and látebras with an acute accent on the first syllable, whereas manipolos and fenéstras were spoken with an acute on the penultimate syllable; because some deemed that kind of penultimate syllable wholly short, inasmuch as it did not end in a consonant, while others deemed it long, because, although it did not end in a consonant, the next syllable began with two consonants, and because the nature of the letters l and r, which is fluid, produces now a long and now a short syllable. No Roman word of two or more syllables, moreover, ends with an acute accent, nor does any have a circumflex at the end save one whose latter part runs out into a syllable long by nature, as ergo and pone.

{{272}} The context of speech very often takes away or alters the proper accents of individual words. It takes them away from those which refer to something following, such as prepositions in ante urbem; for here ante has lost the acute accent of its first syllable; likewise post muros. Enclitic words joined on—-que, -ve, -ne—alter accents, provided -que is a copulative conjunction and -ve an expletive, as in Latiumque augescere vultis and stimulove meum cor in Accius’s Pelopidae. Never will an acute accent migrate from first syllables to final ones except in the case of enclitic particles, whose property it is to sharpen the final parts of the words to which they are attached. Greek nouns, when they are turned into Latin, are pronounced according to our rule, unless they remain Greek. Thus Olympus and Caucasus, if they are pronounced as Latin words, appear clearly as to where they ought to take an acute or circumflex according to the principle written above. Yet if they remain Greek (and we accede to this rule in foreign words), it is nevertheless necessary that in the oblique cases they preserve acute accents on the middle syllables, since among the Greeks too we hear them pronounced with no other accent.

{{273}} The acute accent is marked by a stroke ascending from left to right, the grave by one descending from left to right, and the sign of the circumflex is a sigma curved downward above the letters themselves. Accents we call in part fastigia (summits), because they are placed upon the heads of letters, and in part cacumina (peaks), toni (tones), or soni (sounds), while the Greeks call them prosodiai. One must know also that all three accents can befall a single word, as is Argiletum.

{{274}} I think it hath been sufficiently set forth what the accents of syllables are and how they shift; now I shall declare their lengths (longitudines). For every syllable is either short, long, or common. It is short when a vowel is shortened and is aided by no consonants, or when it ends in a short form or letter.

{{275}} A long syllable, on the other hand, is produced in two ways, by nature and by position: by nature, when a vowel is lengthened, or when a syllable holds a circumflex accent, or an acute on the penult, or when any monosyllable consists of a single vowel, or when a diphthong is found, or when the first syllable of any compound verb under another form changes neither its vowel nor its accent; by position, when two consonants follow a short vowel—whether in the same syllable, or in the following syllable, or divided across both—or one double consonant instead of two.

{{276}} The double consonants are x and z; sometimes i and c can become double, as if i stands between vowels, as in aio, Troia; likewise c, as in hoc erat, alma parens. These forms of consonants or double letters, which support short vowels, have power to do so when they are enclosed within the same word; otherwise the consonants of the next word give no support to the preceding one, although Virgil dissents, saying arma virumque cano Troiae, and likewise fontesque fluviosque voco.

{{277}} The diphthongs, moreover, are ae, oe, eu, au, ei; apart from these no diphthong is recorded.

{{278}} Of common syllables there are eight modes. The first is when a short vowel is followed by a liquid subordinated to a mute consonant; for it is long in vasto Cyclopis in antro, and short in vastosque ab rupe Cyclopas. The second mode is when a short vowel ends in a single consonant with h following; for it is long in terga fatigamus hasta, and short in quisquis honos tumuli. The third is when a short vowel is followed by two consonants of which s is the first letter; for it is long in unde spissa coma, and short in ponite spes sibi quisque. The fourth case is when a short syllable terminates a part of speech; for it is long at a pause, as is nam tibi, Thymbre, caput Evandrius, and short in hoc caput, o cives. Likewise another case becomes common from a diphthong: long, as Musae Aonides, and short, as insulae Ionio in magno. For when a vowel follows, a diphthong can become short, as sudibusve praeustis. Another case is when another vowel follows a long vowel; for it is long in o ego infelix, quem fugis, and short in sub Ilio alto. Another case is when a pronoun ends in the letter c with a vowel following; for it is long in hoc erat alma parens, and short in solus hic inflexit sensus. Another case is when z follows a short vowel; for it is long in Mezenti ducis exuvias, and short in nemorosa Zacynthos. Having stated the nature of the three kinds of syllables, it remains to speak of final syllables, wherein the authority of the art and canonical form consist. And first we must treat of the noun.

{{279}} The nominative case singular ending in the letter a is short, as Catilina, Iulia; ending in the letter e it is short in Latin nouns, as sedile, and long in Greek nouns, as Euterpe; ending in i it is long, as frugi; ending in o it is short in Latin nouns, as Cato [in Greek nouns it is long, although authority varies; indeed Virgil wrote et te, magne Cato]; ending in u it is short, as cornu. Ending in al it is short, as tribunal; in el short, as mel; in il short, as vigil, save for the single Etruscan name Tanaquil; in ol it is long, as sol; in ul short, as consul. Ending in m it is short, as tectum, although an example of this rarely occurs, because m caught between vowels is elided as if through the harshness of mytacism. Ending in n it is lengthened, as Titan; en in neuters is short, as carmen, in other genders long, as lien, Siren; ending in on it is long, as Memnon. Ending in ar it is long only in monosyllables, as Nar, far; ending in er it is short in Latin nouns, as puer, save for the single monosyllable ver, while in Greek nouns it is lengthened, as aer; ending in ir it is shortened, as vir; ending in or it is short, as auctor; ending in ur it is shortened, as murmur, save for the single monosyllable fur. Ending in as it is lengthened in Latin nouns, as facultas, paupertas; in Greek nouns it is short only when the genitive ends in dos, as Arcas, Arcados. Ending in es it is long in Greek nouns, as Anchises; in Latin nouns, if of the fifth declension, it is lengthened, as dies; if of the third, it is long when the genitive singular does not increase by a syllable, as labes, labis, or when increasing it retains a long e before the final syllable, as quies, quietis; for if it changes it into i, as miles, militis, or shortens it, as seges, segetis, it is shortened, save for Ceres, pes, and the like. Ending in is it is short, as agilis [save for the three monosyllables glis, vis, lis]. Ending in os it is short in Latin nouns when the genitive does not have a naturally long syllable before the final one, as os, ossis; in Greek nouns it is shortened when the genitive ends in a diphthong, as Delos, τοῦ Δήλου. Ending in us it is short, as doctus, but becomes long if the genitive increases by a syllable and has a long u before the final syllable, as virtus, and in the single indeclinable word pus. Ending in t it is shortened, as caput. Ending in c it forms only two nouns: allec, which is lengthened, and lac, concerning whose declension there is doubt.

{{280}} The genitive singular is lengthened when it is either like the nominative, as senatus, or has a diphthong, as Iuliae, or ends in i, as docti; in all others it is shortened. The dative singular in Latin is lengthened, as Pompeio; in Greek it is shortened if it ends in the letter i, as Palladi. The accusative singular in Greek is shortened only when it ends in a or on, as Thesea, Delon; in Latin, however, it is short, as doctum. The vocative singular ending in the letter a is shortened in all Latin nouns and in Greek feminines, as tabula, Musa; in masculines it is long, as Aenea. Ending in e it is short in Latin, as docte; in Greek it is lengthened, as Tydide, save for those whose Greek nominative ends in os, as Phoebos, Phoebe. Ending in i it is long in Latin, as Mercuri; in Greek it is shortened, as Nai. Ending in o it is short in Latin, as Cato, although Virgil judges otherwise; in Greek it is lengthened, as Dido. Ending in u it is shortened, as cornu; ending in consonants it follows the rule of the nominative, save for Greek nouns, which often change through the variety of dialects, as Diomedes. The ablative singular is always lengthened except when it ends in e in those nouns only which are of the third declension, as a pariete.

{{281}} The nominative and vocative plural in the masculine and feminine gender are lengthened, as fluctus, terrae; in neuters they are short, as fata; in Greek nouns also, when they end in s or a, as rhetores, poemata, whereas in others they are long, as Musae. The genitive plural is short, as doctorum; in Greek nouns it is long if of Greek declension, as Philaenon. The dative and ablative plural, if they end in is, are lengthened, as doctis; if in bus, they are shortened, as hominibus; yet if the declension is Greek, the dative ending in in is shortened, as Arcasin; otherwise it is long. The accusative plural in masculine or feminine nouns is lengthened, as doctos, Iulias; in neuters it is shortened, as moenia. In Greek nouns, however, if it ends in as and comes from a genitive ending in os, it is shortened, as Arcadas; otherwise it is lengthened, as Musas.

{{282}} In all pronouns the nominative singular is found short, except for the two monosyllables tu and qui. The genitive singular pronounced with i or ae is long, as mei, meae; otherwise it is short, as illius. The dative singular is always lengthened, as nostro, save for mihi, tibi, sibi, which are taken indifferently. The accusative singular is shortened, as illum, save for me, te, se. The vocative singular does not differ from its nominative. The ablative singular is long, as ab illo. The nominative and accusative plural are shortened only when they end in a, as nostra. The genitive plural is never lengthened, as illorum. The dative and ablative plural ending in is are lengthened, while those ending in bus are shortened.

{{283}} In all verbs, moods, tenses, numbers, persons, and conjugations, this uniform rule holds: whatsoever person ends in a is lengthened, as canta. Ending in e it is short, as lege, unless it comes from the second conjugation, as doce; for then it is long. A person ending in i is always lengthened, as nutri. That which ends in o is short, as audio, although authority differs even in these; for Virgil makes the first person of the first conjugation long, as canto quae solitus and terra, tibi mando. Yet monosyllables must also be lengthened, as do, sto, flo. A person ending in u is lengthened. That which ends in am is short, as legebam. Ending in or it is shortened, as legor. Verbs ending in the letter s are lengthened if the penultimate letter is a or e, as amas, doces, save for the monosyllable es and those compounded from it, as ades; if, however, it has i before s, it is shortened, as legis, legitis, save for the second person singular of the present indicative of the fourth (lengthened third) conjugation, as nutris, audis, and vis from the verb volo; if, however, it has u before s, it will be short, as nutrimus. Those ending in the letter t are short, as legit. Those ending in c are lengthened, as produc. Participles, although they are inflected through cases, nevertheless differ in every respect from nouns.

{{284}} Monosyllabic adverbs, as huc, or those compounded from them, as illuc, are all lengthened, save for bis and ter; those ending in a are long, as una; those ending in e are lengthened, as docte, pulchre, save for those which either are not compared, as rite, or are defective in comparison, as bene, impune; those ending in i are long, as heri, except ibi and ubi and those compounded from them, as sicubi, and likewise quasi; those ending in o derived from themselves are short, whereas those derived from other words are lengthened, as falso, although authority varies; those ending in u are long, as noctu. Those ending in l ought to be shortened, as semel; those ending in m, n, r are short, as cursim, forsan, fortiter; those ending in s are lengthened only when they have a before it, as alias; those ending in c are lengthened, as illuc.

{{285}} The nominative singular of all future participles is short, as lecturus; the genitive and dative singular are lengthened, as lecturi, lecturo, save for the present participle, which shortens the genitive, as amantis. It is agreed that the accusative and vocative are short, as lecturum, lecture; the ablative singular is shortened only, as amante, legente. The nominative and accusative plural in masculine or feminine participles are lengthened, as lecturi, lecturos, lecturae, lecturas; in neuters they are shortened, as lectura. The genitive plural is short, as lecturorum. The dative and ablative plural ending in is are lengthened, while those ending in bus are shortened.

{{286}} Copulative, disjunctive, and expletive conjunctions are short, unless position makes them long. Of causal and rational conjunctions, those ending in a, save for ita and quia, are lengthened, as propterea, interea; those ending in i, save for the single word nisi, are lengthened, as si; those ending in n, if they have i before it, are lengthened, as sin; otherwise they are short. It is agreed that all the rest are short.

{{287}} Prepositions which end in a alone rejoice in lengthening, as contra, extra, and the single monosyllable a. All prepositions governing the ablative are shortened except monosyllables which either consist of vowels or end in vowels, as e and de. There is no doubt that prepositions governing both cases are short.

{{288}} Interjections are long if they are monosyllabic, as heus; if, however, they are disyllabic or trisyllabic, since they retain the form of parts of speech, they must be judged by the example of those to which they are similar, as papae. These rules, as I have said, must be observed in final syllables across all parts of speech, except for syllables long by position and diphthongs.

{{289}} «Swiftly completed is the page of syllables; now words must at last be yoked. For this is the approved order for our matter, which shall be divided into two parts. For first must be told proportion, which the Greeks call analogy, and presently those forms which, rejecting rule, are coined anew and which the learned commonly call anomalies, whereby the twice-fourth portion of speech is prepared or the unbroken course of the tongue is wounded—so much as, set within a single little book, can satisfy serious fastidiousness».
{{290}} Analogy, therefore—which in Latin is called proportio—is the observation of words similar to one another. For first, all Latin nouns terminate in twelve letters: five vowels, six semivowels, and one mute, t, as caput. A, then, the first of the vowels, terminates masculine nouns, as Catilina, Iugurtha; feminine nouns, as Musa; common nouns, as advena; Greek feminine nouns also, as Helena, Andromacha; and pronouns as well, as altera, sola, illa. If from these thou takest away a and addest ius, thou wilt make the genitive singular, as we say alterius, solius, illius; if, however, thou addest i, the dative, as alteri, soli, illi. Those, on the other hand, which are Greek neuters, as poema, toreuma, have three cases alike in each number; in the genitive singular they assume tis, in the dative they drop s and end the ablative in short e, and in the plural dative and ablative they preserve the form of the genitive singular, although they can also take bus.

{{291}} Nouns ending in short e which are Latin are neuter, as monile, sedile. Besides the cases which they have alike, these merge the dative with the ablative, as huic and ab hoc monili, sedili; since the ablative, if it ended in e, would make four cases alike, which the declension of neuter nouns does not admit. For those which end in long e in the nominative are Greek of the feminine gender, as Agave, Autonoe, and must be declined after the manner of the Greeks, so that the nominative, dative, vocative, and ablative are alike, the genitive ends in s, and the accusative in n. Those nouns, however, which convert e into a, as Andromache, Andromacha, are declined like Latin nouns ending in a. Latin nouns ending in the letter i are of all genders and indeclinable (monoptota), as frugi, nihili. Gummi and sinapi, however, are foreign nouns of the neuter gender and of the singular number only, yet their cases run through in the same way except for the genitive, which takes s, as sinapis, gummis, although haec gummis is also correctly said.

{{292}} The letter o terminates no noun in the neuter; for they are either masculine, as Cicero, or feminine, as Iuno, hirundo, or common to both genders, as homo. Their declensions issue into two types; for either they retain the letter o in the oblique cases, as unio, unionis, or they convert it into i, as cupido, crepido; cupidinis, crepidinis. Both types, moreover, increase by a syllable in the oblique cases except in the vocative singular. Usage, however, hath prevailed that caro be declined outside this analogy, and likewise Anio, which are unique in their declension and are declined without examples of similar words; for they make huius carnis, Anienis. Duo and ambo, since they are always plural, must not be numbered among those I have mentioned above, for they are declined according to their own usage. Of Greek nouns ending in the letter o, those which have passed from their nominative form into our pattern, as leo, draco, are declined like Cicero, Milo; whereas those which preserve their original nominative intact, as Io, Ino, are declined in the Greek manner, so that they make in the genitive Ius, Inus, in the accusative Iun, Inun, and similarly in the remaining cases, although usage says hanc Io. Turbo, if it is a proper name, is declined like Cicero; if, however, thou signifiest the force of the wind or a boy’s spinning-top, it is declined like cupido.

{{293}} In the letter u only neuter nouns terminate, as cornu, veru, which in the plural makes verua. Among the ancients specua was also said. The plural declensions of these nouns lack all doubt; for three cases, as in all neuters, are alike, likewise the dative and ablative end in the syllable bus, and the genitive joins the syllable um to the ablative singular. Inquiry is made, however, concerning the genitive singular, which some have formed in us and others in the letter i, and likewise concerning the dative, which some form in i, as genui, cornui; those who follow the ancients make it like the ablative—huic genu, cornu—since in the plural we say genubus and cornubus, and when the last syllable is dropped, the dative singular remains, just as happens in civibus and suavibus. And as for our now saying genibus and cornibus, the letter i hath passed into the place of the letter u, just as we likewise say optimum and maximum, whereas optumus and maxumus used to be said. There are some who say genuis and cornuis in the genitive case; yet the genitive ought not to have more syllables than the dative and ablative. Although, therefore, senatus and exercitus are dissimilar, nevertheless genus and cornus must be said in the genitive in the same manner as senatus and exercitus. Furthermore, whatsoever nouns or participles end in is in the genitive singular increase by a syllable in the dative plural, as Catonis, Catonibus; according to which principle, if the genitive were genuis, cornuis, it would have made genuibus so as to increase by a syllable.

{{294}} Nouns ending in the letter l have a twofold form. The first is that in which masculines stand, as Hannibal, Hasdrubal—and there are scarcely any other proper names save Punic ones, except for the name of Sol; likewise feminines, as the Etruscan name Tanaquil, and common nouns, as vigil, pugil; all of which have the same declensions. The second type is of neuters, as mel, fel; which differ from the preceding declension in that they are monosyllabic and double the letter l throughout the oblique cases. For those who end proconsule in the letter e in the nominative, looking to the nature of the preposition pro (since pro is placed only before the ablative), do not consider that when proconsul is said, it is spoken not as two words, but as a compound noun, like procurator, propugnator; nor is it any hindrance to the comparison that these latter nouns can form verbs from themselves, as procuro, propugno, for then the preposition vanishes. Proconsul, however, does not make proconsulo, although consul makes consulo. Nevertheless, if proconsule were said in the nominative case, it would be indeclinable (monoptoton); yet on account of usage let proconsul be said, so that it may be declined like those nouns which end in the letter l, as vigil, mugil, pugil, since in the genitive plural we say mugilum and pugilum; for if the nominative were mugilis like agilis, it would make mugilium like agilium.

{{295}} In the letter m neuter nouns also terminate, as telum, scamnum. Yet let us observe this: that those which have i before the final syllable in the nominative ought to double that same letter in the genitive singular, and likewise in the dative and ablative plural, as lilium, folium, since the genitive has either as many syllables as the nominative, as scamnum, scamni, or more, as caput, capitis, and never fewer. Furthermore, the dative ending in the letter o converts it into i in the genitive, as scamno, scamni; yet the usage and authority of the ancients preferred to say ingeni, consili, and imperi in three syllables. Whether vasum or vas ought to be said is a matter of inquiry, since whatsoever neuters do not end in the letter m in the nominative, if they admit a plural, fall into bus in the dative and ablative. Thus monile, monilibus; genu, genibus; pecus, pecoribus; nomen, nominibus; marmor, marmoribus; according to which vas ought to have made vasibus and in the genitive horum vasum, since whatsoever singular neuters end in the letter e in the ablative have a genitive plural of as many syllables as the ablative singular, as a nemore, nemorum; capite, capitum; yet this noun is declined by anomaly, and will be in the nominative singular vas, genitive vasis, dative vasi, ablative a vasi; in the plural number, however, it will be declined like scamna. Lucretius, nevertheless, uses vasi in the genitive, as rarique facit lateramina vasi.

{{296}} In the letter n terminate masculine nouns, as flamen, pecten; nouns common to two genders, as tibicen, fidicen—which the most frequent authors do not approve, for they say both tibicinam and fidicinam; likewise neuters, as omen, flumen, all of which are declined in one manner with an obvious declension. Gluten also is like these, although Sallust said glutinum. Likewise femina seems as it were similar. A second type is of the masculine gender, which preserves the letter e before the final n, as lien, rien, which itself is nevertheless also declined in the remaining cases like the preceding. For reoreo is the nominative singular, nor is any other case except the ablative in use. Greek nouns which end in n have the preceding letters a, e, i, o, as Alcman, Cephen, delphin, Phaethon, of which those ending in an, en, in are declined after the example of the preceding, as fulmen, numen, fidicen; whereas those ending in on, if they are declined like Greek nouns with a long o, as Cleon, lengthen that same letter among us as well, as Cicero, Scipio. Those, however, which from a lengthening in the nominative case are shortened in the remaining forms, as Amphion, Creon, Agamemnon, lose the letter n in the nominative among us, and will be compared to those Latin nouns which are pronounced short when the cases are inflected, as virgo, turbo. Those which among the Greeks take the letter t will also have the same among us, as Phaethon, Phaethontos.

{{297}} Of nouns ending in the letter r preceded by a, such as Caesar, lar, far, par, impar, there is one form, save that the neuter doubles the letter r in the remaining cases, as far, farris. The license of poets, however, hath spoken these nouns in the plural, whereas all nouns which we reckon by weight or measure do not admit the plural number, as aurum, plumbum, triticum, oleum. Those err, therefore, who say parium, since these are triptotes in the plural, as hi Caesares, hos Caesares, o Caesares; nor can the accusative have i before s if the genitive has r before u. Yet those who say parium seem to look to the nominative plural of the neuter gender and, since paria is said like suavia, to make parium like suavium.

{{298}} Of nouns ending in the letter r preceded by e there are six types: the first, which admits no increase in the genitive case and ends in the letter i, as aper, niger, macer; apri, nigri, macri. The second, as tener, lacer, puer; teneri, laceri, pueri. The third, as imber, uter, which differs from the preceding in that this type receives the letter s in the genitive, ends in i in the dative, does not increase like the second type, and ends in the letters ium in the genitive plural. The fourth, as mater, pater, frater, which makes the dative plural not like the first type (apris), but fratribus, nor does it increase by a syllable like the second, and in the genitive plural it has one syllable fewer; for we say patrum, fratrum, not like the third type utrium, imbrium. Furthermore, it has three cases alike: hi patres, hos patres, o patres, whereas imber and uter make hos imbris and utris.
