The fifth type is like passer, anser, later, mulier, which differ from the second type in that those end in the letter i with an increase of a syllable, as gener, generi, whereas these end in s, as passer, passeris, and differ in the remaining cases. For the neuters piper and cicer are reckoned in the fifth type; for they are declined like passer in the singular number, beyond which they never pass. The sixth type is like neuter, uter; for they make neutrius and utrius, and are declined like the other pronouns which form genitives in the letters ius and datives in i; furthermore, they have those genitives and datives in common with the remaining genders, that is, the feminine and neuter. With i preceding the letter r there is only one type, vir. Of nouns ending in the letter r preceded by o there are two types: some are lengthened throughout the oblique cases, as sopor, soporis; color, coloris; others are shortened, as arbor, arboris, yet they are declined in one manner. The ancients lengthened the genitives Castoris and Hectoris, yet we shorten them, since the Greeks shorten the genitives of these names. Of nouns ending in the letter r preceded by u there are two types: the first, like satur, which ends in i in the genitive, as saturi; the second, whose genitive issues in the letters is, as sulphur, sulphuris; augur, auguris. Of neuters, moreover, which end in the letters ur, some retain the letter u, as sulphur, sulphuris, while others change it, as ebur, eboris.

{{299}} Of nouns ending in the letter s there are eight forms; for either the letter a is placed before it, as Maecenas, civitas; or e, as verres, moles; or i, as panis; or o, as custos, nepos; elsewhere u, as vetus, Ligus; elsewhere r, as iners; elsewhere n, as serpens; elsewhere p, as praeceps. With a preceding the letter s there are two types: the first, like Maecenas, Laenas; the second, like nostras, Privernas, which differ from the former in that they are common to all genders and ought to assume the letter i in the genitive plural, which is familiar to neuters in the plural nominative, accusative, and vocative. Therefore we say nostratium, Privernatium, because haec nostratia, Privernatia exist; yet we also say praegnatium and optimatium, because the accusative plural has the letter i, as hos and has optimatis and has praegnatis, although the ancients said praegnatum and optimatum; praegnas, moreover, is of the feminine and neuter gender. As and mas, since they are monosyllables, are not bound by analogy, but by a certain declension of their own make assis and maris, and in the plural assium and marium. Fas and nefas are indeclinable (aptota).

{{300}} Greek nouns which among us end in as have three types. The first is like Olympias, Pythias, for it makes Olympiadis and Pythiadis; the second, like Pallas, Thoas, Atlas, for it makes Pallantis, Thoantis, Atlantis; the third, like Aeneas, Pythagoras, Lichas, for it makes Aeneae, Pythagorae, Lichae, just as by our rule nouns which end in ae in the genitive end in a in the nominative, as Catilinae, Catilina. Yet these are Greek; therefore they retain the letter s in the nominative. Some, however, lose the letter s in the nominative, as Nicia, Mela; therefore in these nominatives usage must be observed.

{{301}} Of nouns ending in the letter s preceded by e there are five types: the first, whose plural nominative, accusative, and vocative are alike, while the genitive plural ends in um, as Hercules, proles; Herculum, prolum. The second type is that which differs from the preceding in that it forces the genitive plural into ium and the accusative into is, as nubes, rupes, cautes, in which form masculines are not found. The third consists of those which, even if they have some difference among themselves in declension, nevertheless, since they are equal in the increase of syllables in the oblique cases, are reduced to a single type, as Ceres, bipes, merces; examples of the masculine, however, do not exist save Greek ones, as Chremes, Laches. The fourth type differs from the preceding in that throughout the oblique cases it forces e into i, whereas the former preserves the letter e, evidently because in the nominative they do not lengthen e like the former, but shorten it, as hospes, antistes, ales, comes. The fifth type differs from the preceding in that, increased by a syllable in the genitive, it ends in the letter i—which will also be accounted the dative—ends in em in the accusative, and in long e in the ablative, to which the syllable rum being added forms the genitive plural. Of this plural number it merges the nominative with the nominative and vocative singular, and ends the dative and ablative in the syllable bus, as facies, dies, spes, acies. Yet usage hath shortened rei and spei, perhaps because they are monosyllables.

{{302}} Of nouns ending in the letter s preceded by i, some increase throughout the oblique cases, while others are contained within the measure of the nominative, at least in the singular number. Of those, therefore, which do not increase, there are two types: the first of the masculine gender, as scrobis, mensis—although Lucan said exiguam scrobem—of the feminine, and of the common gender, as canis, iuvenis, whose declension is clear. The second type differs from the preceding in that it ends the ablative in the letter i and the accusative in im, as the two masculines alone Ligeris, Tiberis, and the feminines clavis, pelvis, turris, sitis, tussis. This principle of declining is made manifest by the diminutive, which is pronounced with a long i in all of these. Therefore, as often as we are in doubt concerning the pronunciation of a noun, we shall consult its diminutive. Of those which increase by a syllable, there are two types, one of which shortens the letter i, in which form are the masculines sanguis, pulvis, lapis, and the feminines cuspis, cassis, wherein it matters naught that some preserve i in declension and others pass into e, or likewise that some end in the syllable dis and others in ris in the genitive. The second type lengthens the letter i in the nominative, as glis, lis, Samnis, which they likewise preserve in the oblique cases, and in the genitive plural we say i before the syllable um—glirium, litium, Samnitium—not like the former lapidum, cuspidum, cassidum.

{{303}} Of nouns ending in the letter s preceded by o, since the distinction is slight, there can be a single type. For while all increase by a syllable in the oblique cases, they differ in this, that some in the genitive convert the letter s into t, some into d, and some into r, as nepos, nepotis; custos, custodis; masculine monosyllables, as flos, floris; ros, roris; likewise the neuter os, oris. For ossibus, however, usage forms the nominative os contrary to rule, since it does not have r in the genitive; yet neither can ossum be said, since neuters which end in m in the nominative do not admit the syllable bus in the plural number. Therefore in a monosyllable analogy is not maintained.

{{304}} Of nouns ending in the letter s preceded by u there are six types. In the first, the genitive ends in a single i and the vocative in e, as Marcus, Sextus; genitive Marci, Sexti; vocative Marce, Sexte. The second type differs from the preceding in that it doubles i in the genitive and ends in i in the vocative, as Antonius, Iulius makes huius Antonii, Iulii and o Antoni, Iuli; in the remaining cases they agree with the preceding. The third type merges the nominative with the vocative, preserves those same letters lengthened in the genitive, and ends in u and i in the dative, u in the ablative, and um in the accusative, as senatus, fluctus, exercitus; which in the plural form the nominative together with the accusative and vocative in long us, the genitive in uum, and the dative and ablative in the syllable bus. The fourth type receives an increase of a syllable throughout the oblique cases, in such wise, however, that they have some variety among themselves. For some retain the letter u, now short and now long, as Ligus, Liguris; palus, paludis; virtus, virtutis. Some change that same letter u into e, as vetus, veteris; Venus, Veneris; some into o, as nemus, nemoris, which nevertheless are declined in one manner. Laus and fraus, since they are monosyllables and have two vowels joined together, are reckoned in the same type, because they similarly increase by a syllable. The fifth type is of pronouns ending in us, as unus, solus, totus, which are declined in this manner: unus, unius, uni, unum, unus, ab uno; uni, unorum, unis, unos, uni, ab unis. The sixth ends in eus, as hinnuleus, eculeus, and is pronounced with the letter e doubled in the vocative, as eculee, hinnulee; yet some prefer to say eu in the vocative after the Greek manner, as Tydeu.

{{305}} Neuters ending in the letter s preceded by i are indeclinable (monoptota), as tressis, sexis. With u preceding the letter s there are two types: the first, which forms its genitive in i and has no plural, as vulgus, pelagus (virus Lucretius calls viri, although it more rightly remains uninflected); in the second type are those which increase throughout the oblique cases and end in the letters ris in the genitive singular, as genus, nemus, of which some change u into e, as olus, oleris; ulcus, ulceris, while some change it into o, as nemus, nemoris; pecus, pecoris. Fenus and stercus come into doubt as to whether they change into e or into o, since those which end in the syllable nus change u into e, as vulnus, genus, funus, and we say feneratus. Penus, indeed, ought not to prejudice the example, since it is placed among nouns of doubtful gender. Likewise the ancients used to say stercoratos agros, not sterceratos.

{{306}} Nouns ending in the letter s preceded by n or r are all of one gender, save those which have r before s. Sometimes they take d, as socors, socordis, and sometimes t, as sollers, iners. In the plural also, except for the genitive and accusative, they are declined similarly in all cases. For some end in um in the genitive and es in the accusative, as Mars, Arruns, while some end in ium, as sapiens, patiens, and on this account their accusatives end in is. Most of these nouns, moreover, are common to three genders, and the letter i which the neuters have in the nominative plural they give also to the genitives of the remaining genders with which they are common.

{{307}} In the letter t only a few neuter nouns terminate, as git, which is not declined, and caput, sinciput. Some, when they say lac, add t because it makes lactis, yet Virgil writes: lac mihi non aestate novum, non frigore defit, since among us no nouns end in two mutes, and therefore the ancients said lacte in the nominative.

{{308}} Of nouns ending in the letter x, some—among which are all nouns of common gender—end in ium in the genitive plural, and on this account in i and s in the accusative; the majority, however, end in u and m without a preceding i in the genitive, and on this account in e and s in the accusative. For in the remaining cases they agree, inasmuch as in the singular all have x in the nominative, form the genitive in i and s, end the dative in the letter i, the ablative in e, and with m added define and complete the accusative, while in the plural they end the dative and ablative in the syllable bus. For as to the rest wherein they differ, the ancients indeed said atrocum and ferocum, by which principle there will appear to be a single type of all nouns ending in the letter x. Before this letter x all vowels are placed, as capax, frutex, pernix, atrox, redux. Of these nouns some are lengthened in the nominative, some are shortened, and some agree in the nominative while differing in the oblique cases. For fax and rapax, likewise rex and pumex, and likewise nux and lux vary in their nominative quantity; whereas nix and nutrix, and likewise nox and atrox, agree in the nominative in such wise that they diverge throughout the oblique cases. And this must be observed: that some of these force the letter x into g, and some into c, throughout their declensions. For lex makes legis and grex makes gregis, whereas pix makes picis and nux makes nucis. For in those which are not monosyllables, the letter x is never not converted into c in the genitive, as frutex, fruticis; ferox, ferocis. Supellex, senex, and nix, however, are declined contrary to rule by a certain privilege, since supellex increases by two syllables, which rule forbids, and senex remains disyllabic in the genitive just as in the nominative, whereas all nouns ending in the letter x increase, and nix is converted neither into c like pix, nor into g like rex, but into v, although a consonant cannot pass into a vowel. In the genitive plural, moreover, the ablative singular determines the forms; for an ablative ending in a or o issues in rum; ending in short e, in um; in long e, in rum; ending in i, in ium; and ending in u, in uum. The dative and ablative plural likewise end either in is or in bus, precepts which are most familiar in the schools. Yet as often as they end in is, they terminate in a long syllable, and as often as in bus, in a short one.

{{309}} «Having run through the rules of nouns, it is just that we subjoin in consequence the canons of verbs. The genera (voices) of verbs are five: active, passive, neuter, common, and deponent. The active is that which ends in o and has the signification of acting, as lego, scribo; the passive ends in r and shows the signification of suffering action, as scribor, caedor; the neuter ends in o and has the full signification neither of acting nor of suffering, as sudo, dormio, for thou knowest not whether one acts or suffers. The common and deponent end in r, yet there is this difference: that in the common there are two significations, both of acting and of suffering; for when we say osculor, thou knowest not whether it is “I kiss thee” or “I am kissed by thee.” In the deponent, however, the effect is either of acting, as luctor, or of suffering, as morior. There is also the impersonal, as sudatur, curritur, which is so called because, while it contains all persons, it has no fixed one.

{{310}} The moods of verbs, moreover, are five; yet some have said there are six, some seven, some eight, some nine, and a few ten. Those who say five name these: indicative, imperative, optative, conjunctive, and infinitive, which we also call perpetual. Those who record six add the promissive; those who say seven, the impersonal; those who say eight, the interrogative (percunctativum); those who say nine, the subjunctive, and separate it from the conjunctive; those who say ten ascribe also the hortative; yet reason does not admit these superfluously added moods.

{{311}} That there are three conjugations—which the Greeks call syzygiai (συζυγίαι)—is beyond doubt, which are shown in active and neuter verbs in the second person of the present tense. For as often as the final syllable ends in as, it is the first conjugation; in es, the second; in is, the third, as cantas, vides, audis—if this third be lengthened, whereas if it be shortened, curris. The verbs of all these conjugations admit in the first person singular before the final letter only three vowels—e, i, u—as sedeo, lanio, irruo, and all consonants except f, k, and q, as libo, vaco, cado, lego, traho, impello, amo, cano, scalpo, curro, lasso, peto, nexo. To these are added i and u placed in the position of consonants, as aio, adiuvo. Yet they do not even exclude f, since we say triumfo, although it comes from the Greeks and is more properly written with p and h; furthermore, from the fact that we say faris and fatur, they hold the first person of the verb to be for. And concerning the letter q there is doubt, for we say eliquo and aequo, and in verbs of this kind the letter u is able to hold neither the place of a vowel, as is irruo, nor of a consonant, as is adiuvo.

{{312}} Of all these conjugations there are thirty-six forms in inflecting, excluding defective, impersonal, and inchoative verbs. Verbs of the first conjugation, which end either in the letter o with no other vowel preceding or with any vowel going before, have four forms. Verbs of the second conjugation have seven forms. Verbs of the shortened third conjugation have twenty forms: thus whatsoever verbs in the first person singular of the present indicative end in the letters io have six forms; those ending in uo have two forms; those ending in the letter o with no preceding vowel have twelve forms. Verbs of the lengthened third (fourth) conjugation which in the first person singular of the present indicative end in the letters io have five forms. Whatsoever verbs of whatsoever conjugation end in the first person singular of the present indicative in the letter o, either with no preceding vowel or with any other vowel preceding, their inflection is contained within this number of forms, of which I shall speak severally.

{{313}} Verbs of the first conjugation in the first person singular of the present indicative end either in the letter o with no other vowel preceding, as amo, canto; or in eo, as commeo, calceo; or in io, as lanio, satio; or in uo, as aestuo, continuo. Verbs of the first conjugation in the second person of the present imperative end in the lengthened letter a, as amo, ama; canto, canta; in the infinitive mood, by adding the syllable re to the imperative mood with the lengthening preserved, they end as ama, amare; canta, cantare. Likewise those verbs of the first conjugation which end in the first person singular of the present indicative in the letter o preceded by a vowel, in the absolute (perfect) species of the past tense of the indicative mood, by adding the syllable vi to the imperative with the lengthening preserved, end as commeo, commea, commeavi; lanio, lania, laniavi; aestuo, aestua, aestuavi. In the same mood and same tense, in the inchoative (imperfect) species, by adding the syllable bam to the imperative mood, they end as commea, commeabam; lania, laniabam; aestua, aestuabam. The first conjugation in the same mood and same tense, in the recordative (pluperfect) species, terminates its parts by adding the syllables veram to the imperative mood, as commea, commeaveram; lania, laniaveram; aestua, aestuaveram. In the first conjugation, in the same mood in the future tense, by adding the syllable bo to the imperative mood, they end as commea, commeabo; lania, laniabo; aestua, aestuabo.

{{314}} Those verbs, however, which in the first person of the present indicative end in the letter o with no other vowel preceding are pronounced in the absolute (perfect) and exact (pluperfect) species of the past tense of the indicative mood in four ways. The first is that which has a rule similar to those which have a penultimate vowel in the first person singular of the present indicative, as amo, ama, amavi, amabam, amaveram, amabo, amare. The second is that which converts o into i and lengthens the penult in the preterite perfect and the antepenult in the pluperfect, as adiuvo, adiuvi, adiuveram. The third is that which has a rule similar indeed to the first mode, yet separates itself by dropping the letter a: seco, seca, secabam, secabo, secare; for in the absolute species it makes secui and in the exact secueram. The fourth is that which is pronounced by reduplication of a syllable, as sto, sta, steti, stabam, steteram, stabo, stare; similar to this is do, da, dedi, dabam, dederam, dabo, dare, pronounced with a short letter a contrary to rule in dabam, dabo, dare.

{{315}} Verbs of the second conjugation end in the letters eo in the first person singular of the present indicative, as video, vides; moneo, mones. Verbs of the second conjugation end in the lengthened letter e in the second person of the present imperative, as video, vide; moneo, mone. Verbs of the second conjugation in the infinitive mood, by adding the syllable re to the imperative mood with the lengthening preserved, end as vide, videre; mone, monere. Verbs of the second conjugation in the absolute and exact species of the past tense of the indicative mood are inflected in seven ways; and the first is that which preserves the form of the rule. For this is the form when a verb of the second conjugation in the preterite perfect tense of the indicative mood, by adding the syllable vi to the imperative mood with the lengthening preserved, ends as deleo, dele, delevi, and in the pluperfect, by adding the syllables veram to the imperative mood, ends as dele, deleveram. The second is when the letters e and o of the first person of the indicative mood pass into i, as sedeo, sedi, and in the exact species sederam. The third is when e and o are changed into u and i, as caleo, calui; moneo, monui; calueram, monueram. The fourth is when they change e and o into i and the preceding consonant into s, as mulgeo, mulsi, mulseram. The fifth is when e and o are changed into i and the preceding consonant into x, as luceo and lugeo make luxi, luxeram. The sixth is that which is pronounced by reduplication of a syllable, as spondeo, spopondi, spoponderam. The seventh is that which is resolved into the form of passives, as audeo, ausus sum, es, est; ausus eram, eras, erat. Verbs of the second conjugation in the inchoative species of the indicative mood, by adding the syllable bam to the imperative with the lengthening preserved, end as vide, videbam; mone, monebam. Verbs of the second conjugation in the future tense of the indicative mood, by adding the syllable bo to the imperative mood with the lengthening preserved, end as vide, videbo; mone, monebo.

{{316}} Verbs of the shortened third conjugation in the first person singular of the present indicative end either in the letter o with no other vowel preceding, as lego, legis; peto, petis; or in io, as rapio, rapis; facio, facis; or in uo, as induo, irruo. Those verbs of the shortened third conjugation which in the first person singular of the present indicative end in the letter o with no other vowel preceding end in the second person of the present imperative in short e preceded by the consonant of the first person, as lego, lege; peto, pete; those ending in u and o will retain u and e in the imperative mood, as induo, indue; irruo, irrue. Verbs of the shortened third conjugation in the infinitive mood, by adding the syllable re to the imperative mood with the shortening preserved, end as lege, legere; pete, petere.

{{317}} Those verbs of the shortened third conjugation which end in the letters io in the first person singular of the present indicative express the preterite perfect and pluperfect in six ways. The first is that which follows the complete rule of the lengthened third conjugation; this is that which turns the rule of the imperative mood into lengthening and assumes the syllable vi in the absolute species and veram in the exact, as cupio, cupivi, cupiveram. Yet these same verbs are sometimes pronounced in the absolute species with the letter v removed and i doubled, and in the exact species with that same i shortened, as cupii, cupieram. The second mode is that which drops the letter o of the first person of the verb and lengthens the preceding syllable, whether the vowel be changed or remain the same, as facio, feci; fugio, fugi; in the exact species, however, having dropped i and changed it into e, it assumes the syllable ram, as feceram, fugeram. The third mode is when i and o are converted into u and i, as elicio, elicui, elicueram. The fourth, having dropped the letters io along with the preceding consonant, is pronounced through double s, as percutio, percussi, percusseram; the fifth through x, as aspicio, aspexi, aspexeram; the sixth is pronounced through reduplication of a syllable, as pario, peperi, pepereram.

{{318}} Those verbs of the shortened third conjugation which end in the letters uo in the first person singular of the present indicative are expressed in the past tense, both absolute and exact, in two ways. The first is when they change the o of the first person of the verb into i, as induo, indui, indueram; the second is that which is pronounced through x, as instruo, instruxi, instruxeram.

{{319}} Those verbs of the shortened third conjugation which end in the letter o with no other vowel preceding in the first person singular of the present indicative are inflected in the absolute and exact species of the past tense of the indicative mood in twelve ways. The first is that which follows the complete form of the lengthened third conjugation, as we have shown above in those verbs of the shortened third conjugation which end in the letters io in the first person singular of the present indicative; for just as it is cupio, cupivi, cupiveram, so is it peto, petivi, petiveram. These also sometimes drop the letter v and double i, as petii, petieram. The second is when o is converted into i [as bibo, bibi; the third is] that which changes the letter o of the first person of the verb into i and lengthens the preceding syllable, whether the vowel be changed or remain the same, dropping also the medial consonant, if there was one, in which the first syllable ended, as ago, egi, egeram; lego, legi, legeram. Examples of those which drop the consonant n are these: frango, fregi, fregeram; fundo, fudi, fuderam. The fourth is that which drops the medial consonant and shortens the preceding vowel, as findo, fidi, fideram; scindo, scidi, scideram. The fifth, having dropped the letter o, assumes u and i, as molo, molui, molueram; colo, colui, colueram. The sixth is that which, having dropped o, assumes s and i, as carpo, carpsi, carpseram; scribo, scripsi, scripseram. The seventh is that which, having dropped o and the preceding consonant, is pronounced through double s, as meto, messui, messueram. The eighth is that which is inflected with a similar shortening through a single s preceded by a long vowel, as trudo, trusi, truseram. The ninth is that which is pronounced through x, as expungo, expunxi, expunxeram; ungo, unxi, unxeram. The tenth is that which is pronounced through reduplication of the first syllable, as pungo, pupugi, pupugeram; curro, cucurri, cucurreram. The eleventh is that which doubles the final syllable of compound verbs, as trado, tradidi, tradideram; reddo, reddidi, reddideram. The twelfth is that which is resolved into the form of passives, as fido, fisus sum, es, est; fisus eram, eras, erat.

{{320}} All these verbs of the shortened third conjugation in the inchoative species of the past tense of the indicative mood, by adding the syllable bam to the imperative mood preceded either by the consonant of the first person or by a vowel with lengthening added, end as lege, legebam; pete, petebam, save for those which had i before o; for these, by adding the letter i to the imperative and adding the syllable bam with e lengthened, end as rapio, rape, rapiebam. Verbs of the shortened third conjugation in the future tense of the indicative mood, with the letter e removed and the syllable am added, end as lege, legam; pete, petam; indue, induam, save for those which had i before o in the first person; for these convert e into i and, assuming the syllable am, form the future, as rapio, rape, rapiam; facio, face, faciam.

{{321}} Verbs of the lengthened third (fourth) conjugation end in the first person singular of the present indicative either in the letters eo, as adeo, adis; prodeo, prodis; or in io, as audio, audis; nutrio, nutris. Verbs of the lengthened third conjugation end in long i in the second person of the present imperative, as adeo, adi; prodeo, prodi; audio, audi; nutrio, nutri. These in the infinitive mood, by adding the syllable re to the imperative mood with the lengthening preserved, end as adi, adire; prodi, prodire. Those verbs of the third conjugation which end in the letters eo in the first person singular of the present indicative, in the absolute species of the past tense of the indicative mood, by adding the syllable vi to the imperative, end as adeo, adi, adivi, and in the exact species veram, as adiveram: yet usage, often seeking brevity, drops the letter v and doubles i in the absolute species, as adii, prodii; adieram, prodieram. In the inchoative species also, by adding the syllable bam to the imperative mood, they end as adibam, prodibam.

{{322}} Those verbs, however, which end in the letters io in the first person singular of the present indicative are expressed in the absolute and exact form of the past tense in five ways. The first is similar to the preceding, as nutrio, nutrivi, nutriveram; nutrii, nutrieram, and audii, audieram, as in Virgil audieras, et fama fuit. They differ only in the inchoative species; for all verbs of the lengthened third conjugation which end in the letters io in the first person singular of the present indicative add a long e before the final syllable bam in the inchoative species of the past tense of the same mood, as audiebam, veniebam, operiebam; although the ancients pronounced these without the letter e, as Virgil: nutribat Tyrrhusque pater, cui regia parent armenta. The second mode is when, o being dropped, the preceding syllable is lengthened, as venio, veni, veneram. The third is when i and o are changed into u and i, as operio, operui, operueram. The fourth is that which, having dropped i and o, converts the preceding consonant into s, as sarcio, sarsi, sarseram. The fifth is that which is expressed through x, as vincio, vinxi, vinxeram.

{{323}} Those verbs of the lengthened third conjugation which end in the letters eo in the present indicative end in the future tense of the same mood with the syllable bo added to the imperative mood, as adeo, adi, adibo; prodeo, prodi, prodibo; whereas those ending in io end with the syllable am added to the imperative mood, as audio, audi, audiam; nutrio, nutri, nutriam, although Terence pronounced them with bo. Thence it appears that the pronunciation is twofold; for he said: iam scibo, ubi siet.

{{324}} In the present tense of the indicative mood, among those verbs which end in the letters eo, there are verbs which have a similar preterite from a different present, as luceo and lugeo, for each makes luxi. Likewise cernit and crescit each make crevit. There are others which, with a single first-person form, hold two conjugations, as pando and mando; for they make pandas and pandis, mandas and mandis. Verbs are joined sometimes with single cases: thus with the genitive, as misereor tui; with the dative, as suadeo tibi; with the accusative, as moneo te; with the ablative, as utor illo; sometimes with two cases, as with the genitive and accusative, as memini tui and memini illud, or with the accusative and ablative, as fungor hanc rem and illa re. Let it suffice to have spoken thus much for examples of analogy. For whatsoever words shall be observed not to be bound by these forms must certainly be judged anomalies (anomala). These I shall recount briefly and concisely, so that we may uncover both certain forms which usage hath usurped contrary to reason and others which have fallen short contrary to rule.

{{325}} Since reus and deus are alike in the nominative singular, wherefore in the plural is hi rei said, while usage says hi di contrary to rule, when dei only ought to be said—especially since a genitive ought not to be found longer by two syllables than its nominative, which will happen here if we say deorum? Why do Thoas and Aeas [and Aeneas], although they are alike, make Thoantis and Aeantis, whereas Aeneas does not make Aeneantis, but Aeneae? Why, when we say hic biceps and triceps, does the genitive increase by two syllables contrary to rule, so that we say bicipitis or tricipitis, not bicipis or tricipis? Whence comes it that aliger, frugifer, accipiter have all cases, whereas Iuppiter has only two? Since sanctus, pius, bonus are alike, why do we say sanctior, yet do not say piior? Next, why do we say sanctior, sanctissimus, yet do not say bonior, bonissimus? Since Virgil says fandi atque nefandi, why, when from nefando we say nefarius, is farius not said from fando? Wherefore is seiunctus, when the preposition is dropped, called iunctus, whereas securus and sedulus cannot be spoken when that same preposition is lost? Since we say singuli viri, singulae mulieres, singula scrinia, wherefore do we not say singulus vir, singula mulier, singulum scrinium? Since venor, piscor, aucupor are alike, why are venator and piscator said, yet aucupator is not said, but auceps? Why does volo not have an imperative? Why does fare not have a first person of the verb? Wherefore does soleo not have a preterite perfect? Since canta and lava are alike, why does the one make cantavi and the other not make lavavi? Likewise, of corusca and tona, the one makes coruscavi, while the other does not make tonavi? What of the fact that ego admits only one case? Since calceatus, armatus, togatus, paenulatus appear equal, wherefore do we say calceo and armo, while togo and paenulo are not admitted? The nominative singular ending in us in all positives has a long e in adverbs, as doctus, docte; avarus, avare; parcus, parce: why do bonus and malus shorten e in the adverb—bene and male? Since we say habilis, habiliter, why do we not say facilis, faciliter? Likewise, since we say difficulter, why can faculter not be said? Since audax is spoken audacter, why do we not say verax, veracter, but veraciter? Why do we say singulatim, yet do not say binatim and ternatim? And other things of this sort, which I could recount without number, were I not hastening to the rest».

{{326}} When Grammar had spoken thus, as though about to enter upon a fresh beginning of her subject, Minerva, on account of the weariness of the supernal senate and of Jove, intervened with such words as these: «Unless I am deceived, thou art preparing to set forth the eight parts of speech as though repeating elementary rudiments, adding the causes of solecisms and barbaric forms, and then the other faults of speech which are most celebrated among illustrious bards—which are styled now tropes, now metaplasms, now schemes and figures, and all faults flowing from that same source as from a fountain of ornament, which bear witness either to the error of one ignorant or to a grace affected by the more learned. If thou bringest these things from schoolboy beginnings into the senate of the celestials, thou wilt strip the bloom from the grace of the skill thou hast already traversed. For if thou takest up anything rhythmic and metrical, as thou darest to do among curly-headed schoolboys, assuredly thou shalt be torn in pieces by the onset of Music, whose office thou forestallest. Thou wilt have fulfilled, therefore, the form of the aforesaid instruction if, having recounted the chief matters, thou now withdrawest thyself from the cheapness of vulgar rudiments». To these words of his sister the Delian nodded assent together with the bridegroom, and they compelled Grammar to pass over to the retinue of the maiden. And thereupon the Clarian introduced another of the women to be bestowed with an equal nuptial gift.

## Liber IV. De arte dialectica

{{327}} «She too, binding her utterances in twisted knots, without whom naught follows and naught likewise conflicts, coming into the assembly of the gods above, brings forward the first elements of speech and lays down the axiom of the schools, declaring that utterance consists of ambiguous words and deeming nothing regular unless it be joined in association. Yet though Aristotle himself, pale and worn with toil, at length proclaimed the twice-five modes and turns them over ceaselessly; though Stoic sophisms wind about and mock the senses, undone and never bearing horns upon their brow; though Chrysippus heap up the grain and exhaust the pile, and Carneades wield hellebore of equal might—no pinnacle ever befell so great a race of men to match thee, nor did so fortunate an honor ever fall to thy lot: amidst the temples of the gods it is lawful, O Dialectic, to speak, and in the sight of Jove thou playest the teacher’s part by right.»

{{328}} She therefore who entered at the Delian’s summons was a rather pale woman, yet of exceedingly keen sight and with eyes darting in ceaseless motion. Her hair seemed curly, crimped in becoming waves, and braided; yet drawn down through certain successive tiers, it so encircled the form of her whole head that thou couldst perceive nothing lacking and hold nothing superfluous. She wore indeed the pallium and attire of Athens, yet what she bore in her hands was unexpected and wholly untried in all the schools. For in her left hand a serpent was coiled in monstrous folds, while in her right certain formulas, deftly fashioned in wax of contrasting hues with blooming charm, were held within by the clasp of a hidden hook; yet since her left hand concealed its viperous snares beneath her pallium, her right was offered unto all. In short, if anyone took hold of one of those formulas, straightway caught upon the hook, he was dragged toward the venomous coils of the hidden serpent, which, speedily emerging, first afflicted the man with incessant bites from the poisoned point of its thorny teeth, and thereafter, having wound around him in manifold circuits, constrained him to the proposed conditions. If, however, no one wished to take up any formula, she either engaged those who met her with certain petty questions, or secretly goaded the serpent to creep upon them until its twining embrace strangled the ensnared victims at the questioner’s will.

{{329}} The woman herself, moreover, appeared of rather spare frame and dusky complexion, yet bristling with thorny hairs. She kept uttering things unintelligible to the common crowd: for she asserted that «a universal affirmative is oblique to a particular negative», yet that «both can be converted by connecting equivocals with univocals», and she spoke as though she alone discerned what is true or false, with a kind of confidence as of one divining.

{{330}} She said that she had been reared in the city of the Egyptians, and had passed thence into the school of Parmenides and into Attica, and there, with the captious purpose of her shifty craft, had brought even the greatness of Socrates and Plato under her sway.

{{331}} When, therefore, the twin serpents rising from the Cyllenian’s caduceus essayed to lick this woman—ever crafty in deceitful argumentation and boasting of the many whom she had ensnared—with frequent dartings of their tongues, and then too the Gorgon upon Minerva’s breast hissed with a certain joy of recognition, «Beyond doubt», said Bromius, who is the most facetious of the gods and knew her not at all, «this woman is either brought from the sands of panting Libya, as both her matted hair and her friendship with venomous creatures declare, or we must trust that she is a drug-monger of the Marsian nation—so warmly do the vipers recognize her and the snakes fawn upon her! Or if not, the trick of that hook proves her a most seductive mountebank and an inhabitant of the Marsian bounds.»

{{332}} At this saying, when several of the gods smiled as much as was seemly, Pallas, rather disturbed, checked the jest as it arose, reminding them that this maiden was exceedingly sober—a quality wholly denied to certain deities—and even keener among the sisters who were to be approved, able to be mocked by no one once she had brought forth her assertions. Yet she bade her hand over the things she had brought as proof of her truthful assertion and keenness, and prepare herself to present her art.

{{333}} Then, since Grammar—who, having finished her introduction, stood close by—dreaded to receive them, the coiled circles and gaping jaws of the slippery serpent, together with those alluring figures and hooked formulas, were entrusted to the goddess herself who had tamed even the locks of Medusa. Thus by the ornament of her hair she was proved a true daughter of Cecrops and Attica, and most of all because a throng of pallium-clad scholars and the flower of Grecian youth followed her, marveling at the prudence of the woman’s genius. Jupiter, however, deeming Grecian lightness inferior to Romulean vigor for inducing and fulfilling virtues, commanded her to set forth whatsoever she knew in the Latian tongue.

{{334}} And straightway Dialectic, although she was believed scarcely able to speak Latin worthily, yet with ready confidence and eyes narrowed with a certain flashing of her gaze, formidable even before her words, began thus:

{{335}} «Were not the erudition and diligence of my Varro, celebrated among the glories of Latium, at hand to aid me, I, a woman of the Dorian nation, might be found before an assembly of Romulean speech either altogether rude or quite barbarous. For indeed, after the golden stream of Plato and the skill of Aristotle, the industry of Marcus Terentius first allured me into the Latin tongue and procured for me the power of speaking throughout the Ausonian schools.

{{336}} Striving therefore to obey your commands, I shall neither desert the Grecian order of discourse nor hold back the phrasing of Laurentian (Latin) statement. And first I would have this ascertained: that the Romans and the toga-clad race have not yet been able to coin a new name for me, so that I am called Dialectice, just as I was wont to be called at Athens; and that whatsoever the other arts utter belongs altogether to my jurisdiction.

{{337}} For neither Grammar herself, whom your ears have approved, nor that other famed for the richness of her eloquent mouth, nor she who traces diverse figures with rod and dust, can be unfolded without my principles.

{{338}} Indeed, under my sway and authority stand six rules whereon the remaining disciplines rest. For the first is concerning speaking (de loquendo); the second, concerning utterance (de eloquendo); the third, concerning proposition (de proloquendo); the fourth, concerning the sum of propositions (de proloquiorum summa); the fifth, concerning judgment, which pertains to the judging of poets and poems; and the sixth, which furnishes rhetoricians with what they must say.

{{339}} In the first part, then, inquiry is made what genus is, what form, what difference, what accident, what indeed property, what definition, what whole, what part, what method holds in division, what in partition, what is equivocal, what univocal, what—so to speak—plurivocal; for ye must needs bear with the strangeness of my speech, who have compelled a Grecian woman to discourse in Latin.

{{340}} What words, therefore, belong properly to things and what are borrowed, and in how many ways they are borrowed; what substance is, what quality, what quantity, what relation, what place, what time, what posture, what state, what action, what passion; what things are opposed to one another and in how many ways they are opposed—these are examined in our first part.

{{341}} In the second, however, which I have called de eloquendo, inquiry is made what a noun is, what a verb, what is joined from these; which of them is the subject part of a sentence and which the predicate; what is the mode of the subject and what of the predicate; how far a noun is taken and how far a verb; and how far a complete sentence can be a proposition (proloquium).

{{342}} Next follows the third part, de proloquendo. Herein inquiry is made—so far as suffices for the purpose of today’s brevity—what the differences of propositions are in quantity and what in quality; what is universal, what particular, what indefinite; which are affirmative (aientia) and which negative (negantia); what force each possesses, and in what manner they stand affected toward one another.

{{343}} Thence we advance to the fourth part, which we have declared to be de proloquiorum summa. Herein inquiry is made what a premise (sumptum) is, what an inference (illatio), what a syllogism, what a conterminous conclusion (symperasma); what a predicative syllogism is and what a conditional, and what difference lies between them; how many figures (formae) there are of the predicative kind and what they are, whether they keep a fixed order, and if they do, what is the reason of that same order; how many moods (modi) each figure has, and whether these moods keep a fixed order, and if so, what is the reason of that same order; and further, how many primary and necessary moods belong to the conditional syllogism, what is the order of these also, and wherein they differ from one another. These are the matters which I judge sufficient for present understanding and for what must be asserted. Returning, therefore, to the beginning so that I may traverse the whole, I shall first make known what genus is.

{{344}} Genus is the comprehension of many forms under one name, as animal. Its forms are such as man, lion, horse, and the rest. Yet sometimes certain forms are so subjected to a genus that they themselves can also be a genus to others placed beneath them: as the genus of men, which is a form under animal, is a genus to barbarians and Romans, and can continue to be a genus until, dividing its forms, thou comest to some individual; as if thou dividest men into males and females, again males into boys, youths, and old men, again boys into infants and those who speak; if again thou wouldst divide boy into Catamitus or some other particular boy, that is no longer a genus, because it hath already reached the individual. We ought, however, to use that genus which is nearest to the matter in hand: as, if the question be concerning man, we ought to take animal as his genus, because it is nearest to him. For if we say substance, as far as logic goes it is true, but as far as necessity is concerned it is superfluous.

{{345}} By forms (formae) we mean the same as species. Forms, therefore, are those things which, subjected to a genus, retain its definition and name, as man, horse, lion. Since these are forms of animal, man, horse, and lion can each be called both «animal» and «a body partaking of soul»: thus it is recognized that both the name and the definition belong to the genus.

{{346}} Difference (differentia) is a distinction sufficient for the matter thou hast undertaken: as, if it be asked what difference there is between man and horse, it suffices to say that man is two-footed, horse four-footed. We must observe, however, that because there are many differences in individual things, we can divide each thing diversely as often as we are able to find different differences in it. For if we wish to divide animal, we can divide it into sexes, because some are male and others female; we can divide it into ages, because some are newborn, others youthful, others aged; we can divide it into sizes, because some are small, others large, others middling; we can divide it into variety of motion, because some walk, others creep, others swim; we can divide it into diversity of habitats, because some are aquatic, others terrestrial, others aerial, and others, as some say, fiery; we can divide it into vocal sound, because some speak, others groan, others bark, others howl. Let us know, nevertheless, both that each of these divisions is complete and that all are found in individual things. For male animals can at once be newborn, and small, and walking, and terrestrial, and two-footed, and speaking. Therefore thou mayest use whichever one thou wilt; yet thou oughtest to use that which is suited to the business undertaken. For if thou must speak in praise of men, it will behoove thee to divide animals into rational and irrational, so that thereby it may easily be understood how highly Nature esteemed men among all animals, since to them alone she granted the power of reasoning in order to know her.

{{347}} Accident (accidens) is that which belongs to none save the same form, yet does not always belong to it: as rhetoric belongs to none save man, yet it can also not belong to him, so that although someone is a man, he is nevertheless not an orator.

{{348}} Property (proprium) is that which belongs both to the same form alone and so always belongs to it that it distinguishes each thing from fellowship with all others, as laughter in man. For neither can anyone laugh save man, nor can man, when he wills—so far as lies in his nature—fail to laugh. And difference is distinguished from property in this: that difference separates each thing only from that concerning which the question is raised, whereas property separates it from all things. For when, wishing to distinguish man from lion by a difference, we say that a lion is wild, whereas a man is gentle, we seem to distinguish only that which pertains to the matter undertaken; for by saying «a lion is wild, a man is gentle», we have separated neither man from other gentle animals nor the lion from other wild beasts; whereas when we call man «a laughing animal», by that we have distinguished him from the generality of all other living beings.

{{349}} Definition (definitio) is when the folded knowledge of each thing is clearly and briefly unfolded. Herein three faults must be avoided: lest anything false, anything too broad, or anything too narrow be signified. A false definition: «Man is an immortal animal»; for no man is immortal, and therefore it is false. One that says too much: «Man is a mortal animal»; for by this, although briefly spoken, too much is included, because it pertains to all animals. One that says too little: «Man is a grammatical animal»; for although none save man is a grammatical animal, yet not every man is grammatical. A full definition: «Man is a rational mortal animal»; for by adding «mortal» we have separated him from the gods, and by adding «rational» we have separated him from the beasts.

{{350}} A whole (totum) is that which, having two or more parts set within itself, does not always lend its name, yet never lends its definition, and this is found only in individuals. As, if we designate a man and make his single limbs his parts, we understand that this very thing is a whole because we establish a specific man, and that neither the definition nor the name of that whole can fall upon the parts. For we shall not be able to call either an arm alone or a head «man», nor do the individual limbs receive his definition. Yet it must be noted that sometimes we can say all (omne) for whole (totum), though with a somewhat different sense: for whole is recognized even in single things, all in many. Thus when we say «the man Cicero», because he is one, totus is understood in him; whereas man, because he may be unlearned or an artisan, man or woman, we more fittingly take as omne.

{{351}} Parts (partes) are those things which are understood to be in the whole and of which the whole consists.

{{352}} We ought to divide until we arrive at the individual; and this is done when through differences we reduce genera to a small number and so subject forms to them that these forms too, taken singly, can themselves be genera to other forms placed beneath them: as if we wish first to divide animal briefly, we can do so by differences, in that some walk, others creep, others swim, others fly. Hence again—that is, from each of these forms—we can make genera, so as to say that walking animals are a genus and subject forms to it, in that some are human and others beasts. And from these there can be other forms, through which, if need be, one may arrive at the individual. Yet it is not fitting to do this in every assertion, but in subtle disputation. In a speech, however, we may divide in this manner only when the obscurity of the case demands it; whereas if the case be not obscure, the principle of division ought indeed to be present and handled, yet ought not to be overly conspicuous.

{{353}} Differences for partition do not frequently occur, and thus without them partition can be endless if we wish to proceed as far as the individual. For if we name a specific man as a whole and wish to gather his parts briefly, differences of parts are not available, and we shall be compelled to use the names of specific parts, so as to say head, feet, and the rest; which, if we wish to embrace them briefly, since differences are lacking, we cannot do save by gathering them one by one; and because they are many, it will be either impossible or tedious.

{{354}} Now the difference between division and partition is that in division we run through forms, in partition through parts. Forms are those things which are subjected to a genus and can retain its definition and name; parts are those things which are in a whole and can never receive the definition of the whole, though sometimes its name. We can, nevertheless, take one and the same thing both as a genus and as a whole, yet under a different aspect: such is man, who, if we wish to divide him into youth, old man, and boy, is a genus and these are his forms; whereas if we wish to partition him into head, feet, and hands, he is a whole and these are his parts. For youth, old man, and boy, which we called forms, receive both the name and the definition of man, so that an old man is called «man, a rational mortal animal», and likewise a boy and a youth; whereas head and feet, which we called parts, can receive neither the definition of man nor his name, because neither a head nor feet nor hands can be called «man, a laughing animal».

{{355}} A thing is equivocal (aequivocum) when many things share one name, yet not the same definition, as lion. For as far as the name is concerned, a real lion, a painted lion, and the celestial Lion are all called lion; as far as definition is concerned, the real lion is defined in one way, the painted in another, and the celestial in another.

{{356}} A thing is univocal (univocum) when two or more things share one name and one definition, as «garment» (vestis). For both a cloak (byrrhus) and a tunic have the name of garment and can receive its definition. Therefore this univocal term is understood in the series of a genus, which gives both its name and its definition to its forms.

{{357}} A thing is plurivocal (plurivocum) when one thing is called by many names, as gladius (sword); for both ensis and mucro signify the same thing.

{{358}} Things have their own words which we call «natural» and also «proper», as stone, wood, and the rest.

{{359}} Borrowed words (aliena) are those which we adopt for some reason, either for necessity or for ornament: for necessity, as when we say «the vines bud (gemmare)» or «joyful (laetas) crops»; for here, since a proper word is lacking, we have used a borrowed one, since we cannot say anything else of vines than that they gemmare, nor of crops than that they are laetae. For ornament, however, we say «the crops billow (fluctuare)»; for we could say something else, such as «move», yet because that is not ornate, we use a borrowed word.

{{360}} Borrowed words are formed in three ways: either by likeness, or by contrary, or by difference. By likeness, as are those numbered among the tropes of Grammar, such as this very phrase I mentioned, «the crops billow». Of this kind are also those which signify the part from the whole or the whole from the part in such a way that they possess a fitting nearness of words; for it has been agreed to include these under the kind of likeness. By contrary, words are used when they are taken in a sense opposite to what we say, as we call the Fates Parcae («Sparing Ones») when they spare not, and a grove lucus when it gives no light (lucet). This grammarians call κατὰ ἀντίφρασιν (by antiphrasis). Borrowed words «by difference» are when without any reason they are taken over from other things, as if we should call a man who is neither hard in body nor dull in wit a «stone». Yet it is not fitting to use these; for it is foolish to utter words that either signify nothing or are excessively far-fetched. Proper words, however, and those borrowed by likeness or by contrary, may rightly be used.

{{361}} Before I speak of substance, certain things must be taught. Whatsoever we say is either a subject (subiectum), or «predicated of a subject» (de subiecto), or «in a subject» (in subiecto), or «both predicated of a subject and in a subject» (de subiecto et in subiecto). A subject is primary substance, which itself happens to nothing else inseparably, yet other things happen to it: as Cicero—not the name, but what is signified by that name. «Predicated of a subject» is that which is said of the subject itself and gives to it both its definition and its name, as «man»; for Cicero is both a man and a rational mortal animal. Thus both the name and the definition which is «of a subject» accrue to that same subject, and therefore that which is said «of a subject» is found in genera or in forms.

{{362}} «In a subject» is that which gives neither its name nor its definition to the subject, yet is understood to be in the subject itself in such a way that it cannot exist without it, as rhetoric: for the subject can receive neither its name nor its definition. Neither is Cicero rhetoric, nor is Cicero the science of speaking well; yet it is understood to be in him, though he himself cannot be called by that name. «Both predicated of a subject and in a subject» is that which is predicated of one thing and is in another, as knowledge (disciplina): for this same is predicated of rhetoric as of a subject, and is in Cicero as in a subject. Primary substance, therefore, is the subject; secondary substance is that which is predicated of primary substance itself, as, if «Cicero» is primary substance, «man» and «animal» are secondary. Now all subsequent predications are understood to be in a subject. Let us therefore examine each one singly.

{{363}} Quality is that according to which we are said to be of such-and-such a sort (quales), as whiteness; and from this it may also be understood that quality is in a subject, since whiteness must necessarily be in something without which it cannot exist—and assuredly that very thing in which it is, is a subject. Quantity is that according to which we say how great a thing is, as two-foot; this too must necessarily be understood to be in a subject. Relation (relativum) is that which is called toward something (ad aliquid), as «father», «brother»; and these too are certainly in a subject. ‹…› for it is necessary that all these be toward something, and there are some things of which they are predicated: those which are said to be in the soul will be seen. Place (loci) is what we express by saying «at Rome»: Rome is a substance; this happens to Rome itself. Time (tempore), as «yesterday», «lately», «in the evening»: the things by whose motion time is understood are substances, such as the sun, by whose course we understand time, and things which produce in us some awareness of duration. Posture (situs), as for instance «he lies», «he sits». State (habitus), as «shod», «armed»: man is the substance, and these accrue to him. Action (facere), as «to cut», «to burn». Passion (pati), as «to be cut», «to be burned».

{{364}} Primary substance is that which is neither inseparably in a subject nor predicated of any subject. Now «inseparably» is added to this definition for this reason: that every primary substance, although it is in some place, can nevertheless be separated from it and depart, as Cicero is understood to be in the senate-house in such a way that he can depart thence elsewhere. And a part of a primary substance, although it is in the whole, is nevertheless not inseparably so; for whether in fact or in thought an arm can be separated from our body. Rhetoric, on the contrary, is in the mind of Cicero in such a way that even if by some chance it ceases to exist, it is still not understood to depart elsewhere, since neither when it began to exist was it understood to have arrived from elsewhere.

{{365}} Secondary substance is that which, as hath been said, is predicated of primary substance, as man of Cicero, and animal of man and Cicero; and whatsoever is a genus of primary substance is understood to be secondary substance. Thus, therefore, it is common to all substances not to be in a subject; primary substance, however, is not even predicated of a subject. Substance can neither be intensified nor remitted, that is, it does not admit of more and less. For indeed no man is more a man than another man, nor will that same single man be more a man tomorrow than he was today, nor among different species is a horse more a horse than a man is a man. This, however, must be observed in substances among their own peers—that is, when thou comparest primary with primary, or secondary with secondary. For if thou comparest secondary with primary, primary is more truly substance than secondary: for primary makes the thing clearer, whereas secondary has a certain ambiguity of generality. For when I say «Cicero», I already signify something individual and definite; when I say «man», since all of us are included under this appellation, it is uncertain whom I signify. Thus it comes about that primary substance is more truly substance than secondary, because it shows the thing more definitely.

{{366}} Among its own peers, therefore, substance does not admit of more and less. Likewise substance has no contrary; for nothing is contrary to a man or to a horse. Yet if someone should say that Clodius was contrary to Cicero, he understands not that the substances themselves were contrary, but the qualities that were in them, such as malice to goodness, or vice to virtue, or injustice to justice. It appears, however, to be a property of substance that one and the same substance is capable of receiving contraries through a certain change in itself: as a stone, while remaining the same, can be now white and now black, yet does not cease to be the same stone; and Cicero, at first foolish and afterward wise, does not cease to be the same Cicero.

{{367}} We have said that quality is that according to which we are said to be of such-and-such a sort. One form of qualities is that in which a certain disposition (dispositio) and habit (habitus) of mind is understood to exist, as in all acquired arts—wisdom, grammar, rhetoric, and the rest—which so cleave to the soul that they can hardly be lost. Yet among these some are perfected and some imperfect: as if someone has applied himself to the art of grammar, yet still errs in most matters, it cannot yet be called a habit, but is called only a disposition. Thus not every disposition is a habit, whereas every habit is understood to be a disposition.

{{368}} The second species consists of those qualities which we may rightly call «affective» (passibiles), such as sweet and bitter, hot or cold—not because these same substances suffer anything from them, but because they compel our senses to suffer something. For heat compels him who touches to suffer something, and sweetness him who tastes. Likewise those traits which have grown ingrained in us from some affection of nature, according to which each person is called pale or ruddy, though not as when someone turns pale or blushes from some sudden cause. For these latter are more rightly called «affections» (passiones), not «qualities», since according to them we are not said to be of such-and-such a sort (quales). For it does not follow that one who turns pale is a pale person, or that one who loves is an amorous man, or that one who is drunk is a drunkard. Those, therefore, are affections; these are qualities.

{{369}} The third species consists of those qualities which are understood not from what each thing already is, but from what it is capable of being: as we call wood fragile, not because it is already broken, but because it can be broken. For we call a body wrestling-fit (palaestricum) in two ways: both that which has been trained by the wrestling-school, and that which is so formed by nature as to be suited to this art, even though it has not been imbued with it. The former is rightly called palaestricum from the wrestling-school because it possesses the actual effect of the art, whereas no name is found for the quality itself from which is denominated and derived that palaestricum which does not yet exist, but is capable of existing. Wherefore we must recognize that certain substances are named from qualities whose names are not found: for just as we say good from goodness, we do not likewise say best (optimum) from bestness (optimitate). Thus, therefore, that palaestricum which we understand from its capacity to acquire the art of wrestling has no fixed name of a quality from which it seems to be named; yet it is agreed that it is named from a quality.

{{370}} The fourth species consists of those qualities according to which we understand shapes and figures, as square, round, beautiful, deformed, and the like. Quality admits of more and less, yet not every quality: for no square is more a square than another square, whereas one white thing can be called whiter than another. And in many cases it is debated whether one man can be called more just than another. Many, however, seem to have observed subtly that qualities themselves do not admit of more and less, but only those things which are denominated from them: so that justice itself is one single perfected notion, so that it cannot be said «this justice is more justice than that», yet it can be said «this man is more just than that man». Likewise it cannot be said «this health is more health than that», yet it can be said «this man is healthier than that man». Whence it follows that substance does not admit of more and less, whereas qualities can admit of them through the substances themselves. Likewise quality has a contrary, yet not every quality: for while weakness is contrary to health, nothing is contrary to square or round. Moreover, it must be seen that whatsoever is contrary to a quality must necessarily be a quality: now sweetness is a quality; therefore bitterness and the like are qualities.

{{371}} Quantity is twofold, in that some is discrete and some continuous: discrete, as number and speech; continuous, as line and time. Likewise there is another division of quantity, in that some has a certain relative position (situs) of parts and some has not. For a line is understood with a certain relative position of parts, since it can be stated which part of it is in which place, and it appears to have a right and a left. Number, speech, or time, on the other hand, have none of these, although they can have order, so that there is in them something first and second and last and middle, yet nothing that is understood in place.

{{372}} All quantity lacks a contrary. For what is contrary to two-foot or three-foot? And if anyone says that more and less, which seem to be words of quantity, are contraries, let him know that they are not definite quantity. Thus, if he says something is greater, what is less seems contrary to it; if I ask «greater than what definite thing?» and he answers «than a three-foot measure», it is clear that nothing is contrary. Moreover, those things which are called greater and less in relation to one another are manifestly spoken relatively: for anything compared to a smaller thing is greater, and the same thing compared to a larger thing is smaller. If, therefore, greater and less are contraries, we are compelled to confess—what is exceedingly absurd—that contraries can belong to some thing at one and the same time. Again, we are compelled to understand another absurdity, that one and the same thing is contrary to itself, since one thing compared to different quantities can at the same time be greater and less.

{{373}} Quantity does not admit of more and less: for five is not more five than two is two, nor is this same two more two than another two, nor will two be more two tomorrow than it is today. It is, moreover, a property of quantity that according to it we speak of equal and unequal, just as it is a property of quality that according to it we speak of like and unlike, although in different matters either term may be used by extension.

{{374}} Relation (relativum) is that which, in respect of the very thing it is called, belongs to something or can in any manner be referred toward something: as a son cannot be understood without a father or mother, nor a slave without a master, nor these in turn without those.

{{375}} Relatives, moreover, are spoken in three ways: either of something (genitive), as son; or to something (dative), as neighbor; or toward something (prepositional), as double, since the double is double toward some single. All relatives correspond reciprocally to those things to which they are referred: for a father is father of a son, and a son is son of a father; and they correspond in such a way that some correspond in the same grammatical cases, while others change cases. For I have spoken of the son; likewise we can say of a slave that a slave is the slave of a master, and a master is the master of a slave. These so correspond to each other that they preserve the same cases in conversion. So too the double is double to the single; likewise a greater thing is greater than some smaller thing, and a smaller thing is smaller than some greater thing. It is manifest, therefore, that these preserve their cases in conversion. Knowledge (scientia), on the other hand, although it is relative—for knowledge is knowledge of some knowable thing (scibilis rei)—changes the case of that to which it is referred in conversion. For though we say «knowledge is of a knowable thing», we cannot say «a knowable thing is of knowledge», but «a knowable thing is knowable by knowledge». Likewise perception (sensus) is perception of some perceptible thing, and conversely a perceptible thing is perceptible by perception. These, therefore, are converted not with the same cases preserved, like those mentioned above, but with changed cases.

{{376}} Certain relatives also agree in time with those things to which they are referred, and begin to exist together and cease together: as a slave cannot exist save when a master hath begun to exist, and when the master hath ceased to exist, the slave ceases to exist; and likewise a master, when he hath no slave, cannot be called a master. A knowable thing, on the other hand, is prior by nature to the knowledge of it. For if thou takest away knowable things, there will be no knowledge; whereas if thou takest away knowledge, something can still be knowable, even though he who knows it be lacking.

{{377}} Relatives can have contraries, though not all of them: for knowledge is contrary to ignorance, and enmity to friendship. To double, however, nothing is contrary, nor to greater or less, because whosoever thinks these are contraries is compelled to confess that one and the same thing at one and the same time can be contrary to itself. For a larger thing compared to a smaller is greater, and the same thing compared to a larger is smaller at one and the same time—which can in no wise happen to contraries. For at the time when a man is foolish, he cannot at the same time be wise, nor at the time when he is white, can he at the same time be black. And because we have shown that this can happen in the case of greater and less, we must necessarily confess that greater and less are not contraries; likewise that double is not contrary to single, since this same double can be double toward some single. Not all relatives, therefore, can have a contrary. Likewise some admit of more and less, and some do not: for this man is more a friend than that man, whereas this double cannot be more double than that double, for the reason that if it is double, it is double in such a way that if there were anything more or less, it would not be double.

{{378}} It is asked whether any substance can be predicated relatively. Yet concerning primary substance there is no question that it cannot be predicated relatively: for Cicero cannot be said to be «someone’s Cicero» or be referred toward anything. Likewise a horse—that is, a particular horse, such as Rhoebus—does not belong to someone by the very fact that he is Rhoebus, though insofar as he is a beast of burden, he is someone’s beast of burden. Neither primary substance, therefore, nor any part of it—which is without doubt itself also a primary substance—can be predicated relatively. For just as Cicero is a primary substance, so is his hand (while hand—not his hand, but hand in general, thus a secondary substance—cannot be predicated relatively). For they do not correspond in their conversion: if we say «a hand is the hand of Cicero», Cicero is not «Cicero of a hand», nor is the hand itself a hand for this reason, that it belongs to Cicero, but because it is so structured is it called a «hand» (not because it belongs to someone). Neither primary substance, therefore, as we have said, nor its parts can be predicated relatively. Inquiry is made, therefore, concerning parts of secondary substance; for concerning secondary substance itself there is no question, since man is not «someone’s man». Yet hand in general is the hand of some man, and, so that it may correspond by mutual conversion, the hand of some «handed» creature (manuati), so that we may convert it thus: that a «handed» creature is «handed» by a hand. Likewise a hoof—not the hoof of a primary substance, but of some hoofed creature (ungulati), because a hoofed creature is hoofed by a hoof—so that it may have mutual conversion, which we have declared to be the property of relatives. If, therefore, that definition of relatives stands—that a relative is whatsoever can be said «of something»—it is hard to deny that parts of secondary substances are predicated relatively. If, however, that definition is altered so that relatives are those things which are referred toward something apart from the fact that they are in something, no substance falls under this definition—just as slavery, apart from the fact that it is in that same slave, that is, in some man, is referred toward a master; whereas a wing is the wing of some winged creature in such a way that, apart from its being in some winged creature, it can be referred to nothing.

{{379}} We should certainly be reminded not to be ashamed to coin new words for the necessity of conversion; for if mutual conversion does not correspond, we are not speaking relatively, thus: when I say «wing» (pinnam), wishing to show it to be relative, I am not ashamed to say «something winged» (pinnatum) for the sake of conversion; for pinnatum is so customary that it offends not the ears. If the discussion be of a foot (pede), let one not be ashamed to fashion by similar derivation a word suited to conversion (say, pedatum). Likewise one must know that he who is ignorant of what a relative thing is referred to cannot know at all whether what he speaks of can be referred to anything: thus, when thou sayest «this is double», thou knowest its single—that is, that to which it is double—or if thou art ignorant of that, thou canst not even know at all whether it is double.

{{380}} Action (facere) and passion (pati) can have a contrary, as to heat and to cool, and to be heated and to be cooled. They also admit of more and less, as to burn more and to burn less, and to be burned more and to be burned less.

{{381}} All posture (situs) is predicated denominatively, as to sit from sitting, to stand from standing; and although sometimes the nouns from which postures are denominated are lacking, yet the principle is not lacking.

{{382}} Concerning those three which remain, the examples given above suffice: for when (quando) we say as «yesterday», «tomorrow»; where (ubi) as «at Rome», «at Athens»; state (habitum) as «shod», «armed». Which of these admits of more and less, when it occurs in speech, easily appears.

{{383}} These are the ten categories (praedicationes), from which we necessarily utter something singly. For whatsoever we say at all that signifies something, yet cannot yet be understood as either true or false, is one of these ten—excepting only those, as it were, joints of words. For there are many things set forth by grammarians among the parts of speech which have no force at all by themselves nor signify anything complete unless they have been joined to words, such as conjunctions, prepositions, and whatsoever of that sort they teach.

{{384}} It remains for us to speak of opposites (opposita). Opposites, then, are those things which seem to stand against one another as though face to face, like contraries. Yet not all things which are opposed to one another are contraries, though all contraries are opposites. Things are opposed to one another in such a way that either relatives are opposed, as great to small and half to double; or contraries, as folly to wisdom; or possession to privation (habitus orbationi), as sight to blindness; or affirmative to negative, as «Cicero disputes» and «Cicero does not dispute». These, however, differ somewhat among themselves. For a relative is opposed to a relative in such a way that the very thing which is opposed belongs to that to which it is opposed, or is in some manner referred toward it: for half is opposed to double and is the half of that same double. Thus, therefore, it is opposed to it in such a way that it belongs to it; and small is opposed to great in such a way that it belongs to it, so that the small itself is small in relation to this great to which it is opposed.

{{385}} Contraries, on the other hand, are opposed to one another in such a way that they do not belong to those things to which they are opposed, nor are they in any manner referred toward them: since folly is contrary to wisdom in such a way that it is not the folly of that same wisdom, nor is it folly toward wisdom. It must be known, nevertheless, that certain contraries have an intermediate (medium), while certain others have not. For those which are of such a nature that in the thing wherein they can exist, one or the other is necessarily present by turns, lack an intermediate, as health and sickness. These two contraries exist by nature in the bodies of animals, and are so necessarily present by turns, as we have said, that in whatever animal’s body health is not, sickness is necessarily present, and in whatever body sickness is not, health is necessarily present. White and black, on the contrary, although they are contraries and are naturally found in bodies, do not lack an intermediate, for the reason that it is not necessary for any given body to be either white or black. For white can be absent in such a way that black is not present, and likewise on the other side; therefore some intermediate color can be found, such as yellow or green.

{{386}} Possession (habitus) and privation (orbatio) are opposed to one another in such a way that in the thing to which they can happen, one of the two is necessarily present—at least from that time when nature permits it to be so: as we call toothed him who hath teeth, while we call toothless not him who hath no teeth, but him in whom it is natural to have them, and from that time when nature already permits him to have them. For we rightly call neither a stone toothless, which never hath teeth, nor an infant: for although an infant can have them at some time, yet at that time nature does not yet permit it to have them. This third kind of opposites, therefore, differs from the first—that of relatives—in that sight is opposed to blindness in such a way that it does not belong to that same blindness nor is in any manner referred toward it.
