From the second kind—that is, contraries—it differs from those contraries at least which have an intermediate, in that sight and blindness are understood in respect of eyes so that one of them is necessarily present: herein, therefore, they differ from contraries that have an intermediate, because one of those is not necessarily present in a substance, whereas one of these is necessarily present. From those contraries, however, which lack an intermediate, these same opposites differ in that those must necessarily belong by turns at every time to the thing in which they naturally exist, as health and sickness in an animal’s body—one of them is always in an animal’s body—whereas these can both be absent at some time from that thing in which they can naturally exist: as an infant, while it is not yet the time when it can have teeth, is called neither toothed nor toothless, and the eyes of some living creature, when the time hath not yet come wherein they can see, are called neither blind nor seeing.

{{387}} The fourth kind of opposites is affirmative and negative, as «Cicero disputes» and «Cicero does not dispute». These differ from the foregoing in that those can be spoken singly, whereas these are spoken only in combination. From relatives they differ in this, that those also ‹…› From contraries they differ in this, that contraries, if they be spoken in combination, are either true or false only so long as that exists wherein they can be present; when, however, this hath ceased to exist, they are neither true nor false, as «he is foolish» and «he is wise»: so long as he lives, one of these is true; when he himself hath ceased to exist, both are false, because he who does not exist can be neither foolish nor wise. «Cicero disputes» and «Cicero does not dispute», on the contrary, are so opposed to one another that both while Cicero lives one of them is necessarily true, and when he is dead it is indeed false that he disputes, yet true that he does not dispute. In this manner these are distinguished also from possession and privation: for he who does not exist is neither blind nor seeing. Nor, indeed, be thou moved because we seem already to have spoken somewhat of propositions, concerning which we must speak hereafter; for this was done on the occasion of opposites.

{{388}} A noun (nomen) is that which signifies some thing and can be inflected through cases; a verb (verbum) is that which signifies something and can be inflected through tenses: as «Cicero» is a noun, «disputes» is a verb. These, separated from one another, signify something indeed, yet can be called neither true nor false; when, however, they have been joined together, they can now be both affirmed and denied, as «Cicero disputes»: now it can be said «Cicero does not dispute». The noun, moreover, must be in the nominative case and the verb in the third person. The first person signifies something that can already be affirmed and denied, and falls upon man alone; in it a noun (pronoun) is understood even if it be not spoken, as «I dispute» (disputo) is complete even if thou sayest not «I» (ego). Likewise the second person is itself also subject to truth or falsity, yet it too falls upon man alone, and we do not rightly say «thou disputest» (disputas) to one who can neither hear nor understand what is said. Therefore, although this too is spoken without a noun, yet a noun is understood therein. Otherwise we use either the first or the second person figuratively, as when thou introducest as speaking one who cannot speak, or when we turn our speech toward one who can neither hear nor understand.

{{389}} The third person, on the other hand, belongs not to man alone, but to other things as well, and as soon as it is spoken, it is not immediately understood unless by chance something is said of it which can be understood of it alone: as when we say «it rains» (pluit), this can already be true or false although we add no noun, for it is known who sends rain. When, however, we say «disputes» (disputat), although it already signifies something, yet it can be called neither true nor false if a noun be not added; and although this can be understood of man alone, because it could be said of more than one man, a noun must necessarily be subjoined. And when we say «resists» (resistit), it is the third person, and demands a noun not of man alone, but of whatsoever can resist. The first and second persons, therefore, can both be understood of man alone and, when spoken alone, can be called either true or false, because nouns are also understood with them; the third person, however, cannot in every case be spoken alone, nor is it understood of man alone.

{{390}} Whatsoever, therefore, is joined together from the nominative case of a noun and the third person of a verb is called a proposition (proloquium), in such a way that it is now necessarily either true or false or doubtful. For «man is an animal» we all judge to be true, and «every animal is a man» we all judge to be false; «he disputes», although he necessarily either disputes or does not dispute, is nonetheless doubtful to us—for we understand that one of these is necessary, yet which of them it is we know not. Where, however, there are those verbs which are called impersonal, the sentence is completed not from the nominative case, but receives other cases: as when «it is disputed» (disputatur) is said, it is a complete sentence if thou joinest an ablative, that is, «by Cicero» (a Cicerone); and when «it repents» (paenitet) is said, it is a complete sentence if thou joinest an accusative, that is, «Cicero» (Ciceronem). And there are many such.

{{391}} This, nevertheless, is agreed: that personal verbs complete a sentence only with the nominative case and the third person. There are also sentences which, although they consist of a noun and a verb, yet can be neither affirmed nor denied, which some have chosen to call not propositions (proloquia), but utterances (eloquia): such is the imperative mood when we say «Run!» (curre). This is already a complete sentence—for what thou sayest can not only be understood, but also done—yet it cannot be denied. For he who says «Do not run!» does not deny this: for this is not opposed to what was said, «Run!», in such a way that the one is true and the other false, so that a question can arise. For out of affirmation and negation a question undoubtedly arises, as «he runs» and «he does not run»: the question is whether he runs. «Run!» and «Do not run!», however, do not raise the question whether he runs. Nor indeed can any question be understood here whether he ought to run, for that arises out of affirmation and negation, such as «he ought to run» and «he ought not to run». The optative mood acts in the same way: for although it is a complete sentence when we say «Would that I might write!» or «Would that I might not write!», no question can arise hence whether he writes. There are many such forms; let these suffice for an example.

{{392}} Whatsoever, therefore, is joined together from a noun and a verb, if it be a complete noun and a complete verb, necessarily makes a sentence, yet does not necessarily make a proposition if there is nothing that can now be both affirmed and denied. And we have said above that many things are spoken in a complete sentence which nevertheless cannot be affirmed and denied. A complete proposition, therefore, is «Every man is an animal»; and although nature demands that the noun be spoken first and the verb afterward, as hath been said, it does not cease to be a genuine proposition even if thou sayest «An animal is every man».

{{393}} Whatsoever is added to this sentence, we must carefully see to which part it is added. For there are two parts of a proposition: the one, in the noun, is called the subject (subiectiva); the other, in the verb, the predicate (declarativa). For it is laid down as subject what a thing is, and it is declared what can be understood concerning it. When, therefore, we say «Cicero disputes», if «in his Tusculan villa» be added to this sentence, it hath been added to the predicate; if «the Roman» be added, to the subject; likewise if «prudently and copiously» be added, to the predicate; likewise when «with Cato» is said, it hath been added to the predicate. Whatsoever, therefore, is added in the nominative case is added to the subject; whatsoever is added to the predicate is added in various cases and moods. For no other cases can be added to the subject, and none save various cases—excepting the nominative—to the predicate.

{{394}} This, however, must be known: that it can happen that a verb is in the subject and a noun in the predicate—yet a verb in the subject in such a way that it holds some pronoun with itself in place of a noun, and likewise a noun is in the predicate in such a way that it holds with itself some verb whereby it fulfills its office: as if we say «He who disputes is Cicero», «he who disputes» is the subject and «Cicero» is the predicate; yet the pronoun makes the former the subject, and the verb makes the latter the predicate.

{{395}} In this part, therefore, we explain how nouns and verbs may be joined and yet not be able to make a complete sentence; and if they do make one, how sentences may not be subject to falsity and truth, although they are complete; and how one arrives at that point where there is now not only a complete sentence, but one that is necessarily either true or false.

{{396}} The third part follows, wherein we must now speak of propositions themselves, to the understanding of which we attained in the preceding part. Propositions, therefore, have twofold differences in quantity and in quality. The difference of quantity is that some are universal, some particular, and some indefinite. Universal is such as «Every man is an animal»; particular, as «Some man walks»; indefinite, as «Man walks». Whatsoever, therefore, we say indefinitely, we necessarily take particularly, not necessarily universally: since that must chiefly be reckoned which has a sure meaning, the indefinite is taken for the particular. There will be, therefore, two differences in quantity, in that one proposition is universal and another particular; and likewise two in quality, of which the one is affirmative (dedicativa) and the other negative (abdicativa). Affirmative is such as «Every pleasure is a good»; negative, as «Every pleasure is not a good» (that is, no pleasure is a good).

{{397}} Conversion, therefore, does not necessarily follow a universal affirmative: for it does not follow that if every man is an animal, every animal is a man. Conversion, however, does necessarily follow a universal negative: for if every pleasure is not a good, every good is not a pleasure; and if every man is not a dumb animal, every dumb animal is not a man. A particular affirmative, moreover, has a necessary conversion: for if some man is an animal, some animal is a man. A particular negative, on the other hand, does not have a necessary conversion: for it does not follow that if some animal is not rational, some rational thing is not an animal. Universal affirmative and particular negative propositions, therefore, do not necessarily convert; whereas universal negative and particular affirmative propositions are necessarily converted, in such a way, however, that when conversion hath been made, the negation remains in the predicate part. For conversion is made in such a way that the part which was the predicate becomes the subject. Therefore when I say «Every pleasure is not a good», «every pleasure» is the subject and «is not a good» is the predicate; if I convert it to «Every good is not a pleasure», that which was the predicate hath indeed become the subject, yet the negation holds onto that part which hath become the predicate. And if I wish to say thus, «No pleasure is a good», it must be converted thus: «No good is a pleasure».

{{398}} Yet on account of the two propositions which we say are not necessarily converted, we ought to examine all things that are attributed to propositions whereby they can be rightly or wrongly propounded so as to show truth or falsity. Now these are the five already demonstrated above: genus, difference, accident, definition, and property. Definition and property cause those propositions to convert reciprocally, while the other three do so in no wise. For just as «Every man is a rational mortal animal», so «Every rational mortal animal is a man»; and just as «Every man is capable of laughter», so «Everything capable of laughter is a man». Again, in a particular negative we must see what it is not to be a property: for just as it is a property of man to be capable of laughter, so it is a property of what is capable of laughter not to be anything other than man. Therefore if we propound a particular negative thus, «Something capable of laughter is not other than man», it converts without doubt into «Something other than man is not capable of laughter». Likewise from «Something inanimate, irrational, and immortal is not a man», follows «Some man is not inanimate, irrational, and immortal».

{{399}} Likewise there are other conversions which make those same parts of propositions indefinite, with the negation namely shifting its place. For indefinite parts are formed thus: «man», «non-man»; «animal», «non-animal»; and it is indefinite for this reason, that thou only deniest it to be this, without saying what it is. When, therefore, I say «Every man is an animal», if I wish to convert it rightly, I apply negations so that the parts become indefinite: for if it is true that every man is an animal, it is also true that «Every non-animal is a non-man». Cicero used this conversion in his Rhetorica: «Next, if an issue (constitutio), both itself and any part of it, is a rebuttal of the charge, whatsoever is not a rebuttal of the charge is neither an issue nor a part of an issue».

{{400}} Likewise a particular negative can be converted thus: for if «Some animal is not a man», then «Some non-man is an animal»; and in this conversion it must be observed that where there is no negation when we propound directly, there is one when we convert. This conversion in turn is admitted by the two propositions which did not admit that first conversion; only the universal negative does not admit it. For convenience of reference, therefore, let that be called «the first conversion», and this «the second». How these propositions stand affected toward one another will appear more manifestly thus:

{{401}} Let a square figure be drawn with four lines. In the first corner of the upper line let the universal affirmative be written, and in the other corner of the same line the universal negative; likewise below, at the first corner the particular affirmative, and at the remaining corner the particular negative; then let diagonal lines be drawn from the universal affirmative to the particular negative, and from the universal negative to the particular affirmative. The two upper propositions, therefore, cannot be affirmed together, yet can be denied together.

{{402}} For it cannot be true at the same time that «Every pleasure is a good» and «Every pleasure is not a good»; yet it can be true at the same time that «Not every pleasure is a good» and «Not every pleasure is not a good». The two lower propositions, on the other hand, cannot be denied together, yet can be affirmed together: for it cannot be at the same time that «Not some pleasure is a good» and «Some pleasure is a good», whereas it can be both that «Some pleasure is a good» and that «Some pleasure is not a good». The diagonal propositions, moreover, can neither be affirmed together nor denied together. For if it is true that «Every pleasure is a good», it is false that «Some pleasure is not a good»; likewise if it is false that «Every pleasure is a good», it is true that «Some pleasure is not a good». This likewise happens if thou namest the particular first in turn. Likewise if it is true that «Every pleasure is not a good», it is false that «Some pleasure is a good»; and if it is false that «Every pleasure is not a good», it is true that «Some pleasure is a good».

{{403}} Likewise a universal affirmative, when affirmed, necessarily affirms its own particular, whereas when denied it does not necessarily deny it: for if it is true that «Every pleasure is a good», it is necessarily true that «Some pleasure is a good»; whereas if we deny the upper proposition thus, «Not every pleasure is a good», it may happen that some pleasure is a good. A particular affirmative, likewise, when affirmed does not necessarily affirm its own universal, whereas when denied it necessarily denies it: for if it is true that «Some pleasure is a good», it does not follow that every pleasure is also a good; whereas if «Some pleasure is not a good», it is false that «Every pleasure is a good». In the two remaining propositions thou shalt observe the same rule.

{{404}} When we have put forward a complete sentence, wishing to establish something from it, once it has been granted, it is called a «premise» (sumptum). To this single sentence another must also be linked by a sure reason, and this too must be granted for the sake of what we wish to infer; and when this has been granted, it is also called a «premise» (sumptum).

{{405}} From two premises linked to one another by reason is produced an inference (illatio). Which inference cannot be called a «premise» for this reason, that thou dost not wait for thine adversary to grant this also, but it follows even against his will, provided only that it has been validly inferred. And, that this may become plain by an example, let us suppose the question to be whether pleasure is useful. If we propound thus, «Every pleasure is a good», it is indeed a complete sentence; it becomes a premise if the adversary grants it. This being granted, another sentence must be joined to it: «Now every good is useful». If he grants this also, it becomes a second premise; and from these two premises, even against his will, it follows: «Therefore every pleasure is useful».

{{406}} This whole, which consists of two premises and an inference, is called by us ratiocinatio, and by the Greeks syllogismos (συλλογισμός). Reasoning, therefore, is a necessary arrival, from two or more granted propositions, at that which is not granted. There can, therefore, be more premises, yet fewer than two there cannot be. For it is a complete reasoning if, to establish what we wish to show—that pleasure is useful—we wish to arrive at it even through three premises, as: «Every pleasure is according to nature; whatsoever is according to nature is a good; every good is useful; therefore every pleasure is useful». From this it appears that even more may be added, if need be.

{{407}} Sometimes also we conclude by inferring not indeed that which is directly established, but that which necessarily follows from the very thing we ought to infer, thus: «Every virtue is a good; every good is useful; therefore no virtue harms». What was to be inferred was «Therefore every virtue is useful»; from this it necessarily follows that it does not harm, for whatsoever is useful never harms. This is called by the Greeks symperasma (συμπέρασμα), and by us may be called a «conterminous conclusion» (confinis conclusio). Reasoning, therefore, whether having its own proper conclusion or a conterminous one, is divided into two kinds: predicative and conditional.

{{408}} A predicative syllogism is one in which the premises are so linked together that they are completed by something added from without, as in that which was said above: «Every pleasure is a good; every good is useful». We see, certainly, that not the whole of what was set down above is repeated, but one part taken from it—the predicate—which in the second proposition has become the subject; and this second proposition, in order to complete the sentence, sought something from without, namely «is useful». Its inference is formed from that which was added and that which was not repeated, namely: «Therefore every pleasure is useful». Of this kind there are three figures (formae): the first is that in which the predicate part of the first premise becomes the subject of the following, or the subject of the first becomes the predicate of the following. The predicate of the first becomes the subject of the following as in the example proposed above; the subject of the first becomes the predicate of the following if thou wishest to invert it thus: «Every good is useful; every pleasure is a good; therefore every pleasure is useful». The second figure is that in which the predicate part of the first premise is also the predicate of the following, as: «Every virtue is a good, and no pleasure is a good; therefore no pleasure is a virtue». The third figure is that in which the subject part of the first premise is also the subject of the following, as: «Some good is a pleasure; every good is useful; therefore something useful is a pleasure».

{{409}} In the first figure one can conclude universally and particularly, affirmatively and negatively; in the second figure one cannot conclude save negatively; in the third figure one concludes particularly alone: wherefore this order has not been preserved in vain. For deservedly is that called first wherein one can conclude in every mode; likewise rightly second wherein one can conclude universally, albeit negatively; and likewise rightly third, because it is lesser in quantity, wherein one cannot conclude save particularly.

{{410}} Now we must state how many moods (modi) each figure admits. For they admit them within a fixed number, outside which moods whatsoever has been concluded is not rashly to be granted. Now the first figure admits nine moods, the second four, and the third six.

{{411}} The first mood of the first figure is that in which from two universal affirmatives a universal affirmative is produced directly, as: «Everything just is honorable; everything honorable is good; therefore everything just is good». If thou inferrest indirectly, «Therefore everything good is just», it does not follow; yet it can be inferred particularly, «Therefore something good is just», and this forms the fifth mood. The second mood is that in which from a universal affirmative and a universal negative a universal negative is produced directly, as: «Everything just is honorable; nothing honorable is base; therefore nothing just is base». If thou convertest it, «Therefore nothing base is just», this forms the sixth mood; for we said above that a universal negative can be converted. The third mood is that in which from a particular affirmative and a universal affirmative a particular affirmative is produced directly, as: «Something just is honorable; everything honorable is useful; therefore something just is useful». Yet if thou convertest it, there is formed the seventh mood, «Therefore something useful is just», since it was stated above that a particular affirmative can be converted. The fourth mood is that in which from a particular affirmative and a universal negative a particular negative is produced directly, as: «Something just is honorable; nothing honorable is base; therefore something just is not base». This cannot be converted; for we said above that a particular negative cannot be converted. The eighth mood is that in which from a universal negative and a universal affirmative a particular negative is produced indirectly, as: «Nothing base is honorable; everything honorable is just; therefore something just is not base». The ninth mood is that in which from a universal negative and a particular affirmative a particular negative is produced indirectly, as: «Nothing base is honorable; something honorable is just; therefore something just is not base».

{{412}} The first mood of the second figure is that in which from a universal affirmative and a universal negative a universal negative is produced directly, as: «Everything just is honorable; nothing base is honorable; therefore nothing just is base». Here, if thou usest conversion, another mood is not produced, since the inference is made from both subjects. The second mood is that in which from a universal negative and a universal affirmative a universal negative is produced directly, as: «Nothing base is honorable; everything just is honorable; therefore nothing base is just». The third mood is that in which from a particular affirmative and a universal negative a particular negative is produced directly, as: «Something just is honorable; nothing base is honorable; therefore something just is not base». The fourth mood is that in which from a particular negative and a universal affirmative a particular negative is produced directly, as: «Something just is not base; everything evil is base; therefore something just is not evil».

{{413}} The first mood of the third figure is that in which from two universal affirmatives a particular affirmative is produced directly, as: «Everything just is honorable; everything just is good; therefore something honorable is good». The second mood is that in which from a particular affirmative and a universal affirmative a particular affirmative is produced directly, as: «Something just is honorable; everything just is good; therefore something honorable is good». The third mood is that in which from a universal affirmative and a particular affirmative a particular affirmative is produced directly, as: «Everything honorable is just; something honorable is good; therefore something just is good». The fourth mood is that in which from a universal affirmative and a universal negative a particular negative is produced directly, as: «Everything just is honorable; nothing just is evil; therefore something honorable is not evil». The fifth mood is that in which from a particular affirmative and a universal negative a particular negative is produced directly: «Something just is honorable; nothing just is evil; therefore something honorable is not evil». The sixth mood is that in which from a universal affirmative and a particular negative a particular negative is produced directly, as: «Everything just is honorable; something just is not evil; therefore something honorable is not evil». All the moods, therefore, keep a fixed order, and the reason of that same order is that which has been demonstrated in the figures themselves.

{{414}} A conditional syllogism is one whose proposition (propositio) contains both the full argument and the full matter concerning which the question is raised, in such a way that, once the argument has been assumed, that concerning which the question was raised can now be surely inferred, in this manner: let us suppose the question to be whether rhetoric is useful, and wishing to prove it useful, let us take an argument from its definition, which is the science of speaking well. From this argument and that question a conditional syllogism is set down thus: «If rhetoric is the science of speaking well, it is useful». We assume the argument thus: «Now rhetoric is the science of speaking well». Whosoever grants these two, even against his will, must necessarily grant that rhetoric is useful—which was doubtful before the proposition and assumption were granted. Nothing, however, has been added from without in the assumption, which is the property of the predicative syllogism. This first mood is called by dialecticians that from the antecedent (ab antecedentibus), for the reason that the argument is assumed in the very same way as it was propounded, differing only in this, that it was propounded with a condition and assumed without a condition.

{{415}} The second mood is that which is called from the consequent (a consequentibus), in which the argument itself follows the question, not the question the argument, thus: «If rhetoric is not useful, it is not the science of speaking well». The argument is assumed by contrary—that is, through negation—thus: «Now it is the science of speaking well; therefore it is useful».

{{416}} The third mood is that which is called from incompatibles (a repugnantibus), in which it is shown that this cannot be and that not be at the same time; wherein, when one has been assumed, the other is necessarily removed—that is, when being is assumed, not-being is removed—thus: «Rhetoric is not both the science of speaking well and not useful; now it is the science of speaking well; therefore it is useful». It has been established that it is useful by removing what had been propounded, that it is not useful. These three moods can be constructed from a single argument, and all can prove one and the same thing. Nor does it matter in the proposition which part thou placest first, whether that of the argument or that of the question, provided the principle of the condition be preserved: when thou hast said «If rhetoric is the science of speaking well, it is useful», thou canst also propound thus: «Rhetoric is useful, if it is the science of speaking well». One may try this in the other two moods as well.

{{417}} The fourth mood is that which is produced through disjunction, in such a way that when one is assumed the other is necessarily denied, thus: «Either he is healthy or he is sick; now he is healthy; therefore he is not sick».

{{418}} The fifth mood is that which is also produced through disjunction, in such a way that when one is denied the other necessarily is, thus: «Either he is healthy or he is sick; now he is not healthy; therefore he is sick».

{{419}} Two other moods have been added which can be formed from the argument of these two, not however through disjunction, but through the negation of conjunctions. The sixth mood, therefore, is that in which it is shown that both this and that cannot exist together; it is assumed through the affirmation of the one, and concluded through the denial of the other: «He is not both healthy and sick; now he is healthy; therefore he is not sick». The seventh mood is propounded in the same way as the sixth, and in it the assumption is made as in the fifth, and thus it is concluded.

{{420}} To make the matter easier to understand, certain schematic forms are joined to these, so that the force of reasoning may be understood from the things themselves rather than from the words. The form of the first mood is this: «If the first, then the second; now the first; therefore the second». Of the second, this: «If not the first, then not the second; now the second; therefore the first also». Of the third, this: «Not both the first and not the second; now the first; therefore the second also». Of the fourth, this: «Either the first or the second; now the first; therefore not the second». Of the fifth: «Either the first or the second; now not the first; therefore the second». Of the sixth: «Not both the first and the second; now the first; therefore not the second». Of the seventh: «Not both the first and the second; now not the first; therefore the second».

{{421}} It must be known, however, that from one argument the three upper moods can be constructed, and from one argument the other four. The reason of this order is that the first mood is that which is called «from the antecedent», because certainly that which precedes is first; the second is «from the consequent», for the same reason that by its very name it seems to show that it ought to follow the first in order also. There remains what is third in the same argument, «from incompatibles». For now we can pass over to disjunction, which cannot be formed from the same argument. The fourth mood, therefore, will be as it were first in respect of another argument, and there certainly that mood ought to precede in which the assumption is made by affirmation; for affirmation is prior to negation. The fifth ought likewise to be through disjunction, yet after the fourth, for the reason that in the fourth the assumption is made through affirmation, in this through negation; and the same may be understood in the two remaining moods.

{{422}} Syllogisms, moreover, are combined with one another in many ways, so that in a single piece of reasoning one recognizes forms of both the predicative and the conditional kind, as follows: if the question be whether Dialectic herself is useful, it must be propounded: «If to dispute well is useful, the science of disputing well is useful; now to dispute well is useful; therefore Dialectic is useful». Assuredly one must observe in a predicative syllogism what the assumption claims for itself from the proposition; for it is manifest that it takes something from without. Now in that which, as we have said, it claims for itself from the proposition, one must attend to whether it claims it rightly on account of preserving the forms mentioned above. For it comes about that what seemed in the proposition to pertain to the predicate part is shown in the assumption to pertain to the subject; and likewise if thou assumest otherwise, this same pertains to the predicate, as follows: if thou propoundest thus, «Every art must be practiced by frequent exercise; now declamation is the exercise of rhetoric; therefore rhetoric must be practiced by frequent declamation». In the proposition, exercise seemed to pertain to the predicate part; in the assumption, that same exercise likewise pertains to the predicate. How, then, is the figure preserved? Evidently because something was left in the predicate of the proposition which did not appear in the predicate of the assumption, whence the conclusion could be formed—that is, «must be practiced». For if I had wished to assume thus, «Now rhetoric is an art», the whole would have been preserved for the conclusion: «Therefore rhetoric must be practiced by frequent exercise». From this it appears that there are many common terms which can be joined either to the subject or to the predicate according to the nature of the assumption.»

{{423}} As Dialectic was pressing on in such discourse and hastening toward matters no less inextricable than obscure, Pallas, at a nod from Maia’s son who was eager to press forward, intervened:

«Skilled in speech, curb now thy course, lest thy winding exposition weave twisted knots and Hymen refuse to endure these many-coiled intricacies too long. Whatsoever learned disputation hath gathered to be built up across many a volume hath been set forth in compendium; sufficient is the comely fountain of profound knowledge, which brings forth abstruse things while discoursing naught tediously, and passing swiftly leaves naught unknown behind. For as for what remains, sown thick with many a snare, wherewith false deception winds around its captives—whilst in a roundabout course thou contrivest sophisms, or sportest with entangling fallacies, or, deceitful, heapest up sorites little by little, or formest mendacious figures—which true reality reproves, it were an outrage that such things should chatter beneath the ears of the Thunderer, and a dreadful offense; for the supreme celestials hate every falsehood of a base woman. Indeed, if thou turnest it over, what is more cruel than quibbling? Having spoken at length, thou shalt assuredly become an unseemly mountebank if thou buildest snares. Away then with shifty subtlety, and leave the remaining time unto thy sisters.»

{{424}} Hearing these words, Dialectic hesitated a little, yet, ready to obey the goddess’s commands, replied:

«Venerable to me is thy decree, and following thy revered behests I shall draw back my step, and straightway my discourse shall fall silent—although it would have been fitting to grant me one indulgence, that at the price of honor I might bring in a rejoinder whereby Bromius might well understand how the pallium-clad folk of Cecrops’s sons know how to resent insults, and might learn to recognize a viperous nature better than he believed! Then that Marsian sorceress would long since have grown sluggish, nor would he behold me drowsy or drunken, babbling jests at random in the midst of Jove’s proceedings. Yet to thee alone, O Virgin, must service be rendered; we keep silence.» Speaking thus, as though checked, she fell silent; and several of the gods, who at the beginning had mocked her, shuddered.

## Liber V. De Rhetorica

{{425}} Within, trumpets sounded and a hoarse blast rang through the upper air, and the sky bellowed back with an unfamiliar clangor. Troubled, the gods grew afraid, and the throng of lesser celestials trembled; hearts ignorant of the causes faltered, and the crimes of ancient Phlegra were renewed. Then the Rivers and Fauns, Pales, Ephialtes, and the Napaeae looked back toward the chief gods, and, astonished to see them rise with no commotion and exchange words calmly in turn, marveled at the serene stillness across those sacred breasts. Then first Silvanus, having chanced to lay aside his cypress, roused and trembling stretched out his unarmed right hand, demanding the Delian’s bow and the weapons of Hercules. Sighing, he clamored for Portunus’s three-pronged spear; not daring to ask for Gradivus’s lance, accustomed as he was to rustic warfare, he searched for Saturn’s sickle, and distrustful of himself looked back toward the bolts of the Thunderer.

{{426}} Yet while the multitude of earthly gods was disturbed by such alarms, behold! a woman of loftiest stature and exceeding confidence, illustrious also in the comeliness of her countenance, entered the hall. Her head was helmed and crowned with royal majesty, and the weapons in her hands—wherewith she was wont either to defend herself or to wound her adversaries—glittered with a lightning-like flash. Her undergarment was covered in the Latian fashion by a peplum wrapped about her shoulders, which, variegated with the brilliance of all figures, displayed the schemas of every trope, while her breast was belted with gems of the choicest hues.

{{427}} When she clashed her arms in her advance, thou wouldst say shattered thunderbolts rattled like the crash of a lightning-charged cloud with bursting roars; indeed, it was believed that, like Jove, she too could hurl thunderbolts. For like a mighty queen of all things, she had power to drive men whithersoever she wished and draw them back whence she wished, to move them to tears and rouse them to frenzy, and to turn the faces and feelings of cities as well as warring armies—whatsoever throngs of peoples she could reach. She was said even to have tamed the senate, the rostra, and the courts among the race of Romulus, while at Athens she had swayed the assembly, the schools, and the theaters at will and stirred up all Greece to its very depths.

{{428}} When she spoke, what majesty of countenance, what resonance of voice, and what excellence and loftiness of speech were hers! It was worth the effort even for the gods above to hear the genius of such invention, the eloquence of such fertile abundance, the treasury of so capacious a memory and recollection! What order in arrangement, what harmonious modulation in delivery, what gesture in motion, what depth in conception! In fine, spare in slight matters, fluent in middling ones, and a kindler of flames in elevated themes, she rendered all men teachable in doubtful points, yielding in persuasion, discordant in collision, and haughty in praise. Yet when, roused, she cried out with the attestation of the public name, all things seemed to surge, mingle, and catch fire.

{{429}} This woman, therefore, of golden voice, pouring forth, as it were, gems of diadems and kingdoms, was followed by a vast train of illustrious men, among whom two closest to her shone preeminent in diverse attire and nation—the one wrapped in a Greek pallium, the other robed in the Roman trabea. Different was the ring of each man’s speech, although the latter also declared that he had learned Greek letters at Athens and was accounted ready in the studies of the schools and the ever-contentious disputations of the Academy. Both, however, were self-made men sprung from the lap of poverty; and though a Roman knight begot the one and a craftsman’s industry the other, so famously did they flourish in excellence of tongue that after the destinies of senates and undeserved deaths they ascended to the stars by virtue and surpassed the ages in immortality of glory.

{{430}} Concerning the one, however, whom the people of Athens and throngs clad in the pallium followed, this report had gathered: that he was at once the keenest of speakers and more violent than the tempests and roar of the indignant Ocean. Indeed, concerning him a verse of this sort was current: «A formidable man—soon would he accuse even the blameless.»

{{431}} The other, however, whom consular purple and the laurel of a crushed conspiracy wreathed, had scarce entered the senate of the gods and rejoiced to have come into the sight of Jove when he began to cry out somewhat joyfully: «O happy we! O fortunate commonwealth! O glorious praise of my consulship!»

{{432}} Behind these, nonetheless, in separate ranks marched veteran orators bearing before them the summit of distinctions and the merits of their tongues: thou mightest behold Aeschines, Isocrates, and Lysias, and then in the toga-clad ranks Sosantius, the Gracchi, Regulus, Pliny, and Fronto.

{{433}} Yet before them all, and before the very woman who led them all, went an old man bearing a standard and a herald’s wand after the fashion of a Roman lictor; and on the tip of that wand a raven (corax) of trained speech flew ahead as an omen of the approaching woman.

{{434}} Now he who bore the wand was called Tisias, and he seemed older and more exalted than all the rest; for looking back both at the others as his inferiors and at that leader herself, he declared the raven perched above to be their common pledge and daughter.

{{435}} Moved by this proof, very many of the gods believed her to be a most noble woman indeed, yet either a kinswoman of Apollo, if she were Greek, or, if Roman, of the house of Corvinus. To this riddle was added the fact that, fearless and of ready confidence, having kissed the breast both of Pallas and of the Cyllenian himself, she revealed signs of a close familiarity. At length, some of the gods above—hesitating amidst the alarm of the heralding trumpet and wonder at the friendship of the celestials—longed to question prophetic Phoebus, and before supreme Jove had yet inquired, tumultuously asked who she was.

{{436}} And then she, looking upon all the gods above, somewhat more stirred, began thus: «I call to witness greatest Father Jove and the rest of the celestials, whom I have often entreated in many causes, and this very assembly of a vanquished session, that nothing is deemed by me more inconsistent and unseemly than that I—who have always in the forum and in countless courts accused many and defended others, and striving with all my strength for the glory of the contest have won for myself heralds of deserved success from the fates of perilous trials—am now compelled before you, O gods above, whom to please seemed the very likeness and prize of immortality, unwillingly to rehearse the elementary admonitions of young schoolboys and the meager precepts of a hackneyed art. Nor is it poverty that imposes this upon us, since overflowing squadrons of followers are at hand; for apart from those who, stirring the hearts and senses of all, have shattered even the benches of the judges, I have others too who have written out in full the minutiae of precepts and commentaries on the inner principles of the art—while between both columns of my followers shines preeminent my own Tullius, who not only thundered in the forum, the senate, and the rostra with the majesty of his grandiloquent power, but also expounded the precepts of the art itself and consecrated very many books to the use of the ages.

{{437}} Since this is so, the modesty of veteran grandeur and the glory of a name celebrated as far as may be would decline to run through these first rudiments of beginners, did not that which ye command also contribute to the reward of immortality, and did it not kindle confidence of perpetuity to execute even elementary matters when Jove together with the gods above commands it. I shall gird myself, therefore, to run through these drier topics—less likely to please, indeed, than I am wont in public assemblies; although if the ears of Pallas and Arcadian reason grant the assent of benevolent majesty, not even now shall I displease you, greatest celestials.

{{438}} For I am Rhetoric herself, whom some have called an art (ars), others a virtue (virtus), and others a discipline (disciplina): an art, for this reason, because I am taught, although Plato opposes this name; a virtue, say those who have discovered that the science of speaking well resides in me; while those who are not ignorant that the inmost principle of speaking can be thoroughly learned and grasped confidently assert me to be a discipline.

{{439}} My office, moreover, is to speak in a manner suited to persuasion; my end is to persuade by speech that which has been proposed. These very words of my Cicero I cite in witness, whose examples also I shall employ throughout all the courses of my instruction as I proceed.

{{440}} My subject matter (materies), moreover, is twofold: where (ubi) and whence (unde) a speech arises—where, when I take up the members of the question itself; whence, when the matters discovered and the words are joined together.

{{441}} The question itself is either definite (finita) or indefinite (infinita). It is definite when it arises from a specific deed and designates a person, as in Cicero’s speech Pro Roscio it is asked whether Roscius killed his father. It is indefinite when it asks in general whether something ought to be sought, as in the Hortensius it is debated whether one ought to philosophize. In the former I contend frequently and unceasingly, and it hath pleased the Greeks to name it hypothesis (ὑπόθεσις). In the indefinite, however, which claims for itself the confidence of establishing a universal truth, I engage rather when I turn to leisure and philosophical disputations—although very often even that part which is called thesis hath frequently supplied me, panting loftily in forensic cases, with thonged javelins and spears of mighty force. Or does aught else come to mind in the Pro Scauro, when in an interposed disputation it is treated from what causes sudden death befalls, and similarly in the Pro Milone, whether the world is governed by providence? Finally, certain of my followers, moved by an exceedingly keen and subtle reason, declare that there is no question of hypothesis at all: for everything that is treated in defense of defendants and against them in accusations can be applied to general questions.

{{442}} Now that the parts of my office are five admits of no doubt: for there is invention (inventio), arrangement (dispositio), style (elocutio), memory (memoria), and delivery (pronuntiatio). Judgment (iudicatio), which is added by some, is ascribed to all the parts, and therefore cannot rightly be reckoned a separate part itself, although the management of judgment examines what must be said or passed over in silence. Invention is the sagacious and investigative grasping of questions and arguments. Arrangement is that which assigns order to the matters; style, that which seizes upon words either proper or metaphorical, and which coins new ones and combines old ones. Memory is the firm guardianship of things and words; delivery is the modulation of voice, movement, and gesture in accordance with the dignity of the things and words. Yet of these it is certain that invention is the most powerful, whose work is to sift out the questions of a case and find arguments suitable for proof.

{{443}} Of questions, moreover, there is a twofold species: for some are principal (principales), others incidental (incidentes). Principal are the issues (status) out of which the case arises, which Tullius calls constitutiones; incidental are those which arise while the case is being treated, when in refuting arguments or written documents the case is drawn out into several questions—as the principal question is whether Milo killed Clodius lawfully, and the incidental is which of the two laid an ambush for the other.

{{444}} The principal issues, therefore, are three in number: whether a thing is (an sit), what it is (quid sit), and of what sort it is (quale sit). «Whether it is» is conjecture (coniectura), as whether Roscius killed his father; «what it is» is definition (finis), as whether Cornelius diminished the majesty of the state; «of what sort it is» is quality (qualitas), as whether Saturninus was lawfully slain. Yet lest anyone perhaps be disturbed that I pass over the fourth issue which Hermagoras invented, a subtler reasoning holds it to be not an issue, but a subdivision of quality, as I shall show in my exposition thereof.

{{445}} Now we must explain how issues are discovered in a case. They are discovered, then, through accusation (intentio) and rebuttal (depulsio). Accusation is the charging of that deed which comes into court; rebuttal is a kind of denial resisting the accusation, as if it be said: «Thou hast killed thy father», «I did not kill him». Out of these two utterances clashing together arises the question which is called the issue (status), because there, as if for battle, the arrayed line of the pleadings takes its stand. Yet it is easy to see what answer is made to a charged crime; what is not easy is what ought to be charged—whether the deed, as «Thou hast killed a man», or the name of the deed, as «Thou hast committed homicide». And although they seem to be the same, nevertheless in the former charge, if it be denied, there will be conjecture when he says «I did not kill him»; whereas in this latter, where the name of the act is charged, definition too can appear, as if he says «I did not commit homicide»—for it will have to be asked whether homicide is committed whenever any man is slain, or only an innocent man. Because if thou chargest not the deed, but the name of the crime, it will not be clear from the opposing party’s denial what issue arises: for thou wouldst not know what he denies who denies thus, «I did not commit homicide»—whether because he slew no man at all, or because the slaying of a tyrant is not called homicide. That very thing which was done, therefore, must be charged, so that if thine adversary denies it thou hast conjecture, while if, granting the deed, he excludes the name of the crime, thou recognizest definition.

{{446}} If, however, having granted both the deed and the name of the deed, he says that it was lawful or necessary for him to do it, quality (qualitas) is at issue in the case—which joins battle either concerning the matter itself (de re) or concerning the legal action (de actione): concerning the matter, as whether it was lawful for Milo to do what he did; concerning the action, as whether it is lawful for a slave or a debt-bondsman to appeal to the tribunes, or whether it is lawful for a dishonored man to address a public assembly—which species of quality is named transference (translatio). Likewise we must beware lest we charge what belongs to the hearer’s office and quality alone become the issue. For he who judges a case has it as his purpose to effect something, that is, either to condemn someone or to acquit him.

{{447}} Of hearers, moreover, there are three kinds: one is he who decides something according to equity, and he is carefully the judge (iudex); another is he who, doubtful amidst uncertain honor or expediency, awaits in perplexity the persuasion of another’s opinion—the deliberator (deliberator); the third kind is he who weighs the honor or baseness of a deed by free judgment—him it is fitting to name the appraiser (aestimator). These, therefore, are the three kinds of causes which are contained under hypothesis, namely the judicial, the deliberative, and the demonstrative.

{{448}} Yet since hesitation regarding its own office divides all the hearers of the aforesaid matters, we must examine the distinction and peculiar property of each. For the judge, whose office is to condemn or acquit the defendant, or to grant or take away possession, hesitates before he has heard the case as to how he should exercise his office; and he who deliberates is pulled different ways by the doubtful opinion of his mind; and the arbiter of praise weighs with appraising reflection whether someone is rightly praised. Yet while these offices are drawn in different directions, we attend most of all to that distinction of how they vary in time. For deliberation contains a question of future time alone, as if Cato should deliberate whether he ought to slay himself lest he behold Caesar victorious. The judicial kind, on the other hand, is found at times both of past and of future time; yet in a past deed alone conjecture arises, while from the future quality frequently springs, as will be more readily shown in its proper place. The laudatory kind also consists wholly in past deeds, yet is distinguished from the judicial kind by its end: for it is one thing to acquit an innocent man at the command of equity, and another to attend a glorious man with praises in contemplation of his signal merits. Add to this that in the judicial kind the judge doubts concerning the affairs of others; in the deliberative each deliberates no less concerning his own affairs than concerning external ones; and in the laudatory the appraiser listens to judge whether true and fitting things are recounted—although modern flatteries have brought it about that he who is praised often becomes the arbiter of his own eulogy.

{{449}} Therefore we must look rather carefully lest we charge those things which belong to the hearer’s office, as if it should be declared to Verres that he ought to be condemned: for he will reply that he ought not to be condemned, and only quality will appear, namely whether he ought to be condemned. The accusation, therefore, will arise from charging the deed—as if thou sayest to the same man «Thou hast plundered Sicily», and when he answers «I did not do it», conjecture is established. For it is foolish to charge that which remains in the judge’s discretion, or certainly the name of the crime rather than the crime itself. We must beware equally lest, when any law or written document is treated in a case, the accusation be taken from the law, as if thou sayest «The law forbade thee, a foreigner, to mount the wall»; rather must it be said, «Thou didst mount the wall», so that if he denies it, the contest may be not about the law, but about the deed, to which the law is subjoined—and if he confesses the deed, then he may be seen to have acted against the law. Otherwise, if, when the deed is denied, the law or any written document is read aloud, the dispute will appear superfluous, since the altercation contains a question not of law, but of fact. By this fault very many have been led astray.

{{450}} And besides those three which I have asserted to be issues, some have said there are four or five others flowing from the quality of a written document. Although these hold their own places in the division of a case, they never arise in the principal position, because unless there is something to be proved, no one reads aloud a law or any form of writing for proof. That, however, which is proved is the issue, which always arises from the first clash; the remaining questions must be accounted incidental controversies.

{{451}} Thus, therefore, the method of discovering the issue will be easy when we remember that neither the name of the offense, nor the office of the hearer, nor any law or written text is to be charged in place of the accusation. Let the accusation, then, be such as «Thou hast killed a man», and the rebuttal «I did not kill him»: from these arises the question whether he killed him, in which the issue will be conjecture. Yet when the defendant opposes the rightfulness of the deed to the accusation, quality arises—as Milo does not deny the deed, but the dispute is concerning the rightfulness of the deed: for he confessed that he had killed Clodius, yet maintained that he had done so lawfully. To this rebuttal is subjoined a supporting reason (ratio), which is not single or simple: for one and the same deed can appear justifiable on account of various causes—as in this very case, one reason was that Milo had slain a waylayer, and another was his declaration that he had killed a public enemy inflamed with lust for tyranny. In these assertions quality indeed arises, yet different species of it break forth: for the first shines forth as relative quality, the second as comparative. If, however, not the rightfulness of the deed, but the denial of its name is opposed to the accusation—although denial is spoken in place of rebuttal—nevertheless it will not make conjecture, because not the deed, but the name of the deed is overthrown, as in this case: an action for adultery is brought; a husband who had divorced his wife finds her seducer and accuses him of adultery. The defendant speaks against him and does not deny the deed, yet excludes the name of adultery and asks what adultery is—which belongs to a definitive controversy.

{{452}} Conjecture, therefore, is the denial of the deed itself; definition is the denial not of the act committed, but of its name—and for that reason also a rebuttal of an impending charge, because when another name is imposed upon the deed, the danger threatening from the charged crime is warded off, as in this charge: «Thou hast stolen temple goblets from the priest’s house; thou hast committed sacrilege.» He rebuts: «I did indeed carry off the goblets; yet because it was from a house, not from a temple, it must be called theft, not sacrilege.» Herein there also seems to be a kind of denial of the deed: for this offense looks not to an injury against the gods, but to a loss suffered by the priest, since the goblets were taken from a house. Here the deed must be treated by definition on both sides—that is, both what sacrilege is and what theft is.

{{453}} Now we must speak of quality, whose manifold nature first requires a division of its parts, so that, having run through them, the peculiar character of each may be unfolded. Quality, then, is either concerning the matter (de re) or concerning the legal action (de actione): concerning the matter, when the reason or cause of that deed which comes into judgment is treated, or when it is debated what ought to be done, as whether Milo slew Clodius lawfully, or whether Tullius’s house ought to be restored to him; concerning the action, however, when it is asked whether the action ought to be admitted and a trial granted. And since this part is weighed by the equity of law, we rightly and more subtly group it among the species of quality—otherwise than Hermagoras, who preferred to distinguish it as a new issue apart from quality and call it transference (translatio) or demurrer (praescriptio).

{{454}} Yet of this later. Quality, just as it is judged of future time, is so too of past time: quality of the past is juridical (iuridicalis), of the future pragmatic (negotialis); in both there is an assertion of right. In the juridical, assertions of natural right are at issue; in the pragmatic, assertions of statute or custom. The juridical, moreover, is divided into absolute and assumptive: absolute is that which defends the deed itself by its own nature and right; assumptive is that which, finding nothing justifiable in the deed itself, takes refuge in the motive itself and alleges it to be just—as when Milo could not say that it was lawful in itself to slay a man, he built up the Clodian ambushes as the cause of the slaying. Thence it received the name assumptive, because, being unable to defend the deed absolutely, it takes refuge in external causes.

{{455}} This assumptive quality, therefore, has four parts: retort of blame (relatio), shifting of blame (remotio), comparison (comparatio), and confession (concessio). Retort is when the confession of the deed transfers the guilt onto him who suffered it—as Milo transferred it onto Clodius, Orestes onto his mother, and Horatius onto his sister, whom he declared he had slain for this reason, that provoked by her wrong he was driven to the crime.

{{456}} Shifting of blame (remotio) is when the charged crime is shifted by him who is impeached onto another person or onto another thing: onto another person, as Tiberius Gracchus shifted blame onto Mancinus, who was the author of making the treaty which both the senate and the people had condemned; likewise in such a case as: «Let an envoy who has not set out within thirty days be punished with death; the quaestor did not furnish travel funds to the envoy; the time elapsed.» The accused speaks in defense and shifts the charged crime onto the quaestor. Onto another thing, however, he shifts it if, delayed by illness, he shifts the cause of his delay onto his misfortune. Either the deed itself is shifted, if it be said to have belonged to another’s power, or the cause, if something be said to have happened through another’s fault.

{{457}} Comparison (comparatio)—which I know has been called compensation by later writers—is not when a good deed of another time is substituted for a crime, but when the deed is defended by right on the ground that some advantage followed from it. For if a merit of another time is mentioned as if for the sake of compensation, it must rather be referred to deprecative quality, which is contained under the species of plea for pardon (venialis). This comparative quality, however, contends that the act was done rightly, as that famous reply of Verres: «I sold the tithes», said he, «at a high price»—compensating the deed by the utility of the benefit. For in this part there is a confession of the deed, yet the allegation of public service excludes the enormity of the charge; whereas in the plea for pardon there is a single confession of both crime and deed, yet either it is separated from evil intent or, when excuse fails, it breathes toward the pitiable plea of humility.

{{458}} Now this quality of plea for pardon (venialis) is divided into two assertions: exculpation (purgatio) and supplication (deprecatio). For exculpation is when, having confessed the misdeed, we clear our intention and will; of which there is no doubt that there are three modes, namely unawareness (imprudentia), accident (casus), and necessity (necessitas). Unawareness is when we remove something from our intention and knowledge, as one who in hunting hurled a javelin at a wild beast and slew a man hiding behind the nets: this part is styled error, and under it are placed ignorance, intoxication, forgetfulness, folly, madness, and the rest which excuse the error of one who commits an offense. Accident, however, clears the guilt of an event, as one who failed to arrive with victims on the appointed sacred day because of the swelling of a river. And he committed a crime by necessity who slew an innocent man at the command of his general. Between these modes there is this difference: that unawareness deceives, accident prevents, and necessity compels. Supplication (deprecatio), on the other hand, joins no excuse to itself, but sighs into the humility of entreaty and prayers.

{{459}} Let those who have learned such things mark the remaining questions, which they call incidental issues, as I shall show further on. For it does not always happen that the utterance of the first speaker shapes the ground of the case; very often the second speaker strikes the blow of the accusation, as in this case: «Let a brave man have whatever reward he wishes; he who acted bravely asks as his reward marriage with another man’s wife.» Herein he who asks the reward, thinking there is no opposition, makes no accusation, whereas the opposing husband makes the accusation, although he seems to rise in the second place; and against him the brave man will stand by way of rebuttal, as against one accusing the claim to the reward which he had demanded, contending that the reward is just, just as if he were defending some deed by right.

{{460}} Likewise the legal questions, which I have mentioned as occurring incidentally in cases like issues, are not surveyed by the same rule, since they break forth in the second clash; for the first is assigned to quality. Therefore that rule of accusation and rebuttal is disturbed in the aforesaid part of quality.

{{461}} Now I shall take up in consequence what the Greeks call to krinomenon (τὸ κρινόμενον, the point for judgment). For when accusation and rebuttal have marked out the issue and constitution, if it be conjecture, it will at the same time furnish the ground for the judgment; for there is nothing that can be weighed by the court save the denial itself. In quality or definition, however, the point appears in another place than the issue: it is necessary that the rebuttal of a confessed deed have a supporting reason (ratio), which the accuser in turn invalidates; and a kind of secondary question is begotten through the supporting reason and the refutation of that reason. Here we see that the point to be judged can turn, and that this is what the appraiser of justice must weigh by reason. In any case whatsoever all the issues can exist, as well as many supporting reasons and numerous refutations, and accordingly abundant seeds of points for judgment—when, namely, a single deed is defended on manifold grounds, as Tullius defended Milo both because he had slain a waylayer and because he had slain an enemy of the public peace. Among these, therefore, that issue will appear foremost which the orator has adopted for the advantage of the defense. That supporting reason also is for the most part gathered from the orator’s construction; and then, as it were, other rational issues emerge.

{{462}} And if a written document is brought forward for proof, thence will arise legal judgment, of which there are five species. One is when one or more ambiguities are contained in the writing, which is called by the Greeks amphibolia (ἀμφιβολία), as a matter is very often confused through a shared name, such as this: a certain man bequeathed «Taurus», by which name he had owned an exceedingly valuable slave; yet the heir gave the legatee a bull (taurum, that is, an ox), because the ambiguous name created a dispute. Likewise from the division of names by syllable, as one who had two kinsmen, the one named Lesius and the other Milesius, and in appointing his heir spoke thus: HERES ESTOMILESIUS; from which ambiguity of division a contest arose, while he who was called Lesius divided it as «Be my heir, Lesius» (heres esto mi, Lesius), whereas the other, by unbroken continuity of the name, declared Milesius to be the heir.

{{463}} There are likewise modes which dialectical construction embraces. Another species of written question is that which emerges from the letter and intent (verba et sententia) of a writing, as in this: «Let no foreigner mount the wall. When the city was besieged, a certain foreigner mounted it and hurled down the enemy; he is accused.» Here the defendant relies on the intent of the law, the accuser on its letter.

{{464}} The third kind of question is when contrary laws clash from either side, as in that case: «Let no male enter the temple of Ceres. Likewise, let him who hath not brought aid to his parents be punished. In the temple of Ceres a son entered and brought aid to his mother while she was being beaten; he is accused.» Here the conflict is created by the opposition of laws whose interpretation collides, so that it may appear which of the two ought rather to have been obeyed.

{{465}} The fourth question concerning writing is that which is called syllogism (ratiocinatio), when from that which is written we gather by argumentation something else as well which is not written, as in this: «Let it be lawful to kill an exile caught within the borders. A certain man scourged an exile whom he found; he is accused. It is argued by the defender that what is less than what is permitted was lawful to do.» Herein reside four modes: from the similar, from the consequent, from the greater to the less, and from the contrary. From the similar thus: that because the murderer of a father is sewn up in a leather sack, this same punishment ought to await a matricide. From the consequent thus: he who persuaded a tyrant to lay down his tyranny claims the tyrannicide’s reward; he infers that his merit is equal because he restored liberty. From the greater to the less: «If it is permitted to slay a captured exile, it is also lawful to scourge him.» From the contrary: «If a brave man deserves a reward, a deserter is worthy of punishment.»

{{466}} There remains the definitive question, flowing from the ambiguity of a written text, when some word in a law or a will is doubtful and is made clear by definition, as: «Let it be lawful to kill one caught at night with a weapon (telum). A magistrate killed a certain man caught at night with a cudgel; he is charged with murder. He defends himself by the law, yet it is inquired what a weapon (telum) is.» This kind of question differs from the principal issues in this: that the inquiry is not concerning the deed out of which the accusation arose, but solely concerning the definition of the written word. It is clear, therefore, that the principal issues are distinct from these legal questions, and that these latter are called incidental, while the former, from which the case arises, are more rightly named constitutions or issues.

{{467}} Now, having run through the constitutions, let us examine the controversies of the deliberative and demonstrative kinds. For there is no doubt both that there are three kinds of causes and that an issue ought to be present in every cause, although in the deliberative kind some have judged that pragmatic quality (qualitas negotialis) alone resides, because it deliberates concerning the future. Yet quality must for the most part be reckoned so in such wise that it is not denied that the other issues also can occur in this kind. Now we affirm that the issues can be found in such cases in such a manner that we do not hold to the rule of accusation and rebuttal in the way shown in judicial conflicts. For what, or who, will make the accusation so that the advocate and the opponent may clash in the customary order, when we are not forbidden to choose either side in our constructions? Nevertheless, the opponent (dissuasor) seems to seize the part of the accuser: for he who shows that what he opposes is dishonorable or inexpedient certainly seems to accuse the measure itself; while the advocate (persuasor) seizes the part of the defender and clears the handling of the business from any objection of opposition. Whence it must be recorded that the opponent makes the accusation and the advocate makes the rebuttal, and thus when a clash of sides has been made, the issue will be able to appear, as in such a case: «When his army repeatedly flees back to the walls, a general deliberates whether to demolish the walls.» First the opponent denies that this is honorable: when he says that the walls ought not to be razed, does he not seem to accuse the act if the general should do it? Next he says that the army will endure this with resentment and mutiny, wherein conjecture will arise concerning the outcome of the commotion. And presently, if he says it must not be called a victory if the defense of the city is destroyed, definition has entered; for it must be defined what condition of the ramparts makes a victory worthy of celebration. Then if the opponent adds that this ought not to be done without the counsel of the senate, demurrer (praescriptio) also seems to enter. It is certain, therefore, both that accusation is rightly assigned to the opponent and that in deliberative causes all the issues readily appear. We must take care, however, lest the opponent, as though pronouncing sentence, forbid the measure like a judge, when his office is to prevent persuasion by showing the reason why the thing is dishonorable or inexpedient.

{{468}} Now how an issue emerges in the demonstrative kind is not easy to set forth, for the reason that censure does not immediately follow upon praise—so that whosoever is not praiseworthy is accounted blameworthy, or conversely, he who is free from censure is exalted with praises, as if someone were said to deserve praise on the ground that he has not committed homicide. There is, therefore, a middle term which it hath pleased men to call privation, because he who is deprived of praise is not straightway abandoned to censure, or conversely. Nevertheless, the issue of the demonstrative kind appears in this manner: when thou settest up a eulogist and a censurer, and joinest the part of the accuser’s charge to the censurer and the part of the defender to the eulogist. Or this is shaped more subtly: that the hearer, poised between praise and censure, is placed as it were in the position of an adversary. For thou must not praise anyone before thou hast shown him to be praiseworthy; for the hearer’s stance is such that he does not yet believe him praiseworthy, and against this stance the eulogist or censurer has his contest—although in the former construction a clear conflict is proved, as when one praises someone and another accuses him, as Tullius praising Cato and Caesar accusing him in two volumes. From which it is gathered that all kinds of causes are surveyed through the issues.

{{469}} When this has been discovered, thou shalt examine the nature of the case, which is contained in either a simple, a double, or a manifold question. It is simple when one single thing is inquired into throughout the whole pleading, as whether Milo slew Clodius lawfully; whereas that which is inquired into by way of judicial point in a secondary place—which of the two laid an ambush for the other—is not single, but joined out of a double conjecture, which the Greeks name antikategoria (ἀντικατηγορία); yet an incidental question cannot determine the kind of a case. A double case arises sometimes from the matters themselves, as in the Pro Caelio concerning the gold and concerning the poison, and sometimes by comparison, as in the Pro Roscio, whether his son or his enemies slew the father; while a manifold case consists of several questions, as all the Verrine speeches concerning extortion, and in the Pro Scauro inquiry is made into three matters: the murder of Bostar, the wife of Aris, and the tithes.

{{470}} Thereafter the line of conduct (ductus) of the case must be inspected. Now ductus is the tenor of pleading preserved throughout the entire case under some figure. There are five lines of conduct: simple, subtle, figured, oblique, and mixed. Simple is when there is not one thing in the pleader’s design and another in his words, as when thou praisest one who has deserved well and accusest a guilty man. Subtle is when the mind intends one thing and the speech pursues another, as: «A certain man disinherits his son because he has no friends»—here he does not truly disinherit him, but frightens him so that he may acquire friends. Figured is when modesty forbids saying something openly because of indecency, and it is shown by another signification and clothed in coverings. Oblique is when fear prevents speaking something freely, and through certain underground tunnels of speech we point out what must be charged, as in this case: «A tyrant who had laid down his tyranny under an amnesty acted bravely; he asks as a reward the custody of the arms and the citadel; the magistrates speak against him.» Mixed is compounded out of both, when both shame and fear impede freedom of speech, as: «A tyrant who had two sons, one of whom had a wife with whom the father was infamous and whose husband hanged himself, compels his other son to marry her; the son speaks against it»—here he can openly charge neither incest nor tyranny.

{{471}} These lines of conduct must be handled with art and subtly diffused throughout the entire speech. They are separated from a coloring (color) by this: that a coloring is preserved in a single part only, whereas the ductus is maintained throughout the whole case.

{{472}} The ductus is discovered from the generating cause of the dispute (causativum litis), that is, from the matter which creates the controversy—which either belongs to past time, as whether Ulysses killed Ajax (which holds to the simple ductus), or, if it be of present or future time, will admit all lines of conduct. Therefore the ductus arises from the speaker’s design (consilium), and the design springs from the generating cause of the dispute. The generating cause is that which creates the doubt, as in that case of the tyrant the generating cause of the dispute is that the tyrant demands custody of both the citadel and the arms. The First Philippic bears witness to a sustained ductus, for with wondrous subtlety it covertly indicts the tyranny of Antony, so that while saying everything it appears to have said nothing harshly.

{{473}} When all these have been gathered, we must examine the arguments whereby credibility may be brought to bear upon the doubtful point of the question. Now credibility (fides) is produced in three ways: by conciliating (conciliando), by instructing (docendo), and by moving the emotions (permovendo). The first is named ethical, the second apodictic, and the third pathetic. Although it is fitting to use conciliation throughout the entire case, we must nevertheless press it more abundantly in the introductions, while the epilogue ought to flourish most of all in moving the emotions. Instruction, on the other hand, belongs before all else to the statement of facts (narratio), although constructive proof operates in no dissimilar way in refuting questions and charging crimes. Now I shall begin concerning arguments.

{{474}} An argument is a reasoning which lends credibility to a doubtful matter. The doubtful matter is the accusation and rebuttal, or the supporting reason and the refutation of that reason. For when thou hast charged «Thou hast killed him», in order to instruct the judges thou shalt confirm it by argumentation, especially when it is denied. Even «I did not kill him» itself requires argument, although to produce credibility those things also which are called inartificial ought to be employed, such as documents, testimonies, and examinations under torture, which I shall reserve to be discussed afterward. Now let those arguments be treated which are either situated within the matter under discussion or touch upon it. Within the matter itself lie now the whole, now a part of it, now the mark (nota), which the Greeks call etymology; while those touch upon the matter which are drawn to it by the principle of relatives. And these are thirteen in number: from conjugates, from genus, from form or species, from the similar, from the different, from the contrary, from adjuncts, from antecedents, from consequents, from incompatibles, from causes, from effects, and from comparison, whose parts are the greater, the less, and the equal. A relative principle is apparent in all of them: for one conjugate is named in relation to another, and genus and species are referred to one another; even the similar is similar to something, and all the topics (loci) of arguments receive their name not from themselves, but from another.
