{{475}} First, therefore, the whole which is debated as doubtful will have to be defined, and arguments thus handled in this wise: let the doubtful matter be whether eloquence seems useful. Eloquence is a whole; in respect of the whole, therefore, it must be defined thus: «Eloquence is the science of speaking well. Now to speak well is useful; therefore eloquence is useful.» To treat this topic, Dialectic, whom ye lately heard, is an aid—through whom I suppose it is known what genus is, what species or difference, property, accident, and the rest which her precepts contained. Nevertheless, I shall run through these briefly and summarily as I am able.

{{476}} Genus, therefore, is a notion pertaining to many species and differences, as animal, which is referred to man, beast, bird, fish, and the rest—which are separated not only in number, but also in species, because some are aerial, others aquatic and terrestrial, some rational, others devoid of reason; and when these have been gathered under one name, they are called by the name of the genus.

{{477}} Species is that which, depending upon a genus, contains other things separated in number alone: as man contains both Demosthenes and Cicero, who have one species, yet differ in number.

{{478}} Difference is a distinction sufficient for the question: as, if it be asked what difference there is between a man and a lion, it is answered that man is gentle and a lion is wild—which indeed distinguishes neither man from other gentle animals nor the lion from other wild beasts.

{{479}} Property is that which distinguishes some thing from fellowship with all others, as laughter; for this is not common to man with the rest.

{{480}} Accident is that which, placed in something, is neither a part of it nor can be separated from it so as to be able to exist by itself, as color in a body, or a branch of learning in the mind.

{{481}} An argument from a part, however, is uttered not to prove the whole, but another part which contains the question. For a part proved does not prove the whole—as, for example, if the eye sees, the whole body does not therefore see, since it is refuted that the whole body sees; yet that is a dialectical treatise. Now I shall show how an argument is taken from part to part: «If we ought to protect our feet and our arms, certainly we ought to guard our eyes more diligently.» Into the place of this argument another can sometimes also enter, which is called from the less to the greater; yet the principle of the principal argument is not thereby disturbed, because nature permits certain arguments to be doubled. This is wont to happen often in figures as well, of which we shall have to speak afterward.

{{482}} Sometimes an argument from parts is also made in this wise: when, a partition having been introduced and several parts propounded, after the rest have been refuted we conclude one remaining part, provided the question has been so constituted—as if thou sayest: «This horse which thou hast, thou either boughtest, or receivedst as a gift, or it was born at thy home [none of which is true]: therefore thou stolest it.»

{{483}} From the mark (a nota) or etymology, as the Greeks say, we take an argument thus: «If a consul is one who consults for the commonwealth (qui consulit rei publicae), what else did Tullius do when he inflicted punishment upon the conspirators?» In this topic one ought to attend solely to the origin of the word.

{{484}} From those topics, however, which seem to touch upon the matter, arguments are drawn in this wise: first from conjugates (a coniugatis), when, a principal noun having been set down, through its derivation—with case or tense changed—we prove something, as: «If piety (pietas) is a virtue, what has been done piously (pie) ought to be praised»—for it is agreed that virtue is praiseworthy. This topic differs from the preceding in that it is one thing to search out whence a name was imposed, and another to derive the force of an argument through the kinship of a word inflected from another.

{{485}} From genus, moreover, when what holds good in the whole is also brought down to the species: as, if woman is something fickle and changeable, Dido too appears fickle and changeable and capable of turning from love into hatred. By this principle that saying of Cicero is constructed: «For while ye ought to have careful regard for all provinces and allies, ye ought especially to do so for Sicily, O judges.» This topic seems to imitate that from the whole, yet there is this difference: that in the former, arguments are drawn from definition, whereas in this they are drawn from the very principle whereby it is a genus; and that in the genus is a whole which perishes by division, whereas the genus itself remains even when distributed into its parts.

{{486}} From species or form an argument is drawn so as to lend credibility to a general question, as Cicero in the Philippics: «Is there anything that can so properly be called the act of one who has held office in the commonwealth in peaceful garb with power and command, as a law?» For act is the genus, which is proved from the species—that is, a law carried by Caesar; and this he likewise confirms from similar instances: «Ask for the acts of Gracchus: the Sempronian laws will be brought forward. Ask for Sulla’s: the Cornelian.»

{{487}} From the similar by itself: «As Helen was to the Trojans, so hast thou been the seed of war to thy fellow citizens.» Likewise: «As often men sick with a grave disease, when they are tossed with burning fever…» and the rest. Contrary to this is dissimilarity, which is named difference by Cicero, which shows things to be diverse from one another, not opposed—of which Cicero sets down an example against Verres: «Yet thou shalt be judged to have done the same thing, if thou didst it with the same design.» This is shown both in persons and in things, in time, in places, and in other respects which it would now take very long to recount.

{{488}} From contraries, as life to death, from which Terence speaks thus: «For if thou chidest him who brought aid to life, what wilt thou do to him who hath inflicted loss or harm?» And Cicero: «If he was a consul, the legions which deserted a consul deserved cudgeling to death.»

{{489}} From adjuncts (a coniunctis), moreover, credibility is sought when things which are weak taken singly assume the force of truth when joined together, as: «What if it is added that thou wast previously poor? What if greedy? What if bold? What if an enemy of him who was slain?» Because these do not suffice singly, they are therefore set down gathered together.

{{490}} From antecedents, Cicero: «When he did not hesitate to reveal what he intended, can ye doubt what he did?» For the declaration preceded, wherein lies the argument, and the deed followed, whence arises the question.

{{491}} From consequents, conversely, so that the question lies in the antecedents and the argument in what follows—that if this followed, that preceded, as: «If she hath borne a child, she hath lain with a man.» An example from the Verrines: «If the Kalends of January bring an end to a praetor’s edict, why does not its beginning also take its rise from the Kalends of January?»

{{492}} An argument from incompatibles (a repugnantibus), when it is shown that two things cannot cohere together—for instance, that someone is both a parasite and not ridiculous, which by negation are predicated as unable to exist together in this wise: «Gnatho is not both a parasite and not ridiculous.» An example of this topic lies rather in the matter itself than in the form of words: «He, therefore, who had not only been freed from peril by thee, but even adorned with the highest honor, is accused of having wished to murder thee in his own house.» And in the first Pro Cornelio he says it is incompatible that he should wish to crush under the law of bribery those distributing agents whom he had wished to be the ministers of his own preferment.

{{493}} The topic of causes extends widely and is treated with much disputation; yet for now it will suffice to show its form under this example: «Yet since money was given on account of thy decrees, thy judgments, and thy commands, we must inquire not by whose hand it was counted out, but by whose injustice it was extorted.» So too Virgil: «Is it from me thou fleest?»

{{494}} From effects an argument is made when there is doubt in a case, as fate is proved from the fact that men were preserved alive even against their will: for fate is the cause either of life or of death, while those things—being able to live or to die—are the effects of fate. Hence Cicero proves that the life of Aulus Hirtius is dear to the people, because the people applaud him. Such is Virgil’s «Fear proves degenerate souls»: for fear is the cause that the soul is degenerate, which is the effect of fear.

{{495}} From comparison of the greater: «Who can doubt that Verres demanded money from the Sicilians, when he demanded it from Marcus Octavius Ligus?» Virgil: «Thou canst arm concordant brothers for battle»—from which he certainly proves that she can arm strangers too, which is less. Terence: «For he who hath begun or dared to lie to or deceive his father, all the more will he dare to deceive the rest.»

{{496}} From comparison of the less, as: «Publius Scipio, Pontifex Maximus, though a private citizen, slew Tiberius Gracchus when he was only moderately shaking the stability of the commonwealth»; and then he joins the question, where there is something greater: «Shall we, the consuls, endure Catiline, who longs to lay waste the whole world with slaughter and conflagrations?» From this topic Terence has that line: «He, on account of a slight acquaintance, bears this woman’s death as intimately as if she were his kin.»

{{497}} From comparison of equals, Cicero: «And if those days on which we are preserved are no less joyful and illustrious than those on which we are born…» And in the In Pisonem, that it makes no difference «whether the consul himself harasses the commonwealth with wicked harangues and pernicious laws, or suffers others to harass it.»

{{498}} These having been briefly shown, those proofs which are not devised by the orator, but are supplied by the case or by the litigants, reside, as we have said, in three things: in writing (scriptura), as documents; in authority (auctoritas), as witnesses; and in compulsion (necessitas), as torture.

{{499}} From writing an argument is sought when, for the proof of a doubtful matter, either a handwritten bond, or a will, or tablets of an agreement, and the like are read aloud—which are so well known that they seek no examples from a speech.

{{500}} From authority, however, as that Africanus said Tiberius Gracchus had been lawfully slain, or when testimony whereby the truth is laid bare is brought forward, as «He introduces Gnaeus Pompeius to us as a witness concerning the swift dispatch of the grain»: authority, therefore, belongs either to one who judges or to a witness. To this part are joined oracles and the rest of that kind.

{{501}} Compulsion, moreover, lends credibility from torture, or sleep, or madness, or drunkenness, which wrings the utterance of some fact from unwilling lips. All of which, when thou addest conjecture regarding the motive, person, and deed—that is, the testimony itself, or confession, or writing—gain or lose credibility: for both the motive of the speaker is attended to, and his character is examined, and the quality of the writing itself is compared with itself or with the case.

{{502}} It follows that the pleader should bend his attention to those things which conciliate or deeply move the hearers, since we have already declared that these too pertain to producing credibility. Minds are conciliated, therefore, both by the dignity of the person and by that of the matter: of the person—either of the hearer, or of the defendant, or of the orator himself, or of the adversary. Of the hearer thus: «That such as thou didst previously show thyself to the Roman people when thou didst preside as judge over this same court, such thou mayest impart thyself to us and to the Roman people at this time.» Of the defendant, as in the Pro Deiotaro: «Whom I was previously wont to honor together with the entire senate for his perpetual services to our commonwealth.»

{{503}} Of the orator, however, when he speaks of himself not haughtily, but modestly, of which sort is that passage: «Since I had been quaestor in Sicily, O judges, and had so departed from that province as to leave to all the Sicilians a pleasant and lasting memory of my quaestorship and my name, it came to pass that, while they deemed the highest protection for their fortunes to rest in their many ancient patrons, they thought no small defense lay in me as well.» Yet I shall expound these matters more fully in the precepts for introductions. From the person of the adversary, on the other hand, conciliation arises when, by showing his unfairness or arrogance, our own modesty is commended. Yet of this later under introductions, since we are now enumerating not the parts of a speech, but the forms of speaking and the species of producing credibility—in which place we also attend to the power of pathetic utterance, whose force is contained chiefly either in commonplaces or in epilogues. My early beginnings among the ancients, indeed, lacked this stirring of emotions, as will be shown when we treat the precepts of opening a speech; meanwhile, in general terms, I shall indicate with no division of parts by what emotions minds are roused.

{{504}} Hearers, therefore, are moved either by pity, or by hatred, or by envy, or by fear, or by hope, or by anger, and the like. By pity, when we treat someone’s calamities with great sorrow, or when we recount the cruelty of the time or the greatness of the peril, as in the seventh Verrine: «These fathers whom ye see, O judges, lay upon the outer threshold, and wretched mothers passed the night before the prison door, shut out from the last sight of their children—who prayed for nothing else save that it might be lawful to catch their sons’ last breath with their own lips.» From hatred, when the adversary’s deed is shown to good men or to judges to be accursed, as when Verres is shown to boast of the bribery of judges; likewise, «When in avarice, crime, and perjury he judges you to be like himself.» Envy is that which taints hearers with a kind of spite, as is: «Because to thine own friendship and that of other great and noble men, this fellow’s wickedness and audacity have easier access than the virtue and integrity of any one of us»; likewise, «That this man, having snatched everything from every quarter, sporting with impunity, overflows and abounds in wealth.» That too can inflame the minds of hearers, if thou exaggeratest someone’s tyrannical spirit or intolerable power.

{{505}} Fear, moreover, thou shalt arouse either by personal or by common dangers: personal, as «This is a trial in which ye shall judge concerning the defendant, and the Roman people shall judge concerning you»; common, as is «Methinks I behold this city, the light of the whole world and the citadel of all nations, collapsing suddenly in a single conflagration.» By hope also minds are stirred, when benefits or services are promised, as when he pledges Milo’s loyalty to Pompey, and «Ye shall have Caelius’s devotion bound to you and to your children throughout his whole life.» Anger also violently agitates minds, as when Tullius amplifies and cries out that Catiline’s accomplices are sitting in the senate-house: «O immortal gods! Where in the world are we? What commonwealth do we have? In what city do we live? Here, here in our very midst, conscript fathers, are they!» Other similar emotions are mingled in, which, although they avail most powerfully for persuasion, are nevertheless outside the strict matter of the case, nor ought they to appear too openly in the orator, lest he seem to ensnare the judge by ambush rather than lead him by reason.

{{506}} To these things, then, prudently discovered for producing credibility, must be joined the ordering of matters, which part is called arrangement (dispositio), whereby we prudently inspect what is to be said and in what place, what is to be omitted altogether, and also how, when, and where. Twofold, therefore, is the principle of this part: for either the order is natural, or it is fashioned by the artifice of the orator. Natural, when after the introduction follow the statement of facts, the partition, the proposition, the argumentation, the conclusion, and the epilogue; by the artifice of the orator, when we distribute what must be said across the members of the speech—and this we adapt according to the advantage of the case, not according to the sequence of time, as was done in the Pro Milone, when he introduced certain questions before the statement of facts in order to refute prior prejudices, which he altered not from the order of nature, but from the advantage of the case; and in the Pro Cornelio first were refuted the charges that followed his tribunate, and afterward return was made to the tribunate itself—which is named, as we have said, artificial arrangement.

{{507}} In the In Verrem, on the other hand, he kept to the natural order of time, so that he charged him first with his quaestorship, then his legateship, and thereafter his two praetorships, preserving the chronological sequence—which, unless the advantage of the case opposes it, we shall necessarily follow. Yet where the greatest and most invidious charges must first be cleared away, the beginning of the refutation is taken from these: as in the Pro Cluentio, having dispatched the parts of conjecture, he proceeded to the legal demurrer—having obviously altered the order lest, if he defended Cluentius by asserting the letter of the law, he should seem to flee the merits of the case out of distrust.

{{508}} Now that these two parts of my office have been completed, the care of style (elocutio) must be imparted. And since this consists in attention to individual words, it is distinguished from the name of eloquence in this: that eloquence is the excellence of the entire oratorical work, whereas style is accounted a single part of the office—of which Cicero says there are two foundations, as it were, and two summits: the foundations are to speak pure Latin and to speak clearly, the first of which ye learned while Grammar was speaking, when her subtlety was imparted to you; the summits, however, are to speak ornately and copiously, which is a matter not of native talent alone, but of the greatest toil and also of long-continued practice, whereby not only a richer, but also a more brilliant faculty is acquired.

{{509}} Twofold is the principle of this matter: the one, whereby brilliance appears in individual words; the other, whereby the dignity of utterance is preserved in the grace of their combination itself. In individual words either a proper word or a metaphorical and borrowed one is sought out. Proper words are especially archaic ones; for when early leaders either knew not these ornaments of speech or dared not strive after them, they used proper words. Yet because the currency of archaic words has now grown obsolete, those words which have changed with the age must not be too boldly usurped. Passing over, therefore, alucinari (to rave), cerritum (crazed), caperratum (wrinkled), and the like, we shall use those which custom admits—yet not mean or vulgar ones, unless the force of the matter and thought demands it, as when Cicero, wishing to arouse indignation at cruelty, says «they left them with their windpipes cut out» (gurgulionibus exsectis) and «to cut the Roman plebs to pieces with rods». Nor unseemly did Virgil, striving to avoid lowliness, say lychnos (lamps) instead of lucernis.

{{510}} If, however, some thing has no proper words of its own, new ones must be coined or borrowed ones used. Words are coined in two ways: either by a kind of invention or assumed inflection, or compounded by the joining of two words that are in customary use. They are invented chiefly when we translate from Greek, as those who declared poiotetes to be qualitates (qualities), a noun which had never before existed in Latin—wherein both the ears must be consulted and strangeness shunned. Avoiding this, Cicero refused to translate soter as salvator (savior), and said «he who gave safety» (qui salutem dedit), for the former seemed overly strange.

{{511}} By derivation also are words formed which grammarians have named paragogi, as we say florea rura (flowery fields) and campique ingentes ossibus albent (and vast plains whiten with bones), which with a somewhat curled inflection Horace calls albicant. These, as Grammar bears witness, Poetry chiefly employs, although Tullius says grandiferas possessiones (great-yielding estates) and grandiloquos oratores (grandiloquent orators).

{{512}} To this diligence is subjoined the care of metaphorical (translata) words, when either a thing finds no words of its own or we wish something to be unfolded more splendidly. Metaphors are made, therefore, either for the sake of need or for the sake of ornament: of need, when we say «the vine buds» (gemmare) and «the crops run riot» (luxuriare) and declare them «joyful» (laetas), for proper words are lacking and adopted ones are lent; of ornament, however, as «war suddenly blazed forth» (exarsit), when «arose» (exstitit) might have been said. And likewise we can borrow from all the senses: as from the eyes, «the light of liberty and the fragrance of the laws»; from the ears, «laws are silent amidst arms»; and from taste, «O sweet name of liberty!» Yet this affectation of metaphorical and borrowed words ought not to be pursued without moderation; nor ought metaphors to be far-fetched, as if thou shouldst call a profligate man «a Charybdis». We must also avoid making the adoption of a likeness coarse or base, as if thou shouldst call the commonwealth «castrated» by the death of Africanus, or Clodius «the dung of the senate». In this kind of metaphor poets especially have woven allegory, and Cicero too when he says: «When thou hadst cast the senate down from the helm, hadst driven the Roman people from the ship, and thyself, an arch-pirate with a most impure crew of brigands, wast sailing with fullest sails»; and in the In Pisonem: «That I, who in the greatest tempests and billows had steered the ship of the commonwealth and brought her safe into harbor, should dread the little cloud of thy brow and thy colleague’s tainted breath?» Thus, using several words at once, he uttered these things which in their own literal words he would perhaps have spoken more narrowly or humbly. Likewise, in a certain manner, those words are metaphorical which show either the whole from a part, or a part from the whole, or many from one, or single things from many: the whole from a part, as «he strikes upon the stern» (in puppim ferit, for the ship), or «that I am safe with thee within the same walls» instead of «in the same house». This trope grammarians have named metonymy, and the Greeks also catachresis, which we call abusio (misapplication), as when we speak of the «nature» of the gods instead of their substance.

{{513}} In connected words and the texture of speech, however, these rules must be observed: that the construction come forth closely jointed, the period complete, and the diction graced with some figure.

{{514}} Now I shall run through the precepts of composition, whose greatest fault is not to avoid hiatus and harsh combinations, iotacisms, mytacisms, labdacisms, homoeoprophora, dysprophora, and polysigma, or the constant repetition of any letter whatsoever to the point of disgust, as sale saxa sonabant («the rocks resounded with the salt sea») and casus Cassandra canebat («Cassandra sang the disasters»). Mytacism is when the joining of words clashes through the constant repetition of the letter m, as if thou sayest mammam ipsam amo quasi meam animam. Labdacism, where l jars excessively, as if thou sayest: sol et luna luce lucent alba leni lactea. Iotacism, as if thou sayest: Iunio Iuno Iovis iure irascitur. Polysigma, where the letter s is repeatedly doubled, as Sosia in solario soleas sarciebat suas. Homoeoprophoron is when it is said: o Tite tute Tati tibi tanta tyranne tulisti. Dysprophoron, as if someone should say persuasitrices praestigiatrices atque inductrices striges.

{{515}} Harsh combinations between the penultimate and final syllables of words are most of all to be avoided, an example of which is if thou sayest phaleras ablatas gratis, or if a charioteer swears per lora, per flagella, per frena.

{{516}} Hiatus (hiulcae) occurs when in that same place similar vowels, and similarly long, make a colliding and gaping structure, as if someone should say that he begot children secundo omine, and as Tullius in the Pro Milone says auctoritate publica, armare—which indeed, disguising his art, he very often sought deliberately.

{{517}} Similarly we must avoid placing three or four long or short syllables continuously in the same spot, nor should the structure pour itself out into well-known verses—especially heroic or iambic verses—although Cicero does not shun them when he says senatus haec intellegit, consul videt (an iambic senarius), nor does he turn aside from the ending or beginning of a heroic verse when he says o miserum, cui peccare licebat, and in the Academica latent ista omnia, Varro, magnis obscurata et circumfusa tenebris, and in the Verrines he poured forth a full verse mutilated by a single syllable when he says cum loquerer, tanti fletus gemitusque fiebant; nor did he avoid the ending of an elegiac pentameter, as when he says oderat ille bonos. He even runs into the wantonness of a Phalaecian hendecasyllable when he says successit tibi Lucius Metellus. This man, however, is defended both by the length of his work and by his own majesty; otherwise a heroic cadence in prose endings (clausulae) is found to be most faulty. We must observe, however, lest while fleeing the likeness of verse we pass over a good clausula, as if thou shouldst fear to say strepitumque plagarum, whereas pla- is long and makes a good clausula.

{{518}} We must also avoid cacemphaton (foul-sounding double entendre) whether by interposition or transposition of words: for unseemly examples are such as arrige aures, Pamphile and atque ereptae virginis ira, for in these speech grows coarse. We must avoid also freni (bridles/tongue-twisters), which arise from the harshest letters colliding together, as is Terence’s line in the Hecyra: per pol quam paucos reperias meretricibus fidelis evenire amatores, Syra; while words beginning with the same letters, as non fuit istud iudicium iudicii simile, iudices, and ending in the same letters, as fortissimorum, proximorum fidelissimorumque sociorum, are held to be in the same fault. Likewise we must utterly shun stringing together very many short syllables, as is that line of Serenus: perit, abit avipedis animula leporis.

{{519}} These having been briefly imparted, we must set forth the metrical feet wherewith clausulae are becomingly fitted. Indeed, Cicero throws these into a kind of mingled confusion, while he says at one moment that we must close with a ditrochee, at another approves a first paeon for beginnings and a fourth for endings, at another a dochmiac—which consists of short, two longs, short, and long, of which he set down the example amicos tenes—and likewise praises the amphimacer foot and again the dactylic rhythm, now the anapaestic, now the dithyrambic, nor is his opinion fixed. I, however, shall run through more summary rules, so that in this forest I may seem to lead the way along certain clear paths.

{{520}} In monosyllables we must inspect whether the final syllable is long or short. For if it is long, a trochee ought to precede it, as is Cicero’s non scripta, sed nata lex, or debet esse legum in re publica prima vox—which ending is suited only to a suspended sense. If, however, the monosyllable be short, let an iamb or anapaest precede, as Sallust says tota autem insula modica et cultibus variis est. A short after a short or a long after a long does not follow without censure, as if thou sayest ista res mea est, or on the contrary what Cicero says in the Pro Ligario: non tu eum patria privare, qua caret, sed vita vis—which the orator composed by design, not by error. Yet this precept given above concerning the monosyllable we place better in a colon or a comma, not at the end of a period.

{{521}} Disyllables, however, are not rightly closed with an iambic rhythm, or if a penultimate spondee is followed by an iamb, as if thou sayest tenui servos meos, or a pyrrhic instead of an iamb, as consul videt. On the other hand, a good clausula is made of an iamb and a spondee or a final trochee, as if someone says patria continet bonos cives or asserat caput legis. We must beware, however, lest either two iambs or an iamb and a pyrrhic be placed at the end, as if someone says pugnare iuvenes pro parentibus suis. We must beware also lest a pyrrhic come after a pyrrhic and make four short syllables, as if someone says perdidi bona mea, or after a pyrrhic a trochee or spondee, as if thou sayest conqueritur sua fata or imputat sibi demens. Moreover, both a trochee and an iamb, or a pyrrhic instead of an iamb after a trochee, make a bad clausula; for this reproduces unseemly the ending of an elegiac pentameter—for what difference is there whether thou sayest omnia nempe vides or aspice fata mea? Well placed, however, are either two trochees or a trochee and a spondee at the end of a clausula, as if someone says haec est bonorum civium magna cura or haec sunt, quae maximi principes sola curant.

{{522}} For trisyllables terminating a clausula the law is—if only thou wishest it to flow smoothly—that with a penultimate trochee preceding, a molossus follows, whether its last syllable be long or short by metrical license, as is that phrase of Tullius: mare fluctuantibus, litus eiectis. A very bad clausula is made, however, if instead of the penultimate trochee thou placest a spondee beforehand, as if thou sayest mare fluctuantibus, rupes eiectis; likewise very bad if instead of a trochee thou sendest ahead a pyrrhic, as if thou sayest mare fluctuantibus, apex eiectis. Likewise faulty is the ending if the first syllable of the final molossus becomes short, even though a trochee has been rightly placed before it; for then a heroic comma arises, as if someone says litus amicis. Likewise a good clausula is made if instead of the final molossus an ionic a minore is placed after the trochee, as if thou sayest mare fluctuantibus, litus agitanti. Yet in this clausula we must beware lest instead of the penultimate trochee a spondee be placed; for then, if thou resolvest the third syllable of the molossus, thou fallest into the fault into which Cicero fell when he said si te semel ad meas capsas admisero. If, however, after a penultimate trochee thou resolvest the middle syllable of the molossus, thou shalt make a fair clausula, as if thou sayest litus Aemiliae. Likewise after a penultimate trochee the third syllable of the molossus is also fairly resolved, as if thou sayest litus aequabile. Likewise if we resolve the long syllable of the penultimate trochee and the first of the final molossus, an elegant clausula is produced, as is ‹…›.

{{523}} Eironeia (Εἰρωνεία) is dissimulation, a frequent and noble figure in Cicero, wherein we signify one thing by our words and mean another in reality, as is the opening of the Pro Ligario: «A novel crime, Gaius Caesar…» Paraleipsis (παράλειψις) is preterition, when, as though passing over certain things, we nonetheless mention them. Apostrophe (ἀποστροφή) is a pointed turning away toward someone—that is, when we turn our pleading toward someone in such wise that thereby we instruct the judges. Diaporesis (Διαπόρησις) is hesitation, which figure we use when, as though in doubt, we ask advice from the judges themselves on how to begin, as is in the Pro Cluentio: «Indeed, whither to turn, O judges, I know not», and in the Pro Cornelio: «Shall I fight against the partisanship of the noblest men? Shall I lay bare their plans and designs?» and the rest.

{{524}} Erotema (Ἐρώτημα) is interrogation, which figure we use when by questioning we pile up something and heighten the indignation against it. Pysma (πύσμα) is inquiry, which figure differs from the preceding in this: that an interrogation can be answered with a single word, whereas an inquiry cannot be answered save in several words, as when we say: «By what plan, therefore, shall we wage war? What auxiliaries will be prepared for us? Who will there be willing to bring aid, when we have treated our allies so harshly?» Diatyposis (Διατύπωσις) is vivid description or delineation, when, having set things and persons before the eyes, we portray their very shapes and bearings, as Tullius in the Pro Milone: «If ye were beholding as enacted those deeds which ye have now heard recounted», and he portrays Milo sitting in his carriage wrapped in his cloak with his wife, and likewise Clodius riding out from his villa with horse and picked men, and the rest. Anteisagoge (Ἀντεισαγωγή) is counter-induction; this figure is when we confess something to be difficult and adverse, yet bring forward against it other considerations no less strong, as Cicero concerning King Ptolemy: «Difficult is the plan of waging war, yet full of good faith, full of piety», and the rest.

{{525}} Diasyrmos (Διασυρμός) is disparagement or derision; in this figure we dissolve in jest what is said by our adversaries, of which sort is that entire passage in the Pro Murena against Sulpicius concerning civil law. Metastasis (Μετάστασις) is a kind of transference—that is, when we shift a matter away from ourselves elsewhere, yet not so as to establish our whole case thereon; otherwise it begins to be an issue (status), not a figure.

{{526}} Thus far concerning figures of thought (sententiarum figurae); now let us pass to figures of diction (elocutionum figurae). Yet I wish briefly to record how many kinds of style (elocutio) there are and in how many ways it must be used. Familiar, then, to every kind of narrative is that which the Greeks call eiromene lexis (εἰρομένη λέξις, running style), which is so linked together that the next clause always follows smoothly upon the preceding one. This is suited both to history and to narrative, which desires not a rounded or periodic kind of utterance, but a flowing and continuous one, as are those words in the Pro Milone: «I slew, I slew not Spurius Maelius, who by lowering the price of grain and by personal sacrifices…» and the rest.

{{527}} There is another style, consisting of a compass, which the Greeks call periodos (περίοδος), which bounds and rounds off a thought by a certain circumscription, of which sort is that in the Pro Caecina: «If as much as audacity can do in the countryside and in deserted places, so much impudence availed in the forum and in the courts, no less would Aulus Caecina now yield to the impudence of Sextus Aebutius than he then yielded to his audacity in the use of violence.» This compass consists of members, which the Greeks call kola (κῶλα), and of short clauses, which they name kommata (κόμματα).

{{528}} A member (membrum / colon) is a part of a sentence consisting of several words and signifying something completely, in this wise: «Although I fear, O judges, lest it be shameful for one beginning to speak in behalf of a most gallant man to feel fear»; a short clause (caesum / comma), on the other hand, is a part of a sentence consisting of two or more words not yet signifying anything completely—although we also call speech caesa (chopped) when single words signifying anything whatsoever are uttered, as is: «Who is Lollius, who even now is not without a sword at thy side? Who is this Lollius—armor-bearer of Catiline, bodyguard of thy person, inciter of shopkeepers, assassin, stoner of the senate-house?» And in the Verrines: «Those chosen companions were thy hands; thy orderlies, physicians, soothsayers, and secretaries were thy hands.»

{{529}} Now a period consists of two members, and of three, and sometimes of four, although some think it can be completed out of one member, which they call a monokolos periodos (μονόκωλος περίοδος), whereas it is rather a colon. The best speech, therefore, will be produced if it be fitted now from a periodic compass, now from that continuation of running diction, sometimes broken by commata, and at other times joining cola together.

{{530}} A figure of diction, moreover, differs from a figure of thought in this: that a figure of thought remains nonetheless even when the order of words is changed, whereas a figure of diction cannot remain when the order of words is altered—although it can very often happen that a figure of thought is joined with a figure of diction, as ‹…› In irony, which is a figure of thought, epanaphora is mingled, which is a figure of diction.

{{531}} Some figures of diction, therefore, are adapted merely to adorning and, as it were, painting the speech, which we shall now employ. Antitheton (ἀντίθετον), that is, opposition of contraries, when clashing words collide with each other in balanced utterances, or like things are opposed to like, as is Cicero’s: «Didst thou lack a house? Yet thou hadst one. Did money abound? Yet thou wast in want»; or if thou sayest: «In peace most fierce in harassing citizens, in war most sluggish in fighting enemies.» Isokolon (ἰσόκωλον), equalized in its members, which is produced not by words clashing with one another, but by balanced equal clauses, as if thou sayest: «He equipped a fleet most splendid and sturdy; he levied an army most fair and valiant; he gathered a band of allies greatest and most loyal.» Parison (Πάρισον), nearly equalized; this figure differs from the preceding in that there all the words of the members are equal in number, whereas here, with one or two added in any place, the rest run parallel.

{{532}} Homoioptoton (Ὁμοιόπτωτον), alike in cases; for it received its name from the fact that all the cola fall into the same grammatical cases, as is: «To this man accusing your allies, and exhorting you to war, and striving in every way that ye might be in tumult.» Homoioteleuton (Ὁμοιοτέλευτον), ended in a similar manner; it differs from the preceding in that the former ends final words in both similar case and similar sound, whereas this is composed solely of similarity of sound under whatsoever inflection of the word. Paronomasia (Παρονομασία), a slight alteration of verb or noun—that is, when by changing a syllable or a letter it signifies different things, as if thou sayest «that praetor, or rather praedo (robber)». Ploke (Πλοκή), that is, interweaving, wherein the same verb or noun set down continuously signifies different things, as is: «Yet up to that day Memmius was Memmius (himself).»

{{533}} Palillogia (Παλιλλογία), reiteration; in this figure, with a verb or noun repeated, one wishes not different things to be understood, but signifies the repeated word more vehemently, as is: «We, we—I say it openly—we consuls are failing in our duty.» Epanalepsis (Ἐπανάληψις), resumption; this figure differs from the preceding in that the former is joined by the same part of speech repeated immediately or with one or two words interposed, whereas this is formed not with a single part of speech, but with words joined however one pleases, as is: «It cannot, no longer can this commonwealth be free.» Anadiplosis (Ἀναδίπλωσις) is reduplication, best when those words which are placed last in the former member are repeated first in the latter, as is that line of Terence: «Does Demipho deny that this girl is his kinswoman? This girl Demipho denies to be his kinswoman?» Prosapodosis (προσαπόδοσις), return of speech—that is, when a noun (or whatsoever same part of speech it be) is returned at the end of a member from which that same member began, as if thou sayest: «To thee the commonwealth ought to impute its calamities, to thee.»

{{534}} Epanaphora (Ἐπαναφορά), repetition, whenever the same part of speech is repeated at the beginning of successive members, in this wise: «Verres suborned false accusers, Verres judged the case, Verres pronounced sentence.» Antistrophe (Ἀντιστροφή), conversion; this figure differs from the preceding in that there one begins repeatedly with the same part of speech, whereas here one ends in the same, as is in the Pro Fonteio: «The greatest quantity of grain from Gaul, the amplest forces of infantry from Gaul, horsemen most numerous from Gaul.» Symploke (Συμπλοκή), interlacing; for it both begins repeatedly with one part of speech and ends just as often in one and the same, as is: «Who carried the law? Rullus. Who allotted the tribes? Rullus. Who created the decemvirs whom he wished? The same Rullus.»

{{535}} Polyptoton (Πολύπτωτον), from several cases; when a beginning is repeatedly made from the same part of speech, yet that very part is altered through cases by declensions or numbers, as is: «The senate (senatus) ordered, it pleased the senate (senatui), it is certain that the senate (senatum) commanded, he was chosen by the senate (a senatu).» Synonymia (Συνωνυμία) is community of meaning, whenever by a single word we do not sufficiently show the dignity or magnitude of a thing, and therefore bring together several words to the same signification. Tautologia (Ταυτολογία) is the same signification in several words; this differs from the preceding in that there the same thing is signified by single words, whereas in this it is signified by phrases. Klimax (Κλῖμαξ) is a ladder (gradation), as is Cicero’s in the Pro Milone: «Nor indeed did he entrust himself to the people alone, but also to the senate; nor to the senate only, but also to the public garrisons and arms; nor to these alone, but also to the power of him to whom the senate had entrusted the entire commonwealth.» Asyndeton (Ἀσύνδετον) is disconnection, when, with the conjunctions removed whereby words or nouns are linked, we utter each one singly, as is: «I await the force of the edict, the severity of the praetor; I favor the farmer; I desire Apronius to be condemned eightfold.» Diezeugmenon (Διεζευγμένον) we call disjunction, when we separate cola by different verbs assigned to each, whether two or more, in this wise: «Capua they will occupy by planting colonists; Atella they will fortify with a garrison; Nuceria and Cumae they will hold with the multitude of their men; the remaining towns they will bind fast with garrisons.»

{{537}} Antezeugmenon (Ἀντεζευγμένον), yoking together; this figure differs from the preceding in that there single distinct verbs are assigned to single members, whereas here we join several members in one verb, as is: «Whose rank shrinks from a low motive, whose fortune shrinks from a mean one, whose nature shrinks from a base one»—for this final word «shrinks» (abhorret) joined together all the aforesaid clauses. Pleonasmos (Πλεονασμός) is more than necessary, when we add certain words not needed to state the fact, but to heap up grandeur, as if thou sayest «that famous Cato» (Cato ille): though it would have sufficed to speak his name, for grandeur we add «that famous». Elleipsis (Ἔλλειψις) is omission, contrary to the preceding figure, when, with some word left unspoken, we rather pass over a thing to be understood by the hearer, pleasing most by our very swiftness. These are the figures of diction, which indeed ought not to be massed together into the thought of a single conception as though studiously coupled.

{{538}} Now our order supplies the precepts of memory, which indeed is agreed to be natural, yet there is no doubt that it can also be aided by art. Now this art is formed by brief precepts but great practice, whose office in this part is to grasp things and words not only with a firm, but also with a swift apprehension. And we must retain not only those things which have been discovered by ourselves, but also those which have been handled by our adversary in pleading. Simonides, at once a poet and a philosopher, is said to have invented the precepts of this art: for when a banquet hall had suddenly collapsed and kinsmen could not distinguish the crushed bodies, he supplied from his recollecting memory the order and names of those reclining at table. Admonished by this, he understood that it is order which confers the precepts of memory. This order, moreover, must be practiced in well-lit places (loci), wherein the images of things and likenesses of thoughts must be placed—such as a bride veiled in her saffron veil for a wedding, or thou mayest hold fast the sword or weapons of a murderer—which images the place gives back as though entrusted to it on deposit. For just as what is written down is contained by wax and letters, so what is committed to memory is stamped upon places as upon a wax tablet and page, while the recollection of things is contained by images as though by letters.

{{539}} Yet, as we have said, the matter requires great practice and toil, wherein it is found to be customary to observe this: that we write down with our own hand what we wish easily to retain; next, that if what must be learned by heart be somewhat long, divided into parts it will cling more easily; then it will behoove us to affix marks to single items in those passages which we wish most to retain; nor must they be read aloud with a loud voice, but rather practiced in a murmur; and it is manifest that memory is roused more readily by night than by day, when stillness far and wide aids us and attention is not called out of doors by the senses. There is indeed a memory of things and a memory of words, yet words need not always be learned by heart unless time allows leisure for practicing them; and it will suffice to have held the things themselves in mind, especially if nothing comes forth from memory’s natural gift.

{{540}} Among the ancients they called action (actio) what I am well aware is now commonly called delivery (pronuntiatio). It secures for the orator that the hearer is conciliated, is led by persuasion to belief, and grows warm with emotions of the soul. Its parts are three: voice (vox), countenance (vultus), and gesture (gestus); to these, as most think, is added grooming or bearing of the person. The voice consists both of nature and is preserved modified by a certain science: in nature the quality and quantity of the voice are apportioned, while science attains the manner in which thou mayest use it and what it behooves thee to observe.

{{541}} Excellence of voice consists in clearness, firmness, and sweetness—all of which are nourished by observance of food, drink, and sexual intercourse, and especially by moving the body in walking within a short space with a timely return; for this motion, since it affords easy digestion, undoubtedly clears the voice as well, whereas excessive running or a long walk weakens and fatigues it. After this walk let us betake ourselves immediately to our studies, and before we must speak, let us warm and color the voice by reading aloud.

{{542}} Nor must we shout from the beginning, but begin in a low murmur, whence thou mayest gradually raise thyself to a fitting pitch. Moreover, most have said that it pertains to caring for the voice that, while seated, men utter a very few verses in a slow and deep voice, then raise the sound little by little by degrees, and by the same degrees gather back the speech, whereby without injury it may return even to a murmur.

{{543}} The countenance, moreover, must be changed in accordance with the dignity of the thought, yet not in such wise as is the custom of stage-actors, who by twisting their faces offer ridiculous grimaces to the spectators: for those things which delivery and countenance commend must be signified, not exhibited as a spectacle. Yet in this part there is great moderation of the eyes, which are moved now with cheerfulness, now with earnestness, now with a threatening look; nor must the eyes be pressed down with overly heavy brows or laid bare as they strain toward the forehead—a fault which Tullius bitterly censures in Piso. Nor must gestures be tossed about too softly, nor the flanks swayed effeminately, nor the neck jerked unseemly, lest one be drawn into those allurements of Hortensius, which, although he used them gracefully, he did not seem to use in a manly way. In sum, the orator must not maintain that gesture wherewith actors seem to please the stage: in impassioned arguments the hand should be extended more broadly, in conversation or narrative drawn in; and especially in this part we must take care that all things be becoming—which is preserved by good judgment more than by any art of this instruction.

{{544}} Now the order of my promises admonishes me to run through the parts of a speech, which some more subtly have said to be two, others five, and some more. Those who assert two take note of one whereby we instruct the judges and another whereby we move them, and under instruction they assign both statement of facts and confirmation, while under moving they have linked the introduction and the epilogue, because both at the beginning the hearer must be prepared and at the end, when about to pronounce sentence, he must be stirred. Those, however, who have said there are five parts follow a rational order: for there is introduction (exordium), statement of facts (narratio), proposition (propositio), argumentation (argumentatio), and peroration (peroratio).

{{545}} The introduction is the beginning of a speech, preparing the hearer to understand the case; its virtues are three: to render the hearer attentive, teachable, and well-disposed. We make him attentive if we say that a great matter is at stake, or a novel one, or one concerning the commonwealth or very many people; teachable, if something concerning the case is brought forward summarily whereby we may instruct the judge. We make him well-disposed either from the matter itself or from a person—and from a person, either our own, or that of the judges, or that of our adversaries: from our own, if we commend something favorable that we have done, or if we dissolve or extenuate something that harms our case; from that of the judges, if we conciliate them to ourselves either from the attractions of the case itself or from outside; from the person of our adversaries in three ways, if we bring them into hatred, into envy, or into contempt—into hatred, if we show them to be wicked or cruel; into envy, if we show them to be arrogant and overbearing; into contempt, if base and mean.

{{546}} Now of introductions there are two kinds: direct opening (principium) and insinuation (insinuatio). A direct opening is that whereby we immediately, simply, and openly conciliate the hearers to ourselves; insinuation is when by a subtle, winding introduction we steal into the judge’s favor. Herein there are four occasions: first, when the case itself is discreditable; second, when the judge has already formed some preconceived prejudice against us, or is wearied, or some outcry or derision hath intervened.

{{547}} Before all else we must examine what is the character of the subject matter; for there are five kinds: honorable, discreditable, doubtful, humble, and obscure. If it is honorable, we must either use no direct opening or conclude the introduction with amplification; if discreditable, we must use insinuation; if doubtful, the hearer must be made well-disposed; if humble, attentive; if obscure, teachable.

{{548}} Insinuation, moreover, is unfolded in four ways: when we substitute either the matter for the person, or the person for the matter, or one matter for another matter, or one person for another person. For if we must plead in the case of a courtesan, we shall say that the judges ought rather to look to the matter itself; or if a freedman or client of someone is on trial, we shall admonish them to consider the person of his patron rather than of this man—and so for the rest.

{{549}} The faults of introductions, however, are these: commonplace (vulgare), which can fit all cases; shared (commune), which the adversary too can use; adverse (contrarium), which the adversary could use to better advantage; affected (affectatum), which is brought forward either in unusual words or from extraneous subjects; and superfluous (supervacuum), which renders the hearer neither attentive, nor teachable, nor well-disposed. Furthermore, one that is overly long or obscure must be shunned.

{{550}} Of narratives (narrationes) there are four kinds: history, fable, realistic fiction (argumentum), and pragmatic or judicial statement. History is such as Livy’s; fable is neither true nor plausible, as Daphne turned into a tree; realistic fiction contains things not actually done, yet such as could have been done, as in comedies a father is feared and a courtesan is loved; judicial narrative, however, is the exposition of deeds done or plausible.

{{551}} The merits of a narrative are three: that it be lucid, that it be plausible, and that it be brief; and the opposites to these are called faults. A narrative is lucid if we set forth the matter not confusedly, in customary and expressive words, and without a long roundabout circuit; plausible, if we seem to expound nothing affectedly and as though from nature itself; brief, if the statement is not poured out prolixly beyond what the matter demands. Some narratives belong to the case and business itself, others are incidental: those belonging to the case itself are those without which the matter under trial cannot be understood; incidental are those brought in from outside for the sake of proof, or example, or amplification, or arousing indignation, or pleasure.

{{552}} Some have said there are five species of narrative, as Theodorus of Byzantium distinguishes in Greek: prodiegesis, hypodiegesis, paradiegesis, anadiegesis, and katadiegesis. The elements of a narrative are also six: person, cause, place, time, means (materia), and deed. Likewise some narratives are called continuous, others divided: continuous, which are spoken in an unbroken texture without any interruption; divided, in which either an argument or some digression is interposed. We narrate, moreover, in six ways: amplifying something or extenuating it, passing over or reminding, and procuring favor or indignation. After the narrative, indeed, it is fitting to subjoin a digression, which they call parekbasis (παρέκβασις), whereby we either rouse or soothe the judges; yet since it is not a regular part of a speech and is not always to be seized upon, it hath pleased me to pass it over.

{{553}} A proposition (propositio) is either our own, or our adversaries’, or common: our own, as «I charge murder»; our adversaries’, «He says that I committed murder»; common, «It is inquired, therefore, which of us committed the murder.»

{{554}} Propositions belong either to principal questions or to incidental ones, and are either simple or compound. Simple is «Thou livest in profligacy»; compound is «Either thou livest in profligacy or thou lovest a courtesan.» Compound propositions, moreover, are set forth chiefly through partition, which is made in two ways: for either we show simply what we are about to say, or we declare beforehand what is agreed between us and our adversary and what is called into doubt.

{{555}} Propositions are either bare or with a reason subjoined: bare, as «Thou didst aim at tyranny»; with a reason subjoined, «Thou didst aim at tyranny, for thou keepest arms at home.» All, moreover, are taken either simply or by induction: simply, as if thou sayest «I shall prove him a traitor»; by induction, as if we recall what preceded and introduce what follows, as if thou sayest «Since I have proved the law unjust, I shall prove it inexpedient.» We propound, moreover, either by concession or by preterition: by concession, when, granting favor to what we have said before, we introduce something else, as if thou sayest «I grant for the moment that he was slain, yet I deny that he was slain by this man»; by preterition, when we pass over to a second proposition in such wise that we do not meanwhile grant the former, as if thou sayest «Now, since I have shown that an action concerning private matters alone is denied to a dishonored man, I shall show that it is not denied concerning the crime of treason.»

{{556}} Partition (partitio) is that which briefly embraces the order of the entire division, as is in the Pro Quinctio: «We deny that thou didst hold possession of the goods of Publius Quinctius under the praetor’s edict»; and first he begins thus, that there was no cause for demanding confiscation; to this he subjoins, nor could they have been possessed under the edict; and lastly, nor were they possessed. «When I have proved these three things», he says, «I shall conclude.» Thou observest that the body of the entire speech has been arranged along these outlines. Now a partition ought to be such that its single parts contain very many questions within themselves; for if it be drawn out through minor incidental questions as well, the partition itself will be overburdened, and from this the judges will think that an abundance of topics must be avoided. Therefore Tullius too in that same Pro Quinctio partitioned in such wise that single headings contained several heads of questions. For indeed, see how much that first heading—that there was no cause for demanding confiscation—contains: that no money was owed to Sextus Naevius and that suit ought to have been brought otherwise; and that Quinctius did not forfeit his bail. Second, that he could not have taken possession under the edict: because he both had an agent and was defended in his absence and did not go into hiding, and the rest of the creditors remained quiet; and because orders were sent to expel him from the joint estate before his confiscation was decreed, and because he was cast out thence by violence contrary to what the praetor had ordered. By these examples it is manifest that within the partition must be placed the main questions which are to be unfolded in the execution, so that the judge may both become attentive to us and not be weighed down by an abundance of promises. We must also inspect what points are agreed between us and the opposing party and what are called into controversy; and those which are agreed must then be enumerated if they profit us, as Cicero says in the Pro Marco Tullio: «That Marcus Tullius suffered damage is agreed between me and my adversary; that the deed was done by violence with armed men they do not deny; that it was committed by the slaves of Publius Fabius they dare not deny; whether it was done with malicious intent is disputed.» Thou seest, therefore, that in his partition he reasoned from points granted, because that profited him.

{{557}} Argumentation is the utterance whereby we execute the arguments themselves in words, while arguments are the proofs whereby the case is established. Argumentation is divided into two parts: confirmation (confirmatio) and refutation (reprehensio). Of arguments there are two kinds, artificial and inartificial. Now the artificial has four principal topics: before the deed (ante rem), in the deed (in re), around the deed (circa rem), and after the deed (post rem). Before the deed is divided into seven topics: from person, from act, from motive, from time, from place, from manner, and from means (materia). In the deed, however, there are twelve topics: from the whole, from a part, from genus, from species, from difference, through the seven circumstances (which topic also admits within itself from the greater to the less and from the less to the greater), from property, from definition, from name, from manifold appellation, from beginning, from progression or advance, and from completion or consummation.

{{558}} Around the deed there are ten topics: from the similar, whose species are five—historical example, comparison, fable, image (that is, plausible fiction, which is taken from comedy), to which some add apologues also, such as Aesop’s. Next I shall run through the remaining topics around the deed, which are: from the dissimilar, from the equal, from the contrary, through affirmation and negation, relative (ad aliquid, which is inflected in four cases—genitive, dative, accusative, and ablative), from things clashing with one another through possession and loss, from the greater to the less, from the less to the greater, from the preceding, from what is simultaneous or from adjuncts, and from consequents. After the deed there are two topics: from the outcome and from previous judgment.

{{559}} There are other topics from conjunction (ἀπὸ τῆς συζυγίας) or from conjugates, which is as it were joined to the quality of a person, as if we say that he who entertains hostile feelings is an enemy. Likewise from quality, as that if he acted wrathfully, he acted without reason; from quantity, as that if he is a slave, it was done in the same manner; from adjuncts (ἀπὸ τῶν προσόντων καὶ συνθέτων), as the fasces, the toga, and the curule chair are the insignia of magistrates. Likewise through partition, that is diaeresis, through all circumstances—that is, when we partition by persons, times, and the rest which Greek variety distinguishes.

{{560}} Inartificial arguments are divided into prior decisions, rumors, tortures, documents, oaths, and witnesses, which the orator handles in a manner similar to artificial ones. In arguments, however, we must take care lest they be commonplace, lest they be shared with the adversary, lest they be far-fetched, or above the dignity of the case, or beneath its dignity. Concerning confirmation I have spoken briefly; now let us examine the precepts of refutation. For the adversary’s pleading is refuted either by disproving (redarguendo), when we show either the whole argument or its parts to make against him; or by objecting (repugnando), when we show that false premises have been assumed for true ones or that the conclusion does not follow from them; or by counterbalancing (compensando), if against an argument which tells against us we set another in opposition.

{{561}} Of questions there are two kinds: that which is introduced by us to be confirmed is called proegoumenos (προηγούμενος, leading), while that which is brought forward by our adversaries and must be repelled is called anankaia (ἀναγκαία, compulsory). Now the former question, which is introduced by us, is handled in two modes, so that either single questions are introduced separately or several conjointly. We introduce them, moreover, in various ways: either by simple proposition, or by partition, or through figures, which they call dianoias (figures of thought). This topic is multiform: for there are nearly as many occasions for introducing a question in this kind as there are figures of thought.

{{562}} For it is done either by resolution, that is kat' analysin (κατὰ ἀνάλυσιν), or by addition, or by preterition, or by concession, or by rebuke, or by separation, that is aphorismos (ἀφορισμός), or by admonition, or by disguised proposition, or by prepared proposition, or by partitioned proposition, or by likeness to a preceding question or answer, or by inversion—that is, when we treat first that question which stands second, and then that which stands first.

{{563}} Even that question which our adversaries have brought forward we can refute by the same modes; and we are wont to add to it when the adversary has set down something either obscurely or for the sake of amplification rather than proof, or when we make a step toward our own proposition, or when we turn the adversary’s proposition to our own advantage—which we call peristrophe (περιστροφή)—or when, having stated the second question, we treat the first. And this must be done either when we aim at the grace of variety, or when the first question tells strongly against us and ought not to be placed in the foremost position. In hypophora (anticipating an objection) we must beware lest it be stated too fully and copiously, although sometimes, for the sake of ridiculing the adversary, if he amplifies something concerning which there is no dispute, we ought to exaggerate it, or so as to show what is inconsistent. Hypophorae are weakened in three ways: by addition of words, by alteration, and by subtraction. Now hypophora is the objection of the opposing art, and anthypophora is the reply to it.

{{564}} Anacephalaeosis is, as it were, a brief recapitulation of the questions from the head of the case. Anacephalaeosis must not always be used, but only when either we have made a partition or the case has branched out into many questions—yet in such wise that we reweave the sum of each, not the parts of parts. This indeed we shall also use in another part of the speech, when the judge’s memory perhaps falters, which device is then called epimerismos (ἐπιμερισμός). Anacephalaeosis, however, is not a separate part of a speech, but a portion of the epilogue.

{{565}} For the epilogue has three parts: the aforesaid enumeration, indignation, which we call deinosis (δείνωσις), and pity, which we call oiktos (οἶκτος) or eleos (ἔλεος). Now indignation is drawn from the topics of arguments: for by these we not only prove things, but also amplify them; and complaint—that is, pity—is taken from the same topics of arguments as indignation. Nevertheless, peroration must be used not only at the end of a speech, but wherever the subject matter permits—that is, in a digression from the introductions or from a continuous narrative, and sometimes even from the questions. In the epilogue we must generally observe that it be brief, since once the judge has been stirred he must straightway be dismissed to pronounce sentence, while he is either angry with thine adversaries, or moved to pity by thy tears, or stirred by the affliction of the defendants.» As she was still speaking thus, the Cyllenian himself nodded to her to pass over to the company of her sisters and the attendance of the bride. Seeing this, bringing her assertions to an end, she proceeded with ready confidence to the seat of Philology, and having kissed her crown with a resounding smack (for she did nothing silently, even had she wished to), she mingled with the fellowship and company of her sisters.

{{566}} At length is ended the page of the loquacious maiden’s assertions, run through in haste—which yet scarce stitches a ruddy little scroll-boss (umbilicus), wrapped in many a band, onto the plump swelling of our volume. If this be approved and free from disgust, it shall bring forth some things which novelty hath woven, and those things shall be spread abroad which antiquity passes over. Yet if with a sneer of pride thou curlest the tip of thy nostril, it will repent the celestials to have shuddered at arms, and then a place for terror will be stirred, O Silvanus, when thou seekest Saturn’s sickle! Whereas thou, shouting back against the throng of rhetoricians with inverted trumpet-blast, resounding through the Tullian camp, shalt depart whither neither envious hater nor black spite may follow thee in thy renown.

## Liber VI. De geometria

{{567}} «O comely armed maiden, wisdom of things, Pallas, ethereal tinder, mind and skill of fate, genius of the world, sacred prudence of the Thunderer, learning-kindling ardor and diligence of our lot, who makest the judgment of wise care going before, and art the summit of reason and sacred Mind (nous) of gods and men, passing beyond the back of swift and resplendent Olympus, higher than Jove alone, circle of flaming ether, heptad (ἑπτάς) in numbers, prior to fire, third from the Moon, whom those who fashion sacred statues liken in learned attire:

{{568}} Hence threefold crests glow ruddy from thy crown, because thou glitterest as leader and president in bloody war, or because triangular fire flashes and is whirled for thee.

{{569}} Hence they give thee a shield, because wisdom rules the world, or because foaming battles demand the aid of reason; thy quivering spear also shows piercing acumen, yet more Lymphatic is it that a circle encompasses thy buckler.

{{570}} Hence the gifts of spring-leaved olive put forth foliage for thee, because wakeful care learns the arts through thee by the midnight oil:

{{571}} They give thee the gray-eyed owl, because in eyes thou art of one color with fire, and thou art the flower of fire and art renowned as gray-eyed (glaucopis) Athene. Or is the service of the night-seeing bird delivered to thee rather because wakeful skill leads sleepless cares?

{{572}} Upon thy breast they say petrifying Medusa bristles, because wise skill strikes the trembling crowd with stupor.

{{573}} On city citadels the ancients consecrated temples to thee, because reason—and that which is higher—enlarges cities. Hence they tell that thou wast born of thy father without a mother’s bond, because a senate of mothers knows not provident counsel, and thou presidest over the counsels of men; hence thou art called a hero-maiden (virago).

{{574}} O sacred prudence of the learned fountain-born Maidens, alone fulfilling the nine, single mind for all the Muses, I beseech thee: deign to glide down to thine own office, inspiring in us Greek arts in the Latian tongue.

{{575}} I give and hold thanks to thee, O goddess, and paying them forever shall owe thee gratitude; radiant, thou deignest to nod assent to my prayers: I recognize and venerate what I have seen. Finally, in attendance upon the Art now about to enter, there appear the choicest of women, who, carrying a comely sort of small table colored by a sprinkling of greenish-blue powder, advance with somewhat ready confidence to the middle of the heavenly senate. Yet who these are, or what they carry, I—ignorant of what is to be enacted—do not perceive.»

{{576}} Here, witty as she is—she who had taken up the entire tale from the very stir and threshold of its beginnings—playful Satire said: «Unless I am mistaken, my Felix, thou hast wasted a superabundance of oil, enough to drench the wrestling-schools or even the bridegroom himself, and hast burned away an expense of lamp-wick in vain for Mulciber to devour, since thou dost not even recognize Philosophy, the mother of so many schools and so many heroes! When Jove recently promulgated through her the decree and tablet of the celestial senate, and when he dispatched her as marriage-envoy to address the suitor and win Philology’s consent, couldst thou not recognize her even then?

{{577}} Yet because thou now savorest of Arcadia and Midas—especially since the time when toilsome sweating and overly distracting care, bound fast to the parts of forensic pettifoggery, blunted the edge of thy better diligence—thou seemest to me both to have lost thy memory of this matron and to have chosen now not to know her sister as well.

{{578}} For that woman whom thou seest girded for a like office is called Paideia (Education), a lady exceedingly wealthy, and one who despises the riches and treasures of Croesus and Darius in comparison with her own. Conscious as she is of her talents, she is rare in the assemblies of all men, nor does she—haughty as she is—readily grant her countenance even to the foremost nobles; for the most part, however, she has clung to and smiled upon the poor, and especially those whom she has beheld either barefoot, or shaggy with unshorn hair, or half-clad in a squalid cloak. In short, if thou exceptest Marcus Terentius [Varro] and a few Roman consulars, there will be scarcely anyone whose threshold she has entered.

{{579}} Thou seest, therefore, how proud a fastidiousness belongs to each woman; yet both are being made ready for the office of Mercury’s handmaid who is about to arrive. For that object which the bearers have brought in is called an abacus, a device suited for depicting and marking out figures; for thereon are traced straight lines, or circular curves, or triangular angles. It is capable of shaping the whole compass and circles of the universe, and of sketching the aspects of the elements and the very depth of the earth; there shalt thou see depicted whatever thou couldst not unfold to sight in words.» «Without doubt», said I, «she who is about to come transcends Apelles and Polyclitus; for she is recounted as able to portray all things in such wise that labyrinthine Daedalus must be believed to have begotten her.»

{{580}} And with the word I looked forth and beheld a radiant woman carrying a measuring-rod (radius) in her right hand and a solid sphere in her left, clad on her left side in a peplum on which were seen amidst the stars the magnitudes and courses of the constellations, the measures, connections, and shapes of the celestial circles, and even the shadow of the earth reaching into the sky and staining the golden orbs of the Moon and Sun with darkening purple.

{{581}} The garment itself, indeed, shone with the sea-blue hue of the vernal ether, being frequently lent for the use of her own sister Astronomy as well; while the rest of her attire glistened, variegated with diverse colors, painted over with varied arrays of numbers, styles of gnomons, intervals, and the forms of weights and measures. For traversing the earth she wore sandals—the very ones worn down by her measurement of the globe—an untiring traveler.

{{582}} Now when she had entered the senate of the gods, although she could have reckoned off on the instant how many stadia, how many cubits, and finally how many finger-breadths it is from the earth to heaven, nevertheless, touched by the majesty of Jove and the celestials, she hastened toward that little table of the uncovered abacus, glancing round at the adornment of the outer vault and the palaces coffered with stars.

{{583}} She halted, gazing with astonished sight at the starry vault, and becoming was her hair and her dusty feet. The honored sphere itself shone too in her left hand, like unto the universe and the stars; for globe and circles, zone and gleaming constellations ran in linked order, set with equal art.

{{584}} The earth, which standing fast sustains the whirling orb, clung like a point lowest in the middle place. Tender air, penetrable to glass-clear breezes, embracing this, watered it with showers; yet that earth was channelled with marble-gleaming sea by the boundless deep wherewith it is girt. The outer circle of the orb, which gleams with the star-bearing peplum of ether, had covered many lights.

{{585}} Hence bright Titan had kindled his ruddy orb, and presently milky Luna imitated pious day. Thus mightest thou behold the eyes of dusky night shine forth from gold as a sacred flame glitters. Thus Cypris, when Ocean’s waters had bathed her star, had granted all her lights to her own Lucifer. Equal order holds all things under equal laws, nor is this sphere less wondrous than the high house of the gods. At this image of the world the Trinacrian land marveled, fashioned by Archimedes’ star-making hand. O happy care and greater prudence of mind, to have equaled Jove beneath our mortal frame!

{{586}} At length, therefore, having unglued her eyes from admiration of the shimmering ether, surveying and regarding the gods with becoming dignity, since she was acclaimed by all with venerable honor and called mistress of the rest of the Arts known to the gods, the secret of her erudition was sought out, that starting from the very cradle of her instruction she might bring it forth. Then she, drawing back a little the obstacle of her curled hair from her face, resplendent with the bright majesty of her countenance and stirring the powder spread over her abacus, began thus:

{{587}} «Although, having beheld my Archimedes and the most learned Euclid among the philosophers, I might have substituted them for the exposition of the instruction to be built up, lest any part of my assertions remain unpolished or its depth grow dim, nevertheless, since Cyllenius excludes ornament and those men too, uttering nothing in Latin with their purely Helladic eloquence, speak only Attic Greek, it is more fitting that I myself—what scarcely ever happens—impart to you in Romulean words the very things which I taught them.

{{588}} And first the reason for my name must be set forth, lest as a somewhat unkempt wayfarer I be thought to have entered unseemly the gilded senate-house of the celestials and to be about to sprinkle the pavement inlaid with jeweled cylinders with rural dust. I am called Geometry because I am able to explain with their proper ratios the earth which I have frequently traversed and measured, its shape, magnitude, place, parts, and stadia; nor is there any division in the whole diversity of the earth which I do not dispatch in the course of an unfailing description.»

{{589}} At this word, since there were very many in the senate of the gods who had neither sprung from earth nor would ever admit that they had trodden the earth, and since Jove himself also wished rather curiously to search out the hidden recesses of the whole earth—so that nowhere even in this age, I suppose, might that shape-shifting lover fail to hear of comely maidens—Geometry is therefore bidden first to set forth this subject, and afterward to unfold the remaining precepts of the art to be constructed. Then she spoke:

{{590}} «The shape of the whole earth is not flat, as they judge of its position who liken it to an outspread disk, nor concave, as others say who declare that rain descends into the lap of the earth, but round—indeed globular, as Dicaearchus asserts.

{{591}} For the rising and setting of the stars would not be held different according to the elevation or inclinations of the earth if, spread out over a flat surface, the works of the cosmic structure shone at one and the same time above lands and seas, or again if the rising of the emerged sun were hidden by the hollow recesses of a sunken earth.

{{592}} Yet since the latter assertion is refuted by the very worthlessness of a more contemptible and empty notion, it is better to examine that former opinion, to which even the natural philosopher Anaxagoras acceded, although he is believed to build up certain arguments for it: for he says that a flat earth is clearly proved by the rising or setting of the sun or moon, which, as soon as the first gleam of light has emerged, are straightway directed in straight lines to our eyes—which becomes a more indubitable proof, he says, if standing on the shore we leave behind the obstacles of mountains. Yet if this were so, rising stars would be seen at the same time by all dwelling upon the earth, and settings, buried in a single descent, would be able to darken all lands at once, and the assertion of the Romulean bard would be exploded as false, wherein he teaches:

And when first the East breathes upon us with panting steeds, there ruddy Vesper kindles his late-shining lamps.

{{593}} Nay more, all nights and days would agree in equal intervals and always equal hours, nor would certain stars appear to one part of the earth and other stars be denied it.
