Yet while the northern wheel revolves above the zenith of Hesperia, among the Trogodytes and neighboring Egypt it is utterly unknown as an unseen and hidden constellation; while Scythia, Gaul, and Italy itself do not behold Canopus and Berenice’s Hair—stars exceedingly bright—whereas in southern lands they are conspicuous and resplendent and are looked up to as though suspended plumb overhead; at Alexandria too Canopus rises a quarter of a zodiacal sign above the earth, while because of the earth’s curvature it does not see the twin Bears; in Arabia in the month of November, Helice, unseen at nightfall, is beheld in the second watch, whereas at Meroe it is seen only at the solstice and for a scant space, where around the rising of Arcturus it rises with the day; likewise at the port of Patala in India it is seen only in the first part of the night, and on Mount Maleus therein it is visible for only fifteen days of the year: who, therefore, can doubt that by the interposition of a curved globe some stars become invisible while others rise into view by the curvatures of a sphere?

{{594}} To establish belief in a round globe for doubters, this too is added: that eclipses of the sun and moon occurring at sunset have not been seen by inhabitants of the East, and likewise if they happen at sunrise, they are unknown to all Britain and the western regions. Even in central regions they generally vary by differences of hours—just as at the victory of Alexander the Great, Servius Nobilis reported in Arabia that the moon was eclipsed in the second hour of the night, which in Sicily was beheld in the first splendor of its rising. When Vipstanus and Fonteius were consuls, on the eleventh day before the Kalends of May [April 21, 59 CE], there was an eclipse of the sun which, seen in Campania at the seventh hour of the day, is attested in Armenia at the eleventh hour of the same day—which happened surely because the circuit of the sphere continually varies the intervals of time through the curves of its rotundity.

{{595}} Finally, those very vessels which are called horispica or horologia (sundials) are constructed differently according to the diversity of places, and are distinguished by a different gnomon beyond every five hundred stadia, the shadows varying according to the lofty elevations or low inclinations of localities. Hence it is that at Meroe the longest day cuts twelve equinoctial hours and two-thirds of another; at Alexandria, fourteen; in Italy, fifteen; and in Britain, seventeen. At the summer solstice, indeed, when the sun, borne to the summit of the sky, illuminates the lands lying beneath with the continuation of perpetual day, likewise in its winter descent it makes a half-year’s night shudder with cold—a fact which Pytheas of Massilia asserted was discovered in the island of Thule. By these differences of time it has been proved, unless I am mistaken, that the earth must be held to be globular with the curves of rotundity.

{{596}} It follows that we prove what place in the universe and what magnitude it has received. The circumference of the earth, indeed, is two hundred and fifty-two thousand stadia, as was determined by the most learned Eratosthenes by gnomonic calculation.

{{597}} [For scaphia are called round vessels of bronze which distinguish the courses of the hours by the height of a pin set in the middle of the bottom, which pin is called a gnomon; the length of whose shadow at the equinox, measured by the reckoning of its center and multiplied twenty-fourfold, yielded the measure of the double circle.]

{{598}} Eratosthenes, however, having been made certain of the number of stadia from Syene to Meroe through the royal surveyors of Ptolemy, and noting what fraction of the earth it was, multiplying in proportion to the parts, unhesitatingly determined the circumference and measure of the earth—by how many thousands of stadia it was encompassed.

{{599}} The magnitude of the earth has been ascertained with reasonable brevity; let its place and position be taught. That it stands immovable in the middle and lowest part of the universe is shown by manifold proofs: whether because before the ordering of the universe it was in that same place, whence it could not be moved, and thereafter, when the elements were torn apart from the confusion of the primordial mixture, the immense and rolling expanse of waters and the breath of air, diffused on every side around the station of the earth, compressed it and formed a center which, smooth and revolving, enclosed it; or because in a sphere everything that is central is made lowest, while whatever sustains those lines is packed together by equal lines from the circuit of the outermost rotundity.

{{600}} Now the lowest point must necessarily stand still by its own extremity, because there is nothing lower into which it can fall. Moreover, all heavy bodies fall upon it from above, such as rain, hail, snow, thunderbolts, and the very Ocean which plunges into its inner depths, and the hidden streams that roll in its lowest bottom. Thou seest, therefore, that she who sustains the waves and all things, compressed from every side, has nothing beneath her into which she may withdraw. She must, therefore, be believed to be central because she is lowest—a fact which the intervals of the equinoctial season especially make manifest, for sundials show equal limits of hours both before midday and afterward, and both of day and of night.

{{601}} By this proof it is clear that heaven is equally distant from the earth on every side; which is likewise taught by the two circles of the summer and winter solstices, for surely as great an interval as the day has when the sun, lifted to the summits of heaven, illuminates it, so great a space does the longer night represent when the sun sinks into winter. And likewise whatever interval individual signs of the zodiac have contributed to the light, so much do they acknowledge for the shadows of night when the sun glows ruddy in the opposite quarter—which certainly proves on every side that the circles and stars are equally distant, and that the earth is central. Now if this seems fittingly proved, I shall set forth its parts, which I myself have measured out.

{{602}} The orb of the earth is divided into five zones, or rather I say belts (fasciae), according to the diversity of conditions; three of which excessive intemperance of extremes has banished from habitation: for two, bordering upon either pole and occupied by boundless cold and frosts, have afforded causes of desertion through snow-bearing hoarfrosts; while the middle belt, scorched with flames and panting heats, burns up the approaches of living creatures that draw near. The other two, however, tempered by the breath of vital air, have granted habitation to living beings. And these, curving around the entire rotundity of the earth, have encompassed both the upper hemisphere and the lower.

{{603}} For certainly the earth divides for itself two parts of semicircles by a certain distinction: that is, it has one upper hemisphere, which we inhabit and which Ocean surrounds, and another lower hemisphere. Now this upper one takes its beginning from the rising of the sun, while that lower one begins from the setting of the light—which circle the Greeks call the horizon.

{{604}} Yet because those zones embrace the roundness of either part, they make ten regions on both sides with their circuit—of which this one, which is inhabited by us, is one and ascends toward the north wind and the Seven Oxen (the North); the other, which stretches in the opposite direction toward the meridian and the south wind, is that which they are thought to inhabit who are called antoikoi (ἄντοικοι, dwellers opposite in latitude).

{{605}} Similarly from the lower hemisphere there are two: those who are directly opposite to us are called antipodes, while those who are opposite to those whom we called antoikoi are named antichthones (ἀντίχθονες). Yet diversity of seasons separates us from those [antoikoi] by a kind of contrariety: for when we are parched by summer, they are pinched by cold; when here spring blooms with flowery meadows, there subdued summer turns to autumn with mild warmth; here winter appears, there the summer solstice; to us it is permitted to behold the northern lights, to them it is utterly denied.

{{606}} Our antipodes, on the other hand, endure one stretch of winter and the blaze of a common summer together with us, yet opposite nights and contrary days—namely, in our summer they have long nights and short days, and to them the North Star, visible to us, lies hidden without end. So too those who are called antoikoi and their own antipodes know the four seasons of the year in common, and they alone look up to the pole which is in the south, wholly unknown to their antipodes and to us.

{{607}} For those who dwell in the middle belt, sunrise and sunset change daily, and those over whom the sun stands see it rise more swiftly and sink more slowly. At the equinoctial season, however, both rising and setting will appear alike, nor are there any stars denied to their sight, and those which rise together for them come together to their setting. These people behold all days equal to their nights and measure no shadows at midday, and their antipodes experience days and nights under the same length. Since, however, the swift course of the solstice to be traversed passes across these very peoples, and the sun returning again toward winter likewise leads its path across them and withdraws far from them on either side, there is no doubt that they suffer winter twice and summer a second time; and when sunrise comes to them, sunset appears to their antipodes.

{{608}} Now those two regions which I likewise called belts—one of which is crusted over by the chill of the neighboring Wain (Ursa Major), while the other is contracted by the desertion of the breath beyond the south wind—have no separate antipodes of their own, but become antipodes to each other by the contrariety of their habitation, nor do they know any risings of stars on either side apart from the planets, which indeed do not pass overhead for them, but rise from the middle of their flank. The fixed stars of heaven also are seen for six months, and for six months likewise do not appear, and the equinoctial circle is their horizon. Six signs of the zodiac are beheld by each; finally, they endure day and night for six months at a time, so that the ends of the pole and axis are seen overhead by both. Yet this former is lit by the gleam of the North Star, while the other, illuminated by the radiance of the star Canopus, knows not the rest.

{{609}} The conditions of these regions were handed down by the most learned Pytheas, yet I myself traversed them, lest any portion of the earth seem to remain unknown to me. Now the entire circuit and full circumference of this whole earth—to reckon in Roman measurement what I have recounted in computation of stadia—is thirty-one million five hundred thousand paces. In which place I think we must not pass over the opinion of Ptolemy recorded in his geographical work:

{{610}} For he divides the tract of the zodiac and the entire compass of heaven into three hundred and sixty degrees, each of which he reckons to reach the center of the earth in such wise that the breadth of a single degree is stretched there to the measure of five hundred stadia—each stadium being unfolded into one hundred and twenty-five paces, whence five hundred stadia, which belong to one degree, amount to sixty-two miles and five hundred paces. Now those five hundred stadia multiplied three hundred and sixty times make one hundred and eighty thousand; from which the miles gathered by the aforesaid division make twenty-two thousand five hundred. This it behooved me to impart concerning the globe of the whole earth.

{{611}} Furthermore, its length from east to west—that is, from the extremity of India itself to the Pillars of Hercules consecrated at Gades—is eight million five hundred and seventy-seven thousand paces, just as the author Artemidorus also asserted; for Isidorus says nine million eight hundred and eighteen thousand. Yet Artemidorus adds to the aforesaid measurement whatever juts out beyond Gades: for along the front of Spain, around the Sacred Promontory [Cape St. Vincent] to another final promontory which is called Artabrum [Cape Finisterre], he says the land advances nine hundred and ninety-one miles.

{{612}} This measurement can also be made more compendiously if one travels by sea: for from the river Ganges and its mouth, where it pours itself into the Eastern Ocean, through India and Parthyene to Myriandrus, a city of Syria situated on the Gulf of Issus, is five thousand two hundred and fifteen miles; thence by the nearest sea-route to the island of Cyprus, Patara in Lycia, Rhodes, Astypalaea—likewise an island in the Carpathian Sea—Taenarum in Laconia, thence Lilybaeum in Sicily and Caralis in Sardinia, three thousand four hundred and fifty miles; thence to Gades, four thousand two hundred and fifty miles. Which entire measure from the Eastern Sea makes eight thousand five hundred and seventy-eight miles.

{{613}} Likewise another route by land and sea can be traced by the rivers Ganges and Euphrates, Cappadocia, Phrygia, through the Aegean Sea and the Laconian Sea, and likewise through other gulfs as far as the Peloponnese, Leucas, Corcyra, the Acroceraunian mountains, Brundisium, Rome, the Alps, and Gaul, across the Pyrenees mountains also to the Western Ocean, the coast of Spain, and the crossing to Gades; and the whole measure, as Artemidorus bears witness to my assertions, contains eight thousand six hundred and eighty-five miles. Ye have its length recounted in manifold ways.

{{614}} Its breadth, however, from south to north is very much less, for on both sides it is narrowed by the excess of intemperate climate. To this is added that on the northern quarter the nation of the Sarmatians and abundant barbarism bar access to investigating its dimension. Nevertheless, whatever an intrepid expedition has been able to traverse has been competently measured, and the breadth of the habitable earth has been found to be nearly half less than the stated length: for it amounts to five thousand four hundred and sixty-two miles.

{{615}} For from the shore of the Ethiopian Ocean, where it first becomes habitable, to Meroe, thence to Alexandria, Rhodes, Cnidus, Cos, Samos, Chios, Mytilene, Tenedos and the promontory of Sigeum, the mouth of the Pontus, the promontory of Carambis, the mouth of Lake Maeotis, and the mouth of the Tanais, the aforesaid measurement is proved. And if this course be crossed by sea, it shortens the distance by six hundred and seventy-eight miles.

{{616}} Thus far Artemidorus asserts that progress has been ascertained, yet Isidorus added that it stretches twelve hundred and fifty miles further as far as ultimate Thule—which opinion indeed seems to me uncertain, for I declare that there is just as much breadth in the earth itself as is comprised in its length; nor, since the foregoing construction and reason have shown the orb of the earth to be round, could an unequal side belong to a globe. Yet the writers mentioned above were speaking of the habitable earth.

{{617}} The outermost rim of its rotundity, moreover, is encompassed by the surrounding Ocean, as is proved by having been sailed around on every side: for from Gades along the curve of Spain and the Gauls the entire western quarter is sailed even today.

{{618}} When, moreover, the deified Augustus sailed around Germany with a fleet, he traversed the entire Northern Ocean: for coming first to the Cimbrian promontory [Jutland], having measured out a vast sea thereafter, he penetrated as far as the Scythian region and the frozen waves.

{{619}} Likewise, from the eastern border, from the beginning of the East and the Indian Sea, the part which slopes toward the Caspian Sea was rowed through by the fleets of the Macedonians while Seleucus and Antiochus reigned. Moreover, the Maeotic Marsh is accounted a gulf of that same Ocean.

{{620}} Let the waters flowing round the left (southern) side likewise be recounted. From the very Pillars of Hercules, which are consecrated on the shore of Gades, all the way to the Arabian Gulf, the entire Southern Ocean is traversed by ships, as is affirmed by very many examples.

{{621}} For the victories of Alexander the Great furrowed the greatest part of it; when Gaius Caesar, son of Augustus, was stationed in that same Arabian Gulf, the wreck of Spanish ships and cast-up fragments of wreckage came to light; likewise Hanno, while the Punic empire flourished, by sailing around Mauretania and thereafter coursing along the southern curve, reached the borders of Arabia in an exceedingly long voyage; the author Cornelius [Nepos] affirms that a certain Eudoxus, fleeing the snares of a king, came from Arabia to refuge at Gades; Caelius Antipater confirms that he saw a man who, out of eagerness for trade, had sailed from Spain to Ethiopia; and the same Cornelius relates that after Indians were captured [off Germany], he sailed past Germany.

{{622}} I think I have shown the compass of the upper earth, measured out in a circle by the sea sailed around on every side. Now I shall recount the division of the earth itself, so far as a succinct introduction permits. The entire circle of the upper and habitable earth, as most testify, is distributed into three parts: Europe, Asia, and Africa—of which the first and the last are separated by the breaking in of the Ocean.

{{623}} For the force of the Atlantic deep, cutting through the depths of the earth from the Strait of Gades, severed the Libyan side from the Iberian and straightway poured itself into the waters of the neighboring sea. Indeed, from the entrance at Gades to the entry of the inner sea, scarcely fifteen miles are counted along the length of the channel; while its breadth, where narrower, is unfolded in five miles, where wider, in seven, and where broadest, in ten. From here have flowed forth through diverse gulfs and subsiding plains so many seas, so many roarings of surf, and all the swelling billows across diverse waters.

{{624}} Now that bursting-in of the intervening Ocean makes Europe on the left and Libya on the right, and is hemmed in on either side by mountains: for on the side of Europe it is bounded by Calpe [Gibraltar], and on the side of Africa by Mount Abinna [Jebel Musa], which, jutting out on either side, have deserved to be called the Pillars of Hercules, because by the testimony of antiquity the boundary of Hercules’ labor was consecrated in them—inasmuch as trackless wastes of exhausted earth forbade advancing beyond that limit.

{{625}} Finally, this too was believed concerning the power of his sacred valor: that whereas previously nature had separated the seas from the lands and held them fast only by the unbroken circuit of Ocean, having dug through the roots of the aforesaid mountains and torn apart the barrier of the plains, he admitted an unforeseen sea into the sloping basins and hollows of the earth for the use of active mortals, changing the face of the world and the boundaries of nature.

{{626}} From this strait, then, Europe stretches to the left as far as the rushing stream of the river Tanais [Don], beginning from which Asia is bounded in like manner by the channel of the Nile—which Nile, breaking through Asia and Africa, intersects the expanse of the earth with a multitude of streams. Very many, however, have said that Europe is bounded by the jaws of the Propontis, which Propontis, widening after the straits, is carried onward all the way to Lake Maeotis.

{{627}} Nevertheless, the beginning of Europe and the threshold of its commencement is assigned to Spain, a fertile and rich province, to be extolled for its yield of metals and gold, and for its gifts of cinnabar, marble, and gems—which indeed, because it is channelled by the course of the river Hiberus [Ebro], deserved the surname of its own river. That portion of it which borders on the Atlantic Ocean and runs out to the shore of the Strait of Gades is called Baetica, having itself also received its name from the designation of its own river [the Baetis / Guadalquivir]; while that which runs from the border of Urci, where it lies nearer to Rome, is called Tarraconensis from the city of Tarraco, which the Scipios founded—for the Carthaginians founded Carthago [Nova], marking the cities they founded everywhere with a name dear to themselves.

{{628}} Yet the aforesaid flank is cut off from the ridges of the Pyrenees into another province, inasmuch as Lusitania is joined to Baetica on its northern border, to which myth gave its name from the sport (lusus) of Father Liber or of those reveling with him. This too is traversed by a river of the same name, although the Tagus also adorns it with golden sands.

{{629}} There they say the town of Olissipo [Lisbon] was founded by Ulysses, from whose name is called the promontory that separates seas and lands. For from its circuit begins the Gallic Sea and the face of the Northern Ocean, while the Atlantic and Western Ocean ends there, bounded at that point by the jutting headlands of Spain. In its neighborhood also the breeding of mares conceives by the impregnating winds, producing foals swift in running when Favonius himself breathes upon them.

{{630}} Now Baetica surpasses all provinces in the richness of its fertility; it has four judicial circuits (conventus): those of Gades, Corduba, Astigi, and Hispalis, and one hundred and seventy-five towns thronged with a multitude of inhabitants.

{{631}} The same province stretches in length from the border of the town of Castulo to the town of Gades two hundred and fifty miles, and from the seacoast of Murgi it is reckoned twenty-five miles longer; while its breadth runs out to two hundred and twenty-four miles.

{{632}} The entire province [of Tarraconensis], however, has seven circuits: Carthaginiensis, Tarraconensis, Caesaraugustanus, Cluniensis, of the Astures, Lucensis, and of the Bracari; and in its whole compass, excluding islands and towns attributed to others, it numbers two hundred and ninety-four communities. It stretches in length, as Agrippa reckons, four hundred and seventy-five miles, and in breadth two hundred and twenty-four, extending its borders all the way to Carthago—which opinion of Agrippa admits no small error.

{{633}} For all Nearer Spain (Hispania Citerior) draws out its length from the Pyrenees to the border of Castulo through six hundred and seven miles, its coast being reckoned slightly longer; while its breadth from Tarraco to the shore of Oiarso is found to be three hundred and seven miles from the roots of the Pyrenees, where it is narrowed by the proximity of both seas, for gradually widening where it borders on Further Spain it adds just as much and more to the increase of its breadth.

{{634}} That same Pyrenees range, then, borders on the other side upon the confines of the Gauls, from whose roots whatever lies between as far as the Rhine, and likewise between the Ocean and Mount Cebenna and the Jura, is assigned to the Gallic regions. Now the province washed by the inner sea is called Narbonensis; this was formerly called Bracata, and is separated from Italy by the ridges of the Alps and the river Varus; its length, as Agrippa measured it, is three hundred and seventy miles, and its breadth two hundred and forty-eight.

{{635}} In this province the river Rhone, coming from the Alps, passes through Lake Leman [Geneva] and brings causes of fertility to the shoots and fruits of the earth. Its mouths are called by different names: for one is named Hispaniense, the second Metapinum, and the third, through which it flows fuller, Massilioticum. It is a province memorable for its men, towns, and crops, and to be passed over for a single abomination—that there it was held lawful to sacrifice human victims.

{{636}} Yet beyond the mountains of the Alps, where the face of the jutting land is bathed by the illumination of the rising sun, Italy begins and descends—far before all other lands to be extolled by the praises of the ancients for the sake of the city of Rome.

{{637}} Its beginning is held by the Ligurians, and thereafter the region of Etruria, consecrated by the fertility of its soil, took possession—celebrated alike for the treaty of native Aeneas, for the origin of remedies, and for the plowing-up of Tages himself. Next come the Umbrians, Latium, and the mouths of the Tiber, and thereafter Rome herself, capital of the nations, to be compared while she flourished in arms, men, and sacred rites to the praises of your heaven; she is separated from the sea by sixteen miles. Thereafter the coast yielded to the name and lot of the Volsci;

{{638}} presently Campania, the Picentes, the Lucanian flank, and Bruttium are joined thereto; whose promontory, looking toward midday and the south wind, runs out into the sea and embraces with its left wing the waters of the inner gulf—so that if thou contemplatest from the first Alps the right flank and the arm of the long mountain range, comparing the headlands on either side, thou wouldst seem to behold a kind of theater of the waves. For crescent-shaped and in a kind of semicircle Italy curves round to the aforesaid promontory; finally, from Gades itself, where the jaws of the intervening strait open, the seas sent forth come to rest in the Bruttian Gulf, which is the first gulf of Europe.

{{639}} The inner land, however, which is carried toward the Upper Sea [the Adriatic] and the North Wind, is inhabited by the Greek coast [Magna Graecia], the Sallentini, Pediculi, Apulians, Paeligni, Histri, Liburni, and other more obscure peoples. It also bends to the left and likewise forms with two promontories the shape of an Amazonian shield (pelta), stretching Leucopetra with its right horn and Lacinium with its left. Whence, while its length from the Alps at Augusta Praetoria [Aosta] through the City, Capua, and the town of Rhegium, without its curving bend, is one thousand and twenty miles, nowhere does its breadth exceed three hundred miles. From the soil of Italy, then, Liburnia and Histria are separated by one hundred miles; Italy is distant from Epirus and Illyricum by fifty miles, from Africa by less than two hundred miles, as Varro records, from Sardinia by one hundred and twenty, from Sicily by one mile and a half, from Corcyra by less than eighty miles, and from Issa by fifty.

{{640}} Now the entire circuit of Italy stretches two thousand and forty-nine miles; its navel is said to be in the territory of Reate. It is narrowest of all at the harbor which they call the Camp of Hannibal, for there it is scarcely forty miles wide. Yet Italy is also memorable for the river Po, which Greece called Eridanus. Mount Vesulus [Monte Viso], loftier among the mountains of the Alps, begets this river from a wondrous spring, which creates a stream within the borders of the Ligurians, and thereafter the river, plunging into the depths of the earth, emerges again in part of the territory of Vibo. At the rising of the Dog Star it grows wider: for when the snows of the Alps have melted by the blaze of the summer sun, it overflows beyond the banks of its channel, and yielding to the glory of no famous rivers, having received thirty streams, pours itself, rich in great majesty, into the Adriatic Sea.

{{641}} Nor do the poets pass over in silence the remaining memorable places of Italy, such as the town of Scyllaeum with the river Crataeis, who was the mother of Scylla, the whirlpool and twisting eddy of Charybdis, the rose-beds of Paestum, the crags of the Sirens, hence from of old the gifts of most lovely Campania, thereafter the Phlegraean Fields and Circe’s dwelling at Tarracina—formerly an island, now joined to the mainland—and Rhegium severed from the mainland of Sicily, which in compensation for the joined land was banished into an island, and Formiae also, famous as the dwelling of the Laestrygones.

{{642}} In this place I could also run through the founders of cities: such as the Janiculum from Janus, Latium from Saturn, Ardea from Danae, and Pompeii from Hercules when he was leading the procession of the Iberian cattle—from whose labors in Liguria the Stony Plains (Campi Lapidarii) were named, because while he was fighting there they say the sky rained stones. The same hero also gave his name to the Ionian Sea, for he slew Ione, daughter of Autochus, who was besetting the roads as a brigand. Iapyx, son of Daedalus, founded the Iapygians; Dardanus founded Cora; the Pelasgi, Agylla; Catillus, admiral of Evander’s fleet, founded Tibur. Parthenope, now called Naples, was named from the tomb of the Siren called by that name; Praeneste from Praenestes, grandson of Ulysses, though others would have Caeculus as its founder, whom they assert to have been a child of the flames. Diomedes founded Arpi and Beneventum, Antenor Patavium, and the Pylians Metapontum. Yet it is of no concern to me to search out the origins of cities.

{{643}} Now since the boundary of the mainland is marked off by the confinement of the intervening strait, it is not out of place to mention the lands emerging amidst the waters—which, because they are in the salt sea (in salo), are called islands (insulae)—and especially the famous ones; nor indeed ought our survey to be tedious. I pass over, therefore, the Pityussae, named from their pine forest, which is now called Ebusus [Ibiza], in the neighborhood of Carthago Nova, and the Balearic islands, Colubraria, and those which the Greeks call the Gymnesiae, or shipwreck-causing Capraria. On the coast of Gaul also, in the mouth of the Rhone, is Metina, presently that which is called the island of the Blasci, and the three Stoechades, whose individual names are these: Prote, Mese (which is also called Pomponiana), and the third Hypaea, and the remaining small islands opposite Antipolis [Antibes].

{{644}} In the Ligurian Sea, moreover, is Corsica, which the Greeks called Cyrnos, one hundred and fifty miles long and fifty broad; its entire circuit extends three hundred and twenty-five miles, and it has thirty-three communities. On this side of it is Oglasa, and sixty miles below Corsica is Planasia, treacherous to sailors with its deceptive closeness; likewise Urgo and Capraria, which the Greeks called Aegilion; likewise Igilium and Dianium; likewise Columbaria and Venaria. Likewise beyond the mouths of the Tiber, off Antium, are Palmaria and Sinonia, and off Formiae, Pontiae, Pandateria, Prochyta, and Aenaria—so named from the anchorage of Aeneas’s ships, called Inarime by the Greeks—Pithecusa, and Megaris also in the Bay of Naples. Eight miles from Surrentum is Capreae; likewise Leucothea.

{{645}} Next, facing the African Sea, eight miles from Corsica is Sardinia; in which narrow strait are small islands called the Cuniculariae, likewise Phitonis and Fossae. Now Sardinia, named from Sardus son of Hercules, has the shape of a human footprint; in fact, it is called Sandaliotes and Ichnusa, both of which signify the shape of a footprint. On the east it extends one hundred and eighty-eight miles, on the west one hundred and seventy-five, on the south seventy-seven, and on the north one hundred and twenty-five. Off Cape Gorditanum it has two islands called the Islands of Hercules; off Sulci, Enosis; off Caralis, Ficaria and Galata. Some also mention not far off Leberides, Callode, and Heras Lutra. Opposite Paestum is Leucasia, named from a Siren buried there; opposite Velia, the Oenotrides; opposite Vibo, the Ithacesiae, named from Ulysses’ watchtower.

{{646}} Hence Sicily must be recounted, which is called Sicania by Thucydides and Trinacria by many: Sicania from King Sicanus, who arrived in that land with an Iberian band before the Trojan War; thereafter Siculus, son of Neptune, by whom his name was lent to it. It is called Trinacria because it is reckoned triangular with three promontories: for one, which looks south from Pachynus toward the Peloponnese, is separated from Greece by four hundred and forty miles; Pelorias, however, looks toward the west and Italy, separated by a strait of a mile and a half; and Lilybaeum sees the borders of Africa at one hundred and eighty miles. The promontories themselves are separated from one another by land journey thus: from Pelorus to Pachynus, one hundred and seventy-six miles; thence to Lilybaeum, two hundred; thence to Pelorus, one hundred and forty-three.

{{647}} There are five colonies in Sicily, sixty-three cities, very many rivers and springs, and the wonders of Mount Etna vomiting fires through whole nights, whose crater, gaping twenty stadia wide, sprinkles Tauromenium and Catina with hot ash, bellowing with mighty uproar and thundering forth immense crashes from the struggling of its flames.

{{648}} In the Sicilian Strait also there is no doubt that there are islands: toward Africa, Gaulos, Melita [Malta] at eighty-eight miles from Camarina and one hundred and thirteen from Lilybaeum, Cossyra, Hieronnesos, Caene, Lepadusa, Aethusa—which others have called Aegusa—and the rest. There are others on this side of Sicily: opposite the river Metaurus, at twenty-five miles from Italy, are the seven Aeolian islands, called Vulcaniae by our countrymen, with diverse names—the first is called Lipara, the second Therasia, the third Strongyle [Stromboli], in which Aeolus is said to have reigned and to have discerned from the flame bursting forth close by or from its smoke what wind was about to blow, which even today the inhabitants of that place are certain to foreknow. The fourth island is Didyme, the fifth Ericusa, the sixth Phoenicusa, and the last Euonymos.

{{649}} Here is the first gulf of Europe; from Locri, however, where the shore of the Ausonian Sea extends ninety-six miles and parts into three bays, begins the brow of Italy, which is called Magna Graecia, where there is an abundance of rivers and towns, and then the harbor called the Camp of Hannibal, where the breadth of Italy is reckoned at only forty miles.

{{650}} Thereafter, from Cape Lacinium begins the second gulf of Europe, curving in a great sweep and ending at the Acroceraunian promontory of Epirus, from which the town of Crotona is distant eighty-five miles. From here, past very many towns and bays, Italy, jutting far out into the sea, comes to the city of Hydruntum [Otranto], where the Upper and Lower Seas are separated by nineteen miles, whence is the shortest crossing into Greece. And thereafter Italy is led past very many peoples, bays, cities, rivers, mountains, and barbarians to the borders of Illyricum, whose length from the river Arsia to the river Drinius runs five hundred and five miles, and from the Drinius to the Acroceraunian promontory one hundred and seventy-five miles. The coast of Illyricum has more than a thousand islands.

{{651}} The third gulf of Europe, moreover, beginning at the Acroceraunian mountains, ends at the Hellespont, and contains nineteen bays and very many provinces: for there are Epirus, Acarnania, Aetolia, Phocis, Locris, Achaia, Messenia, Laconia, Argolis, Megaris, Attica, and Boeotia; and likewise on the other flank and another sea, Phocis and Locris, Doris, Phthiotis, Thessaly, Magnesia, Macedonia, Thrace—all of Greece. Epirus begins at the Ceraunian mountains, wherein first are the Chaones, from whom Chaonia is named; next Mount Pindus, the Dryopes, and the Dodonaean Molossi, celebrated for the famous shrine of Jove. Behind them, among other peoples, Moesia and Maedia border on the Pontus; the Thracians extend all the way to the Pontus. Presently the loftiness of Rhodope and Haemus is seen. Then the colony of Buthrotum; the Ambracian Gulf receiving the sea through narrow jaws, into which is carried down the river Acheron, famous for the dread of underworld myth. After several tribes comes also the colony of Actium with the temple of Apollo, from which a surname was added to the god himself, and the city of Nicopolis; as one exits the Ambracian Gulf into the Ionian Sea, one is met by a diversity of peoples and the Aetolians, among whom, besides other cities, is a town on the border of the sea named Calydon, most famous for the lineage of Diomedes and Tydeus. Thereafter an inflowing gulf divides Aetolia and the Peloponnese; next in Acarnania is Aracynthus, in Aetolia ‹…› the Ozolae, among whom is the town of Oeanthe, where is the harbor of Apollo. Then next in the plains of Phocis is the town of Cirrha and the harbor of Chalaeon, seven miles inland from which is the town of Delphi beneath Mount Parnassus, famous for the oracles of Apollo; there is the Castalian spring, and then in the innermost gulf is the corner of Boeotia next to Mount Helicon.

{{652}} The Peloponnese is a peninsula drawing angular recesses between two seas, the Aegean and the Ionian, having five hundred and sixty-three miles in circuit and almost as much along its bays. The narrow neck whence it projects is called the Isthmus: across five miles the two different seas beat from either side and wear away the flanks of the passage. In the middle of this interval which I have called the Isthmus is the colony of Corinth, sixty stadia from either shore, looking out upon both seas from its topmost citadel, which is called Acrocorinthos. From the Isthmus, moreover, begins the name of the province of Achaia, which was formerly called Aegialos on account of the cities set in order along the shore; there are very many bays, harbors, mountains, cities, and peoples.

{{653}} From the narrows of the Isthmus itself begins Hellas, called Greece by our countrymen. First therein is Attica, anciently called Acte, which touches the Isthmus on its Megarian side; on its coast is the harbor of Schoenus, the Scironian cliffs six miles in length, and the harbors of Piraeus and Phaleron joined to Athens, which lies five miles back, by a long wall. And Attica ‹…› There are many springs there, among which Callirhoe is extolled. Nor does Boeotian Thebes yield to Athens, wherein are Epicrane, Arethusa, Hippocrene, Aganippe, and Gargaphie.

{{654}} After very many towns, mountains, and peoples follows Haemonia, behind which lies Mount Oeta, and which is also called Thessaly. There was born a king named Graecus, from whom Greece was named; there Hellen, from whom the Hellenes were called. In Thessaly the most famous mountains are Olympus, Pierus, and Ossa, opposite which Pindus and Othrys, the seat of the Lapiths, look toward the west with their peaks, for Pelion beholds the east. All these mountains curve like a theater, in front of which seventy-five cities must be recounted. Neighboring Thessaly is Magnesia, whose spring is Libethris, celebrated for the name of poetic draught. The length of Thessaly has four hundred and ninety miles, its breadth two hundred and ninety-seven.

{{655}} Next comes Macedonia, renowned for its hundred and fifty peoples and its greatest kings, especially Alexander—who, though believed to have been begotten by Philip, his mother Olympias declared had been conceived from a serpent. Conqueror of the world, he had the same limits of travel and victory as Liber and Hercules. This Macedonia, moreover, received into its name the peoples called Edonii and all Mygdonia, Pieria, and Emathia. It draws its boundary to the Thracian border: on the south it is bounded by the Epirotes, on the west by the Dardani and Illyrians, on the north by Pelagonia and Paeonia; between it and Thrace the river Strymon serves as boundary, flowing down from the summit of Mount Haemus. In it is Rhodope, which they assert to be a Mygdonian mountain, and Athos severed from the mainland when Xerxes used the forces of the Persians ‹…› and the land of Orestis, which there is no doubt is named from the son of Orestes and Hermione. There is Phlegra, now a city, then famous for the Giants and the rashness of monstrous battles; next Athamania, conspicuous for the height of its mountains, which alone is asserted not to have been covered even by the deluge of the world, which the loftiness of its mountains certainly brought about.

{{656}} Next follows Thrace, whose barbarian inhabitants have the greatest appetite for death, and on the right bank of the Strymon dwell the Bessi and Denseletae as far as the river Mestus, which encompasses Pangaeus. For Odrysian snows fill the Hebrus, which, flowing among diverse barbarians, washes the Cicones too, on whose border Haemus, lifting its peak six miles high, is blown upon by the neighborhood of the stars. Its back likewise is held by diverse nations, among whom are the Getae, Sarmatians, and Scythians. Thereafter the Sithonian race holds the Pontus, which obtained the glory of Orpheus born among them in the perfection of a bard, for on the Spertian promontory he devoted his life either to sacred rites or to his lyre.

{{657}} Presently comes the region of Maronea and the town of Tyrida, in which the horses of Diomedes were stabled. Around that place is Abdera, to which, when built, the sister of Diomedes assigned her own name—a town adorned rather by the birth of the natural philosopher Democritus. Nor do the poets pass over that same Thrace marked by the tomb of Polydorus. There is the promontory of the Golden Horn (Ceras Chryseon), celebrated for the town of Byzantium, which is distant seven hundred and eleven miles from Dyrrachium; for by that interval the two seas—namely the Adriatic and the Propontis—have withdrawn from one another. There the Hellespont, narrower, divides Europe from Asia across seven stadia.

{{658}} Here too are two cities, of which one, namely Sestos, belongs to Europe, and Abydos to Asia. Next come opposite promontories: Mastusia of the Chersonese, where the third gulf of Europe ends, and in Asia Sigeum, where the place called Cynossema is mounded over with the tomb of Hecuba. On the border is the island of Tenedos, from which a wider gulf extends all the way to Chios, on whose right side lies Antandrus. Finally, the smallest of the islands is widely known to sailors for its likeness to a goat (aix), and from it the Aegean Sea is named. Yet on the Phalarian promontory of Corcyra a crag imitates the shape of a ship, into which fabulous antiquity feigned that the ship of Ulysses was turned.

{{659}} Next Crete, wealthy with a hundred cities, stretches between east and west, to which the daughter of Hesperis, fair among the nymphs, granted her name, or Cretes king of the Curetes, from whom Crete was called first Aeria, then Curetis, and afterward, on account of the mildness of its sky, was named Macaronesos (Isle of the Blessed). It extends in length two hundred and seventy miles; its breadth does not exceed fifty miles. Its entire circuit extends five hundred and eighty-eight miles, with the lofty mountains Ida and Dictynnaeus. Euboea, on the other hand, is separated from the mainland of Boeotia by so slight an intervening Euripus that it is generally joined by a bridge. It is adorned by two promontories: Geraestus toward Attica and Caphereus toward the Hellespont. Its breadth is narrowed beyond the second mile and within the fortieth; while the length of the whole island from Attica all the way to Thessaly is reckoned at one hundred and fifty miles, and its circumference is unfolded at three hundred and sixty-five miles. Its town is Chalcis, opposite Aulis, and Carystus with marble of one color with the waves.

{{660}} In the Myrtoan Sea, moreover, are the Cyclades, of which the better known are Delos and Andros—which received their name from their circular arrangement. Likewise the Sporades, of which Naxos is memorable for the tomb of Homer, and very many others stretch seven hundred miles in length and two hundred in breadth. Between the Chersonese and Samothrace lie certain desolate and now barbarous places.

{{661}} The fourth of the great gulfs of Europe, beginning at the Hellespont, ends at the mouth of Lake Maeotis: for the vast sea flowing between Europe and Asia is compressed into narrows of seven stadia, which narrows they call the Hellespont; here Xerxes, king of Persia, having gathered ships together and built a bridge, led his army across. Now these narrows are drawn out in a kind of channel for eighty-six miles, and again the sea spreads out exceedingly wide and once more contracts; that widening of the sea is called the Propontis, and the second narrows are called the Bosporus, which open five hundred paces wide. Here likewise Darius, father of Xerxes, sent his forces across by a bridge. Its length from the Hellespont is found to be two hundred and thirty-nine miles.

{{662}} Next comes the Scythian gulf of the wide-spreading sea [the Black Sea], in the middle of which Lake Maeotis is joined by its mouth; that mouth is called the Cimmerian Bosporus. It is two miles and five hundred paces wide; between the two Bospori—that is, the Cimmerian and the Thracian—lie five hundred miles. The circuit of the entire Pontus is two thousand one hundred and fifty miles, as Varro also does not fail to mention, who adds that the length of all Europe has six thousand three hundred and thirty-seven miles and five hundred paces. The river Hister, rising in Germany from the peak of Mount Abnoba and swallowing up sixty rivers, is also called the Danube.

{{663}} Thereafter the Scythian shore is crowded with a manifold diversity of barbarians: for there are the Getae, Dacians, Sarmatians, Hamaxobii, Trogodytes, Alani, and Germans. The entire tract from the Hister to the Ocean is two thousand miles, and four hundred miles in breadth as far as the solitudes of Sarmatia. Not far off are a river, a lake, and a town, all under the single name Borysthenes [Dnieper]; near it is the island of Achilles, celebrated for his tomb. Further inland dwell the Auchetae, among whom the Hypanis rises, and the Neuri, among whom the Borysthenes rises, the Geloni, Agathyrsi, Anthropophagi, and behind them the Arimaspi; then the Riphaean mountains and a region overshadowed by murky darkness.

{{664}} Beyond those same mountains, across the North Wind, are the Hyperboreans, among whom the axis of the universe turns in continual rotation—a race to be extolled for their customs, length of life, worship of the gods, mildness of climate, half-year day, and even as the boundary of human habitation. Now the tract of Sarmatia, Scythia, and Taurica has nine hundred and eighty miles in length and seven hundred and ten in breadth. I judge that nothing further in Europe remains to be recounted, since Asia has claimed even the Hyperboreans for itself.

{{665}} If, however, thou returnest to the left past the Riphaean mountains along the tracts of the Northern Ocean, thou shalt recall thyself once more past the peoples of Germany, the Gauls, and the Iberians to the entrance at Gades. For the Arimphaei, already situated in Asia, lead a life like that of the Hyperboreans, held in veneration by all nations, so that fearful fugitives flee to them as to an asylum. Beyond these are the Cimmerians and Amazons at the Caspian Sea, which breaks in from the Scythian Ocean on the quarter of the summer sunrise. Beyond vast stretches of land Hyrcania too is found.

{{666}} Furthermore, those returning along the Atlantic shore of Ocean past farthest Aquitania and the extremities of Europe come back to Britain, which island lies between north and west, eight hundred miles long and three hundred broad; its circuit holds three thousand eight hundred and twenty-five miles. In its neighborhood are the Caledonian Forest and very many islands, among which are the forty Orkneys almost joined together; likewise the Electrides, in which amber is produced. Yet last of all is Thule, in which at the summer solstice continuous day is spent and at the winter solstice perpetual night, one day’s sail beyond which the sea is frozen solid. Now below, Europe borders on Gaul and Spain, with which, did not the inrush of the Atlantic Ocean forbid it, Africa would be mingled: for the Baetican city of Baelo is separated by thirty-three miles from the town of Tingis [Tangier], which is a colony of Mauretania Caesariensis; Antaeus is said to have been the founder of this town.

{{667}} Africa and Libya, moreover, were named from Afer, son of Libyan Hercules. On the border is the colony of Lixus, in which the palace of Antaeus and his wrestling-match with Hercules are celebrated, as well as the Gardens of the Hesperides. There is a winding estuary, to which the rumor of antiquity alluded as the watchful dragon. Not far off is Mount Atlas, lifting its peak from a lap of sands; the inhabitants call it Addiris. It is raised all the way to the confines of the lunar circle, beyond the power of clouds. On the western side where it looks out upon the shores of Ocean, it is wooded and gushing with springs, though barren with crags; where it looks toward Africa, it is wholly fertile. Moreover, it produces trees like the cypress, yet heavy in odor, which clothe themselves in a down as precious as silk. By day it is silent; by night it both glitters with fires and resounds with flutes, panpipes, cymbals, and drums as Satyrs and Aegipans hold revel.

{{668}} Beyond it along the western shore for four hundred and ninety-six miles lie forest glades occupied by Libyan wild beasts. Nor far distant are seven mountains which from the equality of their peaks are called the Brothers, yet full of elephants, and they lie beyond the province of Tingitana, whose length is one hundred and seventy miles. Likewise the town of Siga gazes directly opposite at the Spanish city of Malaca. On the coast also are Cartenna and the larger town of Caesarea; likewise Icosium, also a colony; likewise Rusguniae and Rusuccuru, Saldae also and the remaining communities, and Igilgili and Tubusuptu. Now the river Ampsaga is three hundred and twenty-two miles distant from Caesarea.

{{669}} The length of both Mauretanias is one thousand and thirty-eight miles, and the breadth four hundred and sixty-seven. From the Ampsaga begins Numidia, celebrated for the name of Masinissa; the Numidians are called Nomads. In its interior is the colony of Cirta, and further inland Sicca and Bulla Regia; on the sea coast are Hippo Regius and Tabraca; further inland is the region of Zeugitana, and that which is properly called Africa. This has three promontories: Candidum, Apollo’s opposite Sardinia, and Mercury’s looking toward Sicily, which, jutting out into the deep, form two gulfs ‹…› from Hippo Diarrhytus. Next comes the Promontory of Apollo, and in the other gulf Utica, memorable for the death of Cato, the river Bagrada, and neighboring Carthage, famous of old in arms, now to be revered for its prosperity; finally Maxula, Carpi, Missua, and Clypea on the Promontory of Mercury; likewise Curubis and Neapolis.

{{670}} Presently comes another division: those who inhabit Byzacium are called Libyphoenicians, which region is encompassed by a circuit of two hundred and fifty miles; its sowing yields interest at a hundredfold increase of harvest. Here are the towns of Pupput, Hadrumetum, Leptis, Ruspae, Thapsus, Thenae, Aves, Macomades, Tacape, and Sabrata bordering on the Lesser Syrtis, to which the length of Numidia and Africa from the Ampsaga makes five hundred and eighty miles, and the breadth two hundred.

{{671}} The third gulf is divided into the twin recesses of the two Syrtes, with a shallow and ebbing-and-flowing sea. Now the Lesser Syrtis is three hundred miles distant from Carthage; to the Greater Syrtis, however, one journeys through deserts inhabited by diverse serpents and wild beasts. Beyond these are the Garamantes; above them were the Psylli; in the bend are the city of Oea and Leptis Magna; thence the Greater Syrtis with a circuit of six hundred and twenty-five miles; then the region of Cyrenaica.

{{672}} This same is the Pentapolitan region, renowned for the oracle of Hammon, which is four hundred miles distant from Cyrene. The five greatest cities there are Berenice, Arsinoe, Ptolemais, Apollonia, and Cyrene itself. Berenice, situated on the outermost horn of the Syrtis, where are the Gardens of the Hesperides, the river Leton, and the sacred grove, is three hundred and seventy-five miles distant from Leptis. From it Arsinoe is forty-three miles, and next Ptolemais twenty-two, and afar off Catabathmon, the Marmarides, and on the shore of the Syrtis the Nasamones. Next Mareotis and Paraetonium. Thence Apis, a place in Egypt, from which Paraetonium is at sixty-two miles; thence to Alexandria is two hundred miles. Now the length of all Africa from the Atlantic Sea together with Lower Egypt is three thousand and forty miles; from the Ocean to Great Carthage is eleven hundred miles; from there to Canopus, the nearest mouth of the Nile, is sixteen hundred and eighty-eight miles.

{{673}} Interior Africa, however, toward the south, with intervening deserts, has the White Ethiopians, the Nigritae, and others of monstrous strangeness, beyond whom lie solitudes to be shunned toward the east; the river Niger is of wholly the same nature as the Nile. Amidst the solitudes dwell the Atlantes, who neither have any names among themselves and curse the Sun because it ever scorches them together with their harvests; these people never seem to dream.

{{674}} The Trogodytes abide in caves, feed upon serpents, and hiss rather than speak. The Garamantes consort with women in common without marriage; the Augilae worship the gods of the underworld; the Gamphasantes, naked and unwarlike, never mingle with foreigners. The Blemmyae are headless and bear their mouth and eyes upon their chest. The Satyrs have nothing of human beings save the face; the Aegipans exist just as they are painted; the Himantopodes (strap-footed) crawl rather than walk because of the weakness of their feet. The Pharusi were companions of Hercules. Beyond these is the end of Africa.

{{675}} Next comes Egypt—that is, the beginning of Asia. Asia alone from the mouth of Canopus to the mouth of the Pontus has two thousand six hundred and thirty-eight miles, and from the mouth of the Pontus to the mouth of Lake Maeotis one thousand six hundred and seventy-five miles. Now Egypt stretches inward toward the south until the Ethiopians confront it from behind, while the Nile, divided to right and left, embraces its lower tract, so that thou mightest call Egypt an island of the Nile—for from the compass of the surrounding river it is also believed to hold the shape of the letter Delta. From the beginning of that river’s division to the Canopic mouth is one hundred and forty-six miles, and to the Pelusiac mouth one hundred and fifty-six.

{{676}} Its uppermost part, bordering on Ethiopia, has many prefectures of towns which they call nomes, among which are Menelaites, the region of Alexandria, and likewise Mareotis of Libya. Heracleopolis, moreover, is an island of the Nile, on which are the town of Hercules, Arsinoites, and Memphites, which reach to the apex of the Delta. The Nile itself is believed to rise from uncertain springs, although King Juba indicates that it rises from a mountain of Lower Mauretania out of Lake Nilides, which is proved by the same animals and evidences of similar breeding. In the entire Delta of the Nile, Artemidorus testifies that there were two hundred and fifty towns. On the shore of the Egyptian Sea, however, stands the most illustrious of cities, Alexandria, founded by Alexander the Great, twelve miles from the Canopic mouth, beside Lake Mareotis, which has very many islands and four hundred crossings, and whose length as well as breadth holds one hundred and fifty miles.

{{677}} Thence beyond the Pelusiac mouth is Arabia, reaching to the Red Sea, which is called Erythraean from King Erythras, son of Perseus and Andromeda. It is also called Red from its color: for there is a spring on the shore which, when flocks drink of it, causes their fleeces to begin changing to a red color. A town of this sea is Arsinoe.

{{678}} Now Arabia stretches all the way to the spice-bearing and wealthy land, yet beside it is Syria, distinguished by many names: for it is called Palestine where it touches Arabia, and Judaea, and Phoenicia, and so much as is accounted interior is Damascene; sloping southward is Babylonia, and from the same quarter Mesopotamia between the Euphrates and the Tigris; where it crosses Mount Taurus is Sophene, on this side of it Commagene, and beyond Armenia Adiabene, formerly called Assyria, and where it touches Cilicia, Antioch. Its length between Cilicia and Arabia is four hundred miles, and its breadth from Seleucia to Zeugma, a town on the Euphrates, is one hundred and seventy-five miles.

{{679}} Arabia ends at Ostracine, sixty-six miles from Pelusium. Apollonia of Palestine ‹…› advancing through one hundred and eighty-eight miles; above Idumaea and Samaria, Judaea spreads far and wide. The part of it joined to Syria is called Galilee, separated from its remaining parts by the river Jordan, which stream rises from the spring Paneas ‹…› the second elevation of Judaea from Jerusalem, on which flank is the spring Callirhoe. On the west are the Essenes, who live without sexual intercourse and without all desires. Hence somewhat further inland is the fortress of Masada, where is the end of Judaea; to it is joined the Decapolis, named from the number of its cities.

{{680}} For those returning to the coast of Phoenicia there is the colony of Ptolemais; behind it are the mountains Lebanon and Antilebanon, and in the intervening plains sits Mount Bargylus also; likewise where Syria ends among the towns of Phoenicia is Antioch, which is divided by the river Orontes. Above it is a mountain named Casius, whose height sees the sun through the darkness at the fourth watch of the night.

{{681}} In Syria the river Euphrates rises from a mountain called Capotes; there also is another river, the Marsyas. Between Syria and Parthia is the town of Palmyra, and presently the river Euphrates, by ten days’ sailing upon which one arrives at Seleucia itself, the greatest city of the Parthians. Yet the divided Euphrates goes to the left into Mesopotamia and pours into the Tigris, while by its right channel it makes for Babylonia, which is the capital of Chaldaea.

{{682}} Yet we must return to the coast of Syria, on which borders Cilicia, wherein is the town of Venus and the island of Cyprus, and the river Paradisus. To Cilicia is joined Pamphylia, whose farthest town is Phaselis; and thereafter Lycaonia, turned toward the jurisdiction of Asia, which on that side where it borders on Galatia has fourteen communities.

{{683}} Now to Pamphylia is joined Lycia, from which begins Mount Taurus, witness of almost half the world, which it would traverse had not the seas stayed it. Sometimes it emerges winding and stretches out into the ridges of the Riphaean mountains under a diversity of names: for among other names this same range is Niphates, Caucasus, and Sarpedon; it is also reckoned under the name of Gates—in one place the Armenian, in another the Caspian—and the same is called Hyrcanian, Coraxic, Scythian, and Ceraunian.

{{684}} In Lycia is another mountain, Chimaera, blazing by night. This country had seventy towns; now there are thirty-six. The town with which it ends is called Telmesus. Hence begins the Asiatic or Carpathian Sea and that which is properly called Asia, which was divided by Agrippa into two parts: one of which is bounded on the east by Phrygia and Lycaonia, and on the north by Paphlagonia; its length is four hundred and seventy miles, and its breadth three hundred.

{{685}} The second part is bounded on the east by Armenia, on the west by Phrygia, and on the north by Pontica. Close by is Caria, presently Ionia, and beyond it Aeolis. In Phrygia was formerly Celaenae, changed in name to Apamea; there Marsyas was both born and slain, gave his name to the river, and contended with Apollo. Indeed, part of it is called Aulocrene, whence the river Maeander takes its rise.

{{686}} Phrygia overhangs the Troad; on the north it borders on Galatia, on the south on Lycaonia, Pisidia, and Mygdonia, on the east on Lycia, and on the north on Mysia and Caria. Thereafter comes Tmolus, blooming with saffron, and the river Pactolus. Of Ionia, Miletus is the capital; there too is Colophon, celebrated for the oracle of Clarian Apollo. The beginning of Maeonia is Sipylus, and Smyrna also, most famous through Homer, around which flows the river Meles; for the Hermus intersects the plains of Smyrna, which, rising at Dorylaeum, separates Phrygia and Caria. Beside Ilium lies the tomb of Memnon. Above the Troad, inland, is Teuthrania, which was the region of the Mysians, while the city of Teuthrania is washed by the river Caicus; there, among all the cities of Asia, Pergamum is most illustrious.

{{687}} For Bithynia is the beginning of the Pontus, and opposite Thrace on the east it has its first borders from the river Sangarius. This river mingles with another river, the Gallus, from which are named the Galli, priests of the Mother of the Gods. This country has been called both Bebrycia and Mygdonia, and from King Bithynus, Bithynia. In it is the city of Prusias, which Lake Hylas waters, wherein the boy of that same name is said to have been carried off. There is the place Libyssa, nearest to Nicomedia; in it is recorded the tomb of Hannibal.

{{688}} Thereafter is the coast of Pontus; after the jaws of the Bosporus and the rivers Rhesus and Sangarius is the Gulf of the Mariandyni, in which are the city of Heraclea, the harbor of Acone—where the poisonous herb aconite grows—and the Acherusian cave, which plunges into the depths of the earth.

{{689}} Thence comes Paphlagonia, behind which lies Galatia. Here too is the city of Enetosa, from whose citizens they assert that the Veneti in Italy sprang. There is the promontory of Carambis, which is two hundred and twenty miles distant from the mouth of the Pontus, and the same distance from the Cimmerians. There also are Mount Cytorus and the city of Eupatoria, which Mithridates had built, though after his defeat it was named Pompeiopolis.

{{690}} Cappadocia, however, withdraws inland, running past both Armenias and Commagene on the left, while on the right it encompasses very many peoples of Asia. On the east it rises up to the ridges of Taurus, passes Lycaonia, Pisidia, and Cilicia, and passing above the tract of Syria at Antioch, stretches partly into Sittacene. From Greater Armenia it is divided by the river Euphrates; which Armenia begins from the Paryadres mountains. In Cappadocia are many distinguished cities, among which are Melitene, which Semiramis founded, and Mazaca, which they call the mother of cities, overhung by Mount Argaeus, which never melts the snows of its whitening summit even beneath summer suns. In this Cappadocia the length of Asia is eleven hundred and forty miles.

{{691}} Next begin the Assyrians of Adiabene, whom Media succeeds in view of the Caspian Sea, girt round by the Caucasus mountains. Yet the Caucasus has passes which they call the Caspian Gates, clefts in the crags barred even with iron-shod beams to check the passage of foreigners, although in springtime they are also blocked by serpents. From these to the Pontus there is no doubt that it is two hundred miles. In the Pontus, moreover, are the islands Symplegades, and presently the region of Margiane, alone in that tract vine-bearing, hemmed in by mountains for fifteen hundred stadia, difficult of access on account of sandy deserts which extend for one hundred and twenty miles. Alexander the Great had chosen the loveliness of the aforesaid region and had there founded the first city of his name, which was destroyed and restored by Antiochus son of Seleucus with the name of his father; its circuit has seventy-five stadia.

{{692}} Thence is the river Oxus, which flows around Bactra with a town and river of the same name. Beyond is Panda, a town of the Sogdians, where Alexander founded a third Alexandria to bear witness to the length of his march; for there altars were set up by Liber, and thereafter by Hercules, in testimony of their boundless toil. That part of the earth is cut by the river Iaxartes, which used to be thought the Tanais, until the general Demodamas crossed it, proved it to be a different river, and beyond it erected altars to Didymaean Apollo.

{{693}} Here the Persian border joins the Scythians; yet from the Scythian Ocean and the Caspian Sea, where the course leads toward the Eastern Ocean, lie deep snows at the outset and thereafter a long desert, beyond which the Anthropophagi have rendered advance trackless. Beyond these are the Seres [Chinese], who sprinkle their trees with water so that the down which produces silk may be gathered; they spurn men of other nations and are content to fulfill trade by laying down their wares without speech. Hence is the Attacene Gulf, resembling the Hyperboreans in blessedness, wherein the inhabitants rejoice who, through the encircling shelter of their valleys, know no pestilential breezes.

{{694}} Thereafter comes India—for an error inserted the Cicones in between. India begins from the Emodi mountains: for stretching into the Eastern Sea from the south, invigorated by the life-giving breezes of the healthful west wind, it is quickened by a second summer every year and reaps its harvest twice; instead of winter it endures the Etesian winds. It had five thousand towns, and was believed to be a third part of the world. Father Liber first entered India and celebrated a triumph. In it the greatest of rivers are the Indus and the Ganges; the Ganges comes down from the Scythian mountains. And there is the boundless river Hypanis, which closed the march of Alexander the Great, as the altars placed upon its bank bear witness. The breadth of the Ganges, where wider, is twenty miles, and where narrow, eight miles, with a depth of a hundred feet; there are kings and a diversity of nations abundant both in armies and in elephants. Beyond the city of Palibothra is Mount Maleus, on which in winter shadows fall toward the north, and in summer toward the south, alternating every six months. In that place the Seven Bears appear for only fifteen days in the year.

{{695}} The men are darker-skinned, for the Pygmies dwell in the mountains, and those who border on the Ocean live without kings. Women rule the Pandaean nation, whose first queen was a daughter of Hercules. In that tract also is the city of Nysa, sacred to Father Liber, and Mount Merus, sacred to Jove, whence comes the myth that Liber was brought forth from Jove’s thigh. There also are two islands to be extolled for their mines and yield of gold and silver, as is proved even by their names—for the one is called Chryse (Golden) and the other Argyre (Silver).

{{696}} All the Indians are adorned with dyed hair, some tinted with dark-blue gleams, others with saffron; they deck themselves with gems; they disregard funeral rites and deem it a distinction to be borne on elephants—yet on the island of Taprobane [Sri Lanka] the elephants are larger than the Indian ones, and the pearls are fuller too. This island extends seven thousand stadia in length and five thousand in breadth; it is split by an intervening river and lies stretched out in front of India, to which is a seven days’ voyage, as has been proved by Roman ships. There too that sea, save for deep channels, has a depth of six paces. There the Seven Bears do not appear, and the Pleiades never; they see the moon above the earth only from its eighth to its sixteenth day;

{{697}} there the brightest star is Canopus, and the rising sun is beheld on the left. In sailing they observe no star, but follow the flight of birds which they carry on board; they sail for four months out of the year. The men there are larger in body beyond the measure of all others, with golden-red hair, blue eyes, and a harsh tone of voice, and are joined to no other nation by commerce of speech. With foreign merchants on the riverbank they set down their wares and barely exchange those that please them. Their span of life is prolonged beyond human frailty, so that he who dies at a hundred perishes prematurely; none sleeps by day; their grain supply is always at the same standard; their buildings are low and small; they know not the vine; they overflow with orchard fruits.

{{698}} They worship Hercules; they elect as king him who is gentler, more dignified, and without offspring, and if he begets offspring during his reign, they remove him, dreading hereditary rule. Together with him, however, thirty others sit in judgment, and if an appeal be made, seventy judges are appointed. The king is arrayed in the attire of Father Liber, and if he offends, all intercourse and speech with him are forbidden and he is put to death. They love agriculture and hunting—of tigers or elephants, indeed—and take delight in fishing, especially for sea-turtles, with whose shells they roof houses capacious enough for whole families.

{{699}} Thereafter dwell the Ichthyophagi (Fish-eaters), whom Alexander forbade to feed on fish; and not far off is what is called the Island of the Sun and the ruddy Bed of the Nymphs, in which every living creature is consumed by the violence of the heat. Presently comes the Hyctanis, a river of Carmania, from which the Seven Bears first begin to be seen; next three islands in which sea-serpents are twenty cubits long. In these parts the Red Sea is divided along its shores into a twin gulf: the eastern one is called the Persian Gulf on account of the habitation of the Persians, which bay opens out with a circuit of two thousand and sixty miles; opposite it the other is called the Arabian Gulf.

{{700}} To Carmania is also joined Persis, which begins at the island of Aphrodisias and has passed under the Parthian name; along the coast where it faces the west it is five hundred and fifty miles. There is the noble town of Susa, in which is the temple of Susian Diana. Nearby is Carbyle or Barbita, at one hundred and thirty miles, in which are men who bury gold in the depths of the earth lest it be in anyone’s use. The Parthian kingdom measures nine hundred and forty-four miles. Now all Media, Parthia, and Persis are bounded on the east by the river Indus, on the west by the Tigris, on the north by the Caucasian Taurus, and on the south by the Red Sea. All these together extend in length one thousand three hundred and twenty miles, and in breadth eight hundred and thirty.

{{701}} Yet of the Chaldaean race Babylon is the capital; indeed, Assyria and Mesopotamia are called Babylonia on account of its renown. The city itself embraces sixty miles with walls that are two hundred feet high and fifty feet wide—nay, even larger, for three finger-breadths are added to each foot of our Roman measure. It is flowed through by the Euphrates; there is the temple of Jupiter Belus, who was the inventor of the science of the stars. This city has now returned to solitude, drained by the proximity of Seleucia. Likewise for this same purpose the Parthians also founded Ctesiphon at the third milestone from it, and it is now the capital of their realms.

{{702}} The hidden parts of Ethiopia and the sun-scorched regions are also inhabited by the tribes of the Trogodytes and Ichthyophagi: the former outstrip wild beasts in running, while the latter surpass sea-monsters in swimming. There are also the Gorgades islands opposite the promontory which is called Hesperu Ceras (Horn of the West); these they say the Gorgons inhabited, to which is a two days’ sail from the mainland. Beyond these are the Islands of the Hesperides, which lie far out in the deepest sea. That the Fortunate Isles [Canary Islands] are situated to the left of Mauretania between south and west admits of no doubt: the first of which is called Embriona, the second Junonia, the third Theode, the fourth Capraria, and another Nivaria, which has a cloudy and snow-thickened air; presently Canaria, full of dogs of immense size—all full of birds, wooded, palm-bearing, abounding in pine nuts, plenty of honey, rivers, and silurus fish.

{{703}} I have run briefly through the layout of the lands, although, flying past all obscure places, I could not linger. Yet in order that I may be known to have measured out the orb of the earth and the seas, I shall briefly impart the entire measurement. From the Strait of Gades along the length in a direct course to the mouth of Lake Maeotis is 3,437 miles. The entire circuit, moreover, from the same starting-point along the aforesaid gulfs inside Lake Maeotis is 15,700 miles, and together with Lake Maeotis itself, 18,290 miles. The measure of Europe alone is 8,294 miles. The length of Africa is 3,794 miles, and its breadth into its Cyrenaic part is 910 miles. The length of Asia, however, is 6,375 miles; its breadth from the Ethiopian Sea to Alexandria situated beside the Nile, through Meroe and Syene, is 1,825 miles. The measure of the earth which I myself have traversed, and of the seas, has been set forth; now, as has been commanded, we shall come to the precepts of the art.»

{{704}} She had spoken; yet the Paphian goddess [Venus], her mouth drawn somewhat tighter, was irked by the entangling delay, and presently leaning upon her attendants, reclined her languid back—all the fairer for being weary. Here Pleasure, who had long been reclining amidst rosy maidens, spoke anxiously: «Whence has this harsh rustic woman with hard limbs traversed the circle of the globe, and running over so many mountains, rivers, straits, and crossroads come to wear away our patience? I would believe that she bristles with thorns on her limbs and never plucks her shaggy legs—for so dusty is she and strong with rustic vigor that she might rightly be taken for a man!»

{{705}} At this remark Jest was roused among the attendants of Venus, and Cytherea herself, into whose ear it was whispered close by, smiled becomingly. Whom the Arcadian [Mercury] checked with a cheerful nod and the circumspect glance wherewith he was wont to look upon her on account of the reproaches of the gods. Yet Pronuba, sitting beside her, said: «No wonder if Venus, with her delights and so courteous a retinue, has hastened to sport playfully, for she is both nuptially joyful and Cyllenius ever smiles winningly upon her.» And with the word Geometry is bidden to hasten to what she had promised, yet in such wise that, touching lightly upon the chief points, she not rouse weariness by slowness. Then she spoke:

{{706}} «My entire exposition, which is propagated into infinity, is distinguished by numbers and lines, which are proved to be now corporeal, now incorporeal: for one thing is that which we behold by the contemplation of the mind alone, and another that which we look upon with the eyes as well. Now the former part, which is conceived by the rules and ratios of numbers, is assigned to my sister Arithmetic. The other is the learned knowledge of lines and sight upon this dust, which, begotten indeed from incorporeal things and fashioned for the senses into manifold shapes, is also borne aloft into heaven from a subtle and scarcely intelligible first principle.

{{707}} And this incorporeal and invisible beginning is found to be common to me with Arithmetic: for her monad is the indivisible begetter of numbers, and by me it is called a point (signum), which, being without extension, is divided by no part; with her the dyad makes a line, while for me a line drawn out in length acquires nothing whatsoever of breadth. Likewise a surface (superficies), however far and wide extended, is reckoned by me without depth, while for her number, which can attach itself to all species collectively and singly, is found to be incorporeal unless it falls upon material things. Incorporeal, therefore, are the first principles of both.

{{708}} Now the primary divisions of figures to be formed in my art are two: one which is called plane, which I am wont to name epipedon (ἐπίπεδον) in Greek, and the other solid, which we call stereon (στερεόν). And the beginning of the former is semeion (σημεῖον), which in Latin is called punctum or signum (a point), while that of the latter is surface, which is called epiphaneia (ἐπιφάνεια). A point, then, is that of which there is no part; and if there be two points, they are joined by a line lying between them. A line, moreover, which we call gramme (γραμμή), is length without breadth.

{{709}} Of lines, some are straight, which I call eutheiai (εὐθεῖαι); others are curved into a circle, which I distinguish as circular (cyclicae), some also spiral (helicoides), and others bent (kampulai, καμπύλαι) according to their obliquity. These lines, nevertheless, are bounded on either end by points, just as they themselves encompass a surface. A surface is that which has length and breadth alone and is devoid of depth, as is color on a body; this the Greeks called epiphaneia, as I have said, and its boundaries are lines, whether it be flat or curved.

{{710}} A plane angle is formed in a plane when two lines touch each other and do not form one straight line by their inclination toward one another. When, however, the lines that contain the angle between them are straight, the angle is called rectilinear, as in Greek euthygrammos (εὐθύγραμμος). When a straight line standing upon a horizontal straight line makes equal angles on the right and on the left, each angle is right (directus), and the line standing upon it is called perpendicular, or in Greek kathetos (κάθετος). An angle greater than a right angle is called obtuse, and one less than a right angle acute. A boundary (terminus) is that which is the limit of anything. A figure (forma) is that which is contained by any boundary or boundaries.

{{711}} A circle is a plane figure contained by one line. This line is called the circumference (periphereia), to which all straight lines drawn from a single point set within the circle are equal; and that middle point of the circle is its center. A diameter is a straight line drawn through the aforesaid point which divides the circle into equal parts. A semicircle (hemicyclium) is the figure contained by the diameter and half the circumference, which that same diameter cuts off. Three straight lines in diverse positions make a triangle (trigonum), four a quadrilateral (tetragonum), and many a polygon (polygonum). And these are called plane figures. Of these there are three kinds: one of which is enclosed by straight lines, which the Greeks call euthygrammon (εὐθύγραμμον); another by curved lines, which they call kampylogrammon (καμπυλόγραμμον); and the third, which is fitted together of straight and curved lines at once, which they call mikton (μικτόν, mixed).

{{712}} A rectilinear figure, therefore, is called three-sided (tripleuros), four-sided (tetrapleuros), and many-sided (polypleuros). A three-sided figure has three forms: for a triangle is either isopleuros (ἰσόπλευρος), which in Latin is called equilateral, because it meets with three equal lines or sides; or isosceles (ἰσοσκελής), which has two of its three lines equal, on which it stands as though on legs, and is accordingly called aequicrurium; or scalene (σκαληνόν), which has all three lines unequal to one another.
