Likewise a four-sided rectilinear figure has five species: the first, which is sustained by four equal lines and right angles, which figure is called a square (tetragonon); the second species, which is right-angled, not equilateral, and is called an oblong (ἑτερόμηκες); the third is equilateral, yet not right-angled, and is called a rhombus (ῥόμβος); likewise that which has its opposite sides equal and its opposite angles equal to one another, yet neither all its sides equal to one another nor its angles right, and is called a rhomboid (ῥομβοειδής); outside these forms, whatever is four-sided is called a trapezium (τραπέζιον). Parallel lines are straight lines which, being in the same plane and produced to infinity, meet one another in neither direction. Enough has been said of quadrilaterals, the likeness of which can teach polygonal figures; and in these are contained pentagons, hexagons, and the rest of the rectilinear kind.

{{713}} Next follows the second kind of figures, which is shaped by curved lines and is called kampylogrammon (καμπυλόγραμμον), of which there are two species: one which holds the proportions of a whole circle—for it is whole when straight lines stretched out on every side from a single central point to its circumference are all equal to one another—and another which shows the varieties of an oval or elongated curve (obducti circuli).

{{714}} The third kind of plane figures is that which they call mikton (μικτόν), which is enclosed partly by curved lines and partly by straight ones, as is a semicircle, whose curve, as I said above, is made by a curved line and the other by a straight line which, as I have said, is called a diameter—in Latin distermina—which, if it be in a full circle, passes through its center to either side of the circumference.

{{715}} Among these kinds of plane figures, some are called constructive (ergastica) and others demonstrative (apodictica). Constructive are those which contain precepts for making any figure whatsoever; demonstrative are those which adduce proofs of what they assert. Now by their Greek names they are called thus: the first systaticus, the second tmematicus, the third anagraphos, the fourth engraphos, the fifth perigraphos, the sixth parembolikos, and the seventh proseureticus. Systaticus is ‹that which teaches how to construct a figure;› tmematicus is that which teaches by what steps we may cut off lines to a prescribed measure. Anagraphos is that whereby it is taught by what steps the remaining figure that has been prescribed can be joined and applied to a given line. Engraphos is that which shows by what steps we may fittingly inscribe within a given circle, for example, a prescribed triangle or some other figure. Perigraphos is the method which teaches how we may circumscribe a given circle, for example, with a square figure. Parembolikos is that which teaches how, for example, we may apply a given triangle to a given quadrilateral so that the area of the quadrilateral increases while its shape is not changed. Proseureticus is the method which teaches how, for example, between two given unequal lines we may find a mean proportional which falls short of the greater line by the same ratio as it exceeds the lesser. These are the general methods of constructive figures.

{{716}} The demonstrative methods, however, are passed over because they are common to me with Dialectic, whom ye have heard. Yet all propositions are unfolded in five parts, which are called by the Greeks thus: first protasis, second diorismos, third kataskeue, fourth apodeixis, and fifth symperasma. In Latin, however, we can translate them thus: first the enunciation of the proposition, second the specification of the question, third the construction of the proofs, fourth the demonstration and confirmation of the statement, and last the conclusion. Let this suffice concerning the kinds of plane figures; let us return to the elements of theorems, for certainly their elements are the line and the angle.

{{717}} The nature of angles is threefold: for an angle is either right (iustus), or narrow (angustus), or broad (latus). Right is that which is perpendicular and always the same; narrow is acute and always variable; broad is obtuse and likewise variable, for when it is broader than a right angle, whether by much or by little, it will still be obtuse, and when thou movest it, it will remain in the same species, because variability resides in the lines as they are made greater or less. Of this comparison of magnitudes there are four species: the first is called isotes (equality), the second homologos (corresponding), the third analogos (proportional), and the fourth alogos (irrational / incommensurable). Isotes is when two equal lines are compared to a single middle one, double or equal; homologos is when compared magnitudes agree in ratio; analogos is when a line exceeded by another by a factor of two exceeds a third by the same ratio; alogos, however, is that which agrees with another neither by equality, nor by a half or a third part, nor by double or triple, nor by any aliquot part whatsoever.

{{718}} Every line, moreover, is called either rational (rhete) or irrational (alogos). Rational is that which is first set down or which is compared by a common measure to the line set down; and rational (rheton) is whatever is commensurable therewith. Now a line set down initially, although not yet compared, nevertheless—because it has not yet been compared as irrational to another, and possesses something which it can accomplish rationally of itself alone—is called rational (rhete). A line becomes irrational (alogos), however, once compared, if it is found to be incommensurable in every respect.

{{719}} Lines which agree with one another in measure we call commensurable (symmetrous, συμμέτρους), and those which do not agree incommensurable (ametrous). And not measure in length alone, but also square (power, potentia) makes them commensurable, and they are called commensurable in square (dynamei symmetroi, δυνάμει σύμμετροι); while those equal in linear measure are called commensurable in length (mekei symmetroi, μήκει σύμμετροι). Therefore, since they are compared both in measure and in square, all lines that differ in both square and measure are wholly incommensurable (asymmetroi, ἀσύμμετροι).

{{720}} From these arise thirteen irrational lines (alogoi): the first of which is called the medial (mese alogos, μέση ἄλογος); the second the binomial (ek dyo onomaton alogos, ἐκ δύο ὀνομάτων ἄλογος), of which there are six species—the first called first binomial (prote alogos), the second likewise second (deutera), the third third (trite), and the rest in order; likewise the third kind is called first bimedial (ek dyo meson prote alogos); the fourth second bimedial (ek dyo meson deutera alogos); the fifth kind is called major (meizon alogos, μείζων ἄλογος); the sixth side of a rational plus a medial area (rheton kai meson dynamene alogos); the seventh side of the sum of two medial areas (dyo mesa dynamene alogos); the eighth apotome (apotome alogos, ἀποτομὴ ἄλογος), of which there are six species—called first, second, third, and so on as above; the ninth first apotome of a medial (mese apotome prote alogos); the tenth second apotome of a medial (mese apotome deutera alogos); the eleventh minor (elasson alogos, ἐλάσσων ἄλογος); the twelfth that which produces with a rational area a medial whole (meta rhetou meson to holon poiousa alogos); and the thirteenth that which produces with a medial area a medial whole (meta mesou meson to holon poiousa alogos). All these, combined with other lines, while they either draw out their own powers or receive those of others, demonstrate by diverse ratios definite measures of areas, which the Greeks call choria (χωρία).

{{721}} Let it suffice to have said thus much concerning plane figures. Now let us consider solid figures, which we call sterea (στερεά). A solid figure is that which consists of length, breadth, and depth, whose boundary is a surface, just as a line is in plane figures. Now a solid figure rests upon the surface of plane figures: for upon a triangular base is raised a pyramid, upon a circle a cone or a cylinder, upon a square a cube, and the rest in like manner.

{{722}} The sphere, capable indeed of containing all figures within itself, rests upon the circles into which it is resolved. Solidity, moreover, produces the general figures which are called by the Greeks pyramids; likewise the prism (prisma), that is, a section which has the form of a figure; likewise the cube (kybos), the cone (konos), the cylinder (kylindros), and the sphere (sphaira). To these are added the noble regular solids composed out of them: the octahedron, the dodecahedron, and the icosahedron. In order that we may demonstrate all these in their proper order in the dust, these postulates must first be granted: let it be permitted to draw a straight line from any point to any point; and to produce a finite straight line continuously in a straight line; and to describe a circle with any center and radius; and that all right angles are equal to one another; and to extend any finite straight line as far as seems fit; and that if a straight line falling on two straight lines makes the interior angles on the same side less than two right angles, the two straight lines, if produced indefinitely, meet on that side on which are the angles less than two right angles.

{{723}} The common notions of the mind are three: things which are equal to the same thing are also equal to one another; and if equals be added to equals, the wholes are equal; and if equals be subtracted from equals, the remainders are equal.»

{{724}} When she saw that these postulates were granted, drawing a straight line upon the abacus, she spoke thus: «How can an equilateral triangle be constructed upon a given finite straight line?» At this word, when the many philosophers who stood round in a crowded throng recognized that she wished to construct the first theorem of Euclid, straightway they began to acclaim and applaud Euclid. At whose praises Geometry herself also greatly rejoiced, recognizing that she was exalted and promoted through the glory of her follower; and hastily snatching from his hands his books, which she had noticed him carrying by chance, she offered and presented them to Jove and the senate of the celestials as a proof of the remaining construction and doctrine. By which deed she was approved at once the most learned and the most gracious of them all.

## Liber VII. De arithmetica

{{725}} After the prudent Measurer of the Earth [Geometria] had fallen silent, the Unwed Maid [Pallas] who spurs on the care of the skillful toward the arts bade the counting-board (abacus) stand thus, and bade the dusty expanse be spread with a sea-green cover traced with the outlines of figures. Then also another of the handmaidens was bidden to summon her sister who measured out the adornment of her learned sibling, and without delay she departed. Then once more divine Pleasure (Voluptas) murmured into the ethereal ear of the Cyllenian [Mercury] himself: «While the Mighty-in-Arms [Pallas] is approving learned maidens to be marveled at by the gods above, dost thou, sluggish, protract the long-awaited joys of desired love and elude the honor thou hast won? Do serious discourses stupefy a languishing bridegroom? Does the virgin behold such disdain with favor, nor does care for the marriage-bed nor the boy of thy mistress [Cupid] beset thee, nor dost thou snatch my honey? What hymeneal law is this? In the sacred rite of Venus, Pallas claims for herself the usage of food; how much better does sweet Wantonness (Petulantia) glow in the bridal chamber! Chaste Tritonia represses the marital mind and comes unfavorably to a bride; Dione must be invoked, and it is fitting for thee rather to celebrate Priapus.»

{{726}} Having heard these words, the grandson of Atlas [Mercury], though he could scarcely restrain his laughter, yet lest he be deemed witless and unequal to pleasantries, answered thus in a merry whisper:

«Although thou urgest me, Pleasure, persuading me to enter the bridal chamber, yet a short circuit will bring forth the skilled maidens; and at last, no tardy loiterer in wedlock, I shall not delay the posts of the couch, and whatever delight that Venus of ours shall bring, I shall not deny even to you. Only let her delight in stolen joys and secretly grant rosebuds and lilies to her little breast, lest now the bridal sense, about to feel a deadly bite, tear her disheveled hair, plucked out with black gall.»

{{727}} At this saying, smiling back and gladder than her wont, Pleasure returned to Venus and whispered everything into her ears. She, with delicious softness and a blush breaking across her cheeks, almost betrayed what had been whispered; and then, glancing sidelong with becomingly languid eyes at the son of Maia [Mercury], she enticed him with a look of promise—while the Saturnian [Juno] from close by chastised her with gazes as of one catching her in the act.

{{728}} While these things were going on, Paedia, who had gone out a little while before, entered with another woman of wondrous comeliness, upon whose countenance shone a certain majesty of most noble antiquity, older even than the birth and origin of the Thunderer himself, radiant with its own light. She seemed venerable also by certain marvels of her head: for at first she gleamed from her forehead with a single, scarcely perceptible ray, from which a second breaking forth flowed down in a kind of line from the first; thereafter a third and a fourth, and then even a ninth and tenth, circled her first-honored and venerable crown with double and triple variations. Yet the rays bursting forth in innumerable multitude she drew back together again, thinned into one by wondrous contractions.

{{729}} A certain veil, moreover, wherewith the works of all nature were covered, concealed her manifold and many-formed garment. The fingers of the maiden, however, were darting back and forth and writhing with a certain wellspring of incomprehensible swiftness. As soon as she had entered, folding her fingers into seven hundred and seventeen numbers, she raised them in salutation to Jove. Then Philosophy, as she stood beside the Tritonian, inquired what Arithmetica had conveyed by such a number. To whom Pallas said: «She has greeted Jove by his own proper name.» And at that moment the ray which had first emerged, stretching forward with the brightness of aligned light, illuminated the crown of Jove himself. At these marvels—as an innumerable multitude of rays suddenly burst forth—several earthly and woodland deities, beholding her, thought Hercules was sprouting forth with Hydrean offspring. And then, when a murmur arose among the earth-born gods muttering together, that pitch-black boy was bidden to command silence. Meanwhile Pythagoras, as he stood among the sages, followed the woman all the way to the counting-board, and as she was already eager to display her art, standing by in dutiful service he held before her a kind of torch of milky light. Then she, before she was bidden to set forth what she brought, began thus:

{{730}} «Not unknown to heaven nor unacquainted with the worldly things which I begot, I have come, disdaining nothing indeed of your assembly, although I reckon that each and all of you sprout forth from my branches; and thou above all, whom the primal emission brought forth before all others, recognize, O Jupiter, the fountain of thy singular and first-begotten nature. Nor let the Mercurial office which I hold make me despicable as mother of you all, since I am eager to prove to you the lineage of your arcane lot and primordial origin. And when I am practiced upon earth, let the host of the stars recognize the venerable mother of its own multitude.

{{731}} Before all else, therefore, let the sacred monad (monas) be invoked, which, vibrating before all things, the numbers subsequently associated with it taught to be the first principle. And if this is a form accidentally belonging first to whatsoever exists, and that which numbers is prior to that which is to be numbered, rightly do we venerate it before him whom they have called the chief. Nor shall I dissemble, from the fact that to those who examine it the monad is one alone and from it all other things are begotten, that it is the sole seed-bed of all numbers, the sole measure, the cause of increases, and the fixed state of decreases. Yet it is everywhere a part and everywhere a whole, while remaining perpetual through all things; for that which is before existing things and is not hidden after they have been consumed cannot fail to be perpetual. That this, therefore, has rightly been named Jupiter, father of all things, the causative power of that ideal and intellectual form indeed attests. After whose pattern one god, one universe, one sun, and the singular moon, and even the four elements which exist are recorded as singular. Although Aristotle, one of my followers, from the fact that it is one alone and ever wishes itself to be sought, asserts that it was named Cupid, because it desires itself, inasmuch as it has nothing beyond itself and, devoid of all elevation or coupling, turns its own ardors back upon itself—and others have called this Concord, others Piety and Friendship, because it is so bound together that it cannot be cut into parts—nevertheless it is more rightly named Jupiter, because it is both head and father of the gods.

{{732}} Finally, when the one, having flowed down into anything whatsoever—even though its line is poured forth indivisible and without signification of breadth—nevertheless makes the dyad (dyas). This dyad, because it is the first procreation, was called Genesis by some. And because between it and the monad is the first conjunction and an altogether similar fellowship, it is called Juno, either consort or sister of the preceding. Moreover, it is capable of mediation, for it partakes of both good and evil. The same is Discord, from which opposites arise, inasmuch as it is the first that can be separated from what adheres to it. In good things, however, the same is Justice, because it rejoices in two equal things weighed alike; and the same is Fellowship (Societas), because the bond by which middle things are connected is held in common on both sides. From this number takes its beginning, and it is imaginable corporeality and the first proof of motion. It is also the mother of the elements (for from the dyad is begotten the fourth number of the elements) and the first form of evenness.

{{733}} The triad (trias), however, must be reckoned the chief number of the odd numbers and perfect. For it is the first to obtain a beginning, a middle, and an end, and it composes the center of mediation with equality of intervals toward the beginning and the end. Finally, the Fates, the sisterhood of the Graces, and a certain virgin whom they call "mighty in heaven and in Erebus" [Hecate] are bound to this number. On this account too it is perfect, because it begets the perfect numbers six and nine. Under its auspice prayers and libations are repeated thrice. Harmony contains three consonances—namely, the octave (diapason), the fifth (hemiolion), and the fourth (diatessaron). Into three spaces the course of time alternates, and therefore divination is recorded in threes. The same is cosmic perfection: for we fit the monad to the Demiurge God, the dyad to procreative matter, and the triad consequently to the ideal forms. The soul also is distributed into a triad of reason, spiritedness, and desire.

{{734}} What shall I say of the tetrad (tetras)? Therein lies the sure perfection of solidity: for it is composed of length, breadth, and depth, and the full decad is completed by these four numbers folded together step by step—namely, one, two, three, and four. Likewise the hundred (hecatontas) is heaped up from the decad by fourfold progression—namely, ten, twenty, thirty, and forty, which make one hundred. And likewise from one hundred, four numbers render a thousand—namely, one hundred, two hundred, three hundred, and four hundred. Thus ten thousand and the remaining increments are filled out. What of the fact that there is no doubt that there are four seasons of the year, four quarters of heaven, and four principles of the elements, as well as four ages of human life, four vices, and four virtues? This square number is assigned to the Cyllenian [Mercury] himself, because he alone is held to be a square god.

{{735}} Next follows the pentad (pentas), which number is attributed to the universe: for if out of the four elements it is itself a fifth under another form, it is reasonably distinguished by the pentad. Indeed, it is coupled by natural mingling, for it consists of both numbers of either sex: the triad is masculine, while the dyad is reckoned feminine. It is also called apocatastatic (cyclically restoring), and whether associated with other odd numbers or with its own kind, it always displays itself. For five times five you have twenty-five; five times three, fifteen; five times seven, thirty-five; and five times nine, forty-five. Likewise there are five zones of the earth, five senses in a human being, and just as many kinds of inhabitants of the world—namely, humans and quadrupeds, creeping things, swimming things, and flying things. Who would deny that this number is a diameter? For the perfection of the decad and the circle is cut in half by its hemisphere.

{{736}} Who, moreover, could doubt that the senary (senarius, six) is perfect and proportional, since it is filled out by its own parts? For it contains within itself a sixth of itself, which is one; a third, which is two; and a half, which is three. Likewise the natural properties, without which nothing can exist, are six: magnitude, color, figure, interval, rest, and motion. Likewise there are just as many differences of motion: for we move forward or backward, right or left, up or down—for that other is a certain eternal motion of the circle. Furthermore, this number is attributed to Venus, because it is formed from the commingling of both sexes—that is, from the triad, which is held to be male because it is an odd number, and the dyad, which is female by evenness; for twice three makes six. Also the solid figure of a cube has six surfaces. The tones of the entire harmony are six—namely, five whole tones and two semitones.

{{737}} The same number, compared through the first motion—that is, the dyad—makes twelve, between which two numbers two means are found, namely eight and nine. One of these is reckoned by my own name and rule (for it is called the arithmetic mean), namely nine; for it is exceeded by twelve by the same number by which nine exceeds six, namely by three. The other number, namely eight, is compared by harmonic (musica) proportion: for it is exceeded by twelve by the same fraction of twelve by which it itself exceeds six, namely by a third—for a third of six is two, and a third of twelve is four. Which is put together by geometric proportion: for the means multiplied together—that is, eight by nine—can do the same as the extremes—that is, six by twelve—for each makes seventy-two. Likewise in larger numbers too the means are composed by the ratios of the extremes under the aforesaid senary ratio. For six times seventy-two makes four hundred and thirty-two; similarly eight times seventy-two makes five hundred and seventy-six. Likewise nine times seventy-two makes six hundred and forty-eight; similarly twelve times [seventy-two] makes eight hundred and sixty-four. And these means multiplied by each other yield the numbers of the extremes agreeing with each other. This first number, namely six, is shown to have begotten the musical consonances: for six to twelve is the consonance of the octave (diapason), six to nine is the hemiolios [3:2, the fifth], and six to eight is the epitritos [4:3, the fourth], namely the consonance diatessaron. Whence Venus is called the mother of Harmonia. Likewise this senary, associated with the square and solid quaternary [four], measures out the hours of day and night; for four times six makes twenty-four.

{{738}} How, moreover, shall I commemorate thee, venerable heptad (heptas, seven)? Thou who, because thou fashionest the works of nature without the contagion of birth-pangs, hast possessed among the gods the name of the Tritonian virgin [Minerva]. For all numbers placed within the decad either beget others, or are begotten by others, or at once beget others and are begotten by others: the monad, dyad, triad, and pentad beget others and are not begotten; the hexad, ogdoad, and ennead are only generated; the tetrad both creates and is created; but the heptad, because it begets nothing, is therefore called a virgin, and because it is born of nothing, hence is Minerva, and because it consists of numbers both masculine and feminine, is called the warrior-maid Pallas. For from three and four seven is made, which number embraces the phases of the moon. First it is horned, which the Greeks call menoeides; next the half-moon, which they call dichotomos; thereafter greater than halved, which is called amphikyrtos (gibbous); presently full, which is called pleroselenos; and likewise as it wanes it repeats the three aforesaid phases. This number signifies the month of the moon: for one, two, three, four, five, six, and seven make twenty-eight. Likewise there are seven circles [orbits] and as many planets, as many stars at the summit of the celestial axis, as many days, and as many transfusions of the elements. For out of formless matter comes first fire, from fire air, from air water, from water earth; likewise the ascent takes place, and from earth is water, from water air, from air fire; from fire into incomprehensible matter it can no longer arrive.

{{739}} What? Is not the nature of human beings proved to serve this number? At the beginning, births in the seventh month send forth a human being complete and perfect. Next, the same human being has seven passages in the head prepared for the senses: two eyes, ears and nostrils just as many, and one mouth. Thereafter in infants teeth emerge in the seventh month and are shed in the seventh year. Likewise the second seven-year period stirs puberty and the capacity for procreation; the third, the down upon the cheeks; in the fourth, increases in stature come to an end; the fifth is the full perfection of youthful age. Nature has also hidden away seven vital organs: the tongue, the heart, the lung, the spleen, the liver, and the two kidneys. Likewise seven parts of the body complete a human being: the head down to the base of the neck, the chest, the belly, the two hands, and the feet.

{{740}} The octonary (octonarius, eight), on the other hand, is the first cube (cybus) and is perfect, dedicated to Vulcan. For it consists of the first motion—that is, the dyad, which is Juno: for the dyad times the dyad makes the tetrad, and twice that makes the ogdoad. It is perfect likewise because it is covered by the senary: for every cube has six surfaces; likewise it is filled out from consecutive odd numbers. For the first of the odd numbers is the triad, and the second the pentad; together they make the ogdoad [3 + 5 = 8]. Likewise the cube which comes from the triad—namely, twenty-seven—is yielded by the following odd numbers, namely seven, nine, and eleven, which all together make twenty-seven [7 + 9 + 11 = 27]. Likewise the third cube, which comes from the tetrad—namely, sixty-four (for four times four is sixteen, and four times this is sixty-four)—is also formed from the four odd numbers which follow the preceding ones: namely, thirteen, fifteen, seventeen, and nineteen make sixty-four together [13 + 15 + 17 + 19 = 64]. And thus all cubes are found through the increments of the odd numbers pertaining to their own rank. Assuredly this octonary cube is first of all cubes in the same way as the monad is of all numbers. Every cube, moreover, is also attributed to the Mother of the Gods; for on that account she is named Cybebe [Cybele].

{{741}} The ennead (enneas, nine) is also perfect and is called more perfect because its form is perfected by multiplication from the perfect triad; next, because it holds the end of the first row [units], and therefore is named Mars, from whom is the end of all things. It is also the square boundary of those numbers which increase by addition. For it is also the final part of harmony: since from the ogdoad to the ennead [8:9], the comparison of percussion produces a whole tone. No fewer than nine Muses have men spoken of. In the universe too there are nine zones—namely, the outermost sphere, those of the seven gods [planets], and that of the earth.

{{742}} The decad (decas, ten), however, is to be held above all, containing within itself all numbers of diverse virtue and perfection. Although it is the end of the first row, it fulfills the aid of the monad for the second row [tens]. This concludes the rules, proportions, genera, species, differences, perfections, and imperfections of the numbers of the first row, and is assigned to Janus, although very many have recorded it as apocatastasis (universal restoration).

{{743}} It has been briefly stated what number makes the first row, which of the gods it contains, and what virtues. Now I shall briefly intimate what number itself is and what proportions and forms it preserves among its members. Number is a collection of monads, or a multitude coming forth from the monad and ending in the monad. Now there are four rules of simple number: the first is that which is called even-times-even (a paribus par); the second, even-times-odd (a paribus impar); the third, odd-times-even (ab imparibus par); and the fourth, odd-times-odd (ab imparibus impar), which I shall record below.

{{744}} There are also those which are called prime numbers (primi numeri), which can be divided by no number except by the monad alone; they are seen not to be divided, but to be put together—as, for example, 7, 11, 13, 17, and others like them; for no number can divide them in a single order. Wherefore they are called prime (primi, first), because they arise from no number and are not separated into equal portions. Born from themselves, therefore, they create other numbers out of themselves, since from odd numbers even numbers are made, whereas from even numbers an odd number can in no way be made. Therefore prime numbers too must be held necessary and fair.

{{745}} Yet let us examine all the numbers of the first row under these rules: the monad indeed is not a number; the dyad is even; the triad is prime both in order and in virtue; the tetrad is even-times-even; the pentad is prime; the hexad is even-times-odd and odd-times-even, whence it is also named perfect; the heptad is prime; the ogdoad is even-times-even; the ennead is odd-times-odd; the decad is odd-times-even. Just as in the first row, so also in the following rows are these rules gathered. The first row, therefore, is from the monad up to the ennead [1–9]; the second from the decad up to ninety [10–90]; the third from the hundred (hecatontas) up to nine hundred [100–900]; and the fourth, which is also the last, from a thousand up to nine thousand [1,000–9,000], although several Greeks are seen to have added myriads [tens of thousands] as well.

{{746}} To me, however, that number alone is approved which is contained by the fingers; otherwise it would be a kind of contorted dancing of the arms. Whereby it comes to pass that we harmonize our numbers with the forms and lines of my preceding sister [Geometria]. For to me in the first row is the monad, to her in the point (signum) is the beginning, which has no parts; in the second row from ten, numbers are stretched out as it were like a line; in the third row squares are formed from one hundred and the rest, which are associated as breadth to the first length; in the fourth row there are already cubes, and therefore solidity from one thousand and the rest. My boundaries or limits, therefore, are the monad, the decad, the hundred, and the thousand, while Geometry’s are the point, the line, the plane figure, and solidity. For the monad is as indivisible as a point; the decad among numbers is like a line of length alone; the hundred is a square, which is a surface and is divided into length and breadth—that is, ten times ten makes the square hundred, and this times ten makes the cube thousand.

{{747}} Every progression of odd numbers from the monad necessarily produces squares at each step: take first the monad itself [1]; add the triad: it has made four [4], the first square; with five associated you have made the second square, nine [9]; join seven, you have filled out the square sixteen [16]; add nine likewise, and you complete the square twenty-five [25]. In the same manner the rule advances all the way to infinity. Yet let me return to the divisions above: every number is either even or odd, and is bounded by either one; whatever is added to a finite number is added as finite, nor can an infinite be made out of finites.

{{748}} Every number, indeed, is either even (par) or odd (impar). Even is that which is divided into two equal parts, as 2, 4, 6; odd, that which cannot be divided into two equal parts, as 3, 5, 7. Next, among odd numbers, some are odd from an odd factor alone [prime], as 3, 5, 7; others also consist of a multiplicity [composite odd], as 9, 15, 21, which the Greeks call perissakis perissoi (odd-times-odd). Among those that are even, however, there are several distinctions, ‹…› whether they are and can be called even. The rest are either even-times-even or even-from-odd; and the former the Greeks name artiakis artioi, while the latter they name either perissakis artioi (odd-times-even) or artiakis perissoi (even-times-odd).

{{749}} Even-times-even are 4, because it consists of twice two, and 8, because it consists of twice four; even-from-odd are both those which are made by an odd multiplication of even numbers, as three times two is six or five times four is twenty—which class the Greeks call perissakis artion—and those which complete an odd multitude of numbers by an even multiplication, as when twice three makes six and four times five makes twenty—which class the Greeks call artiakis perisson. Although these numbers are the same, they nevertheless receive different modes of increase. And of these very numbers, some when divided into two parts fall immediately into odd numbers, while others, halved once or several times through even numbers, are resolved into odd numbers short of unity. For twelve and twenty can be divided through even numbers only once [12 → 6 → 3; 20 → 10 → 5]; whereas forty-eight yields twice twenty-four, thence twice twelve, thence twice six—all still even—and finally falls into threes, which are odd. Thus nothing can proceed further by a similar multiplication; nay rather, just as one ascends by reversed doubling, so through halvings it is likewise distributed into several parts. For twenty is twice ten, ‹and four times five,› and five times four, and ten times two.

{{750}} Four species next receive numbers: for some are uncompounded in themselves (per se incompositi, prime), some compounded in themselves (per se compositi, composite), some uncompounded in relation to one another (inter se incompositi, coprime / relatively prime), and some compounded in relation to one another (inter se compositi, having a common divisor); of which the first two are named first (primi) numbers and the two following second (secundi) numbers. Yet that this matter may be learned the more easily, it must be set forth more plainly. The first and smallest measure [divisor] of every number is unity (singularitas, 1), because there is no number that cannot be divided into units. Next, other measures also receive it, such as doublings, which increase by two, and triplings, which increase by three. Since this is so, certain numbers have a measure in unity alone, which cannot be distributed except into units, such as three—for three is only odd; while others have a measure in other numbers as well, such as 4 and 9: for twice two is 4, and three times three is 9—thus we measure the former by doubling and the latter by tripling. And often a number has not merely one such measure, but several: inasmuch as it is easy to measure eight both by quadrupling and by doubling, since both four times two and twice four are 8. Thus it is evident that whatever we measure by some multiplication we can also measure by unity, though not wherever there is a measure of unity is there also a measure of some multiplication. Thus unity is a measure common to all, and to some it is the only one.

{{751}} Since this is so, numbers are called uncompounded in themselves (per se incompositi, prime) which have no measure except that of unity; while compounded in themselves (per se compositi, composite) are those which may be measured not only by unity, but also by another multiplication. And this indeed is the reckoning of single numbers. Two or more numbers joined together, however, are said to be uncompounded in relation to one another (inter se incompositi, relatively prime) when they have no common measure except that of unity, as 3 and 4. For it makes no difference whether 4 has a measure of the double, since that same measure is not in three. On the other hand, compounded in relation to one another (inter se compositi) are those which share in common a measure other than that of unity, as 9 and 12, each of which may be measured by tripling, because three times three makes 9 and three times four makes 12.

{{752}} Now since some numbers are divided into units alone, while others are divided also into certain whole (solidi) numbers, just as they are distinct in reality, so also shall I distinguish them by terms, lest any confusion arise for the careless; and I shall call the submultiples (membra, proper divisors) of each number the whole numbers into which it can be separated, as in 12 there are 3 and 4; whereas units, and any whole numbers mixed with units that are inserted into it, I shall call parts (partes, aliquot/non-aliquot fractions), as in 7 there are either seven units or even twice three with one unit added.

{{753}} Among numbers, some are perfect (perfecti), some more than perfect (ampliores perfectis, abundant), and some imperfect (imperfecti, deficient): the Greeks call them teleioi, hyperteleioi, and hypoteleioi. Perfect are those which are equal to their own proper divisors (partes); more than perfect, those which have more in their divisors than in themselves; and imperfect, those in whose divisors there is less than in themselves. And for the sake of example let us take six. This can be divided into units, into twos, and into threes, since six times one, three times two, and twice three make six; therefore its divisors are 1, 2, 3. Now let these be brought together into one: they make 6 [1 + 2 + 3 = 6]—this is to be equal to its own divisors, and this kind of number stands in a certain virtue, while the rest stand in vice either by excess or by defect. Take, for example, 12: twelve times one, six times two, four times three, three times four, and twice six make 12. Thus its divisors are 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, which joined into one make 16 [1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 6 = 16]; this number is more than perfect. On the other hand, 16 is formed from sixteen ones, eight twos, four fours, and two eights, nor is there any other measure of that number besides these. Brought together into one, however, 1, 2, 4, 8 make no more than ‹15—that is,› less than that from which they arose [1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15]. This is an imperfect number.

{{754}} Other numbers, moreover, are plane (plani), while others also have solidity (crassitudinem) in themselves. The Greeks say that a plane number is one which is contained by two numbers. It is of this sort: in the reckoning of measurements, they judge as much to be contained by a carpenter’s square (norma) as by the whole quadrangle whose corner lies in that square. Likewise they refer to plane numbers those which are arranged along two sides in such a way as to make a right angle and represent the likeness of a square. Therefore, if 4 are extended along one side and 3 along the other, these two numbers by their rule contain 12, and they name that number plane. Solidity, on the other hand, they say is formed from three numbers. Suppose 4 are placed along one side, 3 along the other, and then four are added above. With these numbers they say that height too is filled out above the lower square and twenty-four is enclosed [4 × 3 × 2 = 24, or 4 × 3 × 4]. In which matters—obscurity having been sought superfluously—it is most evident that a plane number is formed by joining units together so that nothing lies above another, whereas solidity is produced by placing number upon number.

{{755}} The plane itself, moreover, has various forms, as numbers are arranged in the likeness of certain figures, which begin from a line and then become either triangles or quadrangles; and those which have four angles are either squares (quadratae) or oblong (altera parte longiores, longer on one side), which the Greeks call heteromekeis. Besides these, there can also be more angles and at times even unequal sides of a number. When, however, it rises up into solidity and produces several solid figures, the cube (tessera) is seen to be the most perfect among them. Now a triangle consists at the fewest in three [3]; a square at the fewest in 4; that which has unequal sides at the fewest in 5; an oblong quadrangle at the fewest in six [6 = 2 × 3]; and likewise a solid, namely the cube, at the fewest in eight [8 = 2 × 2 × 2]. For two make a simple row; three can be arranged so as to have as many angles [an equilateral triangle]; four placed in a square have two on every side; five may be gathered so that there are two on one side and three on the other; and six so as to make a quadrangle which has two on two sides and three on two sides. And if four [more] are placed [atop four], solidity arises and all sides in breadth and height are equal, with two arranged in every direction.

{{756}} Similar plane numbers are those whose sides have the same ratio, such as 6 and 600, since the former has 2 on one side and 3 on the other [2 × 3 = 6], while the latter has 200 on one side and 300 on the other [200 × 300 = 60,000, or proportional sides 2:3 :: 200:300]. In the same way, similar solid numbers are those whose sides stand under the same ratio, such as twenty-four and ninety-six [or similar solids with proportional edges]. For just as in the former one side has 4 and the other 3, whereby the plane holds 12 and the solid 24 [4 × 3 × 2], so in the latter one side receives 8 and the other six, whereby the plane embraces 48 and the solid 96 [8 × 6 × 2]. Now what stands between two and three stands between 200 and 300; and the ratio which is between 3 and 4 is that which is between 6 and 8. This will be clear as soon as I set down below the ratios which exist between numbers.

{{757}} For every number is a part of some larger number; and the larger number proceeds either by multiplication (multiplicatio), or by the ratio of submultiples (ratio membrorum, superparticular ratio), or by the ratio of parts (ratio partium, superpartient ratio), or at once by both multiplication and the ratio of submultiples or of parts. The ratio of submultiples consists in one aliquot part or several; the ratio of parts consists in one non-aliquot part or several. A smaller number, on the other hand, is diminished either by submultiplication (replicatio), or by the ratio of submultiples or of parts, or at times at once by submultiplication and by the ratio either of submultiples or of parts. Nor is there any ratio of number to number that does not fall within these. The Greeks call multiplied numbers pollaplasioi (multiple), submultiplied numbers hypopollaplasioi (submultiple), those exceeding by a submultiple epimorioi (superparticular), those ‹less by a submultiple hypepimorioi (subsuperparticular), those exceeding by parts epimereis (superpartient), and those› less by parts hypepimereis (subsuperpartient); and they use double names for those in which twin ratios are combined.

{{758}} Since this is so, a number has toward an equal number the ratio of equality (aequalitas), which the Greeks call isotes, as two to two or three to three; which ratio belongs also to a perfect number toward its own divisors, and therefore that number is held superior to the rest. For what can be better than the equal? Where, however, one number is greater and the other smaller, immediately there is a difference (distantia) between them, which occurs in all numbers that either precede or are preceded by the ratio of submultiples or of parts; and therefore those numbers are inferior between which and their parts there is some discrepancy. Yet just as the arithmetic difference between two numbers, greater and smaller, is the same, so the ratio (ratio) between the same numbers is reversed: for there is just as much difference between three and four as between four and three [1], whereas the ratio between these same numbers is different [3:4 vs. 4:3], and what that is will become clear below.

{{759}} Since I have set forth that the first ratios lie in multiplication, the ratio of multiplication belongs to six toward three [6:3 = 2:1] and to eight toward four [8:4 = 2:1]; conversely, that of submultiplication belongs to three toward six [3:6 = 1:2] and to four toward eight [4:8 = 1:2]. By the ratio of submultiples (membrorum ratio, superparticular), that number prevails which exceeds by a single whole aliquot part, such as nine toward six [9:6 = 3:2]: for it exceeds by three, which same three is found twice in the number six; and conversely, by the ratio of submultiples, six is surpassed by nine [6:9 = 2:3]. On the other hand, by the ratio of parts (partium ratio, superpartient), that number prevails which contains within itself both the smaller number itself and some non-aliquot part or parts of it, as when 7 is compared with 4 [7:4]: inasmuch as in the number seven there is both 4 and 3 fourth-parts of it. Conversely, therefore, by the ratio of parts, 4 is surpassed by 7 [4:7]. Again, the same number precedes both by multiplication and by the ratio of submultiples (multiplex superparticularis), if for example 8 and 3 are joined [8:3]: for 8 contains both twice three [6] and in addition a submultiple part in two [2/3 of 3]. And both by multiplication and by the ratio of parts (multiplex superpartiens) 5 prevails if compared with two [5:2, though 5:2 is 2 + 1/2]: for in five there are twice two and in addition one part of two. Conversely, in these very numbers the smaller are surpassed by the larger at once both by submultiplication and either by the ratio of submultiples or by that of parts.

{{760}} Yet just as these are the genera of ratios between numbers, so there are several species in each. For—to come first to multiplication and submultiplication—between these there is either the ratio of the double (dupli, 2:1), or of the triple (tripli, 3:1), or of the quadruple (quadrupli, 4:1), and multiplication can also proceed further, while through the same steps in reverse the same number is submultiplied. Therefore two to one, 4 to two, and 8 to 4 have a ratio greater by the double [2:1]; one to two, two to 4, and 4 to 8, smaller by the subdouble [1:2]; likewise 3 to 1 and 9 to 3, greater by the triple [3:1]; 1 to 3 and 3 to 9, smaller by the subtriple [1:3]; 4 to 1 and 16 to 4, greater by the quadruple [4:1]; 1 to 4 and 4 to 16, smaller by the subquadruple [1:4]; and the same ratio of increase and diminution holds in further multiplications.

{{761}} Where, however, between larger and smaller numbers there is a ratio of submultiples (superparticular), the larger prevails either by a half in addition (superdimidio, sesquialter, 3:2), which the Greeks call hemiolios, or by a third in addition (supertertio, sesquitertian, 4:3), which they call epitritos, or by a fourth in addition (superquarto, sesquiquartan, 5:4), which they call epitetartos, and so the ratio proceeds to the fifth in addition (superquintus, 6:5), sixth in addition (supersextus, 7:6), and beyond. Superdimidius is that which contains some number itself and its half; supertertius, that which contains some number itself and its third; superquartus, that which contains some number itself and its fourth; and the same ratio holds in those beyond. Conversely, of these same numbers the smaller toward the larger is either subdimidius (2:3), which the Greeks call hyphemiolios, or subtertius (3:4), which they call hypotritos, or subquartus (4:5), which they call hypotetartos. Three to two and 300 to 200, of which mention was made above, have the ratio of superdimidius; conversely, two to three and 200 to 300 have that of subdimidius. While 4 to 3 and 8 to 6, which were also set down above, have that of supertertius; 3 to 4 and 6 to 8, that of subtertius; 5 to 4 and 10 to 8, that of superquartus; and 4 to 5 and 8 to 10, that of subquartus.

{{762}} The ratio of parts (superpartient), moreover, is in certain numbers next to supertertius (superbipartiens, 5:3), in certain numbers next to superquartus (supertripartiens, 7:4), and this can proceed further. Next to supertertius is where the larger number embraces the smaller itself and some third parts of it [specifically two thirds, 1 + 2/3 = 5:3]; next to superquartus is where it embraces both that smaller number itself and fourths of it [specifically three fourths, 1 + 3/4 = 7:4]. Let us take 5 and 3, and 10 and 6: five precedes three because it contains both three itself and two thirds of it; likewise in 10 there are both 6 and two thirds of six. The ratio next to superquartus, on the other hand, is between seven and 4, and between 14 and 8: in 7 there are both 4 itself and three fourths of it. Herein no one ought to be deceived into thinking any ratio of parts is like superdimidius: for if any number contains some number and half of it, it is superdimidius [3:2]; if it contains some number and two halves of it, it is double [4:2 = 2:1]. Nor indeed, just as two thirds have a ratio next to supertertius [5:3], do two fourths receive a ratio next to superquartus: for if a number contains a number itself and two fourths of it, it is superdimidius, as are 6 and 4—for in six there are both four and two fourths of it [6:4 = 3:2]. Conversely, just as by that ratio which is next to supertertius and superquartus the larger prevail in these numbers, so the smaller have with the larger the ratio next to subtertius [3:5] and subquartus [4:7], and the same ratio proceeds as it is analogous to superquintus [7:5, 8:5, 9:5] or further ones.

{{763}} Hence again several distinctions arise, inasmuch as one number can be increased by the double ‹or triple or quadruple› and either by superdimidius, or supertertius, or superquartus, or further ratios both of multiplications and of submultiples. Let us set down 4 and 10: of these, 10 is increased by the double and superdimidius [2½ × 4 = 10]; for twice four is 8, and then half of 4 is made in two. Now let us set down 4 and 14: of these, 14 is increased by the triple and superdimidius [3½ × 4 = 14]; for three times four is 12, and then half of four is made in two. Let us advance further to 4 and 18: of these, 18 has increased by the quadruple and superdimidius [4½ × 4 = 18]; for four times four is 16, and then half of four is made in two. Again, let there be 3 and 7: of these, 7 is increased by the double and supertertius [2⅓ × 3 = 7]; for twice three is 6, and then a third part of three is made in one. Let there be 3 and 10: of these, 10 has increased by the triple and supertertius [3⅓ × 3 = 10]; for three times three is 9, and then a third part of three is made in one. Let 3 and 13 be set down: of these, 13 is increased by the quadruple and supertertius [4⅓ × 3 = 13]; for four times three is 12, and then a third part of three is made in one. Let us now take 4 and 9: of these, 9 has both more than the double and superquartus [2¼ × 4 = 9]; for twice four is 8, and then a fourth part of 4 is in one. Between 4 and 13 is the ratio of the triple and superquartus [3¼ × 4 = 13], and likewise between 4 and 17 that of the quadruple and superquartus [4¼ × 4 = 17]. And the same happens in further numbers, and in like manner the smaller numbers among them have with the larger the ratio both of some submultiplication and of subdimidius, or subtertius, or subquartus, or some further ratio.

{{764}} As could appear from these examples, multiplication begins from the smallest ratio and passes continuously to greater and greater ratios, just as does the ratio of submultiples or of parts [in reverse]; while submultiplication begins from the greatest ratio and passes continuously to smaller and smaller ratios. That ratio is called greater which adds more, and smaller which adds less: therefore the ratio of the triple [3:1] is greater than that of the double [2:1], and that of the quadruple [4:1] greater than that of the triple; conversely, that of the double is smaller than that of the triple, and that of the triple smaller than that of the quadruple.

{{765}} Multiplication, therefore, begins from the double, thence passes to the triple, to the quadruple, and ever to greater ratios. On the other hand, the ratio of submultiples (superparticular) begins from superdimidius [3:2], passes next to supertertius [4:3], superquartus [5:4], and ever arrives at less and less. All these ratios lie between two terms. Thus are the numbers: for example, the ratio of the double lies between 2 and 1, of the triple between 3 and 1, of the quadruple between 4 and 1, and under these same ratios of names are the lowest terms, far ‹smaller› by comparison with their multiples; ‹nor› are there smaller terms under the double than two and 1, or under the triple than 3 and 1, or under the quadruple than 4 and 1. Above these, then, as far as you carry out the same ratios, the terms are increased by these numbers. And therefore the Pythagorean Thymaridas named those terms which are lowest pythmenes (roots / lowest terms), because just as a vessel rests upon its own bottom, so numbers of the same ratio are added upon those; and the same holds also in the ratio of submultiples.

{{766}} For the lowest terms (pythmenes) of superdimidius are between 2 and 3, of supertertius between 3 and 4, and of superquartus between 4 and 5; and thereafter numbers are filled out under the same ratios. Nor is the condition of the ‹ratio of parts› different, which itself begins from the third part [5:3], just as the ratio of submultiples begins from the hemiolios [3:2], and first embraces the lowest terms and then passes on to larger ones.

{{767}} From these facts it is likely that multiplication was invented first, next the ratio of submultiples, and then that of parts. For no difficulty appeared in reaching the double, then the triple and quadruple. Next, from the double was made the condition of superdimidius, from the triple that of supertertius, from the quadruple that of superquartus, and the same befell in those beyond. For he who saw the double began by this very fact to understand the half, because just as four is the double of two, so two is the half of four. As, therefore, by adding two to two he made four, so again by adding two to four he made superdimidius [6:4 = 3:2]; and as from two by the triple he filled out six, so by adding two to the number six he found supertertius [8:6 = 4:3], and the same occurred in those beyond. Next, when numbers were encountered set down without the ratios of deliberate judgment, it was inquired how many and what fractional parts of one number were in the other. From these steps it came about that there is no number that does not have some thoroughly explored ratio to another number; after this, a not overly difficult observation flowed forth in numbers by a twofold reckoning.

{{768}} Since I have set forth the classes of numbers and of the ratios arising among them, I shall return again to them individually and point out what observations belong to each. I shall begin with even and odd numbers. An even number proceeds by every multiplication so as to remain even: 2, 4, 8, and 16 are increased by the double; 2, 6, 18 by the triple; 1 [or 4], 4, 16, 64, 256 by the quadruple, and the same occurs in further numbers. An odd number, on the other hand, perishes immediately under an even multiplication and falls back into an even number, whereas it can increase by an odd multiplication so as to remain odd. For twice three makes six, and likewise twice four makes 8; in the same way four times three makes 12, and four times five makes 20; whereas three times three makes 9, and three times 9 makes 27; likewise five times three makes 15, and five times five makes 25, and the same happens in all multiplications. Whereby it comes about that whether the multitude of even numbers summed is even or odd, the total completed is even—as 2, 4, 6, 8, which is an even multitude of numbers, make 20; and 2, 4, 6, which is an odd multitude of numbers, make 12, both even numbers.

{{769}} Likewise an even multitude of odd numbers makes even numbers: thus 3 and 5 make 8, which is even. Only an odd multitude of odd numbers preserves odd numbers: for 3 and 5 and 7 make 15, which is also odd. For the same reason, whenever an even number multiplies either an even or an odd number, the product is even: for doubling, whether it multiplies two, makes 4, or 3, makes 6—both even. An odd number, on the other hand, if it multiplies an even number, makes an even number; if an odd number, then at last it yields an odd number: for tripling, if it multiplies two, produces 6, which is also even; if 3, it produces 9, which is odd.

{{770}} Furthermore, if an even number is added to an even number, it remains even—as if 4 is added to two, the sum is 6. If an odd number is added to an odd number, it becomes even—as if 5 is added to three, the sum is 8. In one way alone does an odd number result: if a number is added to a number not of the same kind, but ‹odd› to even, or even to odd. For whether one increases 4 by adding three, or 3 by adding 4, the result will be 7, which is odd. Next, from an even number, of whatever kind is subtracted, of that same kind remains; for an odd number it is the opposite, so that what remains is not of the kind subtracted. Therefore, if an even number is subtracted from an even number, what remains is even—as if two are taken from 8, 6 remain. If an odd number is subtracted from an even number, what remains ‹is odd—as if 3 are taken from 8, 5 remain. Conversely, if an odd number is subtracted from an odd number, what remains› is even—as if 3 are taken from 7, 4 remain. If an even number is subtracted from an odd number, what remains is odd—as if two are taken from 7, 5 remain.

{{771}} Next, even-times-even (par ex paribus) is any number that has an even half, as is the case in 12, whose half is in the number six, itself also even. Likewise even-times-even is any number that has grown from two by doubling, such as 4, 8, 16, or any that has grown from others in such a way that it can fall back into an even number, which happens also to those increased by the quadruple or octuple or the like. On the other hand, any number that has an odd half is even-times-odd (par ex imparibus), such as six, whose half is in three. If, however, any number neither grew from two by doubling nor has an odd half, it is indeed even-from-even, yet it arises from one that is even-times-odd, such as 12: for this number neither arose from two by doubling nor has an odd half, but grew from the number six by doubling, while that six is even-times-odd, namely twice three.

{{772}} Let us pass now to uncompounded (prime) and compounded (composite) numbers, which I also proposed are named first (primi) and second (secundi). No even numbers are uncompounded in themselves (prime) except, as I set down above, two; all others whatsoever that are uncompounded in themselves are odd, such as 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, and the like. Compounded in themselves (composite), on the other hand, are all even numbers which are either from even or from odd numbers: for we measure both 4 and 8 by doubling, of which one is halved into twos and the other into fours; and it is easy to do the same in 6 or 10, since the former is resolved into threes and the latter into fives. Besides these, many odd numbers are compounded in themselves—namely, all those whatsoever that are multiplied by an odd number. For whether three or five or any other odd number multiplies odd numbers, the odd number thus produced is also compounded in themselves: let three multiply itself, three times three makes 9; let five multiply itself, five times five makes 25; or let either three multiply five or five multiply three, they make 15; and all these numbers—9, 25, 15—are compounded in themselves, as are all odd numbers whatsoever of the same lot.

{{773}} Uncompounded in relation to one another (relatively prime / coprime), however, are no two even numbers, whether they are from even or from odd numbers, because there are none that do not have some common measure. For even if we take two even numbers, one from even and the other from odd—namely, 4 and 6—nevertheless they are compounded in relation to one another, because doubling is common to them, whereby twice two is 4 and twice three is 6. Among odd numbers, first are all those which are uncompounded in themselves (prime): for they cannot have any common measure besides unity when they have not even any proper measure of their own. Therefore 3, 5, 7, and all similar numbers, just as they are uncompounded in themselves, so also are they uncompounded in relation to one another; and in the same lot is also that even number which stands under the same right, two: for not even this is compounded with three or five or the like.

{{774}} Furthermore, whichever of these numbers that are uncompounded in themselves is joined with another number, even one compounded in themselves, brings it about that these two numbers are uncompounded in relation to one another—as if 3 and 4 are joined. For what difference does it make whether one has some measure besides that same unity, if the other does not? And although two or more numbers may be compounded not only in themselves but also in relation to one another, ‹one number uncompounded in itself› brings it about that all of them are uncompounded in relation to one another, because although some measure is common to several, none is common to all besides unity. This occurs if 4, 6, 8, and as many similar numbers as you please are set down, and then 3 is added to them: for although the first three numbers can be compounded with one another, nevertheless these four are not compounded with one another.

{{775}} Not only, however, does the addition of a number that is uncompounded in themselves cause several numbers not to be compounded with one another, but it can also happen that numbers which are compounded in themselves, when put together, are uncompounded in relation to one another, where although they admit some measures, they nevertheless admit different ones; and this occurs both between two odd numbers and between an even and an odd number. Let us take 9 and 25: each of these is compounded in themselves. For the number nine has a measure in three, and 25 has one in the number five. Yet they are not compounded with one another, because neither does 9 admit the measure of the number five, nor 25 that of three. The same happens between 8 and 9, an even and an odd number: for we can measure neither 9 by doubling or quadrupling, nor 8 by tripling. Thus numbers compounded in themselves can also be uncompounded in relation to one another; those which are compounded in themselves cannot immediately be compounded with one another as well.

{{776}} Compounded in relation to one another, on the other hand, are all even numbers—as could also appear above—whether they are from even or from odd numbers; next, certain odd numbers, such as 9 and 15, since both numbers fall into threes; then certain even and certain odd numbers, such as 9 and 12, inasmuch as tripling is common to these too—for three times three is 9 and three times four is 12. That is worthy of note: that with an odd number can never be compounded an even number which arose from even numbers [even-times-even, powers of 2], but only one which arose from odd numbers; so much so that even when its lot is changed, some fellowship of right still survives. Therefore 9 can be compounded neither with 4, nor with 8, nor with 16, nor with any similar number; whereas it is compounded with 12 and 24, which took their beginning from three.

{{777}} And not even with all even numbers that are even-from-odd can every odd number that is compounded in themselves be compounded, because it may not fall into the same measure. Therefore 9 and 50 cannot be compounded, because 50 admits no tripling, which is the sole measure of the number nine besides unity; and this occurs because not even 25, which doubled made 50, admitted three. Therefore, if ever the odd number from which an even number was made has the same measure which the other odd number holds, then at last with that odd number can the even number made from the former be compounded; where that did not precede, neither does this follow. Therefore 9 and 50 are not compounded with one another; whereas 9 and 30 are compounded with one another: for 30 arose from doubling 15, and already 9 and 15 could be compounded with one another, since their common measure lay in three. From these principles arise the remaining rules which pertain to this class of numbers.

{{778}} Out of two numbers uncompounded in relation to one another, whether both or one is compounded in themselves, the measure [divisor] of the one is not compounded with the other. Let there be 4 and 9: these are compounded in themselves, yet uncompounded in relation to one another. Now the measure of four is in two, and of nine in three; yet neither is two compounded with 9, nor 3 with 4. Again, let there be 5 and 4, one of them uncompounded in itself: four has its measure in two, and two and 5 are not compounded. If two numbers are uncompounded in relation to one another and one of them multiplies itself [is squared], the number thus produced is not compounded with the former one. Let there be 3 and 4: these are uncompounded in relation to one another. Whether three multiplies itself—9 and 4—or four does the same—16 and 3—they will be uncompounded in relation to one another. If two numbers uncompounded in relation to one another multiply themselves, the numbers produced from them will be uncompounded in relation to one another: as if we take the same 3 and 4 and each multiplies itself, 9 and 16 will also be uncompounded in relation to one another.

{{779}} If two numbers are uncompounded in relation to one another and one of them multiplies itself, and the number thus produced again multiplies itself [or is multiplied by the original, yielding the cube], the number thus produced is not compounded with the other. Let there be 2 and 3; let either number multiply itself, making twice two = 4 and three times three = 9; again let the same numbers multiply these, making twice four = 8 and three times 9 = 27. Let us now take 2 and 27, or 3 and 8: they are equally uncompounded in relation to one another. If two numbers are uncompounded in relation to one another and each multiplies itself, and then again multiplies its product by itself [cubes], these too which are thus produced ‹are uncompounded in relation to one another›, as in those very numbers set down above: for 8 made from two and 27 made from three are not compounded with one another. If two numbers are uncompounded in relation to one another and are joined into one [summed], this number thus produced cannot be compounded with either of the former. If 3 and 5 are joined into one, they make 8; this can be compounded neither with 5 nor with three.

{{780}} If a number is divided into two numbers uncompounded in relation to one another, it cannot be compounded with either of them. Let 9 be divided into 4 and 5: 9 can be compounded neither with ‹4› nor with 5. If two numbers are joined with a third in such a way that all are uncompounded in relation to one another, and then of the two one multiplies the other, the number thus produced will equally not be able to be compounded with that same third number. Let there be two numbers 4 and 8 [or e.g. 4 and 5]; let 3 be added to them: they are mutually coprime [pairwise coprime with 3]. Let the first two multiply each other: four times 8 or eight times four makes 32; this and 3 will be uncompounded in relation to one another.

{{781}} Every number that is uncompounded in themselves (prime) cannot be compounded with another number except one of which it is itself a measure. ‹…› Therefore 3 is compounded with 9, and 5 with 15, because three times three is 9 and five times three is 15. ‹If therefore› any number does not contain something of it, it cannot be compounded with it. If, two numbers being set down, the smaller is ‹always› subtracted from the larger and that which remains is not a measure of the one that was subtracted next before it [Euclidean algorithm], these numbers are uncompounded in relation to one another. Let there be 3 and 8: let threes be taken from the larger number as many times as possible [twice], and two remain; in this there is no measure of three [2 does not divide 3]. Therefore 3 and 8 are also uncompounded in relation to one another. If three numbers are the lowest of all those which stand under the same ratio [continued geometric proportion], any two of them joined into one [summed] are not compounded with the third. Let there be three numbers 9, 12, 16: of these, each following number is supertertius [4:3] to the preceding, nor will any three smaller numbers be found joined in this way. Let 9 and 12 be blended into one: they make 21. This will not be compounded with 16 [nor 12 + 16 = 28 with 9, nor 9 + 16 = 25 with 12].

{{782}} If an odd number cannot be compounded with some number, neither is it compounded even with the double of that number. Let there be 5 and 8: these are uncompounded in relation to one another. Let 8 be doubled: it makes 16; not even with this can five be compounded. If two numbers and another two numbers are set down such that neither of the first two can be compounded with either of the following two, not even that number which is formed by summing the first two can be compounded with either of the following [as a rule stated by Martianus, though not universally true for all sums—e.g. 4 + 8 = 12]. Let there be two numbers 4 and 8 and likewise another two, 5 and 7: neither of the first two can be compounded with either of the following two. Let 4 and 8 be blended into one: they make 12; not even this is compounded with 5 or 7.

{{783}} The lowest numbers of those which stand under the same ratio are uncompounded in relation to one another (relatively prime), as in the ratio of the double the lowest are 2 and 1, and in that of the triple 3 and 1, and these are not compounded with one another. Conversely, however large the numbers taken that are uncompounded in relation to one another, they are the lowest of all those which stand under the same ratio. Let there be 200 and 101: these are not compounded with one another; and there is between them a ratio of parts, because 200 exceeds 101 by 99 parts [101sts], nor can that ratio exist between any smaller numbers.

{{784}} Now since the reckoning of a measure (mensura, divisor) brings it about that some numbers are uncompounded or compounded in themselves, and some uncompounded or compounded in relation to one another, it seems not out of place to subjoin immediately a discussion of measures. Every number is either uncompounded in themselves (prime) or, if it is compounded in themselves (composite), has a measure in some number that is uncompounded in itself—as 9 and those which grow from it by tripling have a measure in three, and likewise 15 in 5. Yet of those which are even-times-even, the smallest measure [greater than 1] is in two; while of those which are even-from-odd or are odd, the smallest measure can also be in larger numbers, all of them however odd. Of a single composite number, moreover, either the smallest or the greatest measure [proper divisor] is easily found: for when the number is halved [or divided by its smallest prime factor], the nearest measure is the greatest, and the last is the smallest. Take for example fifty: let this be halved—its half is 25; in this lies its greatest measure. Again, let us observe what lower measures it has: there are ten times five = 50, likewise five times ten, and likewise twice twenty-five; nor is any of these smaller than that which is from two—this, therefore, is the smallest measure [greater than 1] of fifty.

{{785}} On the other hand, if two numbers are compounded in relation to one another, a greater and a smaller, it may be asked how their greatest and smallest common measure [GCD and smallest common prime divisor] is found. It is necessary to subtract the smaller from the larger number as many times as possible; then, as much as remains from the former, to take away that same amount from the smaller as many times as possible [the Euclidean algorithm]. The number which remains will be the greatest common measure of those numbers. Let there be two numbers 350 and 100: let hundreds be taken from 350 as many times as possible—that is, thrice: 50 are left. From the other number, one hundred, let 50 be subtracted [once], and 50 will remain from it. This number [50] is the greatest common measure of 350 and 100: for fifty times two is one hundred, and fifty times seven is 350. From this it also appears that whatever smaller number measures two numbers will also measure their greatest common measure. The smallest common measure [greater than 1] of the same numbers is found thus: when the greatest has been found, the smallest measure of that very number is sought; the same is also the smallest common measure for the former numbers—as here too the smallest measure of fifty is in two; therefore the same is also the smallest measure of the former numbers.

{{786}} Of three numbers, however, which are compounded in relation to one another, the greatest and smallest common measure is found thus: the greatest measure of two is sought; if it is also common to the third, the smallest of them, that which was desired has been found; if it is not, the greatest measure of the middle and the smallest is sought in the same way, and that is common to all three. Let there be three numbers 350, 100, 75: let the greatest measure common to 350 and 100 be sought, and let it be found in the number fifty. Let us consider whether this measures that third number which holds 75: if it measured it, it would be common to all three; it does not, however, measure it. Therefore we join 100 and 75, and seek their greatest measure: I take 75 from 100, and 25 remain. This I take as many times as possible from the number that holds 75—that is, twice [leaving 25]. This number [25] is the greatest measure common to 100 and 75; in this also lies the greatest common measure of all three numbers. For twenty-five times three is 75, twenty-five times four is 100, and twenty-five times fourteen is 350. Let us now consider what is the smallest measure of this 25: it cannot reach two, nor three, nor four, but lies in the number five; and this same smallest measure will be found common to all three numbers above. For two, which measures both 350 and 100, does not measure 75; 3, which measures 75, does not measure 350 and 100; 4, which measures 100, again does not measure 350 and 75. Five first can measure all of them, because five times fifteen is 75, five times twenty is 100, and five times seventy is 350.

{{787}} When two numbers are given, moreover, the smallest number which they measure [Least Common Multiple, LCM] is found thus: let the given numbers be two and 3. These are uncompounded in relation to one another; let one of them multiply the other: twice three or three times two makes 6. This is the smallest number which the two measure. No one could name the greatest number which they can measure, but those same numbers will measure every number whatsoever that is formed by multiplying the number six. On the other hand, let two numbers be given that are compounded in relation to one another, 9 and 12: by a similar multiplication one does not arrive at the same result, because a smaller number than that which is thus produced can also have a measure in these; therefore it must be found by another way.

{{788}} Let us see what are the lowest numbers under the same ratio as these. Now for the number nine the lowest of the same ratio [9:12 = 3:4] is in three, and for twelve in two [i.e. 12:9 = 4:3, or dividing by their GCD 3 gives 3 and 4, though Martianus writes in duobus]. Now let either of the smaller numbers multiply not its own number, but the other—that is, either 3 × 12 or 4 × 9 [Martianus writes II VIIII]: three times twelve is 36, twice nine is 18. Of these let us consider whether the smaller number, which is 18, has a measure in both 9 and 12: it has one in 9, but in 12 it does not. Let it therefore be dismissed, and let the larger be taken, which is 36. This is the smallest number which both 9 and 12 can measure: for both nine times four and twelve times three are 36. By the same reasoning, those same two numbers measure every number whatsoever that is formed by multiplying 36.

{{789}} When three numbers are given, moreover, the smallest number which they measure [LCM] is found thus: let the given three numbers be 2, 3, 4; let that smallest number be taken which has a measure in both doubling and tripling—that is six. Let us consider whether the third of the three, namely four, also measures this: if it measured it, that which was sought would have been found. Now it does not measure it; let us look therefore to the smallest number which 4 and 3 [or 6 and 4] measure: that is 12; therefore this is the smallest number which all three of them can measure—for twice six, three times four, and four times three make 12. And hence also those same 3 numbers measure every number that is formed by multiplying 12.

{{790}} Wherever two numbers measure some number, the smallest number that has a measure in those two [their LCM] is also a measure of that same number. Let 12 be given: both 2 and 3 measure this. Now the smallest number which the two measure is six; and indeed that same six also measures 12, for six times two is 12. The same happens in that number which any three numbers measure: for this too is measured by the smallest number that has a measure in those three. Let 24 be set down: 2, 3, and 4 measure this; and the smallest number that has a measure in those same three ‹is 12›. And indeed this too measures 24, for twelve times two is 24.

{{791}} If two numbers ‹which are the lowest of those standing under the same ratio are set down with two larger numbers› in such a way that the same ratio stands between the larger and the smaller, as many times as the larger measures the larger, so many times does the smaller measure the smaller.
