Let the numbers be 2 and 3, and then 8 and 12. The same ratio stands between the larger and the smaller numbers: for both 3 to two and 12 to 8 are superdimidii [3:2]. Now 3 measures 12 four times, for four times three is 12; and indeed two also measures 8 four times, for four times two is 8.

{{792}} ‹If› as many times as unity measures some number, so many times another number measures another, it will come about that as many times as unity measures the first of the second pair, so many times that number which entered into the measure of unity will measure the further number. Let there be 1 and 5, and 6 and 30. Unity measures the number five five times, and six does the same in 30; again, unity measures six itself six times, and indeed the number five also measures 30 six times.

{{793}} If two numbers multiply one another, and some number that is uncompounded in themselves (prime) measures the number thus produced, it is necessary that the same prime also measure one or the other of the first two [Euclid, Elements VII.30]. Let the number eight multiply ten, making 80; two measures this, for twice forty is 80; and indeed the same 2 measures both 8 and 10, since twice four is eight and twice five is 10.

{{794}} Whenever numbers of the same proportion, which the Greeks call analogia [geometric progression], are set in order, if the first measures the last, it will also measure the second and all in succession; if it measures the second, it will also measure the last and the middle ones; finally, if it measures any single one, it will measure all. Conversely, if it does not measure the last, neither does it measure the second nor any other; if it does not measure the second, neither does it measure the last nor any other; if it does not measure any middle one, neither does it measure any other. Let there be 3 and 9, 27, 81, 243: between all these stands the ratio of the triple; now three measures 243, for three times eighty-one is 243. Therefore the same measures the number nine, since three times three is 9; and because it measures this, it also measures the last, and because either one, also the rest, and because any middle one among them, both the earlier and the later ones. Two, on the other hand, because it does not measure 243, measures neither 9 nor the middle ones; because it does not measure 9, neither does it measure 243 nor the middle ones; because it measures none of the middle ones, neither does it measure the further ones.

{{795}} If as many numbers as you please come together from one in the same proportion [geometric progression starting from 1], as many uncompounded in themselves (prime) numbers as measure the last number, just so many will also measure the one next to one [the common ratio]. Let the numbers growing by the double be 1, 2, 4, 8, 16: of these, 2 measures 16, and the same measures itself. Again, let there be 1, 12, 144, 1,728: the numbers uncompounded in themselves which ‹measure 1,728› are 2 and 3, because twice eight hundred and sixty-four is 1,728, and likewise three times five hundred and seventy-six. And indeed the same 2 and 3 also measure 12, which is next to one, since twice six and three times four are 12.

{{796}} If as many numbers as you please from one stand in the same proportion, a smaller number always measures a larger through some one of the others which stand under the same proportion. Let there be 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64. Of these, 2 measures 4, 4 measures 8, 8 measures 16, 16 measures 32, and 32 measures 64 by doubling [2]. Again, 2 measures 8 by quadrupling [4]; by the same 4 measures 16, 8 measures 32, and 16 measures 64. And likewise by octupling [8], 2 measures 16, 4 measures 32, and 8 measures 64; nor is any number found that does not both measure a larger one and do so by no other measure than one which is among those same numbers.

{{797}} If as many numbers as you please from one stand in the same proportion and the one next to one is uncompounded in themselves (prime), the greatest of them will come into measure [be divisible] by none except ‹those› which are of the same proportion. Let there be 1, 3, 9, 27: between these stands the triple proportion, and the number next to one [3] is uncompounded in itself. Therefore no number can measure 27 except either 3 or 9, which stand under the same proportion. Which does not happen so if ever the number next to one is composite. Let there be 1, 4, 16, 64: the number next to one [4] is compounded in itself; therefore the last, which is 64, also admits other measures than those which are in this series—both 2 and 8 and 32, since twice 32, eight times eight, and thirty-two times two make 64. Moreover, the smallest number which two numbers uncompounded in themselves (primes) measure, no other number uncompounded in itself will measure. Let 5 and 7 be taken: they measure no smaller number than 35; for five times 7 and seven times five are 35, and no other number uncompounded in itself can measure this—not 2, not 3, not 11, not 13, not 17, and still less any further numbers.

{{798}} If a square number measures a square number, in its side [square root] also is the measure of the other’s side. Let there be two square numbers 4 and 16: four measures 16, for four times 4 is 16. And indeed in the side of four is two, and in the side of 16 is 4: 2 measures four, for twice two is 4. From this it also appears that of two square numbers, if in the side of one is the measure of ‹the other’s side›, in the square number itself too is the measure of the other square.

{{799}} If a square number does not measure a square number, neither in the side of one is there a measure of the other’s ‹side›. Let the square numbers be 4 and 9: four does not measure the number nine; therefore neither does two, which is in the side of four, measure 3, which is in the side of the number nine. From this it also appears that of two square numbers, if in the side of one there is no measure of the other’s side, neither in the one square number is there a measure of the other square.

{{800}} If a cube (tessera) measures a cube, in the side [cube root] of the one also is the measure of the other’s side. Let there be two cubes 8 and 64: eight measures 64, inasmuch as eight times 8 is 64. And indeed, since in the side of that cube which holds 8 is 2, and in the side of that which holds 64 is 4, 2 is a measure of four. From this it also appears: if of two cubes in the side of one is the measure of the other’s side, the one cube is also a measure of that other cube. Conversely, if a cube does not measure a cube, neither in the side of one is there a measure of the other’s side. Let there be two cubes 8 and 27: 8 does not measure 27; therefore, since in the side of that cube which holds 8 is 2, and in that which holds 27 is 3, 2 does not measure three. From this it also appears that if in the side of a cube there is no measure ‹of the side of the other cube›, not even that cube measures that other cube.

{{801}} In every number, that number which has a measure [divisor] in some number also acquires from that same number the name of the fractional part (membrum) which forms the measure. Let there be 9: three measures this, and the third part of the number nine is in three. Let there be 16: four measures this, and the fourth of 16 is 4; and the same will be found in all other numbers. It follows, moreover, that if a number has a fractional part, in that number that number has a measure which shares a common name with that part: as the [third] part of the number nine is in three, and three measures it.

{{802}} «Let it suffice to have recounted these numbers and measures cursorily; the rest it is fair for the Cecropian chairs [Athenian schools] to set forth—if indeed any breath still remains upon our altars, or if philosophers’ cloaks are folded back into their ancient garb in dispute. Space has already warned me to close my discourse with utterances, lest weariness of me seize the assembly of the gods above, and I, ancient Arithmetica, be driven from the star-bearing sky.» Thus she spoke, and falling silent she joined herself close to her nearest sister.

## Liber VIII. De astronomia

{{803}} While these things were taking place, and while the sacred senate of the gods marveled at those winding mazes of consonant and conflicting numbers, recognizing that this woman herself—venerable in the loftiness of her august excellence—was not idly believed to be the parent of the celestials; while the throng of sages too, who stood where they had been bidden, and especially Pythagoras with all his followers, and Plato discerning the dark mysteries of his Timaeus, revered her with secret praises; and while Pallas, glancing repeatedly at the virgin bride, inquired what she approved concerning the erudition of the dower-maiden, herself joining in the nod of one who admired, and Cyllenius, glorifying in the splendor and brilliance of no foster-daughter more than hers, exalted himself in this woman’s grandeur: so, while Phoebus lingered a little while before introducing another of the dower-maidens, lest admiration for the former should too hastily slip away, there was a brief, sacred, and reverent silence.

{{804}} Meanwhile Silenus, who had followed Euhan and was standing behind him, shriveled and leaning forward—whether worn out by the languor of old age, or weighed down by anxious concentration amidst the marvels of that learned voice, or else, normally swollen with wine-cups and then further enticed by the pretext of nuptial license, having drenched himself with a flood of unmixed wine—had long since relaxed into sleep, when by chance he suddenly let out a snore rattling like the croak of a frog gulping acorns. Startled by this racket and the harshness of the sudden sound, a great many of the gods turned around toward him; and when they caught sight of the old man puffing out his sleep and sweating out his sodden debauch, the laughter of the bystanders burst forth—most of all in the quarter where he was hemmed in. Then, since nuptial license was believed not to forbid jests, the retinue of Venus and the household slaves of Bromius broke into such peals of laughter, with sides shaking violently, that they provoked very many others, who were striving to hold back their mirth, into bursting out with a saucy roar and the indulgence of unrestrained guffaws. Finally, impatient as ever and bold and forward in his shameless assaults, Cupid ran up to him briskly and gleefully, and as the old man had rested his bald, reddish pate upon his staff, Cupid gave it a resounding slap with the flat of his hand, rousing almost everyone to laughter by that echoing smack, as though permission had now been granted.

{{805}} Then the old man, scarcely opening his bleary eyes in the dim light, looked around at those laughing and, smarting from the blow that had awakened him, stared dully about, wiping his slobbering face with the back of his hand. Then, roused by the scolding of Lyaeus, he snatched up his staff, and while he strove to set his steps in motion as Lyde called him away, having at last recovered his sight, he beheld the senate of the gods. Suddenly struck with terror, the tottering old dotard tried to break into a run. Then, movement failing him, he stood all the more entangled in himself, and his feet gave way backward in a tremor of fright; stumbling and straining, he stuck fast, turned back, and scurried again. At last, overcome and panting, swollen old age collapsed. Thereupon the laughter grew louder still, and Pleasure knew no bounds, until at Bacchus’s command a Satyr caught up the fallen figure, hoisted him flat on his back across his shoulders, and carried off the bloated, sprawling-limbed old man, doubled over his neck like a pair of wineskins.

{{806}} While this briskness of playful merriment was seething, that Satire who had always made it her care to shape my senses said: «Truly, Felix—or Capella, or whoever thou art, no less beast-like in sense than in name—thou hast begun to play the fool and lose thy wits by adding to this unseemly laughter. Dost thou say so at last? Dost thou not consider that thou hast stirred up guffaws in the council of Jove, and that beneath the censorship of the gods and of Pallas it is a fearsome thing to liken anyone to a babbling madman?

{{807}} And at what moment, too, do Cupid and the Satyr leap about in the impudence of saucy daring? Why, at the very moment when a starry virgin, fairer than all the dower-maidens, is about to enter that venerable senate-house and the sight of the gods! Away with thee, and henceforth cloak not trifling escapades under the law of Hymen and the veil of licensed fault. At least listen with no reluctance to the maxim of Bias of Priene, and unless thou art an ass at the lyre (ὄνος λύρας), know the fitting season (καιρὸν γνῶθι).» Softened by such stern and sharp lashes from Satire, who was otherwise charming enough, while I wrapped myself in excuses for what had been admitted as though saucily, I inquired at last which of the maidens was being readied to be brought in. And thus she began, her spleen not yet subsiding wherewith she had assailed me:

{{808}} «Now is the time at hand to sing the path of the star-bearing seat, the course of the poles, and where the sacred constellations lift their far-wandering curves. I see the coffered ceilings of the flash-bearing sky suddenly gleam, struck by a pulse of light. On one side, Boötes, the herdsman wont to guard the twice-seven Triones, shines with Hyperborean radiance; on the other, where the earth slopes down beneath the inclined axis, bright Canopus presents himself with his unfamiliar star. Likewise the chariots of Phoebus and his swift passages, and the blazing horns of the ever-changing Moon—nay more, the middle circles which the oblique summits bind together, and the track whereon the orbit sweeps through ruddy signs—already I seem to behold them: dost thou persist in fashioning trifles and prefer cheap fictions to the star-speaking maiden?»

{{809}} While Satire was still chanting in this strain, that forbidden and most harshly chastised laughter came over me once more. «Bravo!» said I, «my Satire—has bile made a poetess of thee? Hast thou begun to thirst for the springs of Permessus’s flood? Dost thou already foresee splendors and the countenances of the gods? Whither has that spirit suddenly departed, whereby with ever-mocking and charming wit thou wast wont to deride the bombast of bards among the ravings of madmen, content with bantering quips and Attic salt, abandoning no less the tragic buskin of rhetoricians among frantic things—and why didst thou rage with hot-headed fury, rebuking me with supercilious, censorious clamor when I laughed at Silenus’s sleep? Must I then banish fictions, and shall no admixture of charm and jest refresh the weariness of the listeners? Come back to thy senses, moreover, with a verse of the Paelignian youth [Ovid], and unless thou knit thy brow in tragic gloom:

Laugh, if thou art wise, O maiden, laugh.»

{{810}} After my Satire and I had wrangled for a good while with alternating reproaches, the Delian [Apollo] stepped forth to introduce another of the dower-maidens. And lo, a kind of globe of ethereal light and a hollow mass of transparent fire—enclosing within itself, as it appeared, a maiden—glided in, revolving gently with a mild rotation. Illuminated by the brilliance of this approaching light, many of the gods shone back, and especially the planetary deities, whose bearing, motions, and whatever in them was believed unknown flashed forth; then the very fabric of the outermost heaven vibrated back with the gleams of that same light. Astonished at this marvel, the gods of air, earth, and sea, and whatever deities the enclosed depths of earth conceal, supposing that Astraea and Themis—or surely Libyan Urania—had appeared, most honorably offered her a seat in the assembly.

{{811}} And lo, suddenly there leaped forward a maiden jeweled and no less becomingly covered with eyes across all her limbs; starry was her crown and quivering her locks; moreover, her wings with crystalline feathers and her pinions for flying through the universe were closely ruffled with gold. In one hand she carried a gleaming cubit-rule, in the other a book in which the pre-measured paths of the gods and the sidereal courses and retrogressions, together with the very hinges of the poles, appeared traced out beforehand in metals of diverse colors. When she had come into the midst while many of the gods smiled upon her and the rest marveled at her beauty and splendor, she began thus:

{{812}} «For many a reason—and none to be despised or slighted upon consideration—it would have been fitting for me to keep silent concerning whatever works belong to our diligence. For I do not think it becomes modest propriety to expound to those very beings who are in motion their own movements and courses, or to wish to teach the gods what they themselves do; and furthermore, through immense stretches of ages, lest I be vulgarized by profane garrulity, I lay hidden, shut away in the inner sanctuaries of the Egyptians. Indeed, for nearly forty thousand years I remained concealed there in reverent observation; and would that, after the onset of the cataclysmic flood and the restoration of the city of Athens after a long interval, no smooth allurements nor coaxing, long-haired, cloaked philosophers had brought me into Greece—better kept secret than divulged! Never, assuredly, would your journeys, stations, and returns have come into the knowledge of men and the defilement of mortal cares.

{{813}} Yet since, wandering somehow, I have come into Greek notice, it ought to have sufficed whatever was published by Eratosthenes, Ptolemy, Hipparchus, and the rest, so that the necessity of speaking might not burden me further. Nevertheless, because the office of Cyllenius, my foster-father and instructor, does not permit me to remain silent, and the cleverness of the bride likewise invites me to unlock the secrets of my study, before you, celestials and gods of heaven, who will review your own courses, I will not hold my peace.

{{814}} The universe, then, rounded into the shape of a sphere out of the four elements—and those in their entirety—revolving around the earth, which is fixed in the middle and lowest place, with the eternal sweep of heaven, marks it off by a circular ordering. Yet at the very threshold of my exposition I do not disdain to give ear to the natural philosophers, who judge that the delicacy of subtle bodies, drawn round by their own compulsions, is not separated into distinct cut-out tracks and intervals of circles, but that natures clinging together by their own currents are diffused on every side in a spherical circuit and orbs; around the center of which—which they have called centron—they record first the wave (cyma) of water, next that of air, third that of fire, and then, in a fifth tempering of corporeal substance, that ethereal splendors fly round, in which region the obliquity of the sun, the moon, the planets, and the zodiacal circle wheels about, which they have called the kyklophoretikon cyma (the circular-moving wave). The tranquility of this substance also holds that outermost and encircling course which, because it is studded with no stars, is called anastros (starless).

{{815}} If, therefore, the entire circuit of the revolving substances is found to be homogeneous with itself, no circles can diversify the ethereal tract. We, therefore, shall speak of circles not as though feigning corporeal divisions within a fluid substance, but in order to demonstrate the ascents and descents of the wandering stars in relation to us. Nor indeed shall I attach to the solidity of the cosmic structure either the axis or the poles which mortals have affixed to a bronze sphere—which is called cricote (armillary)—as a shorthand for understanding, since nothing is more solid than the earth that could support it, and since the poles would project as though from the hollowed sockets of a perforated outer sphere, and certain openings and hinges would have to be imagined—which, as has been ascertained, could in no way happen to subtle and ethereal bodies.

{{816}} Wheresoever, therefore, for the sake of understanding and the exposition to be set forth, I shall speak of an axis, or poles, or circles, let it be weighed with a certain ideal discernment—not as divisions distinct in the sky itself, but as ratios of spatial intervals. And so let it be understood when I call the universe elevated (evexus) or depressed (devexus), since it is alike in all its parts and is lifted high or hidden according to the condition of the horizon and the position of the lands.

{{817}} With this prefatory warning, then, I suggest that—whereas a certain Roman [Varro], not altogether ignorant of me, records that stellae were named from stando (standing), sidera from considendo (settling together), and astra from Astraeus, and the Greeks have filled the sky with mythical fictions—I shall rather expound the precepts of my discipline; and I affirm that ten circles of the universe are spoken of, of which some are parallel, ‹some oblique, and some drawn through the poles. And the parallel circles,› which in Latin we may call equidistant (aequistantes), have the same poles as the universe itself. For poles are those points which, by a line drawn from the center of a circle to its circumferences, mark off with equal ratio the measure of a bisected half.

{{818}} Now of the parallels, the first is that which, ever visible and touching the borders of the horizon, rises without ever setting; it is called the septentrional (arctic) circle because, along with the other constellations that will be named, it encloses the twin stars of the Septentriones (the Bears).

{{819}} The second of the parallels, next to the greatest, is the solstitial (tropic of Cancer), to which the summer sun approaches and, turned back at the limit of the solstice, departs.

{{820}} The third is the equinoctial (celestial equator), middle and greatest of all, across which the sun passes twice—both when it rises into summer blaze and when it descends into winter—borne through a middle division of the universe, and balances the measure of night with equality of daylight.

{{821}} Next to this is the brumal (tropic of Capricorn), reaching which at the winter limit the sun is turned back once more and rises toward the north wind.

{{822}} The fifth and last is the austral circle, which is also called antarctic. This circle, submerged and scarcely touching the fixed extremity of the horizon with its higher rim, is nevertheless shown by calculation to be of the same spatial extent as the septentrional circle, to which it is also found to be directly opposite.

{{823}} Next in order I judge it fitting to explain the colures, of which a certain part turns above, while another turns hidden below; concerning these I am not unaware that the definitions of writers vary. For some assert that a circle is drawn from the northern pole to the southern, and thence passing beneath the earth is lifted up again to the arctic apex; others, on the contrary, placing the starting-point of the circle at the southern pole, have brought it back again through the arctic summit whence it rose—and these, intersecting another circle from the east, have divided the circuit of the universe into four quarters.

{{824}} I, however—embracing what my Hipparchus established in the truth of his writings—declare that these circles come from the signs of the zodiacal cycle and, both intersecting each other twice and cutting all the parallels at equal angles, reach the poles. For one, starting from the eighth degree of Aries and encompassing the universe through the summits of the poles, returns to the same point; the other, with a similar embrace, encompasses the universe starting from Cancer—which we shall make clearer afterward.

{{825}} Now, however, the oblique circles must be declared: of which the signifer (zodiac), marked off into twelve divisions, touches two of the parallels—the solstitial and the brumal—and cuts the middle equinoctial twice, neither dividing nor being divided at equal angles. It affords a path for the sun and moon along with the five wandering stars.

{{826}} The Galaxias (Milky Way), moreover, stretched out in a much greater circuit than the other oblique circles, is proved by the evidence of our own eyes as well; for rising at the border of the septentrional circle and sloping down to the horizon of the antarctic region, it is seen to traverse almost the entire sky. Those who have denied that it is a circle seem to me to talk nonsense. There now remains one of the circles, which indeed—since it varies with every place and every moment—I hesitate what to name. Yet because it separates for us the lower hemisphere from the upper and rests everywhere upon the surface of the earth with the curve of an encompassing line, it is called the horizon, finitor, or finiens.

{{827}} Now since the circles set forth have been briefly clarified, I shall next declare their dimensions from the point where I first conceived them. And first let me return to the magnitude of the septentrional circle, in which, after the manner of geometry, I first set down two points to draw the circle—that is, one to mark the centron (center) and the other to mark the peripheria (circumference). Accordingly, at the very pole of the universe I placed a fairly bright star, and from it to the head of Draco—which I had already observed to reach the circle of the horizon—I drew a line; then, keeping the center fixed, I swept the line round in thought and traced out the circle; and encompassing an equal space on every side, the circumference carried round passed through these constellations: from the head of Draco and the right foot of him who is called Engonasis (Hercules), to the middle of Cepheus’s breast, then to the forefeet of Ursa Major, and thence back again to the head of Draco.

{{828}} Adjacent to this circle is the solstitial circle, which we likewise found by a linear measurement drawn from the pole of the universe to the eighth degree of Cancer, whither the sun had approached at the solstice. This same circle, however, with a fuller sweep I curved through these signs: beginning from the eighth degree of Cancer, whose entire body it cuts lengthwise, to the breast and belly of Leo; thence to the shoulders of Ophiuchus; then to the head of Cygnus; then to the hooves of Pegasus and straightway to the right hand of Andromeda; then to the left leg of Perseus and his left shoulder; thence to both knees of Heniochus (Auriga) and right next to the heads of Gemini, from which it returns again to the eighth degree of Cancer itself.

{{829}} Next, the equinoctial circle, measured twice from the limit of the pole and swept round with a line passing through both Aries and Libra, encloses its circle through these signs: from the eighth degree of Aries through its entire body to the drawn-back foot of Taurus; thence to the middle of Orion’s belly; then through the elevated coils of Hydra, Crater, and Corvus to the eighth degree of Libra between the two bright stars of that sign; thence to both knees of Ophiuchus and then through Aquila to the head of Pegasus, from which it returns again to the eighth degree of Aries.

{{830}} The brumal circle, similarly, with a point fixed at the eighth degree of Capricorn, we have found to pass through these constellations: from the eighth degree of Capricorn through its entire body to the feet of Aquarius; thence to the tip of Cetus’s tail, and thereafter to Lepus and the forefeet of Canicula (Canis Major); thence through Argo and the back of Centaurus to the sting of Scorpius; then through the tip of Sagitta back to the eighth degree of Capricorn the circle is recalled.

{{831}} The last of the parallels, which is called the antarctic, encloses as much space as the septentrional. Which stars it is studded with as it runs its course I could indeed recount, for no portion of the celestial globe is held unknown to me; yet since it stretches through regions invisible to the eyes of men in the upper hemisphere, I forbear to speak of it, lest an assertion concerning unverified matters seem to mingle falsehood with truth.

{{832}} With better warrant we demonstrate the colures, even though they too, hiding some part of their curvature, do not present themselves whole to our sight; nevertheless, where inference does not err, they can be demonstrated. Thus the first of them, which takes its beginning from the equinoctial point, starting from the eighth degree of Aries, touches the outermost angle of Deltoton (Triangulum), and presently touching the top of Perseus’s head and his right arm, cutting right next to his hand through the septentrional circle, reaches the pole of the universe; from which, drawn through the tail of Draco to the left hand of Arctophylax and right next to the star of Boötes, it touches the right and left foot of Virgo, in which lies the eighth degree of Libra; whence, divided at the right hand of Centaurus where he holds the Panther, not far from the place where it touched the left hoof of Centaurus, it is plunged into the region of the hemisphere invisible to us; emerging thence beneath Cetus, through its body and the neck of the turned-back beast to its head, it returns thence to the eighth degree of Aries.

{{833}} The other colure, which is also called tropikos (solstitial), rises from the eighth degree of Cancer to the left forefoot of Ursa [Major], right through its breast and neck; thence it reaches the pole of the universe, and thence, carried through the haunches of Ursa Minor and then through Draco and the left wing and neck of Cygnus, it touches the tip of Sagitta’s barb and right next to the beak of Aquila, from which it descends to the eighth degree of Capricorn; thence, plunging not far off into the invisible region, it rises again beneath Argo, and cutting both its rudder and its upright stern, returns to the eighth degree of Cancer.

{{834}} There is no doubt that two circles now remain, which I mentioned above are called oblique. One of these, the signifer (zodiac), is not like the others, which I drew as mere lines, but is proved to be the broadest of all; when I divided it into twelve spaces, not ignorant of their ratio, I assigned thirty degrees to each. Moreover, I stretched the breadth of that circle into twelve divisions, so that its breadth might have as much space as twelve degrees assign to its length. Why this was done is easy to show in the place where I shall begin to speak of the sun, which alone is carried along the middle line of that same circle.

{{835}} There is no doubt that the circuit of this circle is carried round through twelve exceedingly bright signs. Now the Galaxias is confirmed no less by eyes than by reason; its breadth, exceeding rule and for the most part irregular, from the constellation of Cassiopeia ‹…› is compensated in breadth at the sting of Scorpius.

{{836}} There remains alone the circle of the horizon, which, because it is ever varied by the diversities of the rising and setting sky, cannot retain a fixed order of stars.

{{837}} Now it is fair to explain what interval natural spacing has placed between all the circles. For between the septentrional circle, which I divided into eight spaces, and the solstitial circle there lies open as much space as lies between eight and six. For that same interval is contained by equal spaces, whereby it comes about that the larger circle exceeds it by the same interval plus a third part thereof. Another interval, between the solstitial and equinoctial circles, is less than the preceding interval in the ratio that the number four bears to six. From the equinoctial to the brumal is similar; and from the brumal to the austral circle is such as that which lies between the septentrional and solstitial circles; while the austral circle itself has the same distance to its own pole as the septentrional displays.

{{838}} Having now briefly completed the account of the spaces and circles, let us run through the constellations which are called inerrantia (fixed). For there is no doubt that the entire sky shines with thirty-five constellations, unless perhaps someone wishes to associate with them their adjuncts, even though they are reckoned by the names of animals—such as Capra (the Goat), which is set upon Heniochus, or the Haedi (Kids), which are borne on his shoulders, or Serpens, which Ophiuchus holds, or the Panther, which Centaurus carries—which constellations are to be regarded as parts of the greater ones. These thirty-five, then, are separated by the circuit of the intervening zodiac; for some are northern (aquilonia) and others southern (austrina). Indeed, from the region of the zodiac, those depicted toward the Septentriones are called northern, while below it are numbered the southern. To the north, therefore, belong both Bears, Draco, which glides winding between them, Arcturus—whom others call Boötes—Corona Ariadnes, and Nixus (the Kneeler), whom others call Engonasis, Lyra, Cygnus, Cepheus, Cassiopeia, Perseus, Deltoton, Heniochus, Andromeda, Pegasus, Ophiuchus, Delphinus, Aquila, and Sagitta. The southern constellations are these: Hydrus, Crater, Corvus, Procyon, Orion, Canicula, Lepus, Eridanus, which flows down from Orion’s foot, Cetus, Centaurus, the ship Argo, Piscis Austrinus, Caelulum (the Chisel / Graver), and Ara. For we better believe the Water that flows from Aquarius’s urn to be part of that sign, and the star which some call Canopus and others Ptolemaeon—which, invisible to northern lands, begins to appear on the border of Alexandria—I shall reckon as part of the river Eridanus.

{{839}} These the zodiac divides, which makes up twelve equal portions of signs, yet has eleven constellations. For Scorpius occupies both its own space with its body and that of Libra with its claws, whose upper part the feet of Virgo have occupied, and the greater part Scorpius; hence some of the Greeks said Chelae (Claws) rather than Libra. I pass over the names of the twelve signs because they are commonly known.

{{840}} I understand that order would have required me to show in which circle or space each constellation lies. Yet both because this demands a great deal of exposition contrary to my purpose of brevity, and because certain constellations, divided by half or a third, scatter their limbs across different circles, I leave behind this murky obscurity. Indeed, to pass over the rest, the inner hand of Arcturus is placed within the septentrional circle and the rest of his body is assigned to the other division; the body of Cepheus, with his chest split down the middle, is attributed to different circles; Nixus, treading with his left foot on the crown of northern Draco, rises with his head into the solstitial circle, giving one arm to Lyra and the other to Corona; and other things of this sort, no less unpleasing than tedious.

{{841}} Rather, we must attend to which constellations rise and set as each sign rises. For when Cancer rises, Corona Ariadnes and half of Piscis Austrinus set, as well as Ophiuchus from his feet up to his shoulders and the Serpent which he holds except for its jaws and whole head, and half of Boötes; while all of Orion rises, along with the beginning of Eridanus and the bright star on the tongue of Canicula. When Leo rises, the remainder of Corona is hidden, along with the rest of Piscis Austrinus and the remaining parts of Ophiuchus, Serpens, and Boötes, as well as Aquila and the right side of Nixus; while the head of Hydrus, Lepus, Procyon, and the foremost part of Canicula rise. When Virgo rises, Lyra, Delphinus, Sagitta, the greater part of Cygnus, the last part of Eridanus, and the head and neck of Pegasus set; while the foremost part of Hydrus as far as Crater, all of Canicula, and the stern of the ship Argo rise.

{{842}} As Libra rises, the remaining portions of Pegasus and Cygnus, the head of Andromeda, the shoulders of Cepheus, Cetus, and the bend of the river Eridanus set; while half of Corona, the right foot of Nixus, Boötes, the rest of Hydrus except the tip of its tail, and that part of Centaurus which is shaped in the form of a horse rise. When Scorpius is born, the remaining part of Andromeda sets, likewise that part of Cepheus which lies outside the septentrional circle, Cassiopeia, and part of Orion; at the same time all of Corona Ariadnes, the head of Ophiuchus, the whole body of Nixus except his left hand, the very tip of Hydrus’s tail, and all of Centaurus except his forefeet rise. When Sagittarius is born, Orion, Canicula, and the feet of Heniochus sink; while all of Ophiuchus, the left hand of Nixus, Lyra, the head and shoulders of Cepheus, and the forefeet of Centaurus rise.

{{843}} When Capricorn rises, all of Heniochus together with his Goat and Kids, the left side of Perseus, the stern of Argo, and Procyon set; in turn, Cygnus, Aquila, Sagitta, and Altarium (Ara) rise. When Aquarius rises, the equine part of Centaurus and the head of Hydrus set; and the horse Pegasus rises. When Pisces has risen, all of Hydrus, the remaining part of Centaurus, and Crater set; moreover, the right side of Andromeda and Piscis Austrinus rise. As the sign of Aries rises, the feet of Centaurus and Altarium set; while the left side of Andromeda, Perseus from his head to his belly, and Deltoton rise. When the sign of Taurus rises ‹… when the sign of Gemini rises,› the feet of Boötes and Ophiuchus from his feet up to his knees set; while the remaining part of Cetus and the left foot of Orion rise.

{{844}} Account must also be taken of the difference in the times in which they rise or set. For those signs which rise obliquely (transversa) and set upright (recta) have swifter risings than settings; on the contrary, those which rise upright and are buried obliquely rise more slowly. For the sign of Cancer rises upright and sinks inclined, although it curves slightly toward Capricorn; it rises in ‹two hours and a› twelfth part of an hour, and sets in an hour and eleven-twelfths (deunx): in this sign the difference is smallest. Leo, however, rises in two hours and a third part of an hour, and sets in an hour and a half and a sixth part. Virgo rises in two and a half hours and a sixth part of an hour, and similarly Libra, and sets in an hour and a third part. Scorpius, on the other hand, diminishes its rising and increases its setting; for it rises in two hours and a third part, and sets in an hour and a half and a sixth part of an hour. Sagittarius rises in two hours and a twelfth part of an hour, and sets in an hour and eleven-twelfths of an hour.

{{845}} Conversely, those which rise obliquely and set upright occupy shorter risings than settings. Finally, among these is the sign of Capricorn, which rises in an hour and eleven-twelfths, and sets in two hours and a twelfth part of an hour. The next sign, Aquarius, rises in an hour and a half and a sixth part of an hour, and sets in two hours and a third part of an hour. Following this, the sign of Pisces rises in an hour and a third part of an hour, and sets in two and a half hours and a sixth part of an hour; Aries keeps the same measure for each time. ‹Taurus rises in an hour and a half and a sixth part of an hour, and sets in two hours and a third part of an hour.› And Gemini rise in an hour and eleven-twelfths, and set in two hours and a twelfth part.

{{846}} This is the inequality which distinguishes the lengths of days and nights. For when the light of the sun enters the beginning of those signs which rise more slowly, while the following signs are being born, the lengthening of the day is brought about; whereas when it enters those which rise swiftly and sink more slowly, with brief days it makes the nights longer. Finally, this reason also answers those who raise what they consider a marvelous question, arguing thus: if all the signs are reckoned at equal spaces and it is necessary that in all days and nights six signs are above the earth, all days and nights ought to be equal. Yet there is no doubt that six signs stand above and just as many lie hidden, while days and nights also vary in differences of length. For the solstitial day has fourteen equinoctial hours and a sixth, while the brumal day has nine hours and a half and a third part; in turn, the brumal nights stretch to the length of the solstitial day, whose hours of daylight the summer night takes on. Amidst so great a variety and diversity of times, it is surely inferred that the signs are not to be held equal.

{{847}} Both the truth of things and our rule refute this conclusion. For by manifold measurement with water-clocks (clepsydrae) it has been shown that all the signs contain equal spaces. For although they have different times in rising and in setting, nevertheless, if thou balancest the risings and settings of all of them, thou wilt see that when summed together they correspond to equal measures.

{{848}} Yet once this question has been set aside, another, subtler objection is raised concerning unequal spaces. For they say: if the spaces of the signs are equal, either the sun traverses certain signs at a slower pace, or the reckoning is false in its diversity of days. Now it is agreed that the sun runs through the sign of Gemini in thirty-two days, but through Sagittarius in twenty-nine, and that to the remaining signs certain portions are either added or subtracted—which certainly would not happen if both the sun were carried at a uniform speed and the signs extended over equal spaces; yet the sun hastens on with one and the same perpetual velocity. It remains, therefore, that equality of spaces must be denied to the signs.

{{849}} This difficulty, however, an inveterate opinion has fabricated, because everyone hitherto has believed that just as the earth is the center of the universe itself and of the outermost sphere, so too is it the center of the solar circle. That this is wholly false admits of no doubt; for just as the spaces of the celestial circuit and of the middle circle are different, so too do they revolve around different centers and points—whence it comes about that the earth is not the center of the solar circle, but is held to be eccentric (eccentros). For by turns, through approaches and descents toward the earth, the solar orbit and course sinks low, and likewise according to the conditions of the signs it is lifted high; and although the sun runs balanced along the linear middle in the breadth of the zodiac, nevertheless the obliquity of its path either depresses or elevates the sun’s orb. For who can doubt that Cancer and Gemini are lifted aloft, as it were, into the heights of the universe, and in turn that Sagittarius and Capricorn are brought down low by fixed deflections? Since the zodiac itself and the signs adhering to the sky keep the same interval from the earth on every side, yet the solar circle, which runs beneath it, is either carried aloft or descends, hence it comes about that the signs appear to be traversed by the sun in differing numbers of days.

{{850}} The account of the celestial signs and circles has now been run through. Next I shall discourse upon the orbs of the planets, which Straton asserts are called not planetae (wanderers) from any errors of their own—for traveling by the same laws as the sun they suffer no license for error—but planontes (those that lead astray), because their diverse variety pours mists of confusion over the attention of mortals. ‹And when I discourse upon these divine splendors in your presence, I shall name them by those designations which the religion of mortals has affixed to the majesty of each one of you,› although men have also called them by other names besides those marked by their own divinities.

{{851}} For they call Saturn Phaenon, Jupiter Phaethon, Mars Pyrois, Venus Phosphoros, and Mercury Stilbon; while to the Sun and Moon the diversity of nations has attached innumerable names. Between these seven and the fixed stars this difference above all must be observed: that the fixed stars are moved solely by the revolutions of the sky while keeping their own station, whereas these seven are both swept along by the cosmic revolutions and moved by their own proper courses.

{{852}} For as much as the rotation of day and night carries them backward, so much do they strive to make up in differing periods—that is, in a month like the Moon, or in a year like the Sun, or in thirty years like Saturn, and the rest in periods assigned according to the breadth or narrowness of the spaces which they traverse.

{{853}} All these stars, although they seem to advance toward the east, nevertheless do not strive against the universe with a rigid motion, but obliquely, fixed within the tracks of the zodiac; otherwise, from the contrary motion of its parts, the universe could not stand. Indeed, the teaching of the Peripatetics even contends that these stars do not move forward against the universe, but are outstripped by the velocity of the universe, which they are unable to match. Even if this be true, it cannot run counter to my rules. For whether Saturn, contending with the excessive velocity of the universe, is scarcely surpassed by tiny distances, and the Moon, because she moves more slowly, is overtaken within the thirtieth day by the same part of the sky—or whether, striving against the universe, the Moon is swifter because she traverses her orb in a shorter circuit, and Saturn slower on account of the breadth of his wide-spread orb—whichever thou preferrest, it does not conflict with my rules, since the motion of each is governed by its own ratios.

{{854}} Yet these seven stars have one motion in common, in that all seek the east, and another, in that all vary with differences and accidents of time. For of these, five stars undergo stations and retrogressions, whereas the Sun and Moon are swept along in a continuous course. Likewise, these two luminaries are darkened in turn by bodies intercepting them, whereas the five stars know no eclipse; likewise, three of them together with the Sun and Moon encircle the orb of the earth, whereas Venus and Mercury do not go around the earth.

{{855}} Although it must be known in general that the earth is eccentric to all the planetary orbits—that is, it does not hold the middle of their circles, which is undoubtedly their center—this general rule must also be noted for all seven: that while the universe revolves with a uniform rotation along the same track, the planets daily take on both new positions and different circles; for not one of these stars rises from the same place whence it rose the day before.

{{856}} If this is so, there is no doubt that the Sun has one hundred and eighty-three circles, through which it either returns from the summer solstice to the winter solstice or is lifted up again from the latter to the solstitial line; for through the very same shifts of circles it travels back and forth. Now while the Sun has the aforesaid number, Mars makes twice as many circles, the star of Jupiter increases twelvefold, and Saturn is multiplied twenty-eightfold in traversing those circles which are also called parallels—and the motions of all these occur together with the universe and go around the earth in risings and settings.

{{857}} For although Venus and Mercury display daily risings and settings, nevertheless their circles do not encompass the earth at all, but wheel in a looser circuit around the Sun. In fact, they place the center of their circles in the Sun, so that they are carried sometimes above it, and more often below it, nearer to the earth; from the Sun Mercury is separated by one sign and a half [45 degrees], ‹while Venus is separated by forty-six degrees.› Yet when they are above the Sun, Mercury is nearer to the earth; when they are below the Sun, Venus is nearer, inasmuch as she curves in a vaster and more wide-spread orb.

{{858}} Now as for the Moon, which is nearest to the earth, through what winding courses she is carried I shall record below. Beyond her orbit some contend lies the circle of Mercury and Venus, and others that of the Sun itself; next come those of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—all of which, that we may measure them in their proper magnitudes, a thing astrologers have not readily wished to do, our assertion must begin from a single concession of Geometry, which she herself supplies at present and which was established by Eratosthenes and Archimedes: that in the circumference of the earth there are four hundred and six thousand and ten stadia, so that from this it is proved by undoubted ratios that the circle of the Moon is a hundred times greater. And this circle in turn is found to be six hundred times greater than the Moon itself.

{{859}} Both of these facts are demonstrated in true dimension both by frequent eclipses of the Sun—showing how much shadow the Moon cast when interposed—and by comparing that shadow itself with the body of the Moon. Unless it be tedious, I shall show how I measured them. Often an eclipse of the Sun occurring in the clime Diameroes (through Meroe) darkened the entire solar disk from every side, whereas in the neighboring clime—that is, Diarhodu (through Rhodes)—only a portion of the darkening appeared, while in the clime Diaborysthenus (through the Borysthenes), with no part obstructed, the Sun shone forth whole. Whence, since it is known how many stadia the clime Diarhodu contains, I found that the shadow which the Moon had cast contained one-eighteenth part of the earth. Yet because the body from which a shadow is cast in a cone is larger than the shadow itself, from the climes in which the Sun was partially darkened on the right and on the left it was ascertained that the Moon itself is three times larger than its shadow. Therefore it was deduced that by the aforesaid ratios the Moon is found to be six times smaller than the orb of the earth.

{{860}} The Moon, moreover, is six hundred times smaller than its own orbit—which thou mayest test with water-clocks ‹…› set a vessel that drains, and as soon as the entire Moon has emerged above the horizon, remove the first vessel and set another in its place until she rises again the following night. Adding to the intervals of the circle both the degrees she advances and the body of the Moon itself—since there is no doubt that this too belongs to the circle—thou wilt find the water increases six hundredfold, whence thou mayest infer that her circle is so many times greater. If, therefore, the Moon herself is one-sixth the size of the earth, and her circle is six hundred times greater than the Moon, thou seest that the lunar circle is a hundred times greater than the earth.

{{861}} This having been demonstrated, let us examine the other circles. Who can doubt that the solar circle is twelve times greater than that of the Moon, since what she traverses in one month, the Sun traverses in twelve? The circle of Mars is found to be twenty-four times greater [than the Moon’s], that of Jupiter one hundred and forty times, and that of Saturn three hundred and thirty-six times. Whence, if the number be calculated more closely, both how many stadia Saturn’s circle contains and what fraction of it the whole earth is are discovered. For if the Moon’s circle is a hundred times greater than the earth, and Saturn’s circle is three hundred and thirty-six times greater than the Moon’s circle, then Saturn’s circle is thirty-three thousand six hundred times greater than the entire earth.

{{862}} Now let us observe the course of the Moon, which is nearer to the earth. That she possesses monthly light has been established by the assertion of the natural philosophers; whereas, as far as her own nature goes, there is no doubt that she is always of full orb. For since she is illuminated across her entire hemisphere on that side which she turns toward the Sun, even when on the thirtieth day she shows no light to us, above, where she faces the Sun, she shines back with full radiance; then, when departing from the Sun she begins to view him from the side, she acquires light below as well in proportion, until, stationed directly opposite him, she is illuminated on this side which is visible to us.

{{863}} For the brilliance of the Sun encompasses her globe on every side and bestows light upon that whole hemisphere which it then beholds, and the rays of this light reach the earth as well to brighten the night, just as if one were to catch the image of light reflected from a mirror. When this Moon overtakes the Sun in the eastern quarters, she is darkened, and when she leaves him behind in the west, she shines forth.

{{864}} The appearance of her first light, rounded with horns as it were, is called menoeides (μηνοειδής, crescent); when, withdrawing ninety degrees from the Sun, the same ray illuminates half her disk, she is called dichotomos (διχότομος, half-full); when to the aforesaid degrees she adds another forty-five, she is called amphikyrtos (ἀμφίκυρτος, gibbous)—that is, greater than half, less than full; when, withdrawing one hundred and eighty degrees from the Sun, stationed directly opposite, she illuminates the entire side which she turns toward the earth, she is called panselenos (πανσέληνος, full moon); and thereafter waning, she keeps the names just mentioned at the corresponding degrees. She traverses thirteen degrees of her orbit in a day and a night, whereas, according to the breadth of the circles they traverse, in the same interval Mars runs through half a degree, Jupiter a twelfth of one degree, and Saturn a twenty-eighth of one degree.

{{865}} Now the Moon circuits the entire zodiac in twenty-seven and two-thirds days, yet she overtakes the Sun in twenty-nine days and half of a day and night; which happens more slowly because, while she has completed her circle, the Sun himself has likewise moved forward from the same place where he gave her light, and is found in the next sign or even in the one after that. For if she received her light in the last degree of Libra, Scorpius, or Sagittarius, she will overtake him not in the following sign, but in the one beyond it; for she sometimes passes right through these three signs, just as in those opposite to them she generally finds him twice. For since the Sun lingers thirty days in those signs and thirty-two in Gemini, the Moon, which overtakes him in twenty-nine and a half days, will certainly be able to find him there.

{{866}} Moreover, the same Moon sometimes reaches full light in fourteen days, sometimes in fifteen, and most often in sixteen, yet in such a way that she compensates in laying it down. For if she fills out her light on the fourteenth day, she loses it in fifteen days, so that the total count of days is rounded out whole. Furthermore, she completes her own year in three hundred and fifty-four days; for twelve of her conjunctions are accomplished in this number, and thus it comes about that the solar year is reckoned eleven days longer, which is compensated by various intercalations.

{{867}} Let us speak of the degrees of latitude through which the Moon travels. For I intimated above that there are twelve degrees of latitude in the zodiac, through which the wandering stars range in different ways: some are carried through three degrees, others through four, others through eight, and certain ones through all twelve. For the Sun, exceeding no degree, is carried along the middle balance except on the very border of Libra; for there it deflects itself to the south or north by nearly half a degree. The Moon, however, running through all twelve, now advances to the north and now descends to the south, sinking low, ranging six degrees on either side, just as Hipparchus also agrees.

{{868}} Finally, a name has been established for her obliquity, so that she is called helicoides (spiral-like), yet in such a way that descending and ascending she cuts that very solar line—which I showed lies midway between the six degrees on either side—at either acute or wide angles; nor can she return to the exact same position—that is, situated just as she was, in the same month, in the same degree, at the same distance from the Sun, and in the same degree of latitude—except in the two hundred and thirty-fifth month, that is, in the nineteenth year [the Metonic cycle]. For to return to the same point on the same day and with the same proximities to the fixed stars takes fifty-five years; while to arrive at the same planetary aspects under the same configuration, the vast span of the Great Year must be awaited.

{{869}} Now when this same Moon, cutting the solar line, climbs toward the north, she is called hypsos hypsoumene (ὕψος ὑψουμένη, ascending in northern latitude); when she returns from the north toward the solar line, she is called hypsos tapeinoumene (ὕψος ταπεινουμένη, descending from northern latitude); when she descends from the solar line toward the south, tapeinoma tapeinoumene (ταπείνωμα ταπεινουμένη, descending in southern latitude); and when, returning thence toward the Sun, she rises again, tapeinoma hypsoumene (ταπείνωμα ὑψουμένη, ascending from southern latitude). Now these same ascents and descents cause the eclipse of either luminary. For when the Moon, ascending or descending, falls upon the solar line, if it is the thirtieth day—that is, when she is positioned with her whole body directly beneath the Sun—she causes an eclipse of the Sun for the earth; for with her body she darkens the regions lying beneath her, while to other parts of the earth, where he is not covered, the Sun shines forth. And she does not cause this eclipse every month because on the thirtieth day she is not always found on that same solar line, but, positioned either above or below it so that she cannot obstruct him, she passes by.

{{870}} Likewise, an eclipse of the Moon occurs when the Moon, stationed opposite—that is, on the fifteenth day—on the same line as the Sun, is darkened by the conical shadow of the earth. For the Sun casts the shadow of the earth along its own line, and if the body of the Moon enters this shadow, since it cannot see the light of the Sun because the earth stands in the way, it grows dark through the deprivation of its accustomed light; otherwise, when she is in a higher or lower degree of latitude, she shines back with the appearance of full light.

{{871}} Finally, for this reason eclipses cannot be repeated within the sixth month, because neither the fifteenth nor the first day can be found again on the same line of the Sun when she ranges through a latitude of twelve degrees. Hence, if returning from the north toward the Sun she stands on the near side and does not obstruct him, she is said to make a parallaxis en synodo (παράλλαξιν ἐν συνόδῳ, parallax at conjunction); if passing across from the northern side she comes into conjunction without obstructing him, she is recorded as making a katabibasis en boreio synodo (καταβίβασιν ἐν βορείῳ συνόδῳ, descending passage at a northern conjunction); if coming from the south and not obstructing him she receives light, she is said to have made a parallaxis en synodo notio (παράλλαξιν ἐν συνόδῳ νοτίῳ, parallax at a southern conjunction); whereas if coming to the solar circle from the south she obstructs the Sun, she is said to have formed an anabibazon syndesmos (ἀναβιβάζοντα σύνδεσμον, ascending node). These appearances and variations of the Moon confound mortals with their diversity.

{{872}} Now as for the Sun, which we said travels with a twofold motion—since from the east he is either swept along with the universe, or himself stretches out his own circle obliquely against the universe—yet in respect of that motion whereby he revolves with the universe, he changes the line of his rising every day; and since we call all the tracks through which he revolves circles, there is no doubt that they are one hundred and eighty-three in number. For whether he descends from Cancer, he will run through these same circles all the way to the brumal circle, or whether he comes from the winter solstice to the summer solstice, he will revolve back through the very same ones; and these, cutting the zodiac twice, are drawn continuously through opposite signs. For the first circle of Aries is the first of Libra, and likewise the second and the thirtieth; likewise the first of Taurus is the first of Scorpius. Thus, therefore, out of three hundred and sixty-six degrees are made one hundred and eighty-three circles, all of which we call parallels, and on opposite sides, as I have said, they cut the signs at the same degrees. Therefore he completes these annual circles in three hundred and sixty-five days and a quarter of a day, whether he is tending toward the solstitial circle or returning thence.

{{873}} This too must not be passed over in silence: that although there are two hemispheres—one from the equinoctial circle toward the north, the other toward the south from the same equinoctial—nevertheless the Sun traverses each in a different span of time, even though, as I have said, the signs on either side are equal. For that hemisphere which rises toward the solstitial circle is traversed in one hundred and eighty-five days and a quarter of a day and night, whereas that which sinks toward the brumal circle is traversed in one hundred and eighty days; which is surely brought about by the fact, as I said, that the earth is eccentric to the Sun’s circle, and that in the upper hemisphere the Sun is lifted higher, while in the lower it approaches closer to the confines of the earth. There is no doubt, moreover, that a shorter arc is traversed more swiftly and a wider one more slowly.

{{874}} Now when the Sun ascends to Cancer from the equinoctial quarter, he grants summer to the men who undoubtedly live between the solstitial and septentrional circles; when he descends from that same Cancer to equinoctial Libra, he makes autumn; when he withdraws to the brumal line, winter holds sway because, with warmth removed, torpor invades. Again, when he rises from wintry Capricorn to equinoctial Aries, springtime smiles; thereafter, once again in Cancer, scorching summer is renewed. Yet there is no doubt that the antipodes experience everything in reverse: for them Capricorn makes summer and Cancer winter, while the Sun holds the equinoxes as a temperate season for both sides.

{{875}} I think it superfluous now to recount the lengthenings and shortenings of the nights, since the nights of winter are equal in due proportion to the days of summer, and the nights of summer to the days of winter, while the single equinoctial night, which occurs twice a year, is equal to its day. For when the Sun travels from the vernal equinox into Cancer, all days are longer than their nights; likewise, from the autumnal equinox to the winter solstice, the days are shorter than the nights.

{{876}} The shortest day of the year, moreover, is the brumal, which has nine hours, and the longest is the solstitial, which has fourteen hours, although this varies according to the ratios of the climata (latitudinal zones). For there are eight climes: first, nearest the solstitial line, Diameroes (through Meroe); then the second, Diasyenes (through Syene); the third, Diaalexandrias (through Alexandria), which is drawn through Cyrene into Africa lying south of Carthage; the fourth and middle of them all, Diarhodu (through Rhodes), which, led through the middle of the Peloponnese and Sicily, reaches the mouth of the Baetis; the fifth is Diarhomes (through Rome), descending through Macedonia and on the other side through the Gauls and Lusitania to the Tagus; then the sixth, through the Hellespont, Thrace, and the part of Gaul bordering on Germany; the seventh, Diaborysthenus (through the Borysthenes), cutting the Pontic Sea and on the other side Germany and Britain; and the last lies beyond the Maeotic marshes and beneath the Riphaean mountains.

{{877}} Let the days, therefore, be stated according to the climes. In Diameroes the longest day has thirteen equinoctial hours and the shortest day eleven; in Diasyenes the longest day has thirteen hours and the shortest ten; in Diaalexandrias the longest is of fourteen hours and the shortest ten; in Diarhodu the longest has fourteen hours and the shortest nine; in Diarhomes the longest has fifteen and the shortest nine; in Diahellespontu the longest has fifteen hours and the shortest eight; in Diaborysthenus the longest has sixteen hours and the shortest eight; and in Diarhiphaeon the longest has sixteen and the shortest seven. Thereafter, as thou approachest near the pole, the day becomes ever longer and the night shorter; finally, it follows that right beneath the pole of the sphere the day lasts six months.

{{878}} Meanwhile, in each of these climes, as the daylight increases and decreases, it must be known that from the winter solstice the days grow longer in such wise that in the first month a twelfth part of the total time added by summer accrues, in the second month a sixth, in the third a fourth, in the fourth month another fourth, in the fifth a sixth, and in the sixth a twelfth. This too is evident: that the zodiac, curving more gently around Cancer and Capricorn, cuts the equinoctial circle almost straight across.

{{879}} So much concerning the Sun; now it is fitting to examine the courses of the planets, and especially of those which revolve around the Sun in their cosmic journey. For Stilbon (Mercury), tracing his circle in nearly a year, speeds back and forth through eight degrees of latitude with alternating variation. I recorded above that the circles of this planet and of Venus are epicycles (epicycli)—that is, they do not enclose the roundness of the earth within their own circuit, but are carried around on one side, as it were; and in order that they may appear to rise and set from time to time, they are swept up in the revolutions of the universe.

{{880}} Yet this same Stilbon, although held near the Sun by various circles, can never stray farther than twenty-two degrees from him nor stand apart by two whole signs, now passing him by, now standing stationary, or else retrograding. He varies in different configurations; for although he makes brief appearances of short duration, nevertheless he has both a rising and a setting. Indeed, when freed from the solar rays by his distance in degrees he appears quivering before the splendor of the emerging dawn, he is lifted above the earth’s horizon. For he can never rise with an acronychal rising, which can happen to stars stationed directly opposite the Sun (opposition being reckoned in the seventh sign). Thus it comes about that to this planet, which cannot stand farther away than one sign and a small portion of the next, it never happens to be lifted up in an acronychal rising.

{{881}} Finally, he knows no opposite setting either; yet he makes an evening rising when, after sunset, he shines forth in the freedom of his own light; likewise he has two settings—one when, accustomed to appear before the Sun, he is hidden by the brightness of the overtaking solar ray, and the other when, drawn into the neighborhood of the Sun by his own retrogression, he goes down into setting together with him. For the light of the Sun can never be more than twenty degrees distant from him, even when he has strayed to his greatest elongation, since he can never be found beyond the second sign. And yet he displays these disappearances and clear risings every fourth month, though not always even so.

{{882}} Venus, on the other hand, who by others is named Phosphoros, was revealed to the earth by Pythagoras of Samos, who investigated her with his calculations. She too is taught to traverse her own circle in about the span of a year—namely, in three hundred and some days—and she ranges through twelve degrees of latitude like the Moon, withdrawing fifty degrees from the Sun’s orb, although she cannot stray more than forty-six degrees. Positioned on her own circle, she encompasses him with manifold variety, because sometimes she outstrips him, sometimes follows behind without overtaking him, sometimes is carried above him, and sometimes lies beneath him, inasmuch as she does not recall her course in the same number of years every time.

{{883}} For when she retrogrades, she illuminates the sky more slowly, beyond the circuit of a year; when, however, she travels in a direct course, she completes her circle even in the eleventh month, now making her rising as Lucifer (Morning Star), now shining forth after sunset and named Vesper or Vesperugo (Evening Star). Alone among the five wandering stars she casts a shadow like the Moon, and alone, long visible against the brilliance of the rising Sun, she does not yield. In her morning appearance she generally lingers for four months, whereas in her evening appearance never more than twenty days; and both her appearances and her occultations are renewed every nineteen months.

{{884}} Now Pyrois, or the star of Mars, traveling beyond the Sun, also moves along his own circle eccentric to the earth, in nearly two years; in latitude he ranges across five degrees. Although risings, settings, stations, and retrogressions appear common to him and the two planets stationed above him, nevertheless he knows his own apogee (altitudo), his own station, and his own apsis distinct from the rest. For his altitudo—that is, where his circle rises highest above the earth—ascends beneath the region of the sign of Leo; and his first station is peculiar to him. For inasmuch as he is closely linked to the Sun, even when positioned in quadrature with him he feels his rays, lingering at the ninetieth degree on either side of him. He also has his apsis and highest withdrawal on the border of Capricorn—that is, beneath its twenty-ninth degree.

{{885}} The star of Jupiter, however, beneficent in all things as befits the ruler of the gods, completes the length of his proper revolution in twelve years, while he ranges across a latitude of five degrees. The apogee (altitudo) of his circle is found in Virgo, and his apsis in the fifteenth degree of Cancer. These ascents and descents bear witness that his circle too is eccentric to the earth.

{{886}} The star of Phaenon—that is, Saturn—loftiest of all, traverses his circle in longitude in a little less than thirty years, while in latitude he ranges through only three or even two degrees. The apogee (altitudo) of this circle rises to its height in the sign of Scorpius, whereas his apsis lies in the twentieth degree of Libra. Both for him and for the two planets below him [Jupiter and Mars], their risings are similar, occurring when the morning ray of the Sun does not press upon them within twelve degrees; then indeed they are said to make their morning rising, while their evening setting occurs when, after the Sun has sunk, they can appear at the same number of degrees’ distance.

{{887}} There is also another rising, which is called acronychal (ἀκρόνυχος), when, as the Sun sinks below the horizon, the bright star of the rising planet emerges on the eastern face of the sky. Their occultations, moreover, take place when, overtaken by the Sun’s ray, they lose the glimmer of their own light. Finally, at one hundred and twenty degrees [from the Sun] they make their morning stations, and presently, in opposition at one hundred and eighty degrees, they make their evening risings; and likewise on the other side, approaching within one hundred and twenty degrees, they make their evening stations, which those who called the former the first stations also call the second. Then the overtaking solar ray, coming within twelve degrees, overwhelms and hides them. Now the diversity of their courses and altitudes, and the causes of their standing still, retrograding, and advancing, are imparted to all the planets mentioned above by the shining ray of the Sun, which strikes them and either lifts them on high ‹…›

## Liber IX. De musica

{{888}} Now beholding the dying fires in the languishing torches, nuptial Venus bids them be renewed, saying: «What limit shall there be? To what end will these schools of cleverness hinder the bridal chamber from its sport? Courteous Pleasure, accustomed to blandishments, has grown stiff, and our boy [Cupid], turning pale, draws down his face; even comely Flora herself, wont to weave garlands for the bedposts, is anxious along with the threefold Grace; nor does the silent pipe modulate a lyric song, nor sweet Melpomene essay to strike the lyre: all things whose custom it is to glow with tender play fall silent in the pile-up of homage to a learned voice; neither does Persuasion soothe bridal hearts with allurements, nor does Stimula entice them with kindled goad; nay more, she who rejoices to loosen undergarment knots and willingly to bind hearts with a charming torch checks her winking eyes, numbed with dread, nor can she endure the visage of fierce Gorgon. If learned espousals assuredly please the gods, at least let learned Calliope bring forth songs: for, at once delighting minds with the modulation of her voice, she will banish weariness with sweet-sounding tones; indeed, wearied—I confess it—by jagged maidens, I am pained and saddened by unaccustomed delays. Bridesmaid Juno, so delightful is it to pluck these serious discourses, nor does care for the starry bridal bed spur thee on: yet I have succumbed, and accustomed as I am to graceful dances, I have no strength to look upon gloomy daughters of Cecrops.»

{{889}} And with the word, leaning back a little and reclining, she yielded herself to the embraces of Pleasure standing behind her: to whose words and assertion all the rustic deities and all the wave-born gods, and very many star-bright gods as well, assented—and especially Lemnian Mulciber [Vulcan], a husband skilled only in smithcraft, joined in praise with readier attestation; then at last, because her graceful posture and weariness itself became her as she sank back, Mars, beholding her from afar with a tender gaze of admiration, broken in voice and more languid than his wont, praised her and was seen to draw deep sighs; nor was Bromius [Bacchus] unequal in the grace of his favor; nay more, the grandson of Atlas [Mercury] himself was shaken by the anxiety of so flaming a desire that he wished to pass over the more ornate arrangements he had ordered for the bridal assembly—of such moment did it seem never to displease Venus.

{{890}} Yet the Father of the celestials, although admonished by his sister’s hint to make haste, nevertheless—lest he detract anything from the nuptial adornments or tear apart the examination of such great erudition by wanton haste—inquired without any show of hurry what number of maidens remained to be tested.

{{891}} To him the Delian [Apollo] suggested that Medicine and Architecture were standing in readiness: «Yet, since theirs is the care of mortal things and skill in earthly matters, nor have they anything in common with the ether and the gods above, not unsuitably, if they are rejected with disdain, will they keep silent in the celestial senate, to be examined more closely thereafter by the bride herself; one, however, who is the chief delight of heaven and the stars, is sought out both by the favor and by the pleasure of this examination, and thy sight cannot be deprived of her without sacrilege.

{{892}} Yet this before all I would have intimated: that, to offer and heap up a reciprocal dowry, the bride’s mother has brought other maidens whom, intending to endow them with opulent gifts, she has resolved to test in this examination; these, therefore—not unequal in number nor disparate in grace, and to be compared in the dignity of their erudition with few of those standing by, nurtured moreover in the more sacred inner recesses of the bride—with what disdain or with what delights, and with what altogether nerveless softness of the starry senate, they are to be passed over, do thou weigh in the majesty of Jovian vigor.»

{{893}} Here the Tritonian [Pallas] spoke: «And yet the maidens whom Phronesis has educated have incessantly held the secrets of the breasts of the gods above, nor has there been any one of you who has not entrusted to them the interpretations of his hidden will. Finally, very many of these have professed the priesthood of the gods, and unless through them it were sacrilege on earth to pour libations to the sacred deities, surely to obtain favorable omens would be utterly denied. Now the maidens who have already passed before us displayed marvels for the sake of erudition; these bring forward proofs of intimacy with you: for amidst the divisions between divine and human affairs, they alone have always joined colloquies between; these, therefore, once they have entered, ye shall recognize and approve, O gods.»

{{894}} While the Delian and Pallas were discoursing thus, their names were inquired into; then Phoebus said: «First shall stand Genethliace (Astrology / Nativity-casting), who, privy to ethereal reason, openly announces the threads spun by Lachesis and what is to be engendered in coming ages; next Symbolice (Divination by Signs / Lots), who, putting together things to come from ominous variations, weighs the outcomes of auspices against the turns of future events; Oeonistice (Augury) is third, through whom that famous tripod announces things to come and our whole oracle has shone forth. Finally, in proof of foreknowledge, the raven sports at my side, and the swan too is joined to me, so that their plumage, matching in color the alternations of time, may bear witness that we foreknow by birds the events of day and night; and the tripod itself promises presages of a threefold course—that is, of the present, the imminent, and the past.

{{895}} At last arrives the three-horse team of ever-supplicating sisterhood [Theurgia / Liturgical Supplication], which, by promising your favor to the multitude, first incited the uncertain minds of mortals to overcome despair in hope of aid, and made a nation destitute of protection advance into worship. This threefold woman, therefore—or three women conspiring under the appellation of a single name—who would dare to hoot off unexamined as they come into heaven, when it is certain that through them we descend to earth?

{{896}} After these, moreover, shall stand that comely maiden flashing with starry lights who has been both called and proved thy Epistularis (Lightning-Divination): to her always, Father, thou art asserted to have entrusted thy thunderbolts and committed the gleam of thy three-forked lightning—she who recognizes the paths, traces, arrival, and departures of fiery-wandering revelation. To this woman, therefore, do we deny speech, through whom the warnings of thy edicts become known to mortals? Why then, best of Fathers, dost thou delay both to introduce them briskly and to approve them?»

{{897}} After the son of Leto had spoken these words, Jupiter commanded Harmonia—who, it was suggested, alone remained of the Mercurial maidens—to come forward, and that the others should then follow in order.
