Here Luna, already warned by the double portion of the spent day, said: «The woman who is about to enter presently I can examine together with you; for the rest, as the border of night draws near, I cannot grant time to those who follow, since I must undertake the Wain and the Bull and my journey along the cosmic course, nor does the boundary of the star-bearing sweep permit me to listen to the expositions of the others; and I confess I would wish, if any authority should adjourn the examination of the maidens, myself also to learn the assertions of so illustrious an erudition—especially since a postponement is reasonably expected, lest weariness, arising from the fatigue of an inquiring mind, shut out the entire course of learned exposition from attention weighed down by toil, and that sought-after and laudable subtlety of inquiry be turned, blunted by manifold prolixity, into hatred of what is to be learned: I deem it fairer, therefore, that so great a discussion be adjourned to meet the ardent thirst of lively ears.»

{{898}} When Luna had spoken thus, all the gods eagerly assented, and thereupon inquiry was made whether the assigned day of reckoning and of conferring a dowry could be adjourned under public law. At this word, that arcane champion of ancient law was consulted with great entreaty by his descendants, and he answered that as a regular rule, even after marriage has been joined, a woman is forbidden by no laws to declare a dowry to her husband. And then Jupiter, instructed by the erudition of the skilled father, said: «I have reason to rejoice, my children, that whatever I have perceived you to wish is asserted to be lawful. For neither shall I, stiff with disdainful pride, shrink back, nor shall it irk me to hasten your counsel the more often or to examine the skill of learned maidens out of the pretense of slothful idleness—especially since, having been unseemly expelled from the earth, it behooves them to cleave to the stars alone.

{{899}} Now, therefore, let us hear the most pre-eminent of women, Harmonia, who alone remains of the Mercurial maidens: for she both can soothe the cares of the gods above before all others, gladdening the ether with songs and rhythms, and desires to celebrate our palaces alone, loathing the sluggishness of earth-born stupidity, which unteachable mortality increases without end in regard to musical arts; in fact, long since condemning men and their ruined wrestling-schools, departing from the globe and the lands, and scarcely caught now by the searches of the Cyllenian tracker, after the oblivion of a long concealment she is recalled and brought back from the hiding-places of her flight:

{{900}} her, therefore, found again after countless ages and restored at last to musical use and song, it is as delightful as it is profitable to hear; while the remaining most learned and sacred sisters of erudition, when the light of day has been renewed, shall be brought in and tested by the examination of a more attentive care.»

{{901}} Admonished by these Jovian utterances, Phoebus stepped forth to admit a maiden altogether suited to himself. Yet the Paphian goddess [Venus], stirred with congratulation for her returning daughter, nodded to her boy to sing a nuptial song beforehand; and so Hymenaeus, kindled at last with brisk vigor, with the Tritonian herself no longer refusing, began thus:

{{902}} «When the golden Moon has subdued the flame-bearing orb, I shall yoke lilies with roses: maiden and god shall unite their pledges on the sacred couch; prepare cinnamon for the bedposts. Though Hesperus guard the untouched girl continually, thou shalt see her a bride, Phosphoros: neither a mother’s tears nor nails pressed with strength shall avail to break the bonds. Fear not the bridal chamber: thou shalt be to him what Juno is to the Thunderer—she who is now sweeter than a sister; if the sacred skill of a learned husband has pleased thee, his kisses will please thee more. Rising Dawn shall behold with rosy eyes the prize of the plucked flower: I myself shall watch the pale maiden in the light hiding herself from sight.

{{903}} Already privy to love, prepare fresh garlands, Napaeae, and strew saffron on the couch and vie in pouring violets from shells upon the bed; deck the kindly pillow; draw forth the hair-pin from thy quiver, Cupid, glad to unbind her hair; remove, Queen Pronuba, the bridal veil wont to shroud maiden modesty. Comely Venus, who alone knows tender pain, shall soothe thee coaxingly; she shall prepare that breast, now hard and ignorant of yielding to a new love. Do thou only, casting down thy comely eyes, store deep within thy mind what I command: embracing the learned-speaking youth with fair arms, render fruitful pledges.»

{{904}} At these words, while Hymenaeus resonated for a good while—as if with a kind of Fescennine license—and redoubled his strains repeatedly, he deemed that he had pleased. A vast multitude of bystanders, accompanying the Tritonian and Dione, went out and hastened eagerly to meet the maiden who had at last been brought back to us. Indeed, both the crowd of maidens who, following their mistress goddesses, had chanced to stand behind, and the throng of heroes who had arrived at command, hurried forward: the former that they might learn the art of playing the lyre, the latter that they might review the memory of her sacred countenance;

{{905}} and without delay, lo, an unattempted sweetness and a song of courtly charm rang out, and melodies resounding beyond all delights of the world filled the hearing of the marveling gods: for it was no simple modulation with the tinklings of a single substance, but a combined blending of all instrumental voices admitted a certain fullness of all-singing pleasure. While this melody soothed for a good while the breasts of the bystanders and of the gods, those who had gone out a little before were seen again, preceding the arrival of the maiden and acting as forerunners of so great a retinue; yet they did not return empty of sweetness nor without some proof or work of resonant modulation: for Eratine, daughter of Cypris, and Himeros, follower of Cupid, and likewise Terpsis from the retinue of Dione, singing together most charmingly, entered first, while the boy played on the single pipe (monaulos).

{{906}} After these, playing the lyre, came Peitho, Pleasure, and the Graces, mingling the voices of the lyre and themselves leaping with harmonic movements; on the right and on the left meanwhile went before a company of heroes and long-haired sages, all murmuring with gentle modulation and low voice in whatever sweetness they chose: yet some wove anew praises of the gods and countless hymns, while others rehearsed the musical tones they had just discovered; through the middle, however, came certain rustic and melodious demigods, of whom the pandura became goat-footed Pan, the whistling pipe of knotless reed became Silvanus, and the country flute became Faunus; yet the following gathering of heroes shone forth with illustrious admiration: for Orpheus, Amphion, and Arion, most learned all, harmonizing upon gilded tortoise-shell lyres, together poured forth a soul-bending concert.

{{907}} With that song wherewith the Thracian [Orpheus] earned the right to burst even the realms of harsh Erebus, forgetful of his Eurydice; at whose chant the wrath of the senseless tigress collapsed; wherewith he is said to have tamed ravening wild beasts; wherewith Ismaros saw its acorn-bearing locks move and its woods run down from the mountain; with that song wherewith Strymon checked its waters and Tanais often flowed backward with reversed streams; wherewith the lamb lay down unharmed beside the jaws of the wolf and the hare brought its muzzle to the pitiless hound: with this he now soothed and resounded in melody, heaping up sacred songs all the more to Jove;

{{908}} and with that song wherewith Amphion gave a path of mind within rigid stone and granted mountains power to take on souls; wherewith he implanted sense in hard crags and taught rocks to wish to follow songs—for he gave walls to Thebes with sweet-sounding strings and a bulwark by the will of song. Nor did deaf seas despise the Arionian lyre when he implored extreme aid, and, though the dread gale of untamed Notus swept the foaming depths of the Scyllaean sea, he stirred up dolphins through all the waters and the grateful beast bore his lyric chants. O truly pre-eminent above the mighty divinities of the gods: Harmonia, who brought forth the praises of these men, who had power to tame Erebus, seas, stones, wild beasts, and to endow crags with sense by her tones!
After these, a more honored chorus of fountain-born maidens [the Muses] overflowed with the nectar of Pegasean voice, which, interspersed with the twin pipes of a certain Phrygian youth [Marsyas or Hyagnis], surpassed all the sweetnesses of the preceding charms:

{{909}} at last, midway between Phoebus and Pallas, sublime Harmonia entered, whose resonant head was adorned with thin plates of flashing gold; her robe also, stiff with beaten and thinned metal, tinkled gently with pleasant rattles tempered in fixed harmony to every movement and step. Her gait, as her mother the Paphian closely followed her, although she moved in fair rosy measures and poised steps, she could scarcely imitate. In her right hand, moreover, she bore as it were a shield circled with manifold rings and interwoven with wondrous tracings, which, tuned by their mutual intersections, sounded forth from those circular strings the concord of all modes; while from the maiden’s left hand hung equally bound very many small images fashioned of gold representing theatrical delights:

{{910}} Yet that orb appeared neither a lyre (chelys) nor a barbiton nor a tetrachord, but an unknown roundness had transcended the melodies of all instruments. Finally, as soon as she had entered and the concords of that same orb resounded, all those sounds which discordant sweetness had commended fell mute as though struck dumb, and Jupiter himself and the celestial gods, recognizing the grandeur of the higher melody which was poured forth in honor of a certain arcane fire and unsleeping flame, revering the inmost and paternal song, all the extramundane intelligences rose up for a brief space in veneration.

{{911}} Then the maiden, bringing to a close her ineffable waking-hymn (egersimon), turning toward Jove and joining her voice also to other measures and rhythms, began thus:

«Thee now, star-sounding Jupiter, I revere in song to be invoked first, by whom the sacred revolution is wont to roll the jeweled stars of heaven by fixed law: since most mighty beneath thy scepter-bearing diadem, begetter of all-kindred gods moving the realms, thou wheelest the universe in perpetual circuit by the Mind which thou suppliest with starry impulse;

{{912}} for the twinkling stars report thee scattering the seeds of flame-bearing tinder; thee the Phoebea lights, testifying by their sacred office while they renew purple radiance for the lands, glorify into ambrosial day; thus thine honor of night has increased its prizes with gilded horns in the monthly lamp; beneath thee the Serpent, gleaming with Wain-lit fires, parts the Parrhasian beasts [the Bears]. Thus, while Earth forbids not the tender circuit of the solid body, she is set upon the Poles and rules and is ruled in turn; thus Nereus is bidden to know the limit of the sea, and thus the upper fire to lick its fuel, so that discordant elements seethe with no strifes and, though set apart, so love their perpetual bond that they ever fear treaty-breaking Chaos. Thou ruler of the celestials, thou best of fathers, binding the stars together in pious embrace and quickening thy children with a perpetual body, hail—thou for whom our lyre is perfected, for whom all-sounding melodies echo twice full!

{{913}} Now, I beseech you, venerable offshoots of heaven, who know how to rouse the manifold barbiton, bring hearts attuned to our songs while mingled strains are borne to the sacred senate that would soothe your fellowship; now in turn, and according to the law of the divinities, I shall sound you hereafter in divided strain, and a gentle melody, pleasing to each in his own tones, shall lead down, urge on, and rouse from their places, and again soothe your goads.

{{914}} Now, therefore, kindly lights who crown the senate of the gods with venerable summit—although Etruscan rites throng to you as twice-six divinities and fatten you with victims, yet nature, shining forth in twin courses, has brought you forward with honored eminence, prefacing Phoebea names to each: for flashing Pallas and comely Leto’s daughter come into this lunar fellowship, while ardent Delius mounts the axle of Phaethon—now I myself shall pray you, chaste summits, our brothers, that the songs they bring to the gods to be wedded may not knit your venerable brows, nor our marriage-hymn bring on disdain: with strumming plectrum the nuptials shall be sung in harmony, and loose Fescennine verses shall come forth to the strings.

{{915}} Now coaxingly pluck the melody, Dione, for harsh rigor yields to love. For the very brine of foam-born Cythera knows how to approve a tender song, and Nereis moves the Muse upon her lyre; while Galatea is sounded upon shells, flaming passion draws the swimming sea-born creatures and the chorus of Phorcus, and unquenched ardor triumphs in the snowy waters.

{{916}} Now coaxingly pluck the melody, Dione, for harsh rigor yields to love. The Maenalian pines have borne a song, and struck with Lycaean measures the skillful grove of Arcadia resounds with the hemlock pipe: for the swift maiden of half-bestial Pan, turned into talking reeds, gives forth sound, and while the god chances to press her with his lips, she breathes out melody as though at his kisses.

{{917}} Now coaxingly pluck the melody, Dione, for harsh rigor yields to love. The shape-shifting boy Cupid himself is busy with torches, and binding his sweet-stringed bow with a rosy bough, he has left it in holiday idleness while the Muse prepares her reeds: for the charm of song he denies care to his arrows and has abandoned his tender reed-bed to our melody. Now, therefore, warmth and song yoked together soothe hearts; it pleases and is fitting that in turn we sing and love.

{{918}} Now coaxingly pluck the melody, Dione, for harsh rigor yields to love. The prudent maiden [Leda] was mother of the fair Laconian [Helen], yet enticed by melody she knew not how to beware of guile: for the white swan, wrapped in snowy wings—seeing that his grace was lesser with silver feathers and could not please purple-tinted eyes overmuch—suddenly began to rouse tender chants and Phaethontian muses, preparing fraud under feigned old age: thus he is brought to her mouth, snatches a rosy kiss as the maiden turns round, and folded in her lap carried off the prize of her modesty.

{{919}} Now coaxingly pluck the melody, Dione, for harsh rigor yields to love. The goddess of the second light [Diana / Luna] preferred the Latmian song to the stars of the sacred sky; following the cave, in fact, leaving the heights of heaven, burning with passion she awarded the palm to pastoral pipes; and driving away the gold of light, she throws open the folds of bleating sheep and rejoices wrapped in the cloak of squalid meadows: and then, privy to love, by night she pastures she-goats on the mountain; gazing at the stars of the universe, she cracks her whip the louder, nor has the lovesick care of an enticer to ample song compelled her to heed the tasks of the gods or bear their whispers. She scorns the honor of the night: she prefers the swineherd’s cave and rests upon hard rock, and after the realms of the Thunderer, straw is sweeter than grass.»

{{920}} All the gods were delighted and soothed by such songs of Harmonia, for no less sweetness rebounded from her strings than from the modulation of her voice; finally, Jupiter marveling, inquiry was made by what industry this had been procured, or with what resources watchful care might promise that it could be attained and learned, so that the inmost affection of minds might melt into so sweet and coaxing a softness. And then the maiden, when she saw that the precepts of her art were sought from her with the intention of examining her erudition, moderating her musical strains a little, as the Delian and Pallas also exhorted her, began thus:

{{921}} «Long since, indeed, loathing earth-born creatures and disdainful of mortals, I strike the starry orbs of heaven, wherein it is forbidden to expound the precepts of my art, since the very swiftness of the revolving cosmic machine both sings in harmony with and recognizes the melody that accords with the all-sounding beats of its measures. Yet, since the virgin who has emerged from the earth to be wedded must now dispel with celestial vigor the vanishing forgetfulness of prolonged interruption, I shall run through what has been commanded, if first I review the repudiated benefits conferred upon ungrateful mortality.

{{922}} While that immensity of inconceivable fashioning had begotten me—surely sister and twin to heaven—accompanying the courses of the starry revolution and the very rollings of the entire mass, uniting the rapid celestial splendors by measures, I did not abandon numbers: rather, when that Monad and first formation of intellectual light watered souls flowing forth from their fountains into the dwellings of the earth, I was bidden to descend as their moderator; in short, dispensing the congruence of things, I myself tempered the numbers of intelligible motions and the impulses of the entire will:

{{923}} a truth which Theophrastus labored to show was owed to all mortals; the Pythagoreans also taught, softening fierceness of soul with pipes or strings, that a bound treaty of souls clings to bodies; nor did I disdain to weave hidden numbers into limbs as well:

{{924}} this Aristoxenus and Pythagoras also testify. Finally, with most bounteous kindness I gradually disclosed the notion of my delight: for at Delphi I demonstrated strings through the Delian cithara; pipes resounded through the Tritonian, my companion, and Lydian Marsyas; the Mariandyni and Aones blew into reeds in praises of the celestials; I permitted the Egyptians to essay the pandura, nor did I deny myself to pastoral pipes imitating either the songs of birds or the rustling of trees or the murmurs of rivers; throughout the whole globe I invented lyre-players, cordax-dancers, sambuca-players, and water-organists for the convenience of human utility;

{{925}} through me, indeed, men enticed your succor and appeased the wrath of the underworld through dirges. What of the fact that wars and victories on every side were won by my songs? For the Cretans fought to the cithara, and the Lacedaemonians to pipes, nor did they enter upon the fates of battles before it had fallen to their lot to sacrifice to the Muses. What? Did not the Amazons wield their arms to reeds? One of whom, who had come out of eagerness to conceive a child, when she greeted Alexander, having been presented with a pipe-player, departed giving thanks as for a great gift. Who knows not that pipe-players went before the Lacedaemonians in Greece and the Sybarites in Italy into battles? Even now it is well known that trumpets sharpen not only prancing steeds and wars, but athletic contests and the striving of limbs.

{{926}} What? Were not the offices of peace celebrated by our songs? Many of the Greek cities, indeed, recited their laws and public decrees to the lyre. Full often I sounded forth a healing song for disturbances of souls and bodily diseases: for I restored the frenzied to sanity by symphony, which the physician Asclepiades also imitated. When the disorderly mob of the common people roared against the fathers of cities taking counsel, repeated song checked kindled seditions. Damon, one of my followers, tamed drunken youths who were correspondingly shameless and wanton by the gravity of his measures: for, bidding the pipe-player sound a spondee, he broke the madness of their tipsy disturbance. What? Did I not bring aid to bodily afflictions by continual healing? The ancients cured fever and wounds by incantation; Asclepiades likewise healed the deafest men with a trumpet; Theophrastus applied pipes to affections of the soul. Who knows not that sciatica is driven out by the sweetness of the pipe? Xenocrates freed the mad by instrumental measures; it is ascertained that Thaletas of Crete put diseases and pestilence to flight by the sweetness of the cithara; Herophilus weighed the pulses of the sick by comparing them with rhythms.

{{927}} That the senses of animals, moreover, are unhesitatingly drawn by my songs, the Thracian lyre-player [Orpheus] at least thoroughly taught, in whom not fable but truth begot glory: for whence are stags captured by pipes, fishes detained in the lagoon of Alexandria by rattling sounds, and Hyperborean swans drawn by the song of the cithara? It is ascertained that Indian elephants are held spellbound when soothed by instrumental voice, it is proved that birds are lured by pipes, that rattles break off the wailing of infants; strings persuaded dolphins of the friendship of men.

{{928}} What? They say that serpents are lured and burst asunder by chants, that acorns and harvests are charmed across fields, that ghosts are summoned and the Moon suffers eclipse; is it not ascertained by the persuasion of antiquity itself that in Lydia there are what are called the Islands of the Nymphs—which Varro too, a more recent witness, testifies that he saw—which, advancing from the mainland into the middle of the lake at the song of pipes, first move in a circle and thereafter return to the shores?

{{929}} On the Actian shore the sea sounds like a cithara. At Megara a stone plays the lyre at the blow of any stroke. I could run through countless benefits conferred by me upon mortals, so that I might be seen not to have left the earth out of eagerness to flee, but to have justly condemned the sluggishness of ungrateful humanity.

{{930}} Yet now I shall leap down to the precepts of my art, that I may bestow the promised gift upon the maiden about to be wedded. And since my office is skill in modulating well, which is contained in rhythmical and melodic structures, I shall discourse first upon melodic matters. I declare that whatever sounds properly is either a tone (tonus), or a semitone (hemitonium), or a quarter part of a tone, which is called a diesis. Now a tone is an interval with legitimate quantity, which is contained between two notes differing from one another. A semitone is that which holds the half of a tone; of dieses, however, there are three intervals: for the first and shortest, which is named tetartemoria, is so called from receiving the fourth part of a tone; it is also called enharmonic because we measure out the enharmonic genus of modulation chiefly through it. The second is greater than that: for it is named tritemoria since it has the third part of a tone, and likewise it is called chromatic because the chromatic genus of modulation is divided through it. The third, however, has the fourth part of a tone plus half of a fourth [3/8 of a tone] and is called the hemiolia of the enharmonic division because it fills out the measure of one and a half [enharmonic dieses].

{{931}} Now a tone is also very frequently called a note (sonus): and the notes across each and all of the tropes are eighteen in number, of which the first is called among the Greeks proslambanomenos, while among the Romans—because supreme Jupiter has decreed that we use the same name—it is called adquisitus (the added note); the second hypate hypaton, that is, principal of principals; the third parhypate hypaton, that is, subprincipal of principals; the fourth hypaton diatonos, that is, stretched note of principals; the fifth hypate meson, that is, principal of middles; the sixth parhypate meson, which is subprincipal of middles; the seventh meson diatonos, that is, stretched note of middles; the eighth mese, that is, middle; the ninth trite synemmenon, that is, third of conjuncts; the tenth synemmenon diatonos, that is, stretched note of conjuncts; the eleventh nete synemmenon, that is, last of conjuncts; the twelfth paramese, that is, next to middle; the thirteenth trite diezeugmenon, that is, third of disjuncts; the fourteenth diezeugmenon diatonos, which is stretched note of disjuncts; the fifteenth nete diezeugmenon, that is, last of disjuncts; the sixteenth trite hyperbolaion, that is, third of extremes; the seventeenth hyperbolaion diatonos, that is, stretched note of extremes; and the eighteenth nete hyperbolaion, that is, last of extremes.

{{932}} These, then, are the notes which compose modulation aptly and with reason: and all modulation consists of lowness (gravitas) of sound or highness (acumen); lowness is called that which softens by a certain relaxation of pitch, whereas highness is that which is stretched to the thinned edge of a slender and elevated modulation.

{{933}} Out of the aforesaid notes, accordingly, which rightly suit both individual tropes and all of them, there are three consonances (symphoniae), of which the first is dia tessaron, which in Latin is called ex quattuor (the fourth), and receives four notes, three intervals, and two and a half tones—for I have called a tone a productio; it is also of five semitones, which are equivalent to half of full and whole tones, and ten dieses, the meaning of a diesis being, as I said above, the fourth part of a tone. And this consonance stands in the epitritic ratio: for epitritos is that number which contains both the number three and a third of three, which is one, as four is to three [4:3].

{{934}} The second consonance is quinary and is called dia pente (the fifth), and consists of five notes which are divided among themselves by four intervals; it has three tones and cuts off a half—that is, three and a half tones—seven semitones, and fourteen dieses, and possesses the hemiolic ratio, which form contains both the same number compared with it and its half, as three is to two [3:2]. The third, dia pason, which is called ex omnibus (the octave), receives eight notes, seven intervals, six tones, twelve semitones, and twenty-four dieses, and consists of the diplasic ratio—that is, of the double [2:1].

{{935}} The tropes (modes), moreover, are fifteen, yet five are principal, to each of which two tropes adhere: that is, the Lydian, to which adhere the Hypolydian and Hyperlydian; second the Iastian, with which are associated the Hypoiastian and Hyperiastian; likewise the Aeolian with the Hypoaeolian and Hyperaeolian; fourth the Phrygian with the two, Hypophrygian and Hyperphrygian; fifth the Dorian with the Hypodorian and Hyperdorian. Now among these tropes there is a certain friendly concord whereby they are related to one another as sisters, as between the Hypodorian and the Hyperphrygian, and likewise between the Hypoiastian and the Hyperaeolian, and likewise a fitting and apt correspondence between the Hypophrygian and the Hyperlydian, which are coupled only in double octaves; while the mesai of the lower tropes become proslambanomenai for those which are higher. Moreover, each of the tropes also forms five tetrachords. A tetrachord is a certain disposition of four notes arranged in order whose outer notes must be consonant with each other. But of this later. Now let us return to the fixed order of our beginnings: for the matters just stated were spoken first so that the subject might be grasped more deeply.

{{936}} At the outset, indeed, when Lasus, a man from the city of Hermione, first divulged me to mortals, only three branches of my art were thought to exist: hylikon (material), apergastikon (productive / compositional), and exangeltikon (enunciative / performative), which is also called hermeneutikon (interpretive). And hylikon is that which harmonized out of persistent and similar elements—that is, sound, rhythms, and words: yet of these, those which belong to melody are called harmonics, those to numbers rhythmics, and those to words metrics. Apergasikon is a certain handling of material effecting its exercise, of which there are likewise three parts—that is, melopoeia (melodic composition), rhythmopoeia (rhythmic composition), and poiesis (poetic composition). Exangeltikon, on the other hand, is seen to belong to presentation and has three parts—organikon (instrumental), odikon (vocal), and hypokritikon (dramatic / gestural)—which the order of subjects will arrange below: now we shall speak of the primary voice (vox), as of the parent of all sound.

{{937}} Every voice is divided into two genera, continuous (continuum) and divided (divisum): continuous is like ongoing conversation, divided is that which we observe in musical modulation; there is also an intermediate kind, which is mingled of both and neither preserves the continuous manner of the one nor is cut up by the frequent division of the other, as all poems are recited in the manner of declamation. Of these, that which we draw out into divided and fixed parts is named diastematic (diastematica) and must be adapted to that part which is called harmonics.

{{938}} This harmonics, indeed, has seven parts of discussion: first, concerning notes (soni); second, concerning intervals (spatia); third, concerning systems (systemata); fourth, concerning genera (genera); fifth, concerning tones / modes (toni); sixth, concerning modulations / changes (commutationes); seventh, concerning melodic composition (modulatio), which we call melopoeia.

{{939}} And first concerning notes, wherein lies the element of the art. For a note holds the same place with us as a point in geometry and a unit in arithmetic. We call notes phthongoi: and a phthongos is defined as a small particle of modulated voice produced at a single pitch (intentio); and the pitch of which we speak is tasis, wherein the voice stands and persists. A note is called phthongos either specifically or generally; yet they have a general name and specific designations which in Greek are called idiaitata: so that just as we consider how we ought to write, so this virtue of the phthongos teaches what sharpness of pitch we should put forth or more gently relax.

{{940}} Now of these, some act and others are acted upon: tension (intentio) or relaxation (remissio) act, while highness (acumen) and lowness (gravitas) are acted upon. Raising (productio)—that is, epitasis—is the movement of the voice from a lower pitch to a high pitch, while anesis is the opposite: for from the summit of highness it descends into something low and grave. Now lowness of sound occurs when a breath is drawn from the depths, whereas highness is sent forth from the surface of the mouth. There are, therefore, innumerable notes, yet specifically across each trope only twenty-eight can come together, whose names I recorded above.

{{941}} The first of these is the adquisitus (proslambanomenos), which is called by such a name because it agrees with none at all of those which are named tetrachords, but is added from without, as though acquired, on account of its partnership with the mese, with which it is accustomed to be consonant. This adquisitus is separated by one whole tone from the principal of principals (hypate hypaton), which principal of principals, because it is placed first in the tetrachord, has received the name of a kind of ruler; finally, subprincipal (parhypate) is called that which is joined immediately beneath the principal.

{{942}} Of the principals, moreover, the enharmonic and the chromatic—which we perhaps scarcely rightly call colorabile (able to be colored), and it received this name because whatever is found lying between the principal colors, white and black, is called colorabile in Greek signification—therefore the enharmonic, the chromatic, and likewise the diatonic, which we call extenta (stretched), are the markers of the genera of modulation: for thus is established the manifold system of tetrachords. Now the principal of middles (hypate meson), because it is first of the middle tetrachord, received that name; subjoined to which is what is called the subprincipal of middles (parhypate meson); finally, the remaining three obey their counterparts: and this tetrachord is called of middles because it is placed in the middle between the tetrachord of principals and the tetrachord of conjuncts.

{{943}} After the aforesaid notes, the one that follows is named middle (mese): which is called middle because it is both the end of the low mode and the head of the coming highness in all tropes, and by its bond, as it were, both low modulation and high are connected—as in the Lydian mode, where the upright iota is its musical notation. Now after the mese, stretching upward by a semitone, will be the third of conjuncts (trite synemmenon), which in the same mode—that is, the Lydian—will have a supine lambda as its notation. After this follow those three which were expressed by us above: enharmonic, chromatic, and diatonic, which is also called paranete, which in Latin we call paenultima (next to last); after which is associated the last (nete), which is called last of conjuncts because it holds the end in this tetrachord. Now the entire tetrachord of conjuncts is so called because it is joined directly to the mese itself, which first completed the perfect consonance; and when the mese produces the following whole tone, that note which follows is named paramese (next to middle), because the sound of the conjunct modulation impinges quite closely upon it;

{{944}} thereafter comes the third of disjuncts (trite diezeugmenon), which finishes the full system of the octave, whence it is called third of disjuncts; after this follow the remaining notes: and the tetrachord of disjuncts received this name because it is torn away by one and a half tones from the end of the mese. After this is placed the tetrachord of extremes (hyperbolaion), which received the name extreme (excellens) because in each trope it rises into every height of pitch and reaches the summit in each modulation.

{{945}} Of these notes, therefore—that is, phthongoi—there are some which must stand fixed and persist, while others are movable (vagi); finally, some are named barypyknoi, others oxypyknoi, and ‹others apyknoi›. Now spissum (pyknon, dense cluster) is called a certain composite quality of three notes. Barypyknoi are those which hold, as it were, the first [lowest] positions of the pyknon; mesopyknoi, those which possess the middle positions; oxypyknoi, those which hold the last [highest] positions; and apyknoi, those which, in the arrangement of the three notes that form the pyknon, are joined by no genus or law [of dense clustering].

{{946}} Now standing and persistent (stantes atque perseverantes) are called either the apyknoi or the barypyknoi, which claim for themselves a certain form and character of principal notes: and they are named statarii (stationary) by some because they cannot receive different extensions; others, however, are held to be movable and wandering (vagi et errantes), because at times they have received wider intervals and at times smaller ones. Yet of these, some are called parhypatoeideis and others lichanoeideis: the first are so called because they are subjoined immediately to the first notes; lichanoeideis are named from the finger [the index finger, lichanos] which, as servant of the art, moves for individual songs—namely, the first finger after the thumb.

{{947}} Furthermore, some of these agree with one another, while others clash and rebound. The former are symphonoi (consonant), because they are yoked together; diaphonoi (dissonant)—that is, disagreeing—are those which, when struck, clash with one another; homophonoi (unison) are those which bear a different notation of pitch yet preserve the same level of sound. There are also other differences among notes: the first is by pitch (intentio), whereby a note differs in highness or lowness; the second is by perception of intervals, when it is joined with one or more intervals; the third is by conjunction of systems, according as it admits one or several; ‹…› and that which is called according to character—that is, kat’ ethos (κατ’ ἦθος): for high notes signify one character, and lower notes another.

{{948}} Now we must discourse concerning diastemata (intervals). A diastema is an interval of pitch within which a high and a low note are enclosed. Among intervals, some are smaller—such as those which lie in the enharmonic diesis—while the greater are those which across each trope make the double octave (bis ex omnibus), than which we can find nothing greater in the tropes.

{{949}} Moreover, among intervals some are composite (composita), others disjunct and asyntheta (uncompounded). Uncompounded (incomposita) are those which run in consecutive order, while composite (composita) are those which are coupled together out of diverse intervals. Likewise, some are recorded as logika (rational) and others as aloga (irrational): rational are those of whose agreement we can furnish a numerical ratio, and irrational are those under which no ratio lies. Likewise, some are consonant (convenientia) and others dissonant (discrepantia); and again, some are enharmonic, others chromatic, and others diatonic; likewise, some are artia (even) and others perissa (odd)—the first I call equal, the second overrunning. Now equal are those which can be separated into equal parts, as a tone into two semitones; while perissa are those which are divided into three semitones.

{{950}} Next, some intervals are dense (spissa) and others wider (rariora): dense are those which are gathered through dieses, wider those gathered through tones; and among these, some agree with one another and others clash—though the multitude of clashing ones is excessive. The consonant intervals across each trope are six: namely, dia tessaron, which we call the fourth; dia pente, which is the fifth; thereafter dia pason, which sounds from all [the octave]; also that which consists of the octave and the fourth, or of the octave and the fifth, or twice the octave, which is called dis dia pason.

{{951}} Now that which is called dia tessaron—that is, the fourth—receives four notes, three intervals, two and a half tones, five semitones, and ten dieses, and stands in the epitritic ratio, as four is to three [4:3]. Dia pente, which is the fifth, receives five notes, four intervals, three and a half tones, seven semitones, and fourteen dieses, and stands in the hemiolic ratio, which is three to two [3:2]. Dia pason [the octave] has eight notes, seven intervals, six tones, doubles the semitones [12], quadruples the dieses [24], and turns upon the diplasic ratio, which is one to two [1:2].

{{952}} That which is called octave plus fourth consists of eleven notes, ten intervals, has eight and a half tones, doubles the semitones [17] and multiplies the dieses by the aforesaid ratio [34], and stands in the diplasiepidimoiros rule, as eight is to three [8:3]. That which consists of octave plus fifth receives twelve notes, eleven intervals, nine and a half tones; the semitones are doubled [19], the dieses quadrupled [38], and it stands in the triplasic ratio, as four is to twelve [1:3, i.e., 3:1].

{{953}} That which is called dis dia pason [double octave] has fifteen notes, twice-seven [14] intervals, twelve tones, doubled semitones [24], and forty-eight dieses, and stands in the tetraplasic ratio, as twelve is to three [4:1]. Now a whole tone stands in the epogdoic ratio [9:8], and everywhere we must understand the enharmonic diesis, which consists in a fourfold subdivision.

{{954}} Now we must declare what a system (systema) is. A system is a magnitude of pitch consisting of many intervals, which, although it admits many kinds of divisions, I nevertheless pass over because I have recorded the same things under intervals. There are, however, eight complete and perfect systems in number: the first is that which is completed entire from the adquisitus, which we call proslambanomenos, to the middle, which we called mese; the second is that which stretches from hypate hypaton all the way to paramese; the third is that which is joined from parhypate hypaton to trite diezeugmenon; the fourth is that which is extended from hypaton diatonos all the way to diezeugmenon diatonos; the fifth is that which advances all the way from hypate meson to nete diezeugmenon; the sixth is that which is drawn from parhypate meson to trite hyperbolaion; the seventh is that which runs out from meson diatonos to hyperbolaion diatonos; and the eighth is that which is led from mese to nete hyperbolaion.

{{955}} Having run through these, I shall discourse upon the genera of tetrachords and of modulation. A tetrachord is the division of four strings with a specific quality. The genera of modulation are three: harmonia (enharmonic), chroma (chromatic), and diatonon (diatonic).

{{956}} And harmonia is so called because it is divided by more numerous and narrower intervals; diatonon, because it abounds in whole tones; chroma, because it is composed of semitones—for just as that which lies between white and black is called color, so this is named chroma because it lies between the other two.

{{957}} And harmonia, bounded by the immovable notes, will obey modulation upward in pitch by a diesis, ‹a diesis,› and an uncompounded ditone; toward a low pitch, on the contrary, the entire modulation is lowered in reverse order. Chroma receives melody upward in pitch in this manner: by a semitone, a semitone, and three semitones which come forth uncompounded; toward a low pitch it will run back in the opposite order. Diatonum, however, content with the notes themselves, will receive modulation upward in pitch in such wise as to complete its entire measure by a semitone, a tone, and a tone; while toward a lower pitch it will follow the reverse order—and nowadays we make use chiefly of the diatonic.

{{958}} Moreover, of these we modulate some by agoge (stepwise progression) and others by ploke (interweaving): by agoge is when note follows note in consecutive order; ploke is called when we associate non-adjacent notes. Thereafter, in modulating, one progression is called eutheia, which is straight [ascending]; another anakamptousa, which is returning [descending]; and another peripheres, that is, circling: eutheia is that which rises from low to high, anakamptousa that which sinks down in the opposite direction, and peripheres that which is adapted or serves for both.

{{959}} Yet although the divisions of tetrachords, which we call four-cleft, are innumerable, there are six recognized shades (notae / chroai): one of the enharmonic; three of the chromatic, of which the first is that which is soft and loose (mollis ac soluta), the second that which is hemiolic, and the third that which is toniaea; and two of the diatonic, one soft (mollis) and the other tense (robusta). And the enharmonic receives its intervals from the tetartemoria diesis—that is, the fourth part of a tone; that which we called the softer chromatic is coupled through the tritemoria diesis [1/3 tone]; while the chroma hemiolion itself is formed from the hemiolia diesis of the enharmonic [3/8 tone].

{{960}} Now, however, concerning tones (toni / modes). A tone is a magnitude of interval which is called tonos because through this interval before all others the first pitch that occurs is stretched—that is, from any note to another note, as from mese to paramese, as is the case in the Lydian mode if from an upright iota the signs run together to a lying zeta and pi. Yet since I have taught what, how great, and of what sort semitones and dieses are, and have also shown the names and number of the tropes, let us now speak of tetrachords: for each individual trope guards five tetrachords, as I did not pass over in silence above, and there is no doubt that their outer notes must be fitted to one another.

{{961}} A tetrachord, indeed, is the congruent and faithful concord of four notes placed in order. There are the tetrachords of principals (hypaton), of middles (meson), of conjuncts (synemmenon), of disjuncts (diezeugmenon), and of extremes (hyperbolaion). And the first tetrachord, which is lower, begins from hypate hypaton and ends in hypate meson ‹…› while that of principals and middles is linked together; that which is called of conjuncts will be the tetrachord from mese to nete synemmenon; and divided and set far apart from these, that which is fourth—of disjuncts—is joined all the way from paramese to nete diezeugmenon; while that which is joined from different notes is carried forward into a higher pitch as the fifth, of extremes: but enough has been said of tetrachords.

{{962}} Now let us look at pentachords: and similarly there is no doubt that these too are five. The first, therefore, which is lower, we shall call of principals, which begins from adquisitus (proslambanomenos) and ends in hypate meson. The next upward in pitch is of the same kind and consists of the notes from hypaton diatonos to that note of the middles which in the Lydian mode has an upright iota [mese]. The third is of conjuncts, which is led from meson diatonos to nete synemmenon. The fourth, which is formed by disjunction, is wont to be connected from mese to nete diezeugmenon. The fifth rises into highness of pitch and ends in nete hyperbolaion.

{{963}} These things which I have said are approved by the most learned masters of our art: for I am not unaware that some assert that tetrachords can take their beginnings from semitones, which is situated in diatonic modulation, concerning which I have spoken much above. Assuredly I would have it known and intimated that proslambanomenos is placed in all other systems, yet in those which are not formed through whole tones—in tetrachords—it cannot occur: for in these the beginnings are always drawn from semitones, whereas in the others which are named pentachords, and yet are not subjected to modulation so as to seem to consist of whole tones, it is added; for in these we can place the semitone second.

{{964}} Now concerning modulation (transitus modulantium, metabole). Transitus is the shifting of the voice into another configuration of sound; and transitus takes place in four ways: by genus, as from the enharmonic into either the chromatic or the diatonic; or by system, as from hypate hypaton if perchance into parhypate or into another of the systems, or when we make a transition from conjuncts to disjuncts; or by tone (key/mode), when a melody is carried across from the Lydian into the Phrygian or into another trope; or by melodic style (modulatio), when we leap down from one species of modulating into another, or when a transition is made from a manly melody into effeminate modes.

{{965}} I shall discourse next in order concerning the kind of melodic composition. Melopoeia is the habit of completed modulation; melos is the actualization of a high or lower sound; modulatio is the expression of manifold sound. The species of melopoeia are three: hypatoides, mesoides, and netoides. Hypatoides is that which is called tragic, which consists of lower notes; mesoides, which is also named dithyrambic, which preserves equal and middle pitches; and netoides, which is accustomed to be called nomic, which receives more notes from the highest register. There are also other distinctions which are called tropic melodies, and others homoiologic: yet these are more aptly substituted according to subject matters and cannot bring forward divisions of their own; and these species are also called tropes.

{{966}} The melopoeiae of different composers differ from one another in several ways: by genus, so that one is enharmonic, another chromatic, and another diatonic; by species also, because one is hypatoides, another mesoides, and another netoides; and by tropes, such as Dorian, Lydian, or the rest. Yet everyone who begins a melody must before all else pay heed to the system, and thereafter mingle and compose the notes. Let it suffice to have said thus much concerning the seven parts of our art.

{{967}} Now let us touch briefly upon rhythms—that is, numbers (numeri)—since there is no doubt that this too is a part of us. Rhythm, then, is a certain composition gathered out of perceptible time-units (tempora) connected into some habit and order. Again, it is defined thus: rhythm (numerus) is an ordered connection of diverse measures serving time in proportion to modulation, according as the voice is to be raised or lowered, and that which constrains us from license in modulation to art and discipline.

{{968}} Yet there is a difference between rhythm (rhythmus) and rhythmizomenon (that which is rhythmized), since rhythmizomenon is the material of rhythms, whereas rhythm is applied as a kind of craftsman or form of modulation. Every rhythm, therefore, is discerned in a threefold way—by sight, by hearing, or by touch: by sight, as are those rhythms which are gathered from bodily motion; by hearing, when we direct our attention to the judgment of modulation; by touch, as when with our fingers we examine the indications of pulses. To us, however, it is attributed chiefly in hearing and in sight.

{{969}} Now rhythmics (rhythmice) is the entire art in numbers, which receives certain numbers of its own conversion and obtains legitimate turns. There is also no small difference between rhythm and meter, as I shall record later. Yet because sight and hearing were said to accede to rhythm, these too are divided into three genera in like manner: into bodily movement, into the ordering of sounds and modulation, and into words which reason fitted to measures has bound together—all of which, allied together, make a complete song. Assuredly rhythm is divided in speech by syllables, in modulation by arsis and thesis, and in gesture is completed by determinate figures and schemas.

{{970}} Now the divisions of rhythm are seven: first, concerning time-units (tempora); second, concerning the enumeration of time-units which can fall into rhythm—which are judged rhythmoides, that is, resembling rhythms, and which are distinguished by three terms, namely enrhythmon, arrhythmon, and rhythmoides; third, concerning feet (pedes); fourth, concerning their genera; fifth is what we name rhythmic agoge (tempo/leading), that is, in what manner rhythm and measures are led; sixth, concerning conversions (conversiones); and last, rhythmopoeia, that is, how the creation of rhythm can be fashioned.

{{971}} The primary time-unit (primum tempus), then, is that which, after the manner of an atom, admits neither parts nor moments of division, as is the point in geometry and the monad in arithmetic—that is, a single nature content with itself: yet in words it is found through a short syllable, in modulation through a single note or interval, and in gesture through an incipient movement of the body, which we called a schema; and this will be the shortest time-unit, which I have called indivisible; whereas a composite time-unit is that which can be divided and which is either double or quadruple of the primary—for so far every time-unit of rhythm is extended. And to it there is a limit which is the boundary of a full ratio, and herein rhythm is found to resemble a tone: for just as the latter is divided through four species—that is, dieses—so this too is bounded by a fourfold modulation of time-units.

{{972}} Now of those time-units which are coupled into rhythms, some are named enrhythma time-units, others arrhythma, and the third rhythmoides. Enrhythma are those which preserve order by a fixed ratio, as in the double [2:1] or hemiolic [3:2] or in others which are joined by another ratio; arrhythma are those which agree with one another by no law at all and are joined without fixed ratio; while rhythmoides preserve rhythm in some parts and disregard it in others:

{{973}} of which time-units some are called strongyla (στρογγύλα)—that is, rounded—and others periplea (περίπλεα, overfull). Rounded are those which are hurried along more steeply and easily than a fixed pace and legitimate order demand; periplea, on the other hand, are those which weave in longer delays of composed modulation than is fitting and suspend themselves by a slower delivery. Yet of time-units, some are simple and ‹others multiple›, which are also called podic (belonging to feet).

{{974}} A foot (pes), moreover, is the first progression of rhythm joined through legitimate and necessary sounds; its parts are two, arsis and thesis: arsis is the raising of the voice, thesis the lowering and relaxation of the voice.

{{975}} Now the differences of feet are seven: by magnitude ‹…›; by composition, when we set down some feet as simple and others as composite—simple, as is the pyrrhic, and composite, as are the paeons or their counterparts. And simple are called those which are divided into time-units, while composite are those which are also resolved into feet; ‹…› while others we name alogoi—that is, irrational—of which no ratio is furnished, but an unformed sort of composition is put forth.

{{976}} Next is another difference which is sought by division: of what sort it exists—that is, poia—when those elements which have been connected are divided variously and manifoldly, and we name those which are simple feet multiple. Another is ‹by figure›, which is wont to be made through division; the seventh is that which arises by opposition, that is, when, two feet being taken, one has a longer time-unit that precedes in order while the time-unit that follows is narrower, or when the aforesaid time-units are turned in the reverse order.

{{977}} The rhythmic genera, moreover, are three, which at one time are named dactylic, iambic, and paeonic, and at another equal, hemiolic, and double; finally, the epitritic is also associated: for one, whenever it is fitted to itself, agrees as equal [1:1]; that which is double in relation to a single unit preserves a twofold ratio both of syllables and of time-units [2:1]; the number three to two is hemiolic [3:2]; and four to three makes the epitritic measure [4:3]. Now those which we called equal we shall say are the same as dactylic: in fact, in the dactylic genus the beats are bound to one another by equal right, one to another or two to a doubled number, or however rhythmic equality runs its course. Next follows the iambic genus, which I expressed above as diplasic, in which the beats of the feet preserve a double ratio to one another, whether one to two or twin units to four, or whatever runs to the double. The hemiolic, which was recorded as paeonic, occurs when the beats of the feet follow the ratio and law of the hemiolic, as three are to two. Rhythm also often occurs in the epitritic ratio, when a foot is received therein which stands as four to three.

{{978}} Yet now let us return to our order. The equal genus of rhythm, therefore, is that in which feet advance from two time-units (disemos) all the way to sixteen; and a disemos is called the foot which first is said to consist of arsis and thesis, as is leo (∪ ∪); the double begins from three time-units (trisemos), and leads eighteen syllables all the way to its end; the hemiolic takes its start from five time-units (pentasemos), and is filled out at the number twenty-five; the epitritic makes its beginning from seven time-units (heptasemos), placing its end at fourteen similar units—whose use is difficult. And all these orders of rhythms we have recorded in order that the laws of each may be preserved throughout the whole.

{{979}} Now of rhythms, some are composite, others uncompounded, and others mixed: composite have cohered out of two or more genera; uncompounded are those which consist of a single genus of feet, as are four-time (tetrasemi) feet; mixed are those which are resolved sometimes into feet and sometimes into rhythms, as we ought to understand in the six-time (hexasemos) rhythm. Moreover, of those which are said to be composite, some are gathered by couplings (copulae) and others by period (periodos): for syzygy—that is, copula—is the tight connection of two feet into one which appear to be placed dissimilar to one another; while periodos is a composition of very many feet in which dissimilar and unequal feet are associated with one another.

{{980}} Of dissimilarities, indeed, there are three differences: by magnitude, by genus, and by opposition. By magnitude, when a rhythm is composed out of a disemos or tetrasemos; by genus, when we join together a diplasic or hemiolic rhythm, or what is equally coupled out of several; by opposition—that is, by antithesis—when we either place disemi first before much stronger following feet or apply tetrasemi to following disemi. Yet it will be fitting to know that even a single foot can suffice to complete a period if it alone is inserted unequal to the rest.

{{981}} Now of those rhythms which fall into a foot, the dactylic genus is first; in which genus the uncompounded feet shall be named, which are five in number: namely, the proceleumatic, the dactyl, the anapaest, the simple spondee, and the greater spondee. And the proceleumatic is that which retains both a short thesis and a short arsis: it will use the tetrasemos (∪ ∪ ∪ ∪) more frequently; for its disemos—that is, that which is filled out by two time-units (∪ ∪)—is indeed named a proceleumatic, yet the shorter one, while the greater is that which is made out of four shorts. Now the shorter—that is, the disemos—is called syneches (continuous) because the very continuity and crowding of syllables catching up with one another extends neither any magnitude nor measure of divided power, and therefore it is fitting to use it rarely lest the crowding of short syllables chop up the very poem which ought to be delivered with some dignity. In admixture with other feet that are set down longer, however, it is gracefully fitted, so that by its intervening swiftness it compensates for their protracted delay. Wherefore the proceleumatic which is fitted to rhythms ought to take its beginning from the four-time foot (quadrisemos).

{{982}} The anapaest which is called greater will receive an arsis of one time-unit and will make a thesis of two time-units: for a time-unit is called monochronon even when a long syllable is set down—which long is wont to receive two time-units—or when three short time-units are placed together, or when there are four in number: all of which are reckoned in comparison to a long syllable. Therefore the greater anapaest will receive an arsis which is called monochronos, and is shown to have a thesis of two time-units (dichronon): wherefore, of either time-unit which was in the thesis, placed equal to itself, it behooves it to receive the double time-unit of the arsis, in such wise, however, that with each following time-unit it seems equal to the preceding; wherefore the anapaest apo meizonos (from the greater, — ∪ ∪) is called dactylic by us; whereas the anapaest which is named ap’ elassonos (from the lesser, ∪ ∪ —) is coupled out of two shorts which are in the arsis and one long which is in the thesis.

{{983}} The simple spondee will be that which is subjoined out of a prolonged arsis as well as thesis (— —); while the greater is called that which is seen to admit not only a four-time arsis but also a four-time thesis. By coupling (copula), moreover, double rhythms accede, since one will be the ionic a maiore (— — ∪ ∪) and the other the ionic a minore (∪ ∪ — —): and that which proceeds from the greater will consist of a simple spondee and a proceleumatic, which is undoubtedly a disemos [pyrrhic]; while that which is from the lesser does the reverse. And these rhythms, uncompounded, shall be placed in the dactylic genus, which will be seven in number in all.

{{984}} The dactyl, therefore, is so named because it shapes an order of syllables resembling a human finger; the anapaest, because it strikes back upward in order; the pyrrhic—that is, the proceleumatic—because it is constant either in combat or in a certain boyish game; the spondee, because it has generally served for libations (spondai); and the ionic on account of the unequal sound of its rhythms—for it has two longs and two shorts, in which meter many have often been censured. And enough concerning dactylics.

{{985}} Now let us record iambics: in which genus there are four uncompounded rhythms, two composite by coupling, and twelve by period. Those which are uncompounded are these: the iamb (∪ —), from a half arsis and a double thesis; the trochee (— ∪), from a double thesis [or arsis] and a short arsis [or thesis]; the orthios, which will consist of a four-time arsis and an eight-time thesis, so that this foot is seen to have received twelve time-units, and it has a certain affinity with the iambic foot—for in its first four time-units it chimes with the iamb, with the remaining eight time-units subjoined; thereafter the trochee which is called semantikos, that is, which on the contrary consists of eight first theses and is narrowed in the remaining arses to four shorts.

{{986}} Composite, indeed, are those which are gathered by couplings; and they are these: the bacchius which takes its beginning from a trochee and ends with the close of an iamb (— ∪ ∪ —, choriamb); while that which is a bacchius from an iamb takes its beginning from an iamb and will be fitted in the reverse order to the feet we have named (∪ — — ∪, antispast). By period is that which proceeds as though along a fixed path. Since in this genus there are twelve in number, four are taught to receive across each period one iamb and three trochees; and of these same four, the first, which is shown to have the iamb first, is denominated trochee from an iamb (∪ — | — ∪ | — ∪ | — ∪); that rhythm which receives the iamb second shall be called trochee from a bacchius (— ∪ | ∪ — | — ∪ | — ∪); that which receives the iamb third can be named bacchius from a trochee (— ∪ | — ∪ | ∪ — | — ∪); while that which admits the iamb fourth is called epitritic iamb (— ∪ | — ∪ | — ∪ | ∪ —). Of those, on the other hand, which are formed from one trochee, ‹that which has the trochee first› is called iamb from a trochee (— ∪ | ∪ — | ∪ — | ∪ —) by the learned; that with the second trochee is called iamb from a bacchius, or surely can be named middle bacchius (∪ — | — ∪ | ∪ — | ∪ —); that which receives it third is named bacchius from an iamb (∪ — | ∪ — | — ∪ | ∪ —); and that which receives the trochee fourth is called epitritic trochee (∪ — | ∪ — | ∪ — | — ∪).

{{987}} ‹…› four of these twelve which we have named by period are said to be those which preserve pairs of trochees and iambs across the period. And that which receives the trochees first shall be called double bacchius from a trochee (— ∪ | — ∪ | ∪ — | ∪ —); that which has the trochees second is named double bacchius from an iamb (∪ — | ∪ — | — ∪ | — ∪); when the trochees are placed in the middle, it shall rightly be called middle trochee (∪ — | — ∪ | — ∪ | ∪ —); and when the iambs are in the middle, it is called middle iamb (— ∪ | ∪ — | ∪ — | — ∪). All rhythms—whether uncompounded, or composite by period, or gathered by coupling—are numbered at eighteen.

{{988}} Now the iamb is so named from the fact that the Greeks called disparaging iambizein, and with this verse the ancients disparaged anyone; likewise this name comes from its pouring forth the venom of slander or malice; the trochee is so named from its making a swift return, like a wheel; the orthios is named on account of the nobility of its thesis; the semantikos because, since it is slower in time, it shapes the very signification of a prolonged and lingering pause; while the bacchii are so named because they agree most with Bacchic sounds, and that is the Bacchic sport which is fitted to those songs.

{{989}} In that genus, however, which is named paeonic, there shall be said to be two uncompounded rhythms: of which one is called paeon diagyios ‹from a long thesis and a short and a long arsis› (— ∪ —, cretic); while the other, epibatos, is joined out of a long thesis and a long arsis and a prolonged double thesis and a longer arsis. These are the rhythms of the paeonic genus which we declared beforehand to be uncompounded; nor indeed does rhythm accede in this genus either by conjunction—that is, syzygy—or by period: hence diagyios is so called as though separating double limbs, while epibatos, because it uses four limbs as it were, is coupled out of ‹two arses› and two diverse theses.

{{990}} Now when these genera have been mingled with species of rhythms, the first species will be those which are named dochmiac: of which the first that occurs is composed by this law, that it consists of an iamb and a paeon which is called diagyios (∪ — — ∪ —); this diagyios later writers surnamed the cretic. The second is the species which is shown to consist of an iamb, a dactyl, and a paeon (∪ — — ∪ ∪ — ∪ —).

{{991}} Those, moreover, which are named deducti (drawn-out) rhythms seem to be so called on account of their continuous and composite sound. And there arise rhythms which are also called prosodiac: of which some are formed by three feet—a pyrrhic, an iamb, and a trochee (∪ ∪ | ∪ — | — ∪); others by four, so that a first iamb is fitted to these three feet (∪ — | ∪ ∪ | ∪ — | — ∪); while others are wont to consist of two syzygies—that is, couplings—a bacchius [choriamb] and an ionic a maiore (— ∪ ∪ — | — — ∪ ∪).

{{992}} There are also those which are said to be irrational, which we call alogoi, and which we are also accustomed to call choreioi. They are two in number: of which one looks to the figure of a diiamb and consists of an arsis which is long and two theses, and in time-count resembles the dactylic, while in its parts it is joined to the ionic and iambic rhythm; the other is the rhythm which is named trochaeides, that is, which is seen to have a certain figure and form of a trochee, consisting of twin arses and a long thesis, formed by the reverse of the preceding.

{{993}} Of the mixed genus there are six: namely, ‹the cretic›; the dactylic through an iamb; the dactylic falling into a bacchius—that which comes from a trochee; the dactylic through a bacchius which has flowed from an iamb; the dactylic through a choreios which borrows its beginning from the likeness of an iamb; and the dactylic through a choreios which is seen to be expressed from the likeness of a trochee. And the cretic, chiming from the thesis of a trochee and ‹…›.

{{994}} ‹Rhythmopoeia is the› art of composing rhythm and the full grasp of all figures. This is divided into the same parts as melopoeia, which are these: lepsis (selection), that is, perception, through which we know where and how much rhythm is to be used; chresis (usage), through which we gracefully fit theses to arses; and mixis (mixture), through which, when it is opportune, we mingle them by art. The tropes (moods/characters), moreover, as in melopoeia so in rhythmopoeia, are three: systaltikos ‹…› we call them, and I recorded them above under harmonics.

{{995}} Let us know, moreover, that rhythm is male and melody (melos) is female; for melody is matter which is reckoned without form of its own, whereas rhythm, by a certain work of virile action, furnishes both form and varied effects to sounds.»

{{996}} When Harmonia had run through these matters with a certain restrained sweetness before the attentive gods and throngs of heroes alike, gracefully returning to the charms of songs and chants, she fell silent. And then, as Jupiter rose and the gods went before her modulating a marriage-hymn (koimesis), she came also into the bridal chamber of the virgin, to the great delight of all.

{{997}} Thou hast an old man’s tale, Martianus, which Satire played by lamplight with mingled breath, while striving to teach the Pelasgians arts scarcely friendly to Attic flesh-hooks, and so fell out into nine volumes:

{{998}} for this chatterbox, heaping learned things upon unlearned, stuffs things to be kept silent into things to be spoken, has mingled Muses and gods, and has fashioned raw liberal arts babbling in a rustic mold.

{{999}} Bursting with this very consciousness of worthlessness and swelling with gall and bile, she said: «I might have come forth in a generous cloak to be approved by learned assemblies, courteous as though from the senate-house of Mars; yet by the breath of Felix Capella—whom untaught ages saw raging in lawsuits, weighing out doggish barkings, yet giving truth to the proconsular height, and long since a buzzing drone plucked in his very flower by the sickle of a now whitening wheel; whom the blessed city of Elissa [Carthage] saw as her foster-son, haunting the sluggish neighborhood of yoked oxen on a pittance and scarcely sprinkled with gain, blinking with care and drowsy by daylight—

{{1000}} when could I ever have drawn in a decent cup the Pegasean flood created by this man?» Following, therefore, our witness who has betrayed our lethargy, my son, pardon these trifles as thou readest them through.
